First, the Mandarin numerals for one to ten are simple, as they are not made up of any other numerals in the set; the numerals for eleven through ninety-nine, on the other hand, are complex, as they are concatenations of the simple nu-merals in the set.  Second, the complex numerals of Mandarin are of two kinds: those that name the tens and those that name the units within the tens. The former add shi to a simple numeral; the latter add a simple numeral to the former. As far as meaning is concerned, the names of the tens refer to the product of ten and the number conveyed by the simple numeral; the names for the units within the tens add the number conveyed by the simple numeral to the number conveyed by the ten. All of these observations are captured by the following PROLOG program.                  32  / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS Program 3. mandarin.swipl To see Program 3 generate the pairings in (16), we may enter the fol-lowing:  mandarin(SO, _, ME, _).  Hitting Return and entering semicolons will generate, first the simple pairings, then the pairings corresponding to the higher tens, and then the pairings made by the units within the tens. Notice that the statements of mandarin.swipl classify the number (or meaning) each of these numerals conveys, telling us whether the number in question is (a) a unit or a ten, and (b) whether it is, in either case, a first or a nonfirst. To see why numbers need to be classified this way, we may look at the second batch of lines of this program. These are lines 11-15.  These lines tell us how to form the tens of our set. Or rather the tens other than the first—what Program 3 calls the nonfirst tens. This is because the first ten (or shi) is not formed this way; shi is a simple numeral formed by the tenth line of the program. mandarin([yi],numeral,1,[number,unit,first]). mandarin([er],numeral,2,[number,unit,nonfirst]). mandarin([san],numeral,3,[number,unit,nonfirst]). mandarin([si],numeral,4,[number,unit,nonfirst]). mandarin([wu],numeral,5,[number,unit,nonfirst]). mandarin([liu],numeral,6,[number,unit,nonfirst]). mandarin([qi],numeral,7,[number,unit,nonfirst]). mandarin([ba],numeral,8,[number,unit,nonfirst]). mandarin([jiu],numeral,9,[number,unit,nonfirst]). mandarin([shi],numeral,10,[number,ten,first]).    mandarin(A,numeral,B,[number,ten,nonfirst]):-  mandarin(C,numeral,D,[number,unit,nonfirst]),  mandarin(E,numeral,F,[number,ten,first]),  append(C,E,A),  B is D * F.    mandarin(A,numeral,B,[number]):-  mandarin(C,numeral,D,[_,ten,_]),  mandarin(E,numeral,F,[_,unit,_]),  append(C,E,A),  B is D + F. LANGUAGE, GRAMMAR,  PROLOG / 33  Taken jointly, lines 11-15 of the program make a conditional statement. It is that a nonfirst-ten of Mandarin is a pairing of a sound A with a meaning B provided two conditions hold:  A is the concatenation of a nonfirst unit followed by shi (the first ten). B is the arithmetical product of the numbers conveyed by the conca-tenated sounds.  Notice that reference to the nonfirst nature of the units in this statement is essential. At least if we are to meet Principledness. For we want to make a general statement to the effect that all nonfirst units can concatenate with shi in order to form a ten. We could, of course, make nine separate state-ments to this effect. But this would only fail to capture the generalization (and make the grammar longer).  But this is not the only essential use of meaning categories in this statement. Notice that this statement will only apply to the first ten (shi); this means that it will not apply to the nonfirst tens it creates. This, of course, is as desired. We do not wish to form numerals like *er-er-shi, *san-er-shi—and so on. Turning finally to the third batch of lines of Program 3 (lines 16-20), we note that they make the conditional statement that a possible Mandarin nu-meral is a pairing of a sound A and a meaning B, where  A is the concatenation of a ten and a unit (in that order). B is the sum of the meanings of said ten and said unit.  Notice that this statement applies, in one fell swoop, to all tens and all units, regardless of whether they are firsts or not. Needless to say, this statement could be replaced by eighty-one individual statements (one for each combi-nation of a ten and a unit), but only at the cost of sacrificing principledness (and, massively, simplicity).  It should not escape the reader that this general statement is made pos-sible, once again, by a judicious use of meaning categories. To be more specific, the meaning categories in the conditions to this rule have to men-tion tens and units, but not whether they are first or nonfirst. That's why ten and unit appear in these categories, while the first/nonfirst slots are filled by variables (and unnamed variables, at that, as we do not have to refer to them by name in the rule). We could of course try and contrive these conditional statements so that they would apply to sound rather than meaning categories. But this would just be wrong. There is nothing about the sounds of the nonfirst units that 34  / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS allows them to concatenate with shi in order to make a (nonfirst) ten. Or nothing about the sound of *er-er-shi that makes it unacceptable. Or any-thing about the sounds of tens and units that allow them to concatenate (in that order) to convey a sum. It is the categories that the meanings of these sounds belong to that allow or disallow the complex expressions. Meaning categories, also known as semantic categories, are therefore justified. We close this section by observing that the two conditional statements above involve the statements B is D * F. B is D + F. The first asserts that B is the product of D and F; the second states that B is the sum of D and F. But how does PROLOG know this? Because PROLOG has the interpretation of such statements already built-in. The interpreter was born knowing how to add and multiply—so to speak. 7 Grammars as Deductive Systems A deductive system is a finite set of statements, of which some are condi-tional, and some unconditional. The unconditional statements are called the postulates of the system; the conditional ones are considered the rules of inference of the system. Deductive systems worth their salt have rules of inference that apply to their postulates in order to yield theorems or deduc-tions; the order in which postulates and rules combine to yield a theorem is called a proof of the theorem. Every grammar we have seen so far is a finite set of conditional and un-conditional statements. Consequently, all our grammars are, by definition, deductive systems. Take for instance spanish.swipl. It consists of six unconditional statements and four conditional statements (see Program 2 above). These statements are the postulates and the rules of inference of a deductive system. The theorems of the system are the pairings of catego-rized sounds and categorized meanings that this grammar generates—and hence the language it specifies. The proofs of these theorems are the various orders in which the postulates and the rules of the system combine to derive it.  The proof of a theorem of the system may be represented as a tree such that: Every leaf of the tree is a postulate of the system. Every other node of the tree is the result of a single application of a sin-gle rule of inference to the immediate ancestors of that node in the tree. The root of the tree is the theorem in question.LANGUAGE, GRAMMAR,  PROLOG / 35  Thus, the proof of the theorem in (17) would be the tree in (18).  (17) spanish([adan,ayuda,a,eva], [sentence],    [adam,helps,eve], proposition). The tree in (18) tells us how to deduce the assertion in (17) from span-ish.swipl (when regarded as a deductive system). Thus, to deduce (17) you take the second postulate and apply the second rule of inference to it. This yields a new assertion. You then apply the third rule of inference joint-ly to this assertion and to the sixth postulate. This yields another new asser-tion. You then apply the fourth rule of inference jointly to it and the first postulate. Thus you arrive at (17), Q.E.D. The tree in (18) is one of the most involved proofs that can be drawn within spanish.swipl. A simpler proof can be seen in (19)  (19)  spanish([eva],[nounphrase],[eve],[entity]).    spanish([a,eva],[nounphrase,accusative],[eve],[entity]).  Here you start with the second postulate and apply to it the second rule of inference in order to deduce the pairing of an accusative nounphrase and an entity. An even simpler proof that can be drawn within spanish.swipl can be represented like this:  (20)  spanish([eva],[nounphrase],[eve],[entity]).  (20) represents a proof that consists of the second postulate of span-ish.swipl. Notice that (20) is also a tree—albeit a tree that has only one node. This node is both a leaf and a root. The condition on trees that re-quires that every node other than a leaf be the result of a single application of a single rule of inference is satisfied vacuously here, as (20) contains no node other than a leaf. Trees with only one node may be called degenerate. They correspond to proofs that consist solely of a postulate. Such proofs may be deemed degen-erate as well. A deductive system in which every proof is degenerate is not worth its salt. But this should not be a reason to preclude postulates from being theorems. In terms of grammars, unconditional pairings of sound and meaning are still pairings of sound and meaning.   36  / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS   s p a n i s h ( [ a d a n ] ,  [ n o u n p h r a s e ] ,  [ a d a m ] , [ e n t i t y ] )  S p a n i s h ( [ a y u d a ] , [ v e r b ,  t r a n s i t i v e ] ,  [ h e l p s ] , [ r e l a t i o n ] )  s p a n i s h ( [ e v a ] ,  [ n o u n p h r a s e ] ,  [ e v e ] , [ e n t i t y ] )  s p a n i s h ( [ a , e v a ] , [ n o u n p h r a s e ,  a c c u s a t i v e ] , [ e v e ] , [ e n t i t y ] )   s p a n i s h ( [ a y u d a , a , e v a ] ,  [ v e r b p h r a s e ] ,  [ h e l p s , e v e ] , [ p r o p e r t y ] )  s p a n i s h ( [ a d a n , a y u d a , a , e v e ] , [ s e n t e n c e ] , [ a d a m , h e l p s , e v e ] , [ p r o p o s i t i o n ] )  (1 8)  S am pl e st ru ct ur al  d er iv at io n re la tiv e to  s p a n i s h . s w i p 1  i s h . s w i p 1  LANGUAGE, GRAMMAR,  PROLOG / 37  Notice that proofs like the ones we have just discussed proceed con-structively, building the pairings they admit from scratch. Consequently, they do more than tell us what pairings to admit; they tell us how these pair-ings are put together. Both in their sound and in their meaning. Take for instance the tree in (18). It tells us the way in which the sound in (17) was built (what syntacticians call the constituent structure of this phrase). Thus, the tree tells us that [adan,ayuda,a,eva] is a sentence that consists of a nominative nounphrase [adan] and a verbphrase [ayuda,a,eva]. This verbphrase in turn consists of a verb [ayuda] and an accusative nounphrase [a,eva], which is in turn constituted by a nominative noun-phrase [eva].  Beyond this, the tree in (18) tells us also the way in which the meaning in (17) was built (what semanticists call the compositional structure of this meaning). Thus, the tree tells us that[adam,helps,eve] is the proposi-tion that the entity [adam] has the property [helps,eve], and that [helps,eve] is the property of bearing the relation [helps] to the entity [eve].13 We will say that a tree like (18), which represents the way in which a pairing is built, is the structural description of the pairing. The structural description of a pairing will therefore represent both the constituent struc-ture of its sound and the compositional structure of its meaning. Alternatively, we may want to represent the conditional and uncondi-tional statements that license a pairing relative to a grammar. This is what might be called the derivational history of the pairing relative to the gram-mar. Take for instance the pairing in (17). We could represent its deriva-tional history relative to spanish.swipl as indicated in (21), where PN is the Nth postulate of spanish.swipl (for 1 ≤ N ≤ 6) and RN is the Nth rule of inference of spanish.swipl (for 1 ≤ N ≤ 4). For the sake of uni-formity, we will enumerate the postulates and rules of inference of a deduc-tive system from top to bottom.                                                               13 Trees like these are not new. They are essentially the analysis trees of Richard Montague. This is because the grammars whose action they represent are essentially those Montague proposed as well (see the suggestions for further reading at the end of this chapter). 38  / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS (21)  P1  P6  P2                                                    R2(P2)                                      R3(P6,R2(P2))               R4(P1,R3(P6,R2(P2)))  As a matter of fact, since each node in (21) recapitulates its own deriva-tional history, a more compact representation of the derivational history of (17) could be devised. It would simply be its root, namely  (22) R4(P1,R3(P6,R2(P2)))  Cast this succinctly, the derivational history of an expression will be called the expression’s signature. The signature of an expression is thus its identi-fying mark—one that encapsulates all the information that a grammar that admits it can provide. Ideal deductive systems are both sound and complete. A deductive sys-tem is sound if and only if every statement provable within it is true; it is complete if and only if every true statement is provable within it. Grammars are intended to be ideal deductive systems. For they will be sound if every pairing of sound and meaning derivable from it is indeed a pairing made in the language. And they will be complete if every pairing made in the lan-guage is derivable from the grammar. In other words, a grammar is sound if it does not overgenerate, and it is complete if it does not undergenerate. 8 Why PROLOG? All the points we have made in the preceding section regarding span-ish.swipl could have been made with mandarin.swipl. Or with any of the grammars presented in this book—if not with any explicitly formu-lated grammar. For grammars have been traditionally regarded as theories that consist of two components: a dictionary and a set of rules of construc-tion. But a dictionary (known also as lexicon) is a set of unconditional LANGUAGE, GRAMMAR,  PROLOG / 39  statements. And rules of construction (sometimes referred to as the gram-mar proper) are conditional statements. Consequently, a case can be made that grammars may in general be regarded as deductive systems whose sets of postulates are their dictionaries and whose rules of inference are their rules of construction. The theorems of these systems are the expressions they specify, and their proofs are their structural descriptions. If grammars are deductive systems, then PROLOG is eminently suitable to reason with them, as the PROLOG interpreter is software developed to carry out inferences within deductive systems (or within deductive systems whose statements are cast in the PROLOG programming language). This deductive nature of PROLOG was, incidentally, one of the reasons for measuring the complexity of a computation in terms of the number of infer-ences it involved. PROLOG was created by Alain Colmerauer, Philippe Roussel and Rob-ert Kowalski in the 1970s at the University of Aix, which is located in Mar-seille, France. Its name is short for "Programming in Logic", as the syntax for PROLOG is very close to that of (first-order) logic. The impetus of PROLOG was to mechanize inference so as to carry out automatic theorem-proving (and to do so with a programming language that was close to a hu-man language—French in the case of the creators of PROLOG). The practi-cal interest of this enterprise was to discover new truths of logic and math-ematics; its theoretical appeal was to model an important aspect of human intelligence, namely inference.14  Currently, PROLOG is applied to situations outside mathematics and logic, and is one of the preferred programming tools for natural language programming (NLP)—as well as for modeling certain aspects of cognition (the original goal of Artificial Intelligence or AI). PROLOG exists in stand-ard versions which have allowed it to be widely developed and supported, and to give rise to the particular approach to programming known as Logic Programming. PROLOG is ideal for exploring the consequences of a set of statements without having to worry about how those consequences are arrived at. This is why PROLOG is considered a declarative programming language rather than a procedural one. And this declarative nature of PROLOG is responsi-ble, to a large extent, for the ease with which this programming language can be learned and used. Throughout this book we will use a version of PROLOG developed by Jan Wielemaker and called SWI-PROLOG. Although SWI-PROLOG can be downloaded for free from its website (http://www.swi-prolog.org), we                                                             14  The key aspect of human intelligence for classical Artificial Intelligence, which regards human intelligence as the ability to draw inferences quickly and unconsciously. 40  / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS suggest that you make a voluntary contribution to the project, be it financial or otherwise. As indicated in the Introduction, we will not assume familiarity with PROLOG in this book. But we will not provide a systematic introduction to PROLOG either. We will simply provide as much instruction as is neces-sary to carry out the goals of this book—to show how to pair linguistic sound and meaning efficiently. Readers may support this instruction with tutorials (see Appendix C) and textbooks (see the suggestions for further reading at the end of this chapter). 9 Facing Infinity and Indeterminacy We have seen that one of the things that makes language interesting is that it is infinite. But grammars of the kind developed above can handle infinity comfortably. Consider by way of example Program 4 below.  Program 4. halving.swipl  Program 4 is very small, as it consists of only five lines of code (the first of these lines is an unconditional statement, while the last four are a condition-al one). Yet, it generates an infinite set of expressions,15 namely  one ‘1’ half of one ‘0.5’ half of half of one ‘0.25’ …  The reason this program can do this is that the conditional statement can apply to its own output. And can do so indefinitely many times, producing different expressions every time. In the case at hand, the statement does three things. First, it takes a numeral and appends ‘half of’ to it. Crucially, it                                                             15 You should verify this by loading Program 4, entering english(SO,SC,ME,MC), and following up with semicolons. Remember to enter ‘w’ to override abbreviations.  english([one], [numeral], [1], [number]).  english(A, B, [C], D):-  append([half, of], E, A),  english(E, B, [F], D),  C is F/2.  LANGUAGE, GRAMMAR,  PROLOG / 41  does this even with the numerals it has already appended ‘half of’ to. Se-cond, it takes a number and halves it. Again, it does this even to the num-bers it has already halved. Third, it assigns the sounds and the meanings it produces to the same categories as the sounds and meanings it applied to. Even if the latter were sounds and meanings created by the rule itself. Rules that can apply to their own output are said to be recursive. Recur-siveness is the most important way in which a finite set of finite statements can specify an infinite language. Recursiveness formalizes the celebrated dictum of Wilhelm von Humboldt (1767-1835): language makes an infinite use of finite means (Humboldt 1836, 122). We have also seen that another thing that makes language interesting is the fact that it is a relation between sound and meaning that is not one-to-one but rather many-to-many. This may happen due either to synonymy or to homonymy. Synonymy occurs when different sounds have the same meaning—as sofa and couch. Homonymy (or homophony) occurs when two different meanings have the same sounds—as in bank (the financial institution) and bank (a margin of a river). The grammars we have been developing can also deal comfortably with these issues. The reason for this is that grammatical statements may differ with respect to sound without having to differ with respect to meaning (and, at the same time, may differ with respect to meaning without having to dif-fer with respect to sound). In other words, grammatical statements may specify sounds and meanings independently of each other, thus providing modular accounts of each. Sample statements that may deal with synonymy and homonymy can be found in (23) and (24), respectively (23) english([sofa], [noun], [seat, upholstered,   multipersonal], [property]).  english([couch], [noun], [seat, upholstered,   multipersonal], [property]). (24) english([bank], [noun], [institution, financial],  [property]).  english([bank],[noun],[margin,river],[property]). 10 Other Preliminaries We conclude this chapter by mentioning a number of issues that should be raised before we proceed any further. The first of these deals with the im-portance of accounting for the acquisition of language. For, we humans are not just able to use language; we are also able to discover it. This, in fact, is what we all accomplished when we learned our native languages. We came 42  / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS up with a grammar that (a) specified the fragment of the language we were exposed to and (b) went far beyond that fragment, allowing us to produce and understand expressions that we had never heard before. Interestingly, coming up with a grammar is the exact inverse of using a grammar. Thus, while a language learner must arrive at a deductive system given (some of) its deductions; a language user must arrive at (some of) the deductions given the system.16 Linguists should say how this comes about. Especially because it is such a remarkable feat. Although language is enor-mously complex, just about everyone learns it—from limited data, without explicit instruction, and by age four. Perhaps the account of how we acquire language can be worked into explanatory adequacy. One way to do so would be to say that a grammar attains explanatory adequacy if it can be discovered mechanically given the data a learner is exposed to (in conjunction with some, probably innate, principles that are yet to be discovered). Or perhaps the evaluation of grammars is best carried out independently of their learnability. The truth is that work on these issues is still in its infancy. Yet, two things seem clear already. First, that we are genetically endowed to learn languages (other-wise we cannot explain how we all learn, swiftly and with ease, something as complex as language—and learn it just from the bits and pieces of it that we are exposed to). Second, we must be exposed to language before puberty if we are to learn it natively (otherwise we cannot explain the dismal verbal performance of feral and abandoned children who have been deprived of linguistic interaction with their parents). Another important issue to raise now is the priority of competence over performance. Linguistic competence is knowledge of language; linguistic performance is use of language. This distinction is one we have already encountered when we dealt with the infinity of language. We want to say that to know language is to know infinitely many expressions, but to know infinitely many expressions is not the same as to use them all. The distinc-tion arises also when we want to say that we know a language even if we make mistakes when we use it. Especially when we acknowledge them as such. The mistakes we make may arise through lapses of attention, incom-plete control of relevant vocabularies, unfamiliar contexts of use, flawed communicative strategies, unsuitable levels of education, or high degrees of intoxication. Linguistics wants to represent knowledge of language while ignoring the mistakes made in using it.  The need for the distinction arises also because the data of linguistics should not be limited to the expressions that have been used up to a certain                                                             16 Using two terms distinguished by the American philosopher Charles Saunders Peirce (1839-1914), language use is deductive, while language acquisition is abductive. LANGUAGE, GRAMMAR,  PROLOG / 43  point in time. Large as this corpus may be, it will be necessarily finite. So a grammar for the language that licenses them must go beyond it. This is not to say that performance is not worthy of study; it is. But not before competence is understood. At least to some degree. The question, then, is not whether to study performance, but when; it is a question of what our priorities should be. The issue can be cast in methodological terms. Knowledge of language is but one of many factors that affects performance. Therefore, it is simpler to study the knowledge of language before we study its use. This situation is not unheard of. It is, in fact, an instance of what goes under the rubric of idealization in science—the wisdom of disregard-ing some aspects of your object of study. At least on a first approximation. A classic example of scientific idealization is Newton’s decision to disre-gard the effects of friction when he set out to study movement, pretending that bodies moved in a vacuum. Needless to say, physics didn't leave things where Newton took them. It developed a theory of friction to complement the theory of movement Newton advanced. Taken together they painted a full picture of actual movement—or a fuller one that either one could paint. And did so in a simple and principled way. This was due, to no small meas-ure, to the decision to idealize away friction at the outset. A third preliminary that we should mention at this point is the primacy of the spoken over the written uses of language. As we have stated repeated-ly, language is a relation between sound and meaning. Not a relation be-tween writing and meaning. The reason for this is that writing is only an attempt to reproduce the spoken language in order to access it in the future. It is like a photograph of a tree. It might resemble the tree, but should not be confused with it. The point can be made most clearly with the acquisition of literacy. Children need to speak before they write. To expect otherwise would be absurd. Writing presupposes speaking because it attempts to re-produce it. Beyond this, writing is a nontrivial step in the history of civilization. Not all languages have developed a writing system. In fact, the ones that have are in a distinct minority. If we were to define language as a relation between writing and meaning we would exclude from consideration a ma-jority of true languages—relations between sound and meaning. Fourth, the term grammar may be understood in two different senses: descriptive and prescriptive. Taken descriptively, a grammar is a set of de-clarative statements that purport to describe a language; taken prescriptively, a grammar is a set of injunctive statements that purport to affect a language. Descriptive grammars seek to understand a language by consulting with speakers about the way they speak; prescriptive grammars seek to preserve a language (or a particular stage in the history of a language) by urging its 44  / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS speakers to speak in particular ways. The rules of a descriptive grammar state the way speakers actually speak; the rules of a prescriptive grammar state the way speakers should speak. At their best, prescriptive grammars enhance our communicative skills; at their worst, they stifle individual ex-pression and are used as tools for social discrimination.  Be that as it may, we will use the term grammar in a purely descriptive sense throughout this book, as the prescriptive sense of the term is simply irrelevant to our goal, which is to capture the actual—and not the pre-scribed—nature of language.  Fifth, we say that a description of a language is synchronic if it de-scribes the state of a language at one particular moment in time, and that it is diachronic if it describes the historical development of language—its change through time. Most contemporary descriptions of language are syn-chronic in nature. And all the linguistic descriptions in this book will be synchronic as well. This is not because diachronic descriptions are not worth doing. Far from it. Diachronic linguistics has more than proved its worth. It has produced, on the one hand, monumental reconstructions of entire linguistic families. On the other, it has succeeded in showing that behavior that is erratic when seen synchronically is actually regular when seen diachronically.  The reasons for doing synchronic linguistics here stem again from the purposes at hand. We want to describe the possible pairings of sound and meaning, not their development through time. Furthermore, a case can be made that synchronic linguistics is a preliminary to diachronic linguistics— the same case one could make that one can only do evolutionary anatomy after one has done anatomy pure and simple. That synchronic linguistics is at all possible is shown by the fact that speakers can acquire languages without having any idea about the historical development of the languages they have acquired. Since they are able to pair sound and meaning in a regu-lar and effective way, linguists studying that language should be able to pair those sounds and meanings as well. Finally, we should say a word about the intuitive data of contemporary linguistics. For most of its history, the study of language was based on writ-ten sources. Or on recorded speech elicited through carefully scripted inter-views. This data was then subjected to statistical analyses in order to ensure that it was representative of the universe it was drawn from. Starting in the 1950s, however, a new kind of data was brought into linguistics—the intui-tions a native speaker had of his or her own language. At first, the intuitions pertained to grammaticality. Intuitions of grammaticality are undoctored intuitions of correctness by native speakers. For example the intuition we get that (25a) is somehow correct but (25b) is not. Even if both are fully understandable.  LANGUAGE, GRAMMAR,  PROLOG / 45  (25)  a.   I am  Tarzan.  b.  *Me Tarzan. Asterisks, like the one in (25b), which were originally used in diachronic linguistics to mark expressions that were not attested in the body of data (the written corpus), were then pressed into service to mark expressions which did not elicit, in a native speaker, an intuition of correctness or grammaticality. Eventually, intuitions about meaning were invoked as well. The intui-tion that an expression like bank is ambiguous, for example. Or the intuition that sofa and couch are synonymous. Or the intuition that married bachelor is contradictory while female daughter is redundant. Or the intuition that Kim would be pleasing in (26a) but pleased in (26b). (26)  a.  Kim is eager to please.  b.  Kim is easy to please. Then intuitions appeared about correctness of sound—for example that gleece is a possible word of English but lgeece is not (even though neither one of them is an actual sound of English). And correctness regarding word formation, so that we feel that we may say baker and typist but not *bakist or *typer (even though both are easily understandable). In all of these cases, the intuitions of correctness are “gut feelings” that are felt not in the gut but in the mind of the native speaker. Needless to say, this methodology can be attacked on grounds of subjectivism. But the re-course to intuitions can be defended if the intuitions in question are shared, thus becoming intersubjective rather than merely subjective. Thus, if a lin-guist claims an intuition nobody else acknowledges, the analysis which is based on such an intuition will not carry much weight with others.  The rationale for admitting intuitions into linguistics was principled. It derived from two observations. They are the need to distinguish between competence and performance, and the need to recognize the infinite nature of language (see above). Taken together, these observations imply that some of the recorded data will be incorrect, and some of the correct data will go unrecorded. Consequently, we sometimes need to go beyond a cor-pus (if not against it). 11 Suggestions for Further Reading Many of the ideas presented in this chapter come from two linguists. They are Ferdinand de Saussure (1857-1913) and Noam Chomsky (1928-). Thus, the idea that language is a system of signs comes from Saussure—as does the priority of the spoken over the written, the descriptive over the prescrip-46  / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS tive, and the synchronic over the diachronic. Saussure’s key work is his Cours de Linguistique Générale—a work which was not written by him, but rather by his students Charles Bally and Albert Sechehaye, who gathered the notes they took of his lectures and published them posthumously in 1916. Saussure’s Cours has been translated to English and many other lan-guages. Good commented editions of the work are de Mauro (1972) and Harris (1983). The notion of grammar presented in this chapter is due to Chomsky, as are the arguments for the infinity of language, the distinction between com-petence and performance, recursiveness, and the modularity of grammars. His two most influential works are Syntactic Structures and Aspects of the Theory of Syntax. The first was a groundbreaking work published in 1957; the latter was published in 1965, and represents the classic statement of his views. It should be pointed out, however, that Chomsky’s views on lan-guage and linguistics have changed continuously (some would say inces-santly) since then. As far as I can see, the definition of computational linguistics proposed here is novel. And so is the definition of the complexity of a grammar in terms of the number of inferences it takes to recognize the expressions it generates. The idea that a grammar is a finite set of quaternary relations is, essentially, due to Richard Montague. See Montague (1974) and Dowty et al. (1983). The use of polynomial growth as a criterion of efficiency was originally proposed in Cobham (1965). An evaluation of a number of theo-ries of grammars in terms of complexity has been carried out by Ristad (n/d). There are a number of tried and true textbooks on PROLOG. These in-clude O’Keefe (1990), Bratko (2001), Sterling and Shapiro (2001), Clocksin & Mellish (2003), and Blackburn et al. (2006). Applications of PROLOG to Linguistics were made since the early days of PROLOG (see the discussions of Definite Clause Grammars in most PROLOG textbooks). Different applications can also be found in Pereira and Shieber (1987), Gazdar and Mellish (1989), Dougherty (1994), Covington (1994), Carpenter et al. (1994-), Matthews (1998). The origins of PROLOG have been chroni-cled by its own creators. See Colmerauer and Roussell (1993). 12 Exercises 1. Give another example of an infinite set of English expressions. 2. Notice that your nouns name entities (some of which will be male, and others female), but that your adjectives name properties (which, as such, are neither male nor female). Yet, the sounds of both nouns and adjectives exhibit gender distinctions. 51 2 Phonology: The Nature of Linguistic Sound  1 Introduction Language has been characterized as a relation between sound and meaning. But what, exactly, is linguistic sound? This is what phonology is all about. Or at least phonetics, which is the study of sound from a physical point of view. It might be thought that the sounds of language are the various se-quences of letters of the alphabet—supplemented, perhaps, by spaces, ac-cent marks and other diacritics, and signs of punctuation. This answer would be wrong on several counts. First and foremost, letters are graphic entities. Sounds, on the other hand, are acoustic entities. Whereas graphic entities can be seen, read, or photographed, acoustic entities can only be heard, recorded, or played back. Aside from this, relatively few languages have developed writing systems (a point we made in Chapter 1). And only a fraction of these systems are alphabetical in nature. So reducing sounds to letters is something that could only apply only to a fraction of a fraction of languages.  To give an idea of the variety of writing systems, we may point out that alphabetical writing systems are attempts to represent the individual sounds of a language. In addition, there are writing systems that attempt to repre-sent syllables—the various kana systems of Japanese, for example. And then there are writing systems that seek to represent the properties (or fea-tures) of the individual sounds—Korean hangul, for instance. Moving from sound to meaning, writing systems may also be found that try to represent meanings—or, more accurately, words—instead of sounds. Like the hiero-52 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS glyphic systems of Ancient Egypt and Mesoamerica.  But if sounds cannot be identified with letters, could they be identified with the sounds represented by sequences of letters? Not even that. First, there is no explicit procedure to assign, to each sequence of letters, the sound it is intended to represent. The point can be made most clearly with a poem attributed to T. S. Watt: English As It Is I take it you already know Of tough and bough and cough and dough Others may stumble, but not you, On hiccough, thorough, laugh, and through. Well done! And now you wish,  perhaps, To learn of less familiar traps? Beware of heard, a dreadful word That looks like beard and sounds like bird. And dead: it’s said like bed, not bead; For goodness sake, don’t call it deed! Watch out for meat and great and threat. (They rhyme with suite and straight and debt.) A moth is not a moth in mother, Nor both in bother, broth in brother. And here is not a match for there. Nor dear and fear for bear and pear. And then there’s dose and rose and lose. Just look them up—and goose and choose And cork and work and card and ward And font and front and word and sword And do and go and thwart and cart— Come, come, I’ve hardly made a start! A dreadful language? Man alive. I’d mastered it when I was five! As can be readily seen, one and the same sequence of letters (say O-U-G-H) can represent a wide variety of sounds (cf. tough, bough, cough, dough, through, thought, hiccough). And, conversely, a single sound may be repre-sented by a wide variety of sequences (cf. the main vowel in me, dear, deer, suite, retrieve, receive, Li, and Caesar). In extreme cases, we simply need to know what word we are talking about before we know how to pronounce it! Instances of this common occurrence include R-E-A-D which may be pronounced like reed or like red. Or H-O-U-S-T-O-N, which is pronounced PHONOLOGY: THE NATURE OF LINGUISTIC SOUND / 53  like Hugh’s ton when it is the name of the town in Texas and like house ton when it is the name of the street in New York City. Or B-R-I-D-E-S-H-E-A-D, which could be pronounced like bride’s head, bride’s heed, bride shed, or bride sheed. All of these cases are unpronounceable out of identifying context. But this is another way of saying that we need to know how a word is pronounced in order to know how it is pronounced—or that there is no fully explicit relation between letters and sounds. 2 The Phones of Language To make headway we need to set aside alphabets and other writing systems, and focus on sound itself. To do this we will proceed in two steps. First, we will try to identify the individual sounds of language; then we will try to define the possible sequences these sounds form. The individual sounds of language are called phones. The theory that specifies the possible sequences of phones is called phonotactics. The problem of identifying the phones of language—or at least the known phones of the known languages—is mostly solved. The International Phonetic Association is an organization that was founded in Paris in 1886. Its goals were to identify the phones used by the thousands of known lan-guages and to develop a general notation to represent them. This notation was dubbed the International Phonetic Alphabet (or IPA), and has succeed-ed, to a large extent, in representing the phones found in the 6,000 or so languages of the world. The current version of the IPA is embodied in the 2005 Chart of the International Phonetic Association. This chart has been reproduced in Appendix E. The chart of the International Phonetic Association contains a large number of technical terms. We will get to some of them in the next section. In the meantime, we will illustrate the value of this chart by representing the phones of Standard American English. At least in a first approximation. We do this below, where phones are enclosed in square brackets.1 THE PHONES OF STANDARD AMERICAN ENGLISH 1. Take for instance the problem raised by the sequence of letters O-U-G-H. As we saw above, this string of letters could be pronounced in many ways. With the aid of the IPA, we can distin-guish between all of these pronunciations easily and systematically: Spelling  Pronunciation tough   [taf] bough   [baw] cough  [kof] dough  [dow] through  [θɹu] thought  [θat] or, for some speakers, [θot] hiccough  [hɪkəp]  And, conversely, the similarities in the pronunciations of me, dear, deer—and so on—is represented by means of the symbol [i] in all of them: [mi], [diɹ], [swit], etc. Present tense read is represented [ɹid], while past                                                             2 This is the first of our only two departures from the IPA. PHONOLOGY: THE NATURE OF LINGUISTIC SOUND / 55  tense read is represented [ɹed]. The Texan town Houston is transcribed [hjustən], while the New York street is written [hawstən] (although in fast speech speakers might drop these words' final vowels, also known as schwas, so they would be pronounced [hjustn] and [hawstn] respectively). Similarly, the four possible pronunciations of brideshead would be as fol-lows: (1)  a.  [brajdzhed] as in bride’s head         b.  [brajdzhid] as in bride’s heed  c.  [brajdʃed] as in bride shed d.  [brajdʃid] as in bride sheed The representation of the sound of a linguistic expression in terms of the “letters” of the International Phonetic Alphabet is called the phonetic tran-scription of that expression. Consequently, to represent the sound of an expression in these terms is to phonetically transcribe it. Proficiency in phonetic transcription requires a lot of practice. So we encourage the reader to do the exercises at the end of this chapter. And go beyond them by attempting to phonetically transcribe some of the reader's favorite literary passages. You may leave a space in your transcriptions be-tween word and word as long as you realize that we do not pause between words when we speak. You may also leave in punctuation signs (as long as you realize that they involve much more than pausing). You should not in-clude capital letters in your transcriptions, though, as they do not sound any different than low-case letters. To help you get started with your practice, bear in mind that some of the trickiest things to transcribe in English are diphthongs (a diphthong is a sequence of two non-consonantal sounds in the same syllable). Here are ways to transcribe them:  SPELLING    PRONUNCIATION my    [maj] cow    [kaw] ray   [ɹej] pew   [pju] boy   [boj] row   [ɹow] swan  [swan] swam  [swæm] swell  [swel] sweet  [swit] swollen  [swolən] swoop  [swup] 56 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS Another tip is to check that your English transcriptions never use any of the following letters: c, q, r, x, y. They are not in the allowed set of tran-scription characters for English. There are, to be sure, more phones to English than the ones we have seen thus far. The two Ps in peep, for example, sound differently. As do the two Ts in toot and the two Ks in kook. So far, we have only represented one of the sounds of these pairs. And there are two different L-like sounds in lull. And the Ds in caddy don’t sound the same as the D in candy. Be that as it may, the sounds we have identified above are the fundamental ones. So we will stick to them. At least for now. We will return to these issues in Chapter 5 below. 3 Representing Phones in PROLOG Let’s assume that the phones of language are indeed the ones recognized in the IPA (as modified above). How could we say this in PROLOG? One straightforward way to do so would be to write a program that would simply list all the possible sounds as phones. This program would consist of a ra-ther long list of unconditional statements. This list may begin as follows: phone(p).  phone(b).  … Unfortunately, current text editors cannot properly display all the IPA char-acters that we would need to include in this program. In fact, some cannot even withstand them. So, instead of writing a program that mentions all the phones, we might just have to write a program that will generate them on the fly. To do this, we need to resort to a set of standard encodings of IPA characters, namely their unicode decimal values.5 For the characters above, they are as follows: p 112 f 102 č 269 æ 230 b 098 v 118 ǰ 496 ə 601 t 116 θ 952 l 108 u 117 d 100 ð 240 ɹ 633 ʊ 650 k 107 s 115 j 106 o 111 g 103 z 122 w 119 a 097 m 109 ʃ 643 i 105 ʌ 652 n 110 ʒ 658 ɪ 618   ŋ 331 h 104 e 101                                                               5 For information on the remarkable Unicode Project, visit http://www.unicode.org. For deci-mal unicode values for other IPA characters, visit  http://www.phon.ucl.ac.uk/home/wells/ipa-unicode.htm#numbers or consult International Phonetic Association (1999). PHONOLOGY: THE NATURE OF LINGUISTIC SOUND / 57  Using these values, we may write a PROLOG program that specifies the phones of English. We have called this program phone.swipl, and have listed it as Program 5 in Appendix H. Notice that the first line of that pro-gram is :- ['fulldisplay.swipl']. This is an instruction we give to the PROLOG interpreter to add all the statements of fulldisplay.swipl to those of phone.swipl. All the other lines of this program involve a predicate name. name is a binary predicate that is built-in in PROLOG. name(A,B) is true if and only if A is a string of characters and B is the list of decimal unicode values corresponding to these characters. In the case at hand, A is a string that con-sists of only one character and, consequently, B is a list consisting of only one unicode value. To illustrate, the first actual statement of the program is as follows: phone(A) :- name(A,[112]). This statement says that A is a phone if its decimal unicode value is 112. Since ‘p’ is the character whose unicode value is 112, what the preceding statement is, in effect, asserting, is that [p] is a phone. You should check that phone.swipl is running properly by consulting this program, entering phone(X) at the prompt and following through with semicolons. You should get back all the English phones we have de-fined in terms of their "unicode names". If you encounter any difficulties, go back to Appendices A and B. 4 Phone Properties Identifying the phones of language is the first step towards defining the class of possible sounds of language. The next step is identifying the possi-Remember If p.swipl is any PROLOG program, the line :-[‘p.swipl’] found in a PROLOG program q.swipl is an instruction to add all of the statements of p.swipl to those of q.swipl. Remember name(A,B) is true iff A is a string of characters and B is the list of decimal unicode values corresponding to these characters. 58 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS ble sequences of phones—what we have called the phonotactics of lan-guage. To do this in a principled way we will need to appeal to certain properties of phones, namely the properties involved in pronouncing them. These properties will be binary in nature. This means that, for every prop-erty and for every phone, the property will either apply to the phone or not. ‘Third’ possibilities are therefore excluded. To be more specific, we exclude the possibility that a property may both apply and fail to apply to a phone. Or that a property may apply to a phone—but only to a certain degree. Or only on some contexts but not on others. Or that a property neither applies nor fails to apply to a phone. The properties we have in mind are the following:6 CONSONANTIC: This property is true of sounds articulated with some degree of obstruction in the oral cavity; it therefore applies to all of the consonants (but none of the vowels) of Section 2 above. SONORANT: This property applies to sounds which are naturally (or normally, or most effortlessly) voiced (see below). In English, the class of sonorants includes nothing but the vowels, the nasals (m, n, ŋ), the lateral (l), the approximant (ɹ), and the glides (j, w). NASAL: This property holds only of sounds which resonate in the nasal (rather than the oral) cavity. Consequently, in English, it applies only to m, n, and ŋ. VOICED: This property characterizes sounds that involve vibrating vocal folds (the lip-like organs in the larynx, which is commonly known as Adam's apple). Thus, in English, it applies only to b, d, g, v, ð, z, ʒ, ǰ— as well as to all sonorants. CONTINUANT: This property is true of phones throughout the articula-tion of which the air flows uninterrupted. In English it is true of all phones other than the stops (p, b, t, d, k, g, m, n, ŋ) and the affricates (č, ǰ).  LABIAL: This property applies only to the phones that involve the lips. It therefore describes, in English, the bilabials (p, b, m), the labiodentals (f, v), the lip-rounded glide (w), and nothing else. ALVEOLAR: This property holds of phones which are articulated at the alveolar ridge. Thus, in English, it holds only of t, d, n, s, z, l, and ɹ.                                                             6 I am indebted to my colleague Orhan Orgun for help with deciding on this set of properties. PHONOLOGY: THE NATURE OF LINGUISTIC SOUND / 59  PALATAL: This property characterizes the phones articulated at the pal-ate (or roof of the mouth). In English it holds of the palatals proper (ʃ, ʒ, č, ǰ, j) and the alveopalatal (ɹ), which is both alveolar and palatal. ANTERIOR: This property is true of phones articulated with an obstruc-tion at the alveolar ridge or before. In English it therefore applies to all labial (p, b, m, f, v) and alveolar (t, d, n, s, z, l, ɹ) consonantic phones. VELAR: This property applies to sounds articulated at the velum (or soft palate)—the veil like continuation of the palate. In English, these would be k, g, ŋ, and w. CORONAL: This property holds of phones involving the tip (or crown) of the tongue. Thus, in English, it is true of the interdentals (θ, ð), the al-veolars (t, d, n, s, z, l, ɹ), and the palatals (ʃ, ʒ, č, ǰ, j, ɹ). SIBILANT: This property characterizes ‘whistling’ (or s-like) phones. In English, these phones are only s, z, ʃ, ʒ, č, ǰ.  HIGH: This property is true of vowels articulated with the tongue in a relatively high position. Consequently, it applies in English only to i, ɪ, u, and ʊ. MID: This property applies to vowels articulated with the tongue at middle height. In English, this describes only e, o, ə, and ʌ. LOW: This property holds of vowels articulated with the tongue in a relatively low position. In English, this property is extremely discrimi-nating, as it is true only of two phones: a and æ. BACK: This property characterizes vowels articulated with the tongue in a relatively back position. In English, this describes only u, ʊ, o, and a. CENTRAL: This property is true of vowels articulated with the tongue at mid-back (or mid-front) position. In English, this property, too, is ex-tremely discriminating, as it is true only of two phones: ə and ʌ. TENSED: This property applies to vowels articulated with relatively high muscular tension. Again, only two phones satisfy this property in Eng-lish. They are i and u. STRESSED: This property holds of vowels that may serve as syllabic nuclei. In English they are all except ə. It should be pointed out that every English phone satisfies a unique sub-set of articulatory properties. Each phone may therefore be completely characterized—that is, defined—by the properties in this subset. Take [p], for example. [p] is consonantic, labial, and anterior. And so is [b]. But [b] is 60 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS also voiced, while [p] is not. Along the same lines, [b] is like [m]. But [m] is nasal and sonorant, whereas [b] is not. For a detailed presentation of what English phones satisfy what articulatory properties the reader is referred to the table in Appendix F.  The articulatory properties we have just discussed are usually called phonetic features, and the plusses and minuses in the table in Appendix F are considered their values or specifications. 5 Natural Classes of Phones We may write a PROLOG program to tell us what English phones satisfy what properties. A straightforward way to do so would be to use rules like the following: cns(A) :- name(A,[112]). This rule states that A is consonantic if A is (the phone represented by) the decimal unicode character with value 112. Since ‘p’ is that character, the rule in question in effect asserts that [p] is consonantic. We have written such a program. It is called properties.swipl, and is listed as Program 5 in Appendix H. Notice that the first line of that program is :- [‘phone.swipl’]. As explained above, this is an instruction we give to the PROLOG inter-preter to add all the statements of phone.swipl to those of proper-ties.swipl. This makes sense, of course, because we want the prop-erties to apply to the phones. To have PROLOG list all consonants sequentially, we should consult this program and then enter  cns(A).  (entering semicolons after each non-final response). More interestingly, to have PROLOG list all consonants  simultaneously, we would enter  findall(A,cns(A),B).  The query above is one of several ‘all-solutions’ queries that are built-in in PROLOG. This query is satisfied if and only if B is the list of all As such that cns(A). Thus, upon entering it, PROLOG would respond: B = [p,b,m,t,d,n,k,g,ŋ,f,v,θ,ð,s,z,ʃ,ʒ,č,ǰ,l,ɹ,j,w,h]. PHONOLOGY: THE NATURE OF LINGUISTIC SOUND / 61  Remember  findall(A,B,C)is true iff C is the list of all A such that B. But what if we wanted to find phones that satisfy more than one articu-latory property at the same time? PROLOG can generate them for us by means of a conjunction of queries. Let’s say, for example, that we wanted PROLOG to enumerate the set of voiced consonants. We can make PROLOG do this by entering the following,  cns(A), voi(A).  and entering semicolons after each nonfinal response. But what if we want PROLOG to generate the voiced consonants simul-taneously? Notice that simply entering the following will not do,  findall(A,cns(A),voi(A),B). The reason for this is that findall is a three-place predicate, and the pre-ceding query treats it as if it were four-place instead. Fortunately, the solu-tion to this problem is easy. Simply enclose the complex condition in paren-theses. Thus, instead of the preceding query, enter the following one. findall(A,(cns(A),voi(A)),B). Now PROLOG responds with the following, which is, precisely, the class of voiced consonants.  B = [b,m,d,n,g,!,v,#,z,%,',l,(,j,w].  Notice that certain properties are incompatible with each other. Being anterior and being high, for example. The former is true only of consonants; the latter only of vowels. Doing a findall query on them, say  findall(A,(ant(A),hih(A)),B).  elicits the response B = [ ], so the set of phones that are both anterior and high is appropriately empty. It is often the case that we want to find the phones that do not satisfy a particular property. Thus, the important class of vowels is the class of non-consonants. How can we generate that class? Somewhat surprisingly, we can do it with complex negative queries like the following, 62 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS phone(A), not(cns(A)). to which PROLOG will respond by listing the nonconsonantic phones of English one after the other. To have them listed all at once we would of course write: findall(A,(phone(A),not(cns(A))),B). The reason it is surprising that negative queries would work at all is that we did not say in properties.swipl whether vowels were consonantic or not. PROLOG simply decided that they weren’t because it could not prove that they were! This apparently rash decision is not capricious. PROLOG was designed to assume that the programs it consults are logically complete. This means that if something is true (about predicates defined in these pro-grams), then that something will be provable (from these programs). Con-sequently, if PROLOG cannot prove something about these predicates, then that something must indeed be false. In the PROLOG literature, this design feature is known as the adoption of the closed world hypothesis, and the view of negation embodied in this hypothesis is known as the view of nega-tion as failure (or rather as failure to prove). It should be pointed out that the adoption of the closed world hypothesis is extremely convenient to our purposes, as it allowed us to say, in prop-erties.swipl, what phones satisfy what properties, while remaining silent about what phones do not. This in turn allowed us to cut significantly on the length of the program, which consists of 176 rules instead of 646 (which would be one per phone per property). This is a savings of more than 73%. Seen in retrospect, the adoption of the closed world hypothesis also came in handy when we had to reject nonpairings of sound and meaning. Take for example spanish.swipl. Suppose we proposed it the query in (2). (2) spanish([adam],_,[adam],_). The PROLOG interpreter would respond false. But how could it do so when spanish.swipl says nothing about (2)? Because the interpreter could not derive it and, assuming that spanish.swipl was logically complete, (2) just had to be false. Similar points can be made of manda-rin.swipl. And, indeed, of all the grammars we will discuss in this text. The tools we have presented thus far are quite powerful. They allow us to generate a large number of classes of phones. Consider for instance the queries in (3a)-(3c). (3) a. findall(A, (phone(A), not(cnt(A)), not(voi(A)),   not(sib(A))), B). PHONOLOGY: THE NATURE OF LINGUISTIC SOUND / 63  b. findall(A,(phone(A),hih(A),tns(A)),B). c. findall(A,(phone(A),snt(A),not(nas(A)),alv(A)),B). They allow us to generate the classes in (4a)-(4c), respectively. (4) a. B = [p,t,k].  b. B = [i,u].  c. B = [l,ɹ]. As a matter of fact, using these tools we can generate up to 319 classes of phones.7 Interestingly, large as this number is, it pales by comparison with 234, which is the number of classes that can be made randomly with the 34 phones of English. To be more precise, the classes of phones that can be defined in terms of our articulatory properties is less than 7% of the classes of phones that can be made randomly with 34 phones. We will call the clas-ses in this 7% natural.  To be more precise, we will say that a natural property is a conjunction of one or more fundamental properties (or negations of fundamental proper-ties). A set of phones is then a natural class if and only if it comprises all and only the phones that satisfy a natural property. As a logician would put it, a natural class is the extension of a natural property, and a natural proper-ty is an intension of a natural class (notice that there can be but one exten-sion of a natural property, while there can be many intensions of a natural class). Natural classes are important because they are the only ones for which there is a rationale; they are the only ones that exist on principled grounds. Given our choice of fundamental features, the grounds in this case are artic-ulatory. To remain descriptively adequate, we will refrain from formulating sound rules that involve unnatural classes of phones—i.e. classes of phones which are not definable as conjunctions of fundamental properties (and their negations). If we succeed, natural classes will suffice to describe sounds; they will therefore be the classes of phones that are naturally oc-curring (in the languages of the world)—hence their name. 6 The Syllable Natural properties allow us to formulate the phonotactics of language in a principled way. For phones form syllables before they form sounds. And natural properties play a key role in the formation of syllables. To see how                                                             7 For, notice that there are 3 ways in which each of the 19 properties may appear in the second argument of a findall query: they can be mentioned in the positive, mentioned in the nega-tive, or simply go unmentioned. Not all these classes will be distinct, though. And many will be empty. So the 319 figure in the text is only a maximum. 64 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS this plays out, we will present a deductive theory of the English syllable.8 This deductive theory consists of two postulates and eighteen rules of infer-ence. The rules of inference do five things. First, define syllable as the con-catenation of an onset and a rhyme (R1). Second, define onset (R2–R11). Third, define rhyme as the concatenation of a nucleus and a coda (R12). Fourth, define nucleus (R13–R15). And fifth, define coda (R16–R18). The postulates of the theory will simply ensure that a degenerate sequence of phones—namely the empty sequence—can serve both as an onset (P1) and as a coda (P2). Here is the deductive theory in full:  POSTULATES P1. The empty sequence is an onset. P2. The empty sequence is a coda. RULES OF INFERENCE R1. The sequence of an onset and a rhyme is a syllable. R2. Any consonantic nonnasal phone is an onset (so p, b, t, d, k, g, f, v, θ, ð, s, z, ʃ, ʒ, č, ǰ, j, l, ɹ, j, w, h are all onsets). R3. Any nonvelar nasal phone is an onset (so m and n are both onsets). R4. Any nonsonorant, noncontinuant, nonpalatal phone followed by an al-veolar palatal phone is an onset (so pɹ, bɹ, tɹ, dɹ, kɹ, and gɹ are all on-sets). R5. Any nonsonorant, noncontinuant, noncoronal phone followed by a son-orant, nonnasal, alveolar, nonpalatal phone is an onset (so pl, bl, kl, and gl are all onsets). R6. Any nonsonorant, noncontinuant, nonlabial, nonpalatal phone followed by a velar continuant phone is an onset (so tw, dw, kw, and gw are all onsets). R7. Any nonvoiced, continuant, labial phone followed by a sonorant con-tinuant alveolar phone is an onset (so fl and fɹ are both onsets). R8. Any nonvoiced, continuant, nonalveolar, coronal phone followed by an alveolar palatal phone is an onset (so θɹ and ðɹ are both onsets). R9. Any nonvoiced, continuant, alveolar phone followed by a consonantic, sonorant, nonnasal, nonpalatal phone is an onset (so sl and sw are both onsets). R10. Any nonvoiced, continuant, alveolar phone followed by a nasal non-velar phone is an onset (so sm and sn are both onsets).                                                             8 This system draws heavily from Goldsmith (1990, §3.6.2). PHONOLOGY: THE NATURE OF LINGUISTIC SOUND / 65  R11. Any nonvoiced, continuant, alveolar phone followed by an onset headed by a nonvoiced, noncontinuant, nonpalatal is an onset (so sx is an onset if x is itself an onset–albeit one that begins with p, t, or k). R12. The sequence of a nucleus and a coda is a rhyme.  R13. Any nonconsonantic phone is a nucleus (so i, ɪ, u, ʊ, e, o, ə, ʌ, a and æ are all nuclei). R14. Any mid noncentral phone followed by a consonantic, sonorant, nonnasal, nonalveolar phone is a nucleus (so ej, ew, oj, and ow are all nuclei). R15. Any low back phone followed by a consonantic, sonorant, non-nasal, nonalveolar phone is a nucleus (so aj and aw are both nuclei). R16. Any nonsonorant phone is a coda (so p, b, t, d, k, g, f, v, θ, ð, s, z, ʃ, ʒ, č, ǰ, j, l, ɹ, and h are all codas). R17. Any nasal phone is a coda (so m, n, and ŋ are all codas). R18. Any liquid phone is a coda (so l and ɹ are both codas). This deductive theory can be turned into a PROLOG program that we will call syllable.swipl. We give it below, indicating the rules and postulates its first twenty statements correspond to. (R1)  syllable(A):-  onset(B),  rhyme(C),  append(B,C,A).          (R2) onset([A]):-        phone(A),cns(A),not(nas(A)). (R3) onset([A]):-     phone(A),nas(A),not(vel(A)). (R8) onset([A,B]):  phone(A),not(voi(A)),cnt(A),not(alv(A)),cor(A),  phone(B),alv(B),pal(B). (R9) onset([A,B]):-     phone(A),not(voi(A)),cnt(A), alv(A),  phone(B),cns(B),snt(B),not(nas(B)),not(pal(B)). (R10) onset([A,B]):-      phone(A),not(voi(A)),cnt(A),alv(A),  phone(B),nas(B),not(vel(B)). (R11) onset([A,B|C]):-       phone(A),not(voi(A)),cnt(A),alv(A),  phone(B),not(voi(B)),not(cnt(B)),not(pal(B)),  onset([B|C]). (P1) onset([]).       (R12) rhyme(C):-        nucleus(A),  coda(B),  append(A,B,C). (R13) nucleus([A]):-      phone(A),not(cns(A)). (R14) nucleus([A,B]):-  phone(A),mid(A),not(ctr(A)),  phone(B),cns(B),snt(B),not(nas(B)),not(alv(B)).  (R15) nucleus([A,B]):-  phone(A),low(A),bck(A),  phone(B),cns(B),snt(B),not(nas(B)),not(alv(B)). (P2) coda([]).       (R16) coda([A]):-        phone(A),not(snt(A)). (R17) coda([A]):-       phone(A),nas(A). PHONOLOGY: THE NATURE OF LINGUISTIC SOUND / 67  (R18) coda([A]):-  phone(A),snt(A),not(nas(A)),alv(A). (NEW) syllable(A,B):-  length(A,B),  syllable(A). (NEW) :- ['properties.swipl'].          It will be noticed that the statements corresponding to our two postu-lates P1 and P2 appear interspersed among the rules of inference R1–R13. This is for two reasons. The first is to keep together all the rules that deal with onsets and codas.9 More importantly, we are capitalizing on the fact that the PROLOG interpreter will apply the rules of a program in the order in which they are written, so that the interpreter will generate the empty sequence as the last onset but as the first coda. This is meant to reflect the fact that, as far as the languages of the world go, empty onsets are rare while empty codas common. This will play an interesting role in Chapter 5 below. The statements of syllable.swipl use only two resources we have not explained thus far. The first of these resources is the binary predicate length, which appears in one of the two NEW statements of sylla-ble.swipl. The meaning of this predicate can be gathered by pointing out that length(A,B) is true iff B is the number of members a list A has. The number of members of a list is also called its length. Hence the name of this predicate. The purpose of appealing to list lengths (and a second def-inition of syllable) will become apparent below. The second unexplained resource in syllable.swipl is the vertical bar (or stroke). This resource is found in (the equivalent of) R11, where we find the term  [A, B | C] This term stands for a list L such that the first two items of L are A and B (in that order), while C is a list which gathers the rest of the elements of L. To                                                             9 PROLOG will issue a warning if the clauses defining some predicate are not all contiguous. Programs with such predicates will still run, though. A warning is not an error... Remember  length(A,B) is true iff B is the number of members of a list A. 68 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS check your understanding of this notation, note that the following holds whenever C is a list:  append([A, B], C, [A, B | C]).  Vertical bars may separate the first N members of a list from the rest (where N is any number from 1 to the full length of the list). When N is in-deed the full length of the list we have that  [A1, A2, ..., AN | []] (for, if we have enumerated all the elements of the list to the left of the stroke, the list gathering rest of the elements of this list must be empty). If N = 1, then we have that [A | B]. Here A is called the head of the list and B its tail.  Notice that syllable.swipl also included the instruction to con-sult the statements of properties.swipl. The rationale for this should be obvious. None of the conditions in the rules of this program would be understandable without the aid of the statements of proper– ties.swipl. Needless to say, all the elements of phone.swipl were added to properties.swipl, so they will be added to our syllable pro-gram as well. And one of the latter was the fulldisplay.swipl re-quirement that responses to our queries not be abbreviated. Let’s turn now to what we can accomplish with syllable.swipl. First, we can of course generate a large number of English syllables by en-tering syllable(Syllable). (and following up with semicolons). But it might seem that this way of gen-erating syllables is somewhat random. We can improve on this by generat-ing syllables in order of increasing length. To do this, we may appeal to the other new statement in syllable.swipl. It is a statement that requires the interpreter to find the length of a syllable before computing it. And this PROLOG does by picking lengths in ascending order (and then determin-ing whether there are any syllables of the lengths thus picked). As a conse-quence of this, our program will indeed generate syllables in order of in-creasing length. Try this: Remember  If B is a list, then the following is true:  append([A1,A2,…,AN], B, [A1,A2,…,AN | B]). PHONOLOGY: THE NATURE OF LINGUISTIC SOUND / 69  syllable(Syllable,Length). Or, if you only want the syllables, not their lengths, try this: syllable(Syllable,_). Needless to say, one can also generate onsets, rhymes, nuclei, and codas as follows:  onset(Onset). rhyme(Rhyme). nucleus(Nucleus). coda(Coda).  To generate all the syllables simultaneously, we may use the following findall query:10 findall(Syllable, syllable(Syllable), List). To count how many syllables are in this list, we may issue a complex query consisting of this findall query and a length query: findall(Syllable,syllable(Syllable),List), length(List,Length).  PROLOG will respond by telling us that syllable.swipl can generate 20,608 syllables. Yet, it can efficiently accept well-formed syllables like [s, p, ɪ, n] and reject ill-formed ones like [p, s, ɪ, n]. To verify this, enter the following: syllable([s, p, ɪ, n]). syllable([p, s, ɪ, n]). But we should not look only at what syllable.swipl generates; we should also look at how it generates it. syllable.swipl admits [s,t,ɹ,a,j,k] as a syllable. And does so thus:                                                             10 This query may yield such a long response that it may flood your screen and disable it (al-beit only temporarily). If so, end your session there and then, initiate another one, and then enter the following (at the prompt):  set_prolog_flag(toplevel_print_options,[portray(true),max_depth(6)]). Entering it as indicated above will cause it to override the original fulldisplay (had it been consulted) and display but a small portion of the list of syllables. 70 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS                             This tree represents some of the steps which syllable.swipl followed in order to generate [s,t,(,a,j,k]. Beyond this, it represents the con-stituent structure that the former assigns to the latter. This constituent struc-ture, which is a portion of the structural description of this syllable,11 is also the correct one to assign to strike—at least according to the views in Goldsmith (1990) we have adopted. syllable.swipl is a nice program. Yet, it has its shortcomings— particularly in the area of observational adequacy. With regards to under-generation, syllable.swipl fails to generate syllables with more than one consonant in their codas. Such clusters are found, for example, in the common words six, sixth, and sixths (whose codas are the consonant clus-ters ks, ks!, and ks!s, respectively). syllable.swipl also fails to gen-erate syllables with [j,u] as their nucleus. Such syllables can be found in many common words—pew, cute, few, view, etc. It also fails to generate syllables with consonantic nuclei (cf. the normal pronunciations of the last syllables of meddle, flour, chasm, or lesson). With regards to overgenera-tion, syllable.swipl generates [e, w] as a nucleus and [h] as a coda. None of these are allowed in well-formed syllables of English.12 7 The Sounds of English We are finally in a position to offer a first approximation to the sounds of English. We will say that an English sound is simply a sequence of English syllables. This can be said easily enough in PROLOG with two rules: sound([A]):-                                                             11 See Chapter 1. 12 We observe, though, that [e,w] occurs in the expression of disgust eww! phone(s) phone(t) phone(') phone(a) phone(j) phone(k) onset([t,']) nucleus([a,j]) coda([k]) onset([s,t,']) rhyme([a,j,k]) syllable([s,t,',a,j,k]) PHONOLOGY: THE NATURE OF LINGUISTIC SOUND / 71   syllable(A). sound(A):-  append([B],C,A),  syllable(B),  sound(C). The first of these rules says, essentially, that a syllable is a sound; the se-cond, that appending a syllable to a sound yields a sound.13 Naturally, we would need to give the interpreter an indication of what a syllable is. But this can be done simply by adding to the program an instruction to consult our syllable-defining program: :- [‘syllable.swipl’]. Since a sound is a list of syllables, the length of a sound will be the number of syllables it contains. To generate sounds in order of increasing length we may therefore add the following definition of a binary relation sound:  sound(A,B):-  length(A,B),  sound(A).  Notice that this rule may also be used to query about sounds of a particular length. Enter for example sound(Sound,25) to generate sounds which are twenty-five syllables long! The definition of sound we have just presented has a couple of short-comings. To present them, notice that our definition of English sound makes an interesting prediction. It predicts that every sound will consist of a discrete number of syllables. But sounds seem to consist of rational num-bers of syllables instead. Take for instance the sound [s] of the English plural. Or a sound [d] of the English past tense (as in robbed, whose e is silent). These sounds are less than one syllable long. As are expressions like shh, hmm, and psst. Or take the sound [Intɹ] that we see in intravenous, intrinsic, and introverted. This sound seems to consist of more than one syllable, but less than two! And further difficulties emerge if we consider the morphological process of interfixation we will discuss in Chapter 4 be-low. Moreover, it has been argued that English sounds are sequences of                                                             13 To maintain the integrity of syllables we have defined sounds as lists of syllables—and thus as lists of lists. If we wanted to define sounds as lists of phones instead, we would simply omit the square brackets in the rules in question. 72 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS stress-based units larger than the syllable. These units are called prosodic feet. Take for instance the made-up word supercalifragilistickespialido-cious. If we used a larger font size for the stressed syllables and a smaller one for the unstressed, we would represent this word as follows: su - per - ca - li - fra - gi - lis - tick - es - pi - a - li - do - cious As can be readily seen, what we have here is a perfectly alternating stress pattern of stressed and unstressed syllables throughout the word (starting with a stressed syllable). One way to account for this pattern is to say that English words are sequences of pairs <x,y> in which x is a stressed syllable and y is an unstressed syllable. Such pairs are called trochees (or trochaic feet).14 Accounting for this pattern in PROLOG would not be hard. But is every English word in fact a trochee? On the one hand, there are many words that exhibit the trochaic pattern: kan - sas  (one trochee) cal - i - for - nia  (two trochees) cal - i - frig - gin - for - nia  (three trochees) But there are many words that exhibit other patterns as well: ver - mont (the opposite iambic pattern15) il - li - nois  (one trochee and a half) mis - sou - ri (half and one trochee) con - nec - ti - cut  (two iambs) maine  (less than a trochee) In short, there seems to be just about every conceivable stress pattern repre-sented here—and this is just the limited domain of names of states of the Union.  A discussion of these issues would take us too far afield. So we will simply conclude this chapter by proposing, as a first approximation, that an English sound is just a sequence of English syllables. We acknowledge that, taken on its own, this definition will both undergenerate and overgenerate. It will undergenerate because, as we saw, it will reject perfectly good sounds like [d] or [Intɹ]; it will overgenerate because it will encompass many sounds that are not actual words or phrases of English (cf. bort, twer,                                                             14 The name comes from a metric unit of classical Greco-Roman poetry which alternated long and short syllables. 15 The term iambic also originates with the metrics of Greco-Roman poetry. PHONOLOGY: THE NATURE OF LINGUISTIC SOUND / 73  or sprate). We shall return to the problem of undergeneration in Chapter 5. As to the problem of overgeneration, perhaps that should not be counted against us. For being a sound that English admits is not the same thing as being a sound that English pairs with a meaning and recognizes as a word or a phrase (the former needs to be admitted by English phonology; the lat-ter by English grammar as a whole). 8 Suggestions for Further Reading Phonetics and phonology are sciences with deep historical roots.
