Now we need to see how to compute meanings. But the computation of meaning is not nearly as developed as the computation of sound. This is because we can agree on what a sound is—and have so agreed since the 19th century. Yet, we still don’t agree on what meaning is, let alone know what it is. Naively, the meaning of an expression is the thing that expression refers to; what that expression is about. But this cannot be right. Take for example the two expressions in (1). (1) a.  the smallest planet   b.  the planet closest to the sun These two expressions refer to the same thing—at least in our solar system. They both refer to planet Mercury. Yet, these two nounphrases do not quite mean the same thing. The best way to bring this out is to compare the dif-ference between (2a) and (2b). (2) a.  The smallest planet is the planet closest to the sun.  b.  The smallest planet is the smallest planet. While (2a) conveys an interesting astronomical fact, the sentence in (2b) does not convey an interesting statement at all. In fact, it conveys nothing we didn’t already know (at least if we knew that everything is what it is). 78 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS The reason for this difference is, precisely, that the two expressions in (1) convey different concepts or meanings. Even if they refer to the same thing. Examples like those in (1) can be easily multiplied. The current presi-dent of the United States and the current husband of the first lady of the United States are two nounphrases that refer to the same entity. Yet, these two titles do not mean the same thing. Even though they refer to the same thing (or rather person). Other pairs of expressions that have been used to make the same point are Sir Walter Scott and the author of Waverley. Or the morning star and the evening star. Or 3 + 2 and 6 – 1. In all these examples, the conclusion is invariably the same: the meaning of an expression is not the same thing as its reference.  These observations are not new. Stoicism was a school of Greek philos-ophy formed in the third century before Christ. Best remembered for their views on the destructive role of emotions, the Stoics distinguished between the bearer of a name and the name’s meaning, the latter of which they called the lekton. Similarly, Saint Augustine (350-430) distinguished between the thing a word referred to (what he called res) and the word’s actual meaning (which he called the dicibile). As to the word itself he called that dictio.1 The semantic triad of dictio, dicibile, and res subsisted under different guis-es throughout the Middle Ages. Thus, John of Saint Thomas (1589-1644) distinguished between the conceptus (meaning) and the res (reference) of a word (or vox). But the distinction received its classical formulation from German logician and philosopher Gottlob Frege (1845-1925), who argued for the separation of Sinn (meaning) and Bedeutung (reference). Through Frege, the distinction entered contemporary discussions of meaning in phi-losophy and linguistics. So we know that reference is not meaning. And we’ve known it for a long time. Yet, reference is not irrelevant to meaning. For, to know the meaning of an expression is to know what, if anything, that expression re-fers to given enough information about the world (or, if we are so sorely mistaken about the world that we have no real information about it, then to know the meaning of an expression is to know what, if anything, that ex-pression refers to relative to a sufficiently detailed understanding of the world).  There is, to be sure, more to knowing the meaning of an expression than knowing what that expression refers to. But there can hardly be less. So let us focus on this point and ask what is it that enables us to know the refer-ence of an expression given a rich enough understanding of the world? A moment’s reflection should make the answer clear. It is a procedure for computing a representation of this reference from this understanding. This,                                                             1 Saint Augustine, De Dialectica, Chapter 5. SEMANTICS: THE NATURE OF LINGUISTIC MEANING / 79 in essence, is what we will call the procedural theory of meaning. The goals of this chapter are to flesh out the details of this theory, to implement it in terms of PROLOG, and to justify it on descriptive grounds. As the procedural theory of meaning would have it, the meaning of (1a) is a procedure for computing a representation of the smallest planet of our solar system; (1b), on the other hand, is a procedure for computing a repre-sentation of the planet closest to the sun. What makes (2a) interesting is that it claims that these two procedures arrive at the same result. What makes (2b) trivial is that it declares that one of these procedures arrives at the re-sult it arrives at.  Now, if we were in possession of the knowledge that Mercury was the smallest planet of our system, then we would know that (1a) referred to Mercury. So knowing the meaning of (1a) would indeed allow us to know what this expression referred to (given enough information about the world). And if we had the understanding that Mercury was also the planet that lied closest to our sun, then knowing the meaning of (1b) would have allowed us to know that (1b) was another procedure for computing Mercury. Conse-quently, we would have held (2a) to be true. Yet, if we believed that say, Pluto, was the smallest planet of our system, then we would have taken (2a) to be false.2 Interestingly (2b) would come out true in either case, as world knowledge is irrelevant to the evaluation of its truth value. It should be emphasized that knowledge of the world plays no role in understanding the meanings of (1a) and (1b); only in figuring out their ref-erences. As a matter of fact, we may be ignorant as to what the smallest planet is, be wrong about what the smallest planet is, doubt the existence of Mercury, or even question the very existence of a smallest planet. We would still be able to understand (1a). And do so in the same way as someone who knew that there was a planet that was smaller than all other planets and that this planet was indeed Mercury. The procedural theory of meaning is not new. More than a few years ago, Moschovakis (1993) argued that the meaning of an expression was an algorithmic procedure for computing (a representation of) its reference. The procedural view of meaning presented here is Moschovakis’ proposal. With one minor modification. Our proposal does not require that meanings be algorithmic (and hence terminating). The rationale behind this is straight-forward: there are terms whose references can only be computed with non-terminating procedures. Take, for example, the noun number. I take it that this noun refers to the set of numbers. Since this set is infinite, the proce-                                                            2 Pluto was the smallest planet of our system from its discovery in 1930 until 2006, when the astronomic community demoted Pluto to the category of a planetoid. At that point, Mercury became the smallest planet. At least according to the community of experts. 80 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS dure for generating it must be nonterminating. Notice that this does not mean that the procedure itself is infinite; just that it would run forever (if it weren’t for the fact that computing resources are limited).  2 Implementing the Procedural Theory of Meaning A PROLOG query is an unconditional statement addressed to the PROLOG interpreter. A PROLOG query may or may not contain variables. (3a) is a query that does; (3b) is a query that doesn’t. (3) a. man(X).  b. man(socrates). The variables in a query may be either bound or free in that query. Take for instance the query in (4). We mentioned it in Chapter 2. Variable X is bound in this query (its range of variation is delimited by the findall predicate), while variable Y is free in it (nothing delimits its range of variation). (4) findall(X, cns(X), Y). Other predicates that can bind variables are setof, bagof and allsuch (the first two are built-in in PROLOG; the third will be defined below in terms of findall). In addition to being bound or free, the variables of a PROLOG query may be named or unnamed. Take for example the query in (5). We dis-cussed it in Chapter 1. This query contains four variables. Two of them are named and two are not. The variables named in (5) are SO and ME. The un-named ones are indicated by underscores. (5) spanish(SO, _, ME, _). Now, suppose PROLOG were presented with a query in which some named variables occur free. As we have seen on numerous occasions, PROLOG will begin to look for values for those variables. Crucially, this search will be constrained by the requirement that any values found must satisfy the query, so if the variables were to take on these values, then the query would be rendered true, thus becoming a fact. And if PROLOG suc-ceeds in finding such values, it will dutifully report them to us one at a time. Our implementation of the procedural theory of meaning capitalizes on this behavior of PROLOG, as the implementation we wish to propose holds that the meaning of an expression is the procedure triggered by a query in SEMANTICS: THE NATURE OF LINGUISTIC MEANING / 81 which a particular named variable occurs free. And that the value the proce-dure assigns to the variable will be the reference of the expression.3  To make matters precise, let us say that a referential query is a well-formed PROLOG expression of the form in (6), where ϕ is a named varia-ble that occurs freely in a query ψ, and where the double colon is an infix that stands for the relation that the variable ϕ bears to the query ψ if and only if ψ is true.4 So (6) may be read ϕ is such that ψ. (6)  ϕ :: ψ We have defined the double colon in doublecolon.swipl, which is Program 8bis of Appendix H (we had to do this, as the double colon is not a built-in predicate). As the reader will be able to verify, that short, two-line program, does but two things—one per line. First, it allows the double colon to be an infix so referential queries may be well formed; second, it renders referential queries true whenever the query to the right of the double colon can be rendered true as well. Notice that the definition in (6) will allow us to dispose of the situation in which ψ contains more than one free variable, each associated to its own procedures and values. The procedure intended by a referential procedure ϕ::ψ is only the one triggered in the attempt to satisfy ϕ, and the value intended is only the one assigned to this variable.5  Thus, to make our intentions clear, we will tend to use Reference in-stead of ϕ in our referential queries. In other words, we will tend to use the name Reference instead of ϕ in this connection. Our referential queries will therefore look more like (6′) than like (6). We may read (6′) as the ref-erence is such that ψ. (6′) Reference :: ψ But regardless of the name we choose to give the variable that fills the slot in  _::ψ, we will always regard it as the referential variable of the referen-tial query that contains it. And ψ in a referential query ϕ::ψ will always                                                             3 If the procedure assigns no value to the variable, the expression in question will be nonrefer-ential; if it assigns more than one value to it, then it will be referentially indeterminate instead. Expressions that are not assigned queries of this kind will remain meaningless or uninterpret-ed (see below). 4 Or at least provable by PROLOG from any programs it might have consulted at that point (and from whatever has been built into it). 5 In addition, referential queries provide a simple way to refer to ϕ when it is deeply embedded within ψ. This will come in handy in the sequel. The caret was put to a similar use in the im-plementation of the lambda operator carried out in Pereira and Shieber (1987) and Covington (1994). Current versions of PROLOG do not support these uses of the built-in caret. 82 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS represent a PROLOG query in which ϕ is free. A referential procedure is the procedure triggered in an attempt to respond to a referential query. To illustrate, consider the queries in (7). (7) a.  Reference :: (Reference is 3+2).  b.  Reference :: (Reference is 6-1). (7a) and (7b) are both referential queries (strictly speaking, their parenthe-ses are unnecessary; we use them here merely to enhance readability). Upon receiving (7a), a PROLOG interpreter that has consulted double-colon.swipl will initiate a procedure to assign a value to Reference.  It responds with (8). (8) Reference = 5. In so doing, the interpreter has assigned a value of 5 to Reference, and may then turn to (7b). Once again, the PROLOG interpreter initiates a pro-cedure to evaluate an occurrence of Reference. This time the one in (7b). As it turns out, the interpreter again responds with (8), thus assigning a val-ue of 5 to this occurrence of Reference as well. So the interpreter as-signs one and the same value to both occurrences of Reference in (7). Yet, it followed different procedures to do so—one involving addition and the other subtraction. Our implementation of the procedural theory of mean-ing interprets the expression 3+2 as the procedure to respond to the query in (7a), and interprets the expression 6–1 as the procedure to respond to the query in (7b). This implementation thus succeeds in assigning different meanings to expressions that have the same reference. It should not escape the reader that the coreference of 3+2 and 6–1 is not a matter of chance, but rather a matter of mathematical necessity. As will be seen in Section 11 below, nonsynonymous expressions which are necessarily coreferential present a monumental challenge for possible worlds semantics—one of the leading theories of meaning. Be that as it may, notice that the PROLOG interpreter did not have to consult any program in order to evaluate the occurrences of Reference in (7). But such situations are fairly uncommon. More often than not, the PROLOG interpreter will have to consult a PROLOG program to respond to a referential query. We shall call these programs models. Models may be regarded as theories, views, or understandings of the world. To illustrate, consider the referential query in (9). (9) Reference :: man(Reference). If we were to address this query to the PROLOG interpreter, the interpreter would be unable to assign a value to its referential variable. Since a specifi-cation for man has not been built-in in PROLOG, the interpreter will re-SEMANTICS: THE NATURE OF LINGUISTIC MEANING / 83 spond that the predicate man is undefined. To make the interpreter evaluate Reference we would need to ask it to consult a program that says who, if any, is a man. Programs that can do so can be extraordinarily simple. A program consisting solely of (3b), for example, would do. It should be emphasized that what was at fault here was not the referen-tial query in (9). (9) is a well-formed PROLOG query and, as such, will cause the PROLOG interpreter that fields it to initiate a procedure to re-spond to it. The problem at hand was that the interpreter did not have enough information to come up with a value for the referential variable of the query. This is the information that a model specifying who a man is would provide. It follows that one could interpret a linguistic expression as the procedure triggered by the query in (9). Even in the absence of a model to resolve this query. Under these circumstances, the expression would be meaningful but not referential. Another tricky situation emerges when the reference to be computed is an infinite object. Suppose the PROLOG interpreter had consulted the fol-lowing program.  num(0). num(N):-  num(M),  N is M + 1.   Program 9. number.swipl It should be clear that this is an infinite specification of num (which is in-tended to be short for number). Suppose that we were to interpret the noun number as the procedure triggered by the following referential query. (10) Reference :: (findall(A, num(A), Reference)). Provided with (10), the PROLOG interpreter would initiate a referential procedure to construct a complete list of the nonnegative integers. Since there are infinitely many of them, this procedure would be nonterminating. Yet, like (9), (10) is a perfectly legitimate PROLOG query. And interpret-ing the noun number as the procedure to respond to it is a perfectly valid move. This interpretation would therefore render the noun number mean-ingful. And I dare say referential—even if the procedure to compute the reference in question cannot be completed in a finite amount of time.   84 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS 3 Proper Names The meaning of proper names is a much discussed topic in the philosophy of language. Two main theories have been developed on the subject. They are Direct Referentialism and Indirect Referentialism. Direct Referential-ism holds that proper names do not have any meanings of their own; they simply refer directly to the entities they refer to. Indirect Referentialism, on the other hand, insists that proper names refer to entities indirectly through their meanings. These meanings would be those of definite descriptions— that is nounphrases consisting of the determiner the followed by a phrase consisting of a noun and, possibly, some modifiers. Take, for example the name Aristotle. Or æɹɪstatl, to be precise. Di-rect referentialism would claim that this name has no meaning; only a refer-ence. The reference is Aristotle himself—the individual, not his name—and the name refers directly to him. Indirect referentialism would say instead that the meaning of æɹɪstatl is that of a definite description. Say the disciple of Plato. Or perhaps the best known disciple of Plato. This meaning would then pick Aristotle the man. So the name would refer indirectly to him. Direct Referentialism is usually traced back to John Stuart Mill (1806-1873), and has been forcefully defended in the second half of the twentieth century by Saul Kripke (b. 1940). So forcefully, in fact, that it has become the dominant view on the semantics of proper names. Indirect Referential-ism is usually traced back to Gottlob Frege—whom we’ve met before—and has been espoused more recently by Bertrand Russell (1872-1970) and oth-ers. One problem with direct referentialism is that a prima facie case could be made that proper names have, in fact, meanings. Take for instance Mark Twain and Samuel Clemens. Although these names refer to the same indi-vidual, the same argument can be made here as before to argue that (1a) and (1b) differed in meaning: (11) a.  Mark Twain is Samuel Clemens.  b.  Mark Twain is Mark Twain. But indirect referentialism is not without its share of problems either. For, if proper names have the meanings of definite descriptions, what would these descriptions be? Does Aristotle mean (12a) or (12b)? (12) a.  the disciple of Plato   b.  the teacher of Alexander As far as I can see, direct referentialism has not developed a full-fledged response to the problem in (11). Indirect referentialism, on the other SEMANTICS: THE NATURE OF LINGUISTIC MEANING / 85 hand, has come up with a cogent response to the problem in (12). It is to say that the definite description corresponding to Aristotle is neither (12a) nor (12b), but rather (12c). (12) c. the bearer of the name æɹɪstatl. This solution to the problem of descriptive indeterminacy leads to the met-alinguistic version of indirect referentialism (a metalanguage is a language that can talk about language; the version in question is metalinguistic be-cause it involves a description that talks about language). Fortunately, we do not have to resolve here the intricate issues that have been raised in the enormous literature on the semantics of proper names. This is because the procedural theory of meaning is in the enviable position of being able to accommodate both direct and indirect referentialism.  Let’s turn first to the proper name Aristotle under direct referentialism. Here, the meaning of this proper name is the procedure corresponding to the referential query in (13). (13)  Reference :: (Reference = aristotle). To address the query in (13) the PROLOG interpreter must assign a value for Reference that equals aristotle. But, as far as PROLOG inter-preters are concerned, aristotle can only equal itself. Thus, the query in (13) already contains its own answer; PROLOG will respond to (13) with (13′).  (13′)  Reference = aristotle. Thus, the procedure followed here by the PROLOG interpreter is almost the identity procedure, which is as close as we can get to a null procedure (or to the absence of a procedure)—and hence to a null meaning (or to the absence of meaning).  This seems to be a fair rendering of direct referentialism within our PROLOG implementation of the procedural theory of meaning. So let’s turn next to the proper name Aristotle under indirect referentialism. Here the meaning of this proper name may be construed as the procedure involved in the answer to the referential query in (14). (14) Reference :: (bearer(Reference, æɹɪstatl)). For, suppose this query was addressed to a model that specified that aris-totle was the bearer of the name æɹɪstatl. Suppose, that is, that (14) was addressed to a PROLOG program that contained the following condi-tional statement: 86 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS bearer(aristotle,A):-      name(A,[230,633,618,115,116,111,116,108]). The PROLOG interpreter would therefore respond to the query in (14) with the equation in (13’). In other words, both theories would agree that the reference of æɹɪstatl (the name) is Aristotle (the individual). They would differ, however, as to what the meaning of the former is. For direct referentialism, the meaning is the (quasi) nonprocedure involved in answering to (13) with (13′); for indi-rect referentialism it is the substantial procedure involved in responding to (14) with (13′).  It should be emphasized that a model (or program) did not play a role in the response to (13); only in the response to (14). In other words, responses to (14) will hold only relative to certain models; responses to (13), on the other hand, will hold relative to any possible model. 4 Unary Predicates We intend models as theories of the world, and have characterized them as PROLOG programs that are consulted by the PROLOG interpreter in order to respond to referential queries. To be more specific about models, notice that a model, being a PROLOG program, may consist of two kinds of statements: conditional and unconditional. Predicates that are defined by unconditional statements will be called primes; predicates that are defined by conditional statements will be called composites. Thus, predicates like male, entity, or animate may well be primes. Predicates like fe-male—if defined as non-male animate entity—would be composites. It is tempting to conjecture that the set of primes will be relatively small, will be present in every model, and will be innate. Although it is impossible to say at this point what these primes will be, it is hard to imagine that male, entity, and animate will not be among them. It is also tempting to believe that composites will be predicates defined from primes by means of a relatively small number of operations. What these operations are is also impossible to tell at this point, but it is hard to see how negation and con-junction will not be among them. Finally, we may want to think that the effect of our operations on our primes will be constrained in some way, so that the set of composites could be, for example, the closure of the set of primes under the set of operations. In other words, the set of composites could be what we get when we apply the entire set of operations to the en-tire set of primes—nothing more, and nothing less.  Notice that even if primes are universal, models will still differ with re-gards to the unconditional statements they comprise. For these statements SEMANTICS: THE NATURE OF LINGUISTIC MEANING / 87 will have to say what entities, if any, satisfy what primes—they need to specify who is male, what are the entities, which of these are animate, and so on. This, in fact, is what we meant when we said that the primes were defined by unconditional statements. Even if, in a sense, what is defined here is not actually the prime itself, but only its extension—i.e. the set of entities that satisfy it. At the same time, composites, although defined by conditional state-ments, may still be universal. Thus, the notion of female as an entity that is not male or an immature as an entity that is not mature are definitional or conceptual statements that should not vary from model to model. To make this abstract discussion a bit concrete, let us consider the fol-lowing PROLOG program. We have called it bedrock.swipl—after the TV series created in the 1960s. [see Program 10 below] Notice that bedrock.swipl contains both conditional and uncondi-tional statements. The unconditional statements represent factual knowl-edge as to who is human, who is male, and who is mature. The predicates in these unconditional statements are the primes. The only ones in bed-rock.swipl, in fact. As to the conditional statements, our program pro-vides seven. They define some concepts in terms of others (together with the operations of conjunction and negation). All of these statements repre-sent conceptual rather than factual knowledge. To help the reader see what these concepts are, we have called them female, immature, man, wom-an, child, boy, girl. It should be clear, however, that none of the pred-icates defined thus far are English words; they are only predicates of a model with which English words could be interpreted. Or words of any oth-er language, for that matter. To make this point clear, notice that we could have called these predicates in terms of random numbers (concept001 instead of human; concept002 instead of immature—and so on). But this would have obscured the intended identities of the predicates of our model. So bedrock.swipl is a model with three primes (human, male, mature) and seven composites (female, immature, man, woman, child, boy, girl). Needless to say, this model is not intended as a real-istic model for the interpretation of English; only as an illustrative one. Armed with this background, let us say that a unary predicate is a predi-cate that refers to a set of individual entities—or of entities taken one at a time. The noun boy, for example, is a unary predicate, since it refers to a set of individual boys. Thus, under the procedural theory of meaning, the meaning of boy must be the procedure for answering the referential query in (15) Reference :: (findall(A, boy(A), Reference)).  88 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS   human(fred).  human(wilma).  human(pebbles).  human(barney).  human(betty).  human(bambam).  male(fred). male(barney). male(bambam).  mature(fred). mature(wilma). mature(barney). mature(betty).  female(A):- human(A), not(male(A)).  immature(A):- human(A), not(mature(A)).  man(A):- male(A), mature(A).  woman(A):- female(A), mature(A).  child(A):- human(A), immature(A).  boy(A):- male(A), child(A).  girl(A):- female(A), child(A). Program 10. bedrock.swipl SEMANTICS: THE NATURE OF LINGUISTIC MEANING / 89 A PROLOG interpreter which has consulted bedrock.swipl (and dou-blecolon.swipl) would respond to this query with (16), which is as desired. (16) Reference = [bambam]. Notice that the response is the list consisting of Bambam; not just Bambam. This is the right response; the reference of boy relative to bed-rock.swipl is the set of boys in the model; not the boy in the model. Even if the set consists solely of that boy. It follows that the proper name Bambam and the common noun child are never coreferential—which seems to be intuitively correct. But it might be thought that bedrock.swipl is overly complex, and that all that would be needed to respond to (15) with (16) would be a fact that specifies, directly, that Bambam is a boy. Interestingly, this simpler proposal would fail on grounds of descriptive adequacy. For notice that the sentence in (17a) is redundant, and the one in (17b) is contradictory.6 (17) a.  ?This boy is a child.  b.  ?This boy is not a child. Along the same lines, (18a) is redundant and (18b) is contradictory (at least taking boy and male in their strict anatomical senses). (18) a.  ?This boy is male.  b.  ?This boy is not male. Taken together, the facts in (17) and (18) suggest that the notions child and male are part of the meaning of the notion of boy. For then (17a) and (18a) would be redundant because being male and being a child are already part of the meaning of boy; (17b) and (18b) would be contradictory because they would negate part of the meaning of boy. Needless to say, child and male are part of the meaning of boy relative to bedrock.swipl. But not under the alternative under discussion—which should therefore be dispre-ferred.  But things do not stop here. Notice that (19a) entails (19b), and that (20a) entails (20b). (19) a.  He is a child.  b.  He is a human being. (20) a.  He is a child.  b.  He is not mature (as a human being).                                                             6 Following general practice, we place a question mark at the beginning of semantically questionable expressions. 90 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS To account for these entailments, we should make being human and being immature part of our interpretation of child. But this is what we have done by defining child as a human being who is immature (as a human being). And, once again, this is what the proposal to use a fact to specify directly who is a child (or a boy) would miss. Similar points could be made about all of the notions defined by rule in bedrock.swipl. Thus, a male female would express a contradictory notion. As would a mature immature (entity). And male men and female women would express redundancies. We conclude this section by gathering the referential queries that would elicit the meanings of man, woman, boy and girl: (21) a.  Reference :: (findall(A,man(A),Reference)).  b.  Reference :: (findall(A,woman(A),Reference)).  c.  Reference :: (findall(A,boy(A),Reference)).  d.  Reference :: (findall(A,girl(A),Reference)). A PROLOG interpreter that had consulted bedrock.swipl (and dou-blecolon.swipl) would respond as follows. (22) a. Reference = [fred, barney].  b. Reference = [wilma, betty].  c. Reference = [bambam].  d. Reference = [pebbles]. 5 Binary Predicates: Preliminaries What we have been doing in our models is called componential analysis. Componential analysis is breaking up a complex meaning into its simple components. Or at least into simpler components. Componential analysis has been around for a long time. Leibniz (1646-1716) liked to say that the essences of things were like numbers (essentiae rerum sunt sicut numeri). By this he meant that complex concepts could be derived from simpler ones—and then reconstructed by a combinatoric akin to the construction of numbers as products of primes.7 These views led Leibniz into a search for conceptual primes and their modes of combination. If the search for these conceptual atoms (or letters of the alphabet of human thought) was success-ful, Leibniz reasoned, a universal language could be developed—a language that would assign things their “true names” (like in Chemistry, where water is called by its “true name” H2O). Thanks to this universal language, error would be banished, irrationality would be banned, and human disputes                                                             7 See his Disputatio metaphysica de principio individui, 1663, GP IV 26. SEMANTICS: THE NATURE OF LINGUISTIC MEANING / 91 would be resolved peaceably by means of computation—or so Leibniz thought. For reasons best explained in Pinker (1999: 10-12), the ideal of a Leib-nizian universal language was doomed to failure. The componential impetus behind it, however, has remained very much alive. Componential analysis was embraced by the two major schools of Linguistics of the twentieth cen-tury (Structuralism and Generativism), and constitutes the core of the so-called Natural Semantic Metalanguage—an approach to semantic analysis founded by Anna Wierzbicka and developed by Cliff Goddard.8 Until recently, only unary predicates (cf. boy, child, female) underwent componential analysis. This impeded progress significantly. Consider the nouns parent and child. They are related in a very particular way: they are converses of each other. What this means is that if someone is a parent of someone else, then this someone else is also a child of the original someone. And vice versa: if somebody is a child of somebody else, then this some-body else is also a parent of the original somebody. More succinctly, (23) parent(X,Y) ⇔ child(Y,X). (where X and Y range over arbitrary entities and ‘⇔’ stands for ‘entails and is entailed by’).9 How should we account for the double entailment in (23)? No account seems forthcoming if we said that the meaning of parent corresponds to (24a) and that of child to (24b). (24) a.  Reference :: (findall(A,parent(A),Reference)).  b.  Reference :: (findall(A,child(A),Reference)). A more fruitful approach would be to say that parent and child do not name properties of individual entities, but rather properties of pairs of entities—or that they conveyed binary relations. Expressions naming binary relations are called binary predicates. So let’s say parent and child are binary predi-cates, and interpret the former through the query in (25a), and the latter through the query in (25b). (25) a. Reference ::    (findall([A,B],parent(A,B),Reference)).                                                               8 The website for Natural Semantic Metalanguage can be found at http://www.une.edu.au/bcss/linguistics/nsm. 9 We should perhaps clarify that the noun child used here means offspring (as in the Spanish noun hijo), while the noun child discussed in the previous section is a homophone of the one used here, and means infant instead (as in the Spanish noun niño). 92 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS  b. Reference ::    (findall([B,A],parent(A,B),Reference)). As might be expected, a suitably informed PROLOG interpreter would re-spond to these queries by telling us that the reference of parent will be the set of pairs [A,B] such that A is a parent of B, and the reference of child will be the converse set of pairs [B,A] such that A is a parent of B (or that B is a child of A). The double entailment in (23) now follows from the in-terpretations in (25). Clearly, the key to this account is the claim that parent and child convey binary relations—or that they are binary rather than unary predicates. Converse relations are legion in the languages of the world. Judging from the double entailments in (26), over/under, before/behind, and left/right are all converses of each other. And given the facts in (27), tem-poral relations like before/after and sooner/later are converse as well. (26) over(X,Y) ⇔ under(Y,X).  before(X,Y) ⇔ behind(Y,X).  left(X,Y) ⇔ right(Y,X). (27) before(X,Y) ⇔ after(Y,X).  sooner(X,Y) ⇔ later(Y,X). Converse relations are not limited to spatio-temporal expressions. As a glance at the judgements in (28) will reveal, active/passive pairs of verbs express converse relations as well. And rather systematically (see Chapter 9). (28) eat(X,Y) ⇔ is-eaten-by(Y,X).  loves(X,Y) ⇔ is-loved-by(Y,X).  sees(X,Y) ⇔ is-seen-by(Y,X). Intuitions of converseness are thus a strong argument for the claim that lan-guages contain binary predicates—and must be interpreted as such. 6 Binary Predicates: Kinterms Transitive verbs are the prime examples of binary predicates. As defined in Chapter 1, a transitive verb is one that must combine with two nounphrases to form a sentence. Three examples of transitive verbs were given in (28). They were eat, loves, and sees. But not all transitive verbs have passive forms. Take for example weigh or resemble. Although we may say that the brain weighs three pounds we may not say that ?three pounds are weighed by the brain. Or that ?Kim is resembled by Pat. Although these verbs are devoid of passive counterparts, they and many others are binary predicates. SEMANTICS: THE NATURE OF LINGUISTIC MEANING / 93 As are all of the kinterms of English, to which we now turn. We saw in (25) that the English noun child could be interpreted in terms of the notion parent. As a matter of fact, all of the kinterms of English— and then some—can be interpreted in terms of two kinship-specific notions, namely parent and spouse. All else that is needed are two indepen-dently motivated notions (individual and male), and two built-in no-tions (not and =).10 To make our case, consider the following PROLOG rules (which assume we continue to define female as nonmale individual). father(A,B):-  male(A),  parent(A,B). mother(A,B):-  female(A),  parent(A,B). child(A,B):-  parent(B,A). son(A,B):-  male(A),  child(A,B). daughter(A,B):-  female(A),  child(A,B). grandparent(A,B):-  parent(A,C),  parent(C,B). grandfather(A,B):-     father(A,C),  parent(C,B). grandmother(A,B):-     mother(A,C),  parent(C,B).                                                             10 We hasten to add that this is not the case for all the kinterms of all the languages of the world. Like the redundancy inherent in saying that a husband is married. Or the inconsistency of the notion male wife. Or the entailment that holds between being a sister and being a female who shares a father and a mother with someone. Or the fact that uncleo-raunt and nepheworniece are converses of each other. Yet, the rules above are still insufficient to formulate referential queries that will actually trigger a procedure for the computation of reference. To formulate such rules we need to complement the rules above with facts that will tell us four things: (i) who is an individual, (ii) who is male, (iii) who is a parent of whom, and (iv) who is a spouse of whom. In other words, the preceding rules are but the conceptual portion of a model; as such, they need to be complemented by a factual component in order to have a full model. This factual component comes in the form of unconditional rules that define (the extensions of) primes. This factual component, together with the conceptual counterpart has been provided in kinship.swipl, which is listed as Program 11 in Appendix H.  kinship.swipl is thus a model. Relative to it, queries like the ones in (29) will succeed in eliciting procedures for the computation of refer-ences. These procedures will be the proposed meanings for the nouns hus-band, wife, son, and daughter.    (29) a. Reference ::     (findall([A,B],husband(A,B),Reference)).  b. We hasten to add, though, that these responses will not make sense to the reader until (s)he has consulted the glossary in the header of Program 11, Appen-dix H). 7 Binary Predicates: Deictics A directional adverb is an expression that refers to a set of what we shall call directions. Examples of directional adverbs are hither, hitherto, thither, thereto, hence and thence. We may get an idea of the meanings of these adverbs from the fact that they are involved in double entailments like the following. (30) hither ⇔ towards here  hitherto ⇔ up to now  thither ⇔ towards there thereto ⇔ up to then  hence ⇔ either from here on or from now on  thence ⇔ either from there on or from then on To characterize the meanings of these adverbs, we will turn to another po-tential model (or model fragment). For reasons that will be made clear be-low, we will call it deixis.swipl (see Program 12, Appendix H). deixis.swipl starts by specifying four places and four times: place(p000).    time(t000). place(p001).    time(t001). place(p002).    time(t002). place(p003).    time(t003). One of these places will be taken to be here (i.e. the place where the word here is being uttered or, in this case, read) and one of these times will be regarded as now (i.e. the time at which the word now is being uttered or read). here(p000).   now(t000). These factual specifications may be used to define a concept there as any place other than here. And then as any time other than now: there(P):-   then(T):- SEMANTICS: THE NATURE OF LINGUISTIC MEANING / 99  place(P),    time(T),  not(here(P)).   not(now(T)). At this point, we may define direction as a relation that holds either between two places or between two times: direction(P1,P2):-  direction(T1,T2):-  place(P1),    time(T1),  place(P2).    time(T2). Conceiving of the first member of a direction as the initial point of the di-rection, and of the second one as its terminal point, we may now specify two series of directions: hither(P1,P2):- hitherto(T1,T2):-  direction(P1,P2),  direction(T1,T2),  here(P2).  now(T2).  thither(P1,P2):- thereto(T1,T2):-  direction(P1,P2),  direction(T1,T2),  there(P2).  then(T2).  hence(P1,P2):- hence(T1,T2):-  direction(P1,P2),  direction(T1,T2),  here(P1).  now(T1).  thence(P1,P2):- thence(T1,T2):-  direction(P1,P2),  direction(T1,T2),  there(P1).   then(T1). It should be noticed that these are the eight possible directions that can be defined by taking any one point of a direction as a here, a now, a there, or a then (and saying nothing about the other). We may now use these rules to interpret the English adverbs hither, hitherto, thither, thereto, hence and thence as the procedures triggered by the referential queries in (31a)-(31f), respectively. (31)   a. Reference ::    (findall([A,B],hither(A,B),Reference)).  b. Reference ::    (findall([A,B],hitherto(A,B),Reference)).  c. Reference ::     (findall([A,B],thither(A,B),Reference)). 100 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS  d. Reference ::    (findall([A,B],thereto(A,B),Reference)).  e. Reference ::    (findall([A,B],hence(A,B),Reference)).  f. Reference ::    (findall([A,B],thence(A,B),Reference)). Needless to say, the directional adverbs interpreted through the queries in (31) will inherit the logic of the notions specified in deixis.swipl. Consequently, we will be able to account for all of the double entailments in (30). This fact can be corroborated by addressing the queries in (31) to a PROLOG interpreter that has consulted deixis.swipl (and bearing in mind that a direction is a set of pairs consisting of an initial point followed by a terminal point). This we shall leave as an exercise for the reader. deixis.swipl will support further interpretations. Suppose we were to add to it a specification of the relation head as the ternary relation that holds of anyone that moves from some initial point to some terminal point (the three terms of the relation would therefore be the mover, the mover's initial point, and the mover's terminal point). We could then specify come and go as particular cases of heading—heading hither and heading thither, respectively: come(A,B,C):-   go(A,B,C):-   head(A,B,C),   head(A,B,C),  hither(B,C).   thither(B,C). Evidence for interpreting the English directional verbs come and go in terms of these rules comes from the inconsistencies of (32), the redundancies in (33)—which are therefore acceptable as emphatic utterances—and the en-tailment that home is here in (34a) but there in (34b).11                                                               11  Modern communication technology makes things interesting for us here by allowing the speaker and the addressee to be in different places (with here being different places). Consider for example the following phone conversation between two individuals A and B:  A: Please come to Tokyo!  B: I am already coming to Tokyo. When A is the speaker, only the speaker is in Tokyo; when B is the speaker, only the addressee is. Also interesting is the following sentence: I’ll go to Tokyo next week; please come and visit. The use of go in the first sentence entails that the speaker is not in Tokyo at the time that sen-tence was uttered; the use of come in the second entails that he is in Tokyo (presumably at some future time). SEMANTICS: THE NATURE OF LINGUISTIC MEANING / 101 (32) a. ?Come there!  b. ?Go here! (33) a. Come here!  b. Go there! (34) a. Come home.  b. Go home. An expression is deictic if its interpretation involves the context in which it is uttered. Here and now are therefore deictics, as they involve reference to the place and time in which they are uttered. And all of the expressions de-fined in terms of them are deictic as well (cf. there, then, and all the direc-tional verbs and adverbs interpreted in this section). Deixis is the ability of linguistic expressions to “point” to the context of their utterance (deixis shares the same root as digit, which originally meant finger, which names the appendage most commonly used for pointing).  I and you are deictic expressions as well, as they involve reference to the speaker and the addressee of the contexts in which they are uttered. And all the expressions defined in terms of them (us and y’all, for example) are deictic too. 8 Ternary Predicates A ternary predicate is one that names a ternary relation—a relation that holds of three things at the same time. We have already seen some exam-ples of ternary relations. They were the notions head, and its two deriva-tives come, and go.12 The need to recognize ternary relations is best brought about by verbs of transfer that stand as partial converses of each other (two of their three terms switch places while the third one stays in place). Examples of these verbs are give and receive, buy and sell, borrow and lend, lease and let. For here we have double entailments like the follow-ing: (35) give(A,B,C) ⇔ receive(C,B,A).  buy(A,B,C) ⇔ sell(C,B,A).  lend(A,B,C) ⇔ borrow(C,B,A).  lease(A,B,C) ⇔ let(C,B,A). (in all these cases A stands for one who transfers B to C). And to account for these entailments we seem forced to acknowledge that the relations in ques-                                                            12 As to the English verbs come and go themselves, they are usually analyzed as binary rela-tions between someone and a direction. But a direction is, of course, a binary relation between an initial point and a terminal point. 102 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS tion are true of three things at the same time, and hence that we have ternary predicates. As might be expected, the references of these verbs would be sets of triples, and these references would be computed by a suitable PRO-LOG interpreters responding to queries like (36). (36) Reference ::   (findall([A,B,C], buy(A,B,C), Reference)). These verbs of transfer (to which we might add teach and learn) are not the only kind of ternary predicates found in the languages of the world. Just within English we also have put (cf. John put the money in the bank), com-pare (You are comparing apples with oranges), introduce (The Dean intro-duced the speaker to the faculty), preferred (I prefer Tintoretto to Veronese), and named (The parents named their child ‘Barack’). And we will encoun-ter others below. Verbs that take three arguments are said to be doubly transitive (or ditransitive for short). 9 Sentences: Declaratives We take it that the fundamental fact about the meanings of declarative sen-tences is the following: if we understand the meaning of a declarative sen-tence, and if we have enough information about the world, then we should be able to tell whether the sentence whose meaning we understand is true or false. This means that, under the procedural theory of meaning, the refer-ence of a sentence should be its truth value—and a procedure to compute it should be its meaning. Unfortunately, to formulate referential queries that will yield the desired truth values we will need to formulate a binary relation between sentences and their truth values (none is built in). We will call that relation evalua-tion, and define it in the following program.13                                                              13 The exclamation point in this program stands for Cut, a built-in fact that is true once and once only. Its effect here is to prevent the PROLOG interpreter from checking whether a sen-tence evaluates to falsity after checking that it evaluates to truth. It should be clear that the cut has a welcome effect under the assumption that no sentence will evaluate to both truth and falsity—an assumption PROLOG does not make, of course. But this effect is not critical; the cut could be eliminated from the rule in question without much loss. SEMANTICS: THE NATURE OF LINGUISTIC MEANING / 103  Program 13. evaluation.swipl Thus, the evaluation of some Sentence will be truth if Sentence holds (relative to a given model), and falsity if it does not. Since PROLOG assumes that programs are logically complete (see Chapter 1), this really means that sentences will be assigned truth if they are prova-ble (from the given model) and falsity if they are not. But provability is certainly more than truth. And unprovability less than falsity. Setting these important concerns to a side, we will now formulate referential queries like the ones in (37): (37) a. Reference ::     (evaluation(male(fred),Reference)).  b. Reference ::     (evaluation(male(wilma),Reference)). And, as the reader will be able to verify, a PROLOG interpreter that has consulted both bedrock.swipl and evaluation.swipl will re-spond to (37a) and (37b) with (38a) and (38b), respectively. (38) a. Reference = truth.  b. Reference = falsity. Thus, the meaning of a sentence could be identified with the referential pro-cedure followed by a PROLOG interpreter in its attempt to assign said sen-tence a truth value. What truth value, if any, the interpreter will assign to a sentence will depend on what programs, if any, it has consulted. This mir-rors human behavior, as we take sentences to be true or false depending on our view (or model) of the world. Declarative sentences may be simple or complex. Complex declaratives may arise as the negation of a simple declarative sentence. Or from the con-junction of two or more simple declaratives. Or as the disjunction of two or more simple declaratives. All of these complex sentences can be interpreted without any devices we have not seen thus far. Take for example the nega-tion in (39a), the conjunction in (39b), and the disjunction in (39c). (39) a.  Fred is not male.  b.  Fred is male and Wilma is male.  evaluation(Sentence,truth):-  Sentence, !.  evaluation(Sentence,falsity):-  not(Sentence). 104 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS  c.  Fred is male or Wilma is male (or, perhaps, both are). The meanings of these sentences are the referential procedures elicited by the queries in (40a), (40b), and (40c), respectively. (40) a.  Reference ::     (evaluation(not(male(fred)), Reference)). b. Reference ::    (evaluation((male(fred),male(wilma)),Reference)). c. Reference ::   (evaluation((male(fred);male(wilma)),Reference)). (40a) involves predicating not of a PROLOG statement; (40b) inserts a comma between two parenthesized statements to convey their conjunction, and (40c) places a semicolon between two parenthesized statements to con-vey their disjunction (since disjunction in PROLOG is inclusive, the possi-bility that both disjoints hold simultaneously is already contemplated by the disjunction). Addressing the referential queries in (40) to a PROLOG inter-preter that has consulted bedrock.swipl and evaluation.swipl returns the following references. (40′) a.  Reference = falsity.  b. Reference = falsity.  c. Reference = truth. Thus far, all our complex declaratives have been overtly complex, in-cluding explicit markers of complexity like not, the conjunctive comma, and the disjunctive semicolon. But a case can be made that complex declar-atives may be covertly complex as well (by which I mean that their referen-tial queries involve the evaluation of a complex statement, even if this com-plexity is not explicitly articulated in the sentence). Take for example (41a). Its referential query is (41b). But (41b) is tantamount to (41c)—at least rela-tive to bedrock.swipl—and (41c) evaluates a complex statement. (41) a.  Wilma is female. b.  Reference ::    (evaluation(female(wilma), Reference)). c. Reference ::   (evaluation(not(male(wilma)), Reference)). Other examples of covertly complex declaratives are not hard to find. Sup-pose we represented a binary predicate have as a set of pairs of entities SEMANTICS: THE NATURE OF LINGUISTIC MEANING / 105 such that the first has the second (no insight is claimed for this assumption; have seems to be a prime). Suppose further that we defined a predicate begin that is true of propositions that begin (to be true). With them we can define two relations get and lose as follows. get(Getter,Gotten):-    begin(have(Getter,Gotten)).  lose(Loser,Lost):-  begin(not(have(Loser,Lost))). So getting is beginning to have, while losing is beginning not to have, and sentences like Jack got the nomination and Jack lost the nomination are complex declaratives. Evidence for this analysis is provided by the entail-ment that, if A gets B, then A has B (but did not have it before). And that if A loses B, then A does not have it (but had it before). An expression is said to be inchoative if it refers to the beginnings of some event or situation. The prototypical inchoative expression is therefore begin. Inchoative are also all expressions defined in terms of begin (like get and lose). Here are other inchoatives, defined in terms of at (a bina-ry relation between entities and the places they are at), and know (a relation between someone and something (s)he knows) arrive(Arriver,Place):-    begin(at(Arriver,Place)).  leave(Leaver,Place):-  begin(not(at(Leaver,Place))). learn(Learner,Lesson):-   begin(know(Learner,Lesson)).   forget(Forgetter,Forgotten):-  begin(not(know(Forgetter,Forgotten))).  Interestingly, notice that meet could also be analyzed as an inchoative as begin to know (a human being). Yet, meet seems to lack an opposite with the meaning of begin to forget (a human being). This seems to be an acci-dental gap in the vocabulary of English (as opposed to the systematic gap created by a contradictory notion like that of male wife). Finally, let’s observe the following double entailments: (42) boils(X,Y) ⇔ makes(X,boil(Y))   walks(X,Y) ⇔ makes(X,walk(Y)) 106 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS  drops(X,Y) ⇔ makes(X,drop(Y))  grows(X,Y) ⇔ makes(X,grow(Y)) (in all of these cases, X makes something happen to Y). To account for these entailments we will define one of the members of each pair in terms of the other. Following the usual practice, we will derive the versions to the left of the double arrow from the ones to the right: (43) boils(X,Y):-   makes(X,boil(Y)).  walks(X,Y):-   makes(X,walk(Y)).  drops(X,Y):-    makes(X,drop(Y)).  grows(X,Y):-    makes(X,grow(Y)) So someone boils something if that someone makes that something boil; someone walks something if that someone makes that something walk—and so on. The rules above account for the right-to-left entailments of (42). The left-to-right entailments would follow if (43) in fact contained the complete definitions of boils(X,Y), walks(X,Y)—and so on. Consequently, if any of these statements is true, then the condition for its truth must have held as well.  makes and all the predicates defined with it are called causative. Caus-ative predicates convey the notion of direct or immediate causation. This seems to be what Aristotle called efficient causation. In any event, this kind of causation differs from indirect or mediated causation, which holds when the cause and the effect are not temporally contiguous. Or are mediated by one or more events. Neither English make nor English cause quite convey the notion of direct causation we would have to incorporate into the concept we have called make. Two interesting cases of causatives are give and take.14 These verbs are in a relation that is more antithetical than the one give and receive are in (see above). While give and receive are partial converses, give and take are reversives—they name actions that undo each other. This can be accounted for by the following rules.                                                             14 The ternary relation take defined in the text as ‘make not have’ should not be confused with the binary relation take defined in an earlier section as ‘go with’. The latter corresponds to Spanish as llevar; the former to quitar. SEMANTICS: THE NATURE OF LINGUISTIC MEANING / 107  give(Giver,Gift,Gifted):-     make(Giver,have(Gifted,Gift)).   take(Taker,Taken,Deprived):-   make(Taker,not(have(Deprived,Taken))). We conclude this section with a much-discussed analysis—that of kill in terms of a causative make that involves an inchoative die that involves a negative dead that involves a stative alive.15 kill(Killer,Killed):-    make(Killer,die(Killed)). (CAUSATIVE) die(Entity):-      begin(dead(Entity)). (INCHOATIVE) dead(Entity):-     not(alive(Entity)). (NEGATIVE) alive(Entity). (STATIVE) Evidence for this analysis of kill is provided by the contrast between (44) and (45). (44) a.  He made potatoes boil.  b.  He boiled potatoes. (45) a.  He made him die.  b.  *He died him. Presumably, the reason for the ungrammaticality of (45b) is that English has created a special verb for the notion make die. It is kill. This verb may then “displace” the transitive form died that would have otherwise occupied that position by default (how exactly to implement this “displacement” is not at all obvious, hence the scare quotes we have placed around these words). The same argument can be made, incidentally, for analyzing teach in terms of causative make, inchoative learn, and stative know. teach(Teacher,Learner):-  make(Teacher,learn(Learner)). (CAUSATIVE) learn(Learner):-  begin(know(Learner)). (INCHOATIVE) know(Knower). (STATIVE)                                                             15 A stative expression is one that refers to a state rather than to an event or an activity. 108 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS And evidence for this analysis of teach is provided by the following con-trast: (46) a.  He made the dog walk.  b.  He walked the dog. (47) a.  He made him learn.  b.  *He learned him. Presumably, the reason for the contrast between (46) and (47) is, again, the “displacement” of a transitive learn by a form that was created specifically for the job that the transitive form would otherwise fill by default. Another argument for analyzing kill as cause to die comes from the am-biguity in (48): (48) Smith almost killed Wesson. (48) can mean either (a) that Smith did something that almost ended with Wesson’s life (say, shoot him), or (b) that Smith almost did something that would end with Wesson’s life (say, almost shoot him fatally). The reason this ambiguity argues for the proposed analysis is that the analysis provides two actions for almost to modify: the making and the dying. Simple kill provides only one. A similar argument can be made for the analysis of teach as make learn by (49). (49)  I almost taught Obama Linguistics. (49) can mean that I almost did something that would have led Obama to learn Linguistics (say, he registered for one of my LIN1 classes but then I bailed out from teaching that class at the last minute), or I actually did something that almost led Obama to learn Linguistics (say, he began attend-ing my LIN1 class but dropped out before I could actually teach him any Linguistics). The analysis of kill in terms of cause to die has been often criticized on the grounds that these two expressions are not interchangeable in contexts like the following: (50) a. Babyface made Slinky die on Sunday by poisoning him on Saturday.  b. ?Babyface killed Slinky on Sunday by poisoning him on Saturday. (51) a. Babyface caused Slinky to die on Sunday by shooting him on Satur-     day  b. ?Babyface killed Slinky on Sunday by shooting him on Saturday. But all these facts show is that the notion of causation involved in kill is more direct than the one conveyed by the English verbs make or cause—a point we made above. SEMANTICS: THE NATURE OF LINGUISTIC MEANING / 109 10 Sentences: Interrogatives I shall assume that the fundamental semantic fact about interrogative sen-tences—better known as questions—is that to know the meaning of a ques-tion is to know its answer given enough information of about the world. Consequently, the meaning of a question should be a procedure for answer-ing it. To be more specific we will have to distinguish between several kinds of questions. We will do so according to the answers questions may take. First we distinguish between open and closed questions. A question is closed if it admits only of a yes or no answer. Examples of closed questions would therefore be Is Barney female? or Is Fred husband to Wilma? A question is open if it admits answers other than yes or no. Examples of open questions would therefore be Who is husband to Wilma?, Who(m) is Fred husband to? or even Who is husband to who(m)? We may call the latter a doubly open question, as it asks for two things at once. This is in opposition to the other examples of open questions, which should be considered simply open on account of the fact that they ask for only one thing each. And we can of course have triply open questions as well, like Who gave what to whom? If the meaning of a question is a procedure to answer it, we should pro-pose, as the meaning of a closed question like Is Barney female?, the proce-dure followed by a PROLOG interpreter in order to respond to the follow-ing query. (52) Answer ::   (evaluation(female(barney), Answer)). The response would of course be  Answer = Falsity  (at least if the interpreter had consulted newbedrock.swipl, which is essentially the old bedrock.swipl but with marital information added; see Program 14, Appendix H). Falsity would be the meaning of no, which is the honest answer someone would give to our question if newbed-rock.swipl described his or her view of the world. As to the meaning of Is Fred husband to Wilma?, we should interpret it in terms of the query in (53).  (53) Answer ::   (evaluation(husband(fred,wilma), Answer)). In this case, the answer would be 110 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS  Answer = Truth (at least under the model we have been assuming so far and the definition of evaluation). Truth is, of course, the meaning of yes, which is the honest answer someone holding the views described in newbedrock.swipl would give to our question. It should not escape the reader that both (49) and (50) are referential queries, as each is a query of the form in (6) above. The fact that the refer-ential variables in (49) and (50) is Answer rather than Reference does not affect our point, as the name of the referential variable was not part of the definition of referential query. Consequently, we may continue to say that the meaning of an expression is the procedure followed by a PROLOG interpreter fielding a referential query. Even in the face of questions, which have been traditionally considered to be nonreferential expressions. Like the referential queries of other expressions, the referential queries of questions may be formulated independently of the models the PROLOG interpreter has consulted when the query was addressed to it. In real life, questions are asked by individuals whose models do not provide enough information to answer them. The purpose of asking a question is that the addressee might apply the questioner’s referential query to the former's model of the world (in the hopes that those models might answer the ques-tion and the addressee might then respond to the questioner with that infor-mation). But real-life questions can also be asked when the questioner’s model can resolve the referential query they convey. These questions can be raised for a variety of purposes, be they causing a rhetorical effect, testing the knowledge of pupils, or extracting confessions from the rightly accused (only from the rightly accused can we get true confessions). As to open questions, their meanings will also be procedures followed in response to referential queries. Thus, the meaning of Who is husband to Wilma? may be the procedure corresponding to the referential question in (54). (54) Answer ::  (findall(A, husband(A,wilma), Answer)). The response the PROLOG interpreter would give to this question would be   Answer = [fred] as Fred is the only husband of Wilma in the strictly monogamous world described in newbedrock.swipl. Relative to models that describe worlds less monogamous than Bedrock, the answer PROLOG would give to this question might well be a list of two or more items. SEMANTICS: THE NATURE OF LINGUISTIC MEANING / 111 Complex open questions like Who is husband to whom? would be inter-preted in terms of procedures triggered by the following query: (55) Answer ::  (findall([A,B], husband(A,B), Answer)). The answer to this query (relative to our usual model) would be (56) Answer = [[fred,wilma], [barney,betty]]. And a triply-complex question like Who gave what to whom? would be ana-lyzed along the same lines as a list of triples (a ternary relation). 11 Historical Note We saw in Section 1 that German logician and philosopher Gottlob Frege distinguished meaning from reference. But Frege did more than tell us what meaning wasn’t; he also took a stab at what meaning actually was. Indeed, in one of the most blinding intuitions in the history of semantics, Frege pointed out that the meaning (or sense) of an expression was the way in which that expression presented its reference: Let a, b, c be the lines connecting the vertices of a triangle with the mid-points of the opposite sides. The point of intersection of a and b is then the same as the point of intersection of b and c. So we have different designa-tions for the same point, and these names (‘point of intersection of a and b’, ‘point of intersection of b and c’) likewise indicate the mode of presentation [of that which is designated]. It is natural, now, to think, of there being con-nected with a sign (name, combination of words, letter), besides that to which the sign refers, which may be called the reference of the sign, also what I should like to call the sense of the sign, wherein the mode of presenta-tion [of that which is designated] is contained. In our example, accordingly, the reference of the expressions ‘the point of intersection of a and b’ and ‘the point of intersection of b and c’ would be the same, but not their senses. The reference of ‘evening star’ would be the same as that of ‘morning star’, but not the sense (Frege 1892). Although there seems to be something unquestionably true about this state-ment, it is not immediately obvious what that is. For how, indeed, should we understand modes of presentation?  The classical answer to this question came from a student of Gottlob Frege himself—the philosopher Rudolf Carnap (1891-1970). In a nutshell, his answer was that the meaning of an expression was not the actual refer-ence of that expression, but only its potential reference. In other words, it was not just what the expression actually referred to (given the way things 112 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS are), but what it could have referred to as well (if things were other than what they are). Thus, although the nounphrases in (1a) and (1b) happen to refer to one and the same thing (planet Mercury), they didn’t have to. For it is possible to imagine that (1a) and (1b) could have referred to different celestial bodies (if things were different than they actually are).  Formally, the proposal is that the meaning of an expression is a function that assigns, to each possible set of circumstances, the reference of that ex-pression under those circumstances. The reference of an expression (under a specific set of circumstances) is called the extension of that expression (un-der those circumstances). As to the function itself, it was called the inten-sion of that expression. Circumstances are known also as states of affairs, state descriptions, or indices. But they are best known as possible worlds. Consequently, the theory of meaning proposed by Carnap is called today possible worlds semantics. Possible worlds semantics has been enormously successful, providing a mathematically precise, transreferential theory of meaning. It is not, howev-er, without its shortcomings. Chief among them is the problem of expres-sions which have the same intension yet differ in meaning. Take for in-stance the expressions 3 + 2 and 6 – 1. It should be clear that these two ex-pressions will refer to the same entity—namely the number five. And will do so not just under the present circumstances, but under every conceivable circumstance. In other words, these two expressions will have the same in-tension. It will be the constant function that assigns five to every possible world.  But 3 + 2 and 6 – 1 do not have the same meaning. Thus, while (57a) is a trivial equation, (57b) is not. Thus, (57a) is an equation that anyone can assent to—presumably even one who does not even know what 3 + 2 refers to. (57b), on the other hand, is a more interesting statement. One that cer-tainly requires knowing what both 3 + 2 and 6 – 1 refer to. (57) a.  3 + 2 = 3 + 2.  b.  3 + 2 = 6 – 1. So possible worlds semantics is a fine-grained theory of meaning, as it can distinguish between two or more expressions that have the same refer-ence. But it is not grained finely enough to resolve the semantic differences between nonsynonymous expressions which have the same intension—like 3 + 2 and 6 – 1. A discussion of the various responses to the problem of nonsynonymous cointensionals would take us too far afield. Suffice it to say here that the procedural theory of meaning that we have presented in this chapter—originally proposed in 1993 by Yannis Moschovakis—is able to assign different meanings to expressions that have the same reference in SEMANTICS: THE NATURE OF LINGUISTIC MEANING / 113 every possible world. 3 + 2 and 6 – 1, for example. This, in fact, is what we did in Section 2 above.  Incidentally, the procedural theory of meaning is related to possible worlds semantics in a natural way. The procedural theory of meaning holds that the sense of an expression is a procedure that assigns, to each suffi-ciently informative model, the reference of that expression relative to that model. Since each admissible model is a (sufficiently) complete description of the world, a referential procedure is a procedure for the computation of an intension. So meaning is not a function, but rather the computation of one. Possible worlds semantics is thus related to the procedural theory of meaning as a function is related to the procedures for computing it. Along the same lines, Hamblin (1973) proposed that the reference of a question be the set of answers to it. For, in order to really understand what a question means you must be able to know what would count as a possible answer to it. Our analysis of questions as referential procedures for the computation of their answers is systematically related to Hamblin’s idea. For a referential procedure will indeed exact an answer from any sufficient-ly complete description of the world. If we were to gather these answers together in a set (something we are not advising be done), we would get the set of possible answers to the question. 12 Conclusion The purpose of this chapter has been to implement and to illustrate the claim that meanings are referential procedures. Referential procedures are procedures for the computation of referents. But what are these referents? Up to now, they have all been (a) individuals or (b) relations over individu-als. These relations were ternary like the one conveyed by give (or by an open question asking for the three participants of a giving event), binary like the one conveyed by a transitive verb like love (or by a relational noun like spouse, or by a doubly open question asking for all the pairs involved in a spousal relation), unary like the one conveyed by male (or by a simple open question asking for who is male), or nullary like a truth value (con-veyed by a declarative sentence, a closed question, or a simple yes or a no).16 Examples of individuals referred to by linguistic expressions are deictic points (as conveyed by here, there, now, or then) or entities (which may or may not be conveyed by a proper name). In short, the relations of                                                             16 The decision to regard truth values as nullary relations issues from the fact that relations may be thought of as functions that yield a truth value when presented with the right number of individuals (truth if the relation holds among the given individuals and falsity otherwise). A truth value may therefore be thought of as what a relation yields when presented with no indi-viduals. These relations should therefore be regarded as nullary (or zero-place). 114 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS different dimensions (also known as arities or adities) are as follows. RELATION DEFINITION SOME EXPRESSIONS REFERRING TO IT Nullary A truth value Declarative sentences, closed ques-tions, yes, no. Unary A list of individuals Intransitive verbs, male, human, simply open questions. Binary A list of pairs of indi-viduals. Transitive verbs, kinterms, doubly open questions. Ternary A list of trios of indi-viduals. Ditransitive verbs, triply open ques-tions. To acknowledge their fundamental role, individuals are called zero-order entities. Relations among individuals are therefore said to be first-order entities. But meanings can also be procedures for the computation of higher-order entities.17 Relations of the second order are those that hold over lists of individuals; third order relations are those that hold over lists of lists of individuals—and so on. The set of relations languages can refer to is called the ontology of natural language; the set of individuals which on which such an ontology is defined is called the universe of natural dis-course. (58) The Ontology of Natural Language  a.  Zero-order entities (individuals)  b.  First-order entities (n-ary relations over individuals)  c.  Second-order entities (n-ary relations over lists of individuals)  d. Third-order entities (n-ary relations over lists of lists of individuals)       .  .  . So what are the possible orders of the entities that populate the ontology of natural language in (58)? What are the dimensions of the relations among them? And what are the possible universes of natural discourse? Answers to these questions are essential if we want to determine what, exactly, a possi-ble meaning is. It should be pointed out that the execution of procedures for the computation of higher-order relations is notoriously difficult— exponentially difficult, in fact. Fortunately, this difficulty does not extend to the formulation of these procedures. Or to the pairing of these procedures with sounds. These observations therefore hold out the hope that the com-plexity of referents will not prevent sounds from being paired efficiently with meanings—or with the queries that trigger them.                                                              17 Examples of such meanings will be given below, especially in Chapter 6. SEMANTICS: THE NATURE OF LINGUISTIC MEANING / 115 13 Suggestions for Further Reading The classical formulation of the distinction between sense and reference is Frege (1892). Possible worlds semantics was first presented in Carnap (1947), and its logic has been studied in Kripke (1959). The problem of nonsynonymous cointensionals was already noticed in Carnap (1947), but has been discussed in detail in the formal semantics literature ever since (see, for example, Chierchia and McConnell-Ginet 2000, 257-328).  The philosophical literature on the semantics of proper names is vast. The main sources for direct referentialism are Mill (1843) and Kripke (1972); the primary sources of indirect referentialism are Frege (1892), Russell (1918, IV) and, most notably, Bach (1981, 1994, 2002).  The literature on compositional analysis (better known now as lexical decomposition) is also vast. It starts with seventeenth century Rationalism (see Ishiguro 1972, 36-48; Rutherford 1995, 224-269), and is retaken in the twentieth century by the various schools of Linguistics: Structuralism (cf. Hjelmslev 1943, Ch. 14; Pottier 1963, Greimas 1966, and Coseriu & Geck-eler 1981), Descriptivism (Lounsbury 1964, Bendix 1966, Bendix 1971, Nida 1975), and Generativism (Lakoff 1965, McCawley 1968, McCawley 1971, Lehrer 1974, Dowty 1979, Jackendoff 1983, Jackendoff 1990a, Jackendoff 1990b, Pulman 2005). It is the core of Natural Semantic Meta-language (Boguslawski 1970, Wierzbicka 1972, Wierzbicka 1980, Wierzbicka 1996, Goddard & Wierzbicka 2005).  The PROLOG analysis of kinship terms in terms of two place predicates is proposed as an exercise in many PROLOG textbooks. See for example Bratko (2001). The analysis of deictics presented here draws heavily from Hoffman (1993, Chapter 4). Boole (1847) was the first to regard the truth values of certain complex sentences as functions of the truth values of the simple sentences in them; the idea that sentences refer to their truth values (via the thoughts or propositions they convey) is due to Frege (1892). 14 Exercises 1.
