Thus, while the words of our grammars pair sounds and meanings directly, the phrases of our grammars perform these pairings indirectly through words or other phrases. While this asymmetry suits phrases well, it leaves words wanting. And this, for two reasons: accuracy and principledness. First, the claim that words pair sounds and meanings directly predicts that there can only be finitely many of them. Yet, as the following sequences suggest, the set of words is truly infinite.  van    revolutionary  mini-van   counter-revolutionary  mini-mini-van   counter-counter-revolutionary  ...  ...  ...  ...  ...  ...   ...  ...  ...  ...  ...  ...  ...  ...  ...  ...  And, needless to say, sequences like these can be constructed from any lan-guage; not just from English.  Moreover, if we allowed words to continue to pair sound and meaning directly, then principledness would suffer as much as accuracy. For words are systematically related to one another. Both in sound and in meaning:  bake ~ baker ~ bakers  pretty ~ prettier ~ prettiest  fall ~ falls ~ falling ~ fell ~ fallen 118 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS And more dramatic examples could be drawn from languages other than English, where a single verb may take hundreds of forms depending on the tense, mood, or aspect it conveys. Or the person, number, or gender of its subject. Here is an example from Spanish—a halfway complex case. It con-sists of all the forms of the verb ir 'to go'. ir ~ yendo ~  ido ~  voy ~ vas ~ va ~ vamos ~ vais ~ van ~ fui ~ fuiste ~ fue ~ fuimos ~ fuisteis ~ fueron ~ iré ~ irás ~ irá ~ iremos ~ iréis ~ irán ~ iba ~ ibas ~ iba ~ íbamos ~ ibais ~ iban ~ iría ~ irías ~ iría ~ iría ~ iríamos ~ iríais ~ irían ~ vaya ~ vayas ~ vaya ~ vayamos ~ vayáis ~ vayan ~ fuera ~ fueras ~ fuera ~ fuéramos ~ fuerais ~ fueran ~ fuese ~ fueses ~ fuese ~ fuésemos ~ fueseis ~ fueran The traditional way to solve these problems is to introduce new pairings of sound and meaning; pairings which are neither words nor phrases. Two things would be asked of these new pairings. First, that they pair sound and meaning directly. Second, that both words and meanings be allowed to pair sounds and meanings indirectly through them. These new pairings have been called morphs.  Armed with morphs, words as well as phrases may constitute infinite sets of related signs. Take for instance the case of van, mini-van, mini-mini-van, and so on. We could say that these words consist of two morphs, name-ly mini and van. With these morphs one could form an infinity of words, namely van, mini-van, mini-mini-van, etc. And, if we did things the right way (see Section 3), these words would all be related by the fact that they were all produced by one and the same rule—one which appends the morph mini to a word that contains the morph van (and possibly nothing but the morph van). It goes without saying that morphs may not be an infinite set of related signs; they would be nothing more than a finite set of stipulated, ad hoc, pairings of sound and meaning. In other words, they would be just what we predicted words would be. Cast in a more positive light, morphs are the fundamental, underived, primary, basic, or direct pairings of sound and meaning that we once assumed words were. Morphs have played such an important role in the study of word for-mation that the study of word formation itself is now called morphology. In truth, however, word formation calls for more than morphs; it calls also for the processes by which words can be built from morphs. Two kinds of pro-cesses can be discerned, depending on whether they handle the combinator-ics or the instantiation of morphs. We shall deal with issues of combinator-ics in this chapter and leave instantiation for the next. From the point of view of combinatorics, about ten processes have been traditionally identi-fied. They are MORPHOLOGY I: THE COMBINATORICS OF WORDS / 119 Prefixation Circumfixation Metathesis Suffixation Interfixation Truncation Infixation Reduplication Recategorization Before characterizing them, let us illustrate this rather abstract discussion with a concrete example. 2 Time for a Concrete Example We turn now to a fragment of Spanish (Romance, Spain and her former colonies). It consists of twenty four pairings of sound and meaning. The rationale for this is that Spanish masculine forms are not necessarily male-denoting. Even when they denote—as they do here—sexed entities. The best way to make this point is to see what happens in the plural. For there, as is well known, the masculine is used for “mixed company”. Thus, a plural like amigos ‘friends’ includes both male and female friends. The simplest account of this fact is not that the meaning of masculinity changes mysteriously when it goes from the singular to the plural, but rather that the masculine is always gender-neutral. The reason why a masculine seldom refers to a female in the singular is that it would be pointless (if not misleading) to use a gender-neutral term when referring, knowingly, to a single female (the masculine is of course used in the singular when the sex of the entity referred to is unknown). They are as follows (while we are at it, we might as well provide the values for all the phones we need for Spanish, not just the ones above).3  β: 946 ɲ: 626 x: 120 r: 114  ɣ: 611 ɾ: 638 ʎ: 654    Next we write a program that specifies all the phones of phone.swipl plus the ones above.  We have called this program phoneplus.swipl (see Appendix H, Program 15). With these preliminaries out of the way, we may now develop a gram-mar for the fragment in (1). Let’s begin with the first two lines of (1). No-tice that the words in them are very similar. Both in sound and in meaning. Thus, the two words sound alike except for the very last phone. And they mean alike as well, as one of these words corresponds to English child while the other to daughter (i.e. female child). To account for these two similarities we will recognize a common morph in these two words. We may do so with the following conditional statement: (2) spanish([P1,P2],[morph],[R::findall([S,T],child(S,T),R)]):-   name(P1,[105]),   name(P2,[120]). Once the unicode values 105 and 120 are decoded, the statement in (2) says that Spanish pairs a morph [i,x] with a procedure for computing the child relation (which is intended to be the set of pairs [S,T] such that S is a child of T).4  From the morph [i,x] we may now generate the words in the first two lines of the fragment in (1). Generating [i,x,o] is a straightforward mat-ter. We may do it with the rule in (3): (3)  spanish(A,[word],B):-  spanish(C,[morph],B),  append(C,[o],A).                                                             3 The one new phone is [ʎ], which is a lateral palatal. 4 Since the categories of the meanings of this fragment will play no role in the sequel, we have decided to ignore them.  122 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS All this rule says is that appending a Spanish morph to the list [o] yields a Spanish word which conveys the same meaning as the morph. To generate the word on the second line of (1) we will appeal to the following rule: (4) spanish(A,[word],[R::findall([S,T],(U,female(S)),R)]):-  spanish(B,[morph],[R :: findall([S,T],U,R)]),  append(B,[a],A). The sound part of the rule is simple enough. It says that appending a Span-ish morph to the list [a] yields a Spanish word. The meaning part of the rule has more to it. But not much. It just says that, if there is a morph whose meaning is the procedure triggered by (5a), then there is a word whose meaning is the procedure triggered by (5b). (5) a.  R :: findall([S,T],(U),R).  b.  R :: findall([S,T],(U,female(S)),R). Thus, if there is a morph that refers to a given relation, then there will be a word that refers to the relation you get by making the first term of the given relation female. It follows that the statements in (2)-(4) capture all the rela-tions that hold between the words in the first pair of lines of (1). The words of the next nine pairs of lines of (1) can be generated, in en-tirely analogous ways, by recognizing nine more morphs. They are the morphs defined in rules two through ten in Program 16 of Appendix H. All nine of these morphs will undergo the rules in (3) and (4), which will then generate the words as desired. It should be noticed that the last four words of (1) are generated directly as words (and not as morphs that get appended to [o] and [a] in order to be-come words). The words in this last group are born fully formed as words— much like Athena burst forth fully formed (and fully armed) from the fore-head of Zeus. All the rules we have presented thus far have been gathered into Pro-gram 16 of Appendix H.  This program—which has been called kin-terms.swipl— is a grammar for the fragment in (1). Consequently, it should be judged in terms of explicitness, accuracy, principledness, simplic-ity, and efficiency (see Chapter 1). Explicitness can be tested easily enough. Just address the following query to any PROLOG interpreter that has con-sulted kinterms.swipl: (6) spanish(SO,SC,ME). Hitting Return and following this up with semicolons shows that all the clauses of this program run—so the grammar this program represents is explicit. Checking for accuracy is a more interesting affair. Notice first that all of the words in (1) will be generated by the query in (6). So the grammar does not undergenerate relative to (1). Crucially, however, it overgenerates relative to this fragment. This is because kinterms.swipl will generate not just words of Spanish, but morphs as well. If morphs were added to the fragment, the grammar would not overgenerate relative to it. I take it that this, then, is not a problem with the grammar, but rather with the fragment. It is just not a natural fragment—one that can be generated by an adequate grammar of Spanish. kinterms.swipl is principled, as it expresses, thanks to (3) and (4), 124 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS the relations between the pairs of words in (1) systematically. And it seems to be as simple as it can be. In any case, it is certainly simpler than the ob-vious alternative—the unprincipled grammar that generates each of the words in (1) with a separate statement. And, as we shall see in Chapter 10, kinterms.swipl is very efficient as well. For, the numbers of infer-ences needed to generate the expressions of this fragment grow step-by-step logarithmically. It will be noticed that kinterms.swipl requires the PROLOG inter-preter to consult kinship.swipl. This will ensure that all the referential queries mentioned in the rules will be properly answered by the interpreter. Thus, the referential queries of [s,w,e,ɣ,ɾ,o] ‘parent in law’ and [s,w,e,ɣ,ɾ,a] ‘mother in law’ are (7) and (8)—respectively.5  (7) R :: findall([S,T],parentinlaw(S,T),R). (8) R :: findall([S,T],(parentinlaw(S,T),female(S)),R). Addressing them to an interpreter that has furthermore consulted kin-ship.swipl will yield (9) and (10)—respectively. (9)[[fafa, mo], [famo, mo], [fafa, fabrwi], [famo, fabr-wi], [fafa, fasihu], [famo, fasihu], [mofa, fa], [momo, fa], [mofa, mobrwi], [momo, mobrwi], [mofa, mosihu], [momo, mosihu]]. (10)[[famo, mo], [famo, fabrwi], [famo, fasihu], [momo, fa], [momo, mobrwi], [momo, mosihu]]. 3 Prefixation Having characterized and illustrated morphs, we need to turn to the ten or so combinatoric processes that form words from them—starting with pre-fixation. We do this by capitalizing on an intuition that we all have as speakers—namely that every word has a main morph and, perhaps, a set of one or more secondary morphs. Thus, the main morph in the noun houses is house, and the secondary one is the s that signals plurality. The main morph in the verb runs is run, and the secondary one is the s that conveys that the verb is in the present tense and that its subject (i.e. the one doing the run-ning) is third person singular (i.e. a he, a she, or an it). Incidentally, notice that the s in houses and the s in runs are not the same morph. A morph is a pairing of sound and meaning, and these morphs clearly involve different                                                             5 Or (6a) and (6b) modulo the names of the variables in them, as PROLOG renames variables in the course of a computation.  MORPHOLOGY I: THE COMBINATORICS OF WORDS / 125 meanings—even if they pair them to the same sound. Capitalizing on these intuitions, we will say that the root of a word is its main morph, and that any one of its secondary morphs is an affix. We will also say that the stem of a word is a portion of a word that contains the root and, perhaps, one or more affixes. Notice that the root of a word, taken all by itself, is a stem of the word as well. And that a stem, all by itself, may constitute a word. We may now define prefixation as the morphological process whereby an affix is placed before a stem. Prefixation is an extremely common mor-phological process. To illustrate it, consider the following infinite fragment of English (we ignore phonetic transcriptions here).  (11)   A FRAGMENT OF ENGLISH (PREFIXATION)  van skirt golf  mini-van mini-skirt mini-golf  maxi-van maxi-skirt maxi-golf  mini-mini-van mini-mini-skirt mini-mini-golf  mini-maxi-van mini-maxi-skirt mini-maxi-golf  maxi-mini-van maxi-mini-skirt maxi-mini-golf  maxi-maxi-van maxi-maxi-skirt maxi-maxi-golf  …   …   … …   …   … …   …   … This is an infinite set of English words. Yet, we may generate it, thanks to morphs and recursion, with a finite grammar. A rather short one, in fact: english([v,a,n],stem). english([s,k,i,r,t],stem). english([g,o,l,f],stem). english([m,i,n,i],affix). english([m,a,x,i],affix). english(A,stem):-   append(B,C,A),   english(B,affix),   english(C,stem). english(A,B,C):-   length(A,C),   english(A,B). Program 17. prefixation.swipl  To generate the fragment in (11) , you may try and use the query in (12). 126 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS (12) english(SO,SC). Entering semicolons will produce longer and longer stems from the frag-ment in (11) (but remember that you have to enter a ‘w’ in order to stop PROLOG from abbreviating its responses). All of the prefixed stems, how-ever, will start with one and the same prefix, namely [m,i,n,i]. This is because PROLOG runs rules top-to-bottom and tests strings from left-to-right. Consequently, since the rule for mini happens to come before the rule for maxi in our grammar, the PROLOG interpreter will try and finish check-ing all the stems which start with [m,i,n,i] before testing the strings that begin with [m,a,x,i]. Since there are infinitely many of the former, it will never get to the latter (in a finite amount of time). Interestingly, PROLOG will still accept stems that start with [m,a,x,i]. Try for exam-ple, the following query. (13) english([m, a, x, i, v, a, n], stem). PROLOG will even reject stems that are not in the fragment in (11). Try for example (14). (14) english([v, a, n, m, i, n, i], stem). But what if you wanted to generate any stem of the fragment in (11)—even one that began with maxi—in a finite amount of time? You could bring length into the picture and make PROLOG generate stems in order of in-creasing length. You can do that with the three-place predicate english in prefixation.swipl. More specifically, by entering the query in (15) and following up with semicolons. (15) english(SO, stem, Length). And, if you do not care for knowing the length of the generated stem, you may replace Length by an unnamed variable: (15′) english(SO, stem, _). This use of Length is, of course, a trick. We are tricking PROLOG into generating stems in order of increasing length by requiring it to declare the lengths of the strings as it generates them (which PROLOG does by hy-pothesizing-and-then-checking lengths of increasing sizes starting from zero). But is this trick illegitimate? How do we humans generate a maxi-string without getting sidetracked by infinitely many mini-stems? The ques-tion can be posed at a more general level. Once speakers employ grammars with recursion, how do they avoid being trapped into it? Or into the first recursion they encounter? Maybe we have an ability to “look ahead” and determine that a particular course we have embarked in will not take us to MORPHOLOGY I: THE COMBINATORICS OF WORDS / 127 where we want to go. Or perhaps we are appealing to string-lengths in some way after all. This interesting issue will just have to be left for further re-search. prefixation.swipl is very particular in the way it generates stems. It takes an affix and appends it to a stem in order to form a new stem (to which stem an affix may then be appended). Crucially, it does not take an affix and appends it to another affix to form an “affix cluster” (which would then be appended to a stem in order to form a new stem). In other words, the structural description of maxi-mini-van is:  english([m,a,x,i],affix)                  english([m,i,n,i],affix)   english([v,a,n],stem)                english([m,i,n,i,v,a,n],stem)     english([m,a,x,i,m,i,n,i,v,a,n],stem)  it is not:           english([v,a,n],stem)   english([m,a,x,i],affix) english([m,i,n,i],affix)                   english([m,a,x,i,m,i,n,i],_)                              english([m,a,x,i,m,i,n,i,v,a,n],stem)  But this is exactly the way it should be. The meaning of maxi-mini-van is the coherent ‘large mini van’ rather than the incoherent ‘large-small van’. Although we have skirted meaning in prefixation.swipl, it should be clear that, if the meaning of a whole is to be built out of the meanings of 128 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS the parts, then the first of these two structural descriptions is the one that will lead most straightforwardly to the correct meaning of this stem. But prefixation.swipl is not without its shortcomings. As it turns out, the conditions on each of its conditional statements must be given in exactly the order in which they are given. But this is an odd requirement. The order in which the conditions of a conditional statement are written should not make any difference whatsoever. Especially when couched in a declarative language like PROLOG  (see Chapter 1 on the meaning of de-clarative programming language). The reader is invited to experiment with different orders on the conditions of these rules to gain a better appreciation of this peculiarity—a peculiarity about which we shall say no more here. Except that here we have had a close encounter with left-recursion (see Chapter 10). 4 Suffixation We have defined prefixation as the process whereby an affix is placed be-fore a stem. Suffixation is the process whereby an affix is placed after a stem. To illustrate, consider the following nouns from Spanish: momento ‘moment’ momentito ‘little moment’ momentitito ‘little little moment’ ...   ...   ...   As can be readily seen, these words are formed by appending a stem to an affix it—and doing so an unbounded number of times before appending the result to an affix o.6 This affix it is called diminutive because it selects, from a given set of entities, the ones which have a small (or diminutive) size; the affix o marks the membership of a word in a particular category— in this case, the class of nouns called masculine.  One way to generate the fragment above is as follows                                                                6 Notice that here we are introducing [o] categorematically as an affix while in kin-terms.swipl we entered it syncategorematically instead. Needless to say, in an actual grammar of Spanish we would have to decide on introducing [o] one way or another—but not both. MORPHOLOGY I: THE COMBINATORICS OF WORDS / 129 spanish([m,o,m,e,n,t],[stem]). spanish([i,t],[affix, diminutive]). spanish([o],[affix, masculine]). spanish(A,[stem]):-  append(B,C,A),  spanish(C,[affix, diminutive]),  spanish(B,[stem]). spanish(A,[noun, masculine]):-  append(B,C,A),  spanish(B,[stem]),  spanish(C,[affix, masculine]).  Part of Program 18. suffixation.swip1 To generate the fragment above, simply enter spanish(SO,[noun, masculine]). (and follow through with semicolons). Notice that even though this set is infinite, the grammar above may still reject nouns which do not belong to the fragment. Check, for example, spanish([m,i,n,u,t,i,t,o], [noun, masculine]). Notice also that our grammar will generate our nouns only in one way— namely as represented in the following structural description: spanish([m,o,m,e,n,t],_)     spanish([i,t],_)    spanish([m,o,m,e,n,t,i,t],_)    spanish([i,t],_)     spanish([m,o,m,e,n,t,i,t,i,t],_) spanish([o],_)    spanish([m,o,m,e,n,t,i,t,i,t,o],_) Notice also that this structure is what would be desired for the semantics of our nouns: the notion of ‘little’ needs to combine with things that have siz-130 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS es—moments, for example, even little ones. It really does not make sense for ‘little’ to combine with itself.  It should not escape the reader that the above structure is left-branching (starting at the root, only the left branch will branch). The structure gener-ated in the preceding section, however, was right-branching (starting at the root, only the right branch will branch). So we need both kinds of branching structures to describe language.  Also to be kept in sight is the fact that the order of the conditions in the rules above makes, again, a difference. Specially with regards to rejecting the words which are not in the fragment. The reader is invited to experiment with alternative orders. A more dramatic example of suffixation comes from Turkish (Altaic, Turkey). This language allows all the nouns that can be formed by follow-ing the “word chain” below from left to right (parenthesized elements are optional):        (de)  ev            (im   ( i )  deniz  (ler)                   (iz))  (den)  el            (in                  ( in )         ( le ) Here:  ev ‘house’, deniz ‘ocean’, el ‘hand’, ler ‘plural of the root’, im ‘first person possessor’, in ‘second person possessor’, iz ‘plural of the possessor’, de ‘locative’, i ‘accusative’,  den ‘ablative’, in ‘genitive’, le ‘instrumental’, and where forms without ler are interpreted as singular, forms without the possessor marker are interpreted as referring to unpossessed entities, and forms without a case affix are interpreted as nominative. Thus a word like ev-ler-im-iz-den would mean something like ‘out of our houses’.7 Calculating the combinatorics in question, the word chain above de-scribes one hundred and eighty Turkish words. The grammar below will generate all of them. And nothing but them. The grammar contains only two new things: the category of base and the category of case. A base is a cate-gory of expressions that are larger than stems but smaller than words. A case is a category of affixes that mark the function that a nounphrase plays in a sentence. 8                                                             7 I am indebted to Orhan Orgun for this nice example. 8 Much like a uniform indicates the job someone is performing. These functions include naming a location, referring to the undergoer of an action, identifying a source, pointing to a MORPHOLOGY I: THE COMBINATORICS OF WORDS / 131 The grammar defines three roots (ev, deniz, el) and nine affixes (ler, im, in, iz, de, i, den, in, le) directly. With them it defines two types of stem (sin-gular and plural), two types of base (possessed and unpossessed), two types of noun (nominative and otherwise), and two types of possessor (singular and plural). The statements of the grammar below have been labeled ac-cordingly. MORPHOLOGY I: THE COMBINATORICS OF WORDS / 133 5 Infixation It might be thought that prefixation and suffixation would exhaust the com-binatorial possibilities for a stem and an affix. But this assumes that mor-phological processes respect the integrity of stems and affixes—which they don’t. Take for example infixation, a morphological process whereby an affix is placed inside a stem. Unusual in Europe, this process is fairly com-mon among the Austronesian family of languages. For instance in Bontoc (Austronesian, Philippines), where we find the following alternations be-tween nominal expressions (in the left-hand column) and verbal expressions (in the right-hand column).9   pusi ‘poor’ pumusi ‘to be poor’  fikas ‘strong’ fumikas ‘to be strong’  kilad ‘red’ kumilad ‘to be red’  fusul ‘enemy’ fumusul ‘to be an enemy’  ngitad ‘dark’ ngumitad ‘to be dark’  What this data suggests is that Bontoc takes a nominal expression and in-serts um after the first phone in order to form a verbal expression.10 If so, we can duplicate this process with the following PROLOG program.               Part of Program 19. infixation.swipl The preceding program defines five nominal stems and one affix. The one rule of the program takes the affix and inserts it immediately after the first phone of a stem. To do that, the rule invokes the stroke notation, introduced in Chapter 2, to isolate the first phone A of the stem [A|B]. Then it ap-                                                            9 See Fromkin, Rodman, and Hyams (2009, 86). 10 The last alternation is not a counterexample to this—at least if ng spells the single phone [']. bontoc([p,u,s,i],nominal). bontoc([f,i,k,a,s],nominal). bontoc([k,i,l,a,d],nominal). bontoc([f,u,s,u,l],nominal). bontoc([ng,i,t,a,d],nominal). bontoc([u,m],affix). bontoc(D,verbal):-  bontoc([A|B],nominal),  bontoc(C,affix),  append([[A],C,B],D). 134 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS pends A to the affix C and to the rest B of the stem in order to form a new stem D. In other words, this rule is appending three lists to form a fourth. This is distinctly different from all the cases of append we have seen thus far, where two lists are appended in order to form a third.  But how can three-place append take four lists at a time? Plainly, it cannot. But notice that we are not appealing to three-place append here; we are appealing to a new, two-place version, of append. Built into PROLOG, this new version of append takes a list of lists and appends them all in one fell swoop. The list of lists is its first argument; the resulting list is its second. In other words, this version of append may be used to take any number of lists and concatenate them into one single list.  It should be clear that append/2 is a generalization of append/3, as the former can say anything that the latter can. The generalization is proper as the converse does not hold. To generate our Bontoc fragment, we can now enter the following (and enter semicolons). bontoc(SO,SC). For another example of infixation we will turn to the Native American lan-guage Sioux (Siouan, USA), where we find the following words (See Sapir 1921, 73).  cheti ‘to build a fire’ shuta  ‘to miss’  chewati ‘I build a fire.’ shuwata  ‘I miss.’  cheunti  ‘we build a fire.’ shuunta ‘we miss.’ It would seem that Sioux forms first-person forms of verbs by inserting an affix after the first syllable of the bare form of the verb. If so, we can ac-count for this with the following grammar.      Part of Program 19. infixation.swipl Remember append/2(A,B) is true iff A is a list of lists and B is the list that results from concatenating all of the lists of A into one. On the other hand, append/3(A,B,C) is true iff  C is the concatenation of a list A and a list B. sioux([[ch,e],[t,i]],root). sioux([[sh,u],[t,a]],root). MORPHOLOGY I: THE COMBINATORICS OF WORDS / 135           Part of Program 19 (continued). infixation.swipl It should not escape the reader that the one rule in this grammar of Sioux is identical to the one rule in the Bontoc grammar. The only difference be-tween the two languages in this regard is that the rule in question applies to phones in Bontoc but to syllables in Sioux. To ensure this difference is rec-ognized we need to make sure that the rule is presented with roots in the right format—a nontrivial matter that we assume has been independently resolved. Be that as it may, the accounts above allow us to capture what seems to be a cross-linguistic generalization: infixation occurs after the first element. What this element is varies from language to language—phones in Bontoc and syllables in Sioux, for example.  For our last example of infixation we turn to Ulwa (Misumalpa, Nicara-gua), where we find the following possessive forms of nouns (see Sproat 1992, 46ff):  'window' 'fish' 'dog' 'possum' IMPERSONAL uu bilam suulu wasala 1 SINGULAR uuki bilamki suukilu wasakila 1 INCLUSIVE uuni bilamni suunilu wasanila 1 EXCLUSIVE uukina bilamkina suukinalu wasakinala 2 SINGULAR uuma bilamma suumalu wasamala 2 PLURAL uumana bilammana suumanalu  wasamanala 3 SINGULAR uuka bilamka suukalu wasakala 3 PLURAL uukana bilamkana suukanalu wasakanala This table calls for a fair amount of explanation. For starters, let us say that a noun is possessive if it does two things. First, it refers to a person, a place, or a thing; second, it asserts that this person, place, or thing pertains to someone.11 In Ulwa, this someone might be me (first person singular), me-and-others-including-you (first person inclusive), me-and-others-excluding-                                                            11 Being owned or possessed by someone is a specially clear case of pertaining to him or her—hence the term possessive. sioux([[w,a]],affix). sioux([[u,n]],affix).   sioux(D,verb):-  sioux([A|B],root),  sioux(C,affix),  append([[A],C,B],D). 136 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS you (first person exclusive), you-alone (second person singular), you-and-others (second person plural), a-person-or-a-thing-other-than-you-or-me (third person singular), or some-persons-or-things-other-than-you-or-me (third person plural). Bare nouns (nouns that do not combine with one of the above person indicators) are nonpossessive or impersonal, and thus refer to a person, a place, or a thing—but without asserting that this person, place, or thing, pertains to anyone. At first blush, the data above is perplexing. While the possessive nouns in the first two columns show suffixation, the nouns in the last two columns show infixation instead. Setting this issue aside for the moment and assum-ing infixation was indeed at work in the last two columns, where would this infixation occur? Certainly not after the first phone. Or the first syllable. It could be before the last syllable, but that would counter the emerging gen-eralization that infixation occurs after a first unit. Pondering upon this issue a bit longer we might hit on a solution: the infix occurs after the first foot of the word (see Chapter 2)! This hypothesis would be confirmed by the nouns in the first two columns. For they involve one-foot roots. Consequently, it can be predicted that the affix would appear as suffixes there. Boldened by the confirmation of our prediction, we propose to generate the fragment in question with the grammar below.                  Part of Program 19. infixation.swipl This grammar will in fact generate the fragment in question when prompted to do so with the query ulwa(SO,SC). It will be noticed that the one rule in this grammar is again the same as ulwa([[[u,u]]],[noun]). ulwa([[[b,i],[l,a,m]]],[noun]). ulwa([[[s,u,u]],[[l,u]]],[noun]). ulwa([[[w,a],[s,a]],[[l,a]]],[noun]). ulwa([[[k,i]]],[affix]). ulwa([[[m,a]]],[affix]). ulwa([[[k,a]]],[affix]). ulwa([[[n,i]]],[affix]). ulwa([[[k,i],[n,a]]],[affix]). ulwa([[[m,a],[n,a]]],[affix]). ulwa([[[k,a],[n,a]]],[affix]). ulwa(D,[noun,possessive]):-  ulwa([A|B],[noun]),  ulwa(C,[affix]),  append([[A],C,B],D). MORPHOLOGY I: THE COMBINATORICS OF WORDS / 137 in the previous two grammars. The only trick is getting to it the forms in the right format (here, split into prosodic feet). If this can be secured, our gen-eralization about infixation as a second position phenomenon holds. We close this section with the observation that classical constituent structure trees cannot represent infixation as such. This is because “branch-crossing” is banned from such trees, and what we seem to need to do justice to infixation is something like this:       POSSESSIVE NOUN                NOUN                        FOOT              AFFIX      FOOT          2     PL         suu    ma     na        lu  No such problem arises with our structural descriptions, that can represent infixation straightforwardly and without branch-crossing as follows.        ulwa([m,a],you).     ulwa([n,a],all).                        ulwa([[m,a],[n,a]],youall’s).  ulwa([[s,u,u],[l,u]],dog).         ulwa([[s,u,u],[m,a],[n,a],[l,u]],youall’s dog).  This is because the nodes of our structural description can represent more than just categories; they can represent the effect of any well-defined opera-tion on the input strings—infixation or otherwise. It is at this point that an apparently redundant feature of our trees—their repetition of the sounds of the input strings—pays off. They are not just repeated; they are repeated together with (the effects of) an operation on them. If the operation respects the integrity of the input strings, the repetition is superfluous; but if the op-eration violates the integrity of the constituents, then the repetition is in-formative.12                                                              12 See Ojeda (2005) for a general discussion of branch-crossing or discontinuous constituency. 138 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS 6 Circumfixation Infixation is the process whereby an affix is placed inside a stem. Circum-fixation is the opposite of infixation; it is a morphological process whereby a stem is placed inside an affix. A well known example of circumfixation comes from German (Germanic, Central Europe), a fragment of which is as follows.   küss ‘kiss’  frag ‘ask’ lieb ‘love’  geküsst ‘have kissed’ gefragt ‘have asked’ geliebt ‘have loved’  It would seem that German forms verb participles by placing verbal stems within an affix ge-t (Fromkin et al. 2010, 86). Notice that this is really one affix rather than two. At least you cannot have one without having the other. So claiming it was two would create the problem of explaining why you have to have both or neither. If the preceding is an accurate description of the process, then it can be generated by the grammar below.         Part of Program 20. circumfixation.swipl Notice that here we have another application of append/2—the first term of which is a list of lists which the second concatenates.  Another example of circumfixation comes from Indonesian (Austrone-sian, Indonesia). Here we find a process that turns verbs into nouns (Ander-son 1992, 53):   VERB   NOUN    bisa   kebisaan    bangun   kebangunan    tidakmampu  ketidakmampuan This fragment can be described by the following grammar, which “wraps” an affix ke-an around a verb to turn it into a noun:   german([k,j,u,s],stem). german([f,r,a,g],stem). german([l,i,b],stem). german(B,participle):-  german(A,stem),  append([[g,e],A,[t]],B). MORPHOLOGY I: THE COMBINATORICS OF WORDS / 139  Part of Program 20. circumfixation.swipl A slightly more complex example comes from Chickasaw (Muskogean, USA). Witness the following fragment (Fromkin et al. 2010, 85):  chokma   ‘he is good’ ikchokmo  ‘he isn’t good’  lakna  ‘it is yellow’ iklakno  ‘it isn’t yellow’  palli    ‘it is hot’ ikpallo   ‘it isn’t hot’  tiwwi   ‘he opens (it)’ iktiwwo  ‘he doesn’t open (it)’ To account for this fragment we will use append in a new way. Up to now, we have used append to create new strings from old. But we may also use this predicate to analyze an old string into parts that we may then use to create a new string. Consider in this regard the following grammar.                    Part of Program 20. circumfixation.swipl  The one rule in this grammar makes two uses of append. The first requires a list B to be the result of the concatenation of two lists: C and a list [ _ ], which has to be a list that consists of only one item. Since this list is the second component of append, and since it consists of only one item, this item must therefore be the very last item of B. We have therefore split B in two: its very last item and everything that came before it. The second occur-rence of append then springs into action, taking C (one of the parts isolat-indonesian([b,i,s,a],verb). indonesian([b,a,n,g,u,n],verb). indonesian([t,i,d,a,k,m,a,m,p,u],verb). indonesian(B,noun):-  indonesian(A,verb),  append([[k,e],A,[a,n]],B).  chickasaw([ch,o,k,m,a],affirmative). chickasaw([l,a,k,n,a],affirmative). chickasaw([p,a,l,l,i],affirmative). chickasaw([t,i,w,w,i],affirmative). chickasaw(A,negative):-  chickasaw(B,affirmative),  append(C,[_],B),  append([[i,k],C,[o]],A).  140 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS ed by the first use of append), and circumfixing ik-o around it (the last item of B is then discarded). Notice that the first use of append involved append/3 while the second one involved append/2. The double use of append in circumfixation.swipl was not forced on us. PROLOG has a built-in predicate last that relates a list to its last element. With this predicate we can isolate the last member of a list and then use it, together with the original list, to build a third list—that con-sisting of everything that comes before the last element of the original list. This third list is what we needed for circumfixation.swipl.13 We will work with last in the sequel. 7 Interfixation Infixation is the process in which an affix is placed inside a stem; circum-fixation is the process in which a stem is placed inside an affix. Interfixa-tion is a morphological process whereby an affix and a stem are placed in-side each other. Consider by way of example the following exclamatives of Quechua (Andean-Equatorial, South America; see Cusihuamán 1976):  achacháw!   ‘how surprising!’    achaláw!  ‘how indignant!’  akakáw!  ‘how painful!’  akhakáw!  ‘how hot!’  alaláw!        ‘how cold!’  anyanyáw! ‘how pretty!’  atatáw! ‘how ugly!’ It would seem that Quechua produces interjections by interleaving an ex-clamative ‘root’ a-a-á-w with an adjectival ‘affix’ consisting of two conso-nants. Sometimes these consonants are the same, as in achacháw!; some-times they are different, as in achaláw! If so, then the exclamatives of Quechua can be specified by the follow-ing grammar:                                                             13 It seems that we would still need two statements to construct the list consisting of all mem-bers of a list other than the last. What would be needed to save on a statement is an equivalent of the stroke notation—one that starts from the end of a list rather than from the beginning. MORPHOLOGY I: THE COMBINATORICS OF WORDS / 141 Part of Program 21. interfixation.swipl Notice that this grammar makes three kinds of statements. The first is made at Line 1. It describes a root as a pairing of [a,a,a,w] with how(_). Since we have not developed an interpretation of exclamative sentences, we will simply assume that one is forthcoming, and that it will involve applying a one-place predicate how to an unnamed variable. The second kind of statement made by this grammar can be seen in any of the next seven statements. Each of these statements describes an affix as a pairing of a two-consonant sound with an adjectival meaning—which meaning is given simply via its English gloss.  The third kind of statement is the most complex. It creates an interjec-tion by interleaving the vowels of the root and the consonants of the affix and then pairing this with the meaning of the root—except that we demand that the 1st argument of the meaning C of the root, be the meaning D of the affix. The latter is performed by the condition arg(1,C,D). The meaning of such a statement is described in the box below. We may see the effect of the last line of the grammar above by consulting this grammar and then entering  quechua([a,a,a,w],root,how(_)).  quechua([ch,ch],affix,surprising). quechua([ch,l],affix,indignant). quechua([k,k],affix,painful). quechua([kh,k],affix,hot). quechua([l,l],affix,cold). quechua([ny,ny],affix,pretty). quechua([t,t],affix,ugly).  quechua([A1,B1,A2,B2,A3,A4],interjection,C):-   quechua([A1,A2,A3,A4],root,C),   quechua([B1,B2],affix,D),   arg(1,C,D).  Remember  If N is a number, then arg(N,A,B) is true iff the Nth argument of A is B. 142 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS quechua(SO, interjection, ME).   Much more elaborate examples of interfixation can be found in the Semitic family of languages. Consider for instance the following fragment of Arabic (Semitic, Middle East and South Asia; see Sproat 1992, 51).   katab   ‘write’ kutib  ‘be written by’  kattab  ‘cause to write’ kuttib   ‘cause to be written by’  kaatab  ‘correspond’ kuutib   ‘be corresponded by’  takaatab  ‘write to each other’ tukuutib  ‘be written by each other’  nkaatab ‘subscribe’ nkuutib ‘be subscribed by’   ktatab ‘write’ ktutib ‘be written by’  staktab ‘dictate’ stuktib ‘be dictated by’  In all these examples, a “triliteral root” k-t-b ‘write’ seems to be interfixed with a series of vowels to produce various forms of the verb write. If the vowels are [a, i], the forms are active; if they are [u, i], they are passive in-stead (see Chapters 3 and 9 for more on actives and passives). A similar pattern would emerge if we picked a different triliteral root—say q-t-l ‘kill’.  In other words, we may traverse the following “word chain” from left to right and obtain a variety of grammatical Arabic words (the CV templates on the left are known as binyanim in Hebrew grammatical studies—but notice that the singular of binyanim is binyan—and they are commonly re-ferred as such in contemporary linguistics). An ex-ample of full reduplication comes from Indonesian (Austronesian, Indone-sia):  rumah ‘house’  rumah-rumah ‘houses’  ibu ‘mother’  ibu-ibu ‘mothers’  lalat ‘fruit-fly’  lalat-lalat ‘fruit-flies’  So Indonesian seems to form plurals by reduplicating singulars. If so, this can be captured by the following grammar: arabic([k,t,b],root). arabic([q,t,l],root). arabic([a,a],affix). arabic([u,i],affix). arabic([C1,V1,C2,V2,C3],verb):-  arabic([C1,C2,C3],root),  arabic([V1,V2],affix). arabic([C1,V1,C2,C2,V2,C3],verb):-  arabic([C1,C2,C3],root),  arabic([V1,V2],affix). arabic([C1,V1,V1,C2,V2,C3],verb):-  arabic([C1,C2,C3],root),  arabic([V1,V2],affix). arabic([t,V1,C1,V1,V1,C2,V2,C3],verb):-  arabic([C1,C2,C3],root),  arabic([V1,V2],affix). arabic([n,C1,V1,V1,C2,V2,C3],verb):-  arabic([C1,C2,C3],root),  arabic([V1,V2],affix). arabic([C1,t,V1,C2,V2,C3],verb):-  arabic([C1,C2,C3],root),  arabic([V1,V2],affix). arabic([s,t,V1,C1,C2,V2,C3],verb):-  arabic([C1,C2,C3],root),  arabic([V1,V2],affix). 144 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS            Part of Program 22. reduplication.swipl For an example of partial reduplication, consider the following words from Tagalog (Austronesian, Philippines; see Ohio State Department of Linguis-tics 1994, 145):   bili  ‘buy’ bibili  ‘will buy’  kain  ‘eat’ kakain  ‘will eat’  pasok ‘enter’ papasok ‘will enter’  Judging from these facts, Tagalog forms the future form of a verb by redu-plicating the first syllable of the bare form of the verb. If so, we may ac-count for it as follows.          Part of Program 22. reduplication.swipl For a different example of reduplication, we may turn to Yidiny (Australian, Australia; see Sproat 1992, 57):   mulari ‘initiated man’  mulamulari ‘initiated men’  gindalba  ‘lizard’  gindalgindalba  ‘lizards’  Here, Yidiny forms plurals by reduplicating the first prosodic foot of the singular. If so, we can generate all these forms with the following grammar: indonesian([r,u,m,a,h],singular). indonesian([i,b,u],singular). indonesian([l,a,l,a,t],singular).  indonesian(B,plural):-  indonesian(A,singular),  append(A,A,B). tagalog([[b,i],[l,i]],verb). tagalog([[k,a],[i,n]],verb). tagalog([[p,a],[s,o,k]],verb).  tagalog(C,future):-  tagalog([A|B],verb),  append([A],[A|B],C). MORPHOLOGY I: THE COMBINATORICS OF WORDS / 145 Part of Program 22. reduplication.swipl It should not escape the reader that the process for reduplicating in Tagalog is the same as the process for reduplicating in Yidiny. In both cases, you reduplicate the first unit of the input form. What varies is what this unit is. Capitalizing on this observation, we may use essentially the same rule in both grammars; all we need to do is ensure that the forms serving as inputs for the rules will have been antecedently parsed into syllables and feet, re-spectively (which is not a trivial request). As a matter of fact, we could also use essentially the same rule for the full reduplication of Indonesian. All we need to do for full reduplication is to include the full form before the stroke: indonesian(B,plural):-  indonesian([A|[]],singular),  append(A,[A|[]],B).  A significantly more complex case of reduplication comes from Bambara (Northwestern Mande, Mali; see Culy 1985). We begin by looking at the Bambara words for dog, rice, watch, and search. These words call for rules like the following (since the example we wish to present does not hinge on meaning, we will simplify our semantic representations of Bambara and revert, once again, to English glosses). bambara([[w,u],[l,u]],[noun],[dog]). bambara([[m,a],[l,o]],[noun],[rice]). bambara([[f,i],[l,e]],[verb],[watch]). bambara([[n,i],[n,i]],[verb],[search]). Two of these words are nouns and two are verbs. The verbs can take a suf-fix [D, a], where D is the onset of the last syllable of the verb. After being suffixed in this way, each of these verbs becomes a noun that refers to the set of entities that perform the action conveyed by the verb. Thus, corre-sponding to the verb file ‘watch’, we get the noun filela ‘watcher’, and cor-responding to the verb nini ‘search’ we get the noun ninina ‘searcher’. Such nouns are called agentive. We can generate these agentive nouns with the following rule. yidiny([[[m,u],[l,a]],[[r,i]]],singular). yidiny([[[g,i,n],[d,a,l]],[[b,a]]],singular). yidiny(C,plural):-  yidiny([A|B],singular),  append([A],[A|B],C). 146 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS bambara(A,[noun,agentive],[B,er]):-  bambara([C|[[D,E]]],[verb],[B]),  append([C|[[D,E]]],[[D,a]],A). Next, we note that Bambara may append a noun like wulu ‘dog’ to an agen-tive noun like filela ‘watcher’ in order to form a new noun wulu-filela ‘dog watcher’. This new noun may then be appended to an agentive noun like ninina ‘searcher’ to form yet a new noun wulu-filela-ninina ‘dog watcher searcher’—i.e. someone who searches people who watch dogs. This process may be repeated ad infinitum:  wulu-filela  ‘dog watcher’  wulu-ninina  ‘dog searcher’  wulu-filela-filela ‘dog watcher watcher’  wulu-filela-ninina ‘dog watcher searcher’           .  .  .                               .  .  . This infinite fragment maybe generated by the following statement: bambara(E,[noun],F):-  bambara(A,[noun],B),  bambara(C,[noun,agentive],D),  append(A,C,E),  append([B],[D],F). At this point, readers may want to check their understanding of these rules by generating a long series of nouns via: bambara(SO,[noun], ME). We are finally in a position to introduce the reduplication rule of Bambara. Acoustically, it is a process that takes a noun and reduplicates it fully after inserting an [o] between the original and the duplicate. Semantically, it is a process that takes a noun that means N and yields a quantified noun that means any N. (21) illustrates. (21) a.  wulu-filela ‘dog watcher’  b.  wulu-filela-o-wulu-filela ‘any dog watcher’ This reduplication can be achieved by the following rule bambara(A,[noun,quantified],[any,C]):-  bambara(B,[noun],C),  append([B,[o],B],A). To see what this rule accomplishes enter the following:  bambara(SO, [noun,quantified], ME). MORPHOLOGY I: THE COMBINATORICS OF WORDS / 147 Consider now the class of Bambara nouns of the following form (where a, b, c, d are all positive integers): (22) wulu – (filela)a  – (ninina)b  – o – wulu – (filela)c  – (ninina)d  , Notice that these nouns would mean     any dog (watcher)a (searcher)b where (watcher)a represents a-many instances of the word watcher and (searcher)b represents b-many instances of the word searcher.  The interest of the class of words in (22) is this. Since these words were all created by reduplication then, in (22), a must equal c. And b must equal d. Consequently the words in (22) are really all of the form in (23). (23) wulu – (filela)a  – (ninina)b  – o – wulu – (filela)a  – (ninina)b This means that the grammar of Bambara must carry out a “double match-ing” here: it must match the number of filelas to the left and to the right of the [o] while, at the same time, matching the number of nininas to the left and to the right of the [o]. But this double matching is beyond the generat-ing power of context-free grammars—an important class of grammars in formal language theory (see Culy 1985). This is a fundamental result re-garding the complexity of human language. And, potentially, the tractability of its decision problem (see Conclusion below). 9 Metathesis Metathesis is a morphological process whereby parts of a word are reor-dered or permuted. Consider for example the following fragment of Saanich (Salishan, Vancouver; see Montley 1986, §2.3.5.4.1):  tlek ‘pinch’ telk ‘pinching’  lpex ‘scatter’ lepx ‘scattering’  thleq ‘pierce’ thelq ‘piercing’ It would seem that Saanich forms the progressive form of certain roots by permuting its middle two phones. If this peculiar description is accurate, the forms above can be derived by the grammar below:   148 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS Program 23. metathesis.swipl Metathesis is a very rare morphological process. It is indeed hard to find examples of morphological metathesis other than this. This contrasts with phonetic metathesis, where phones or syllables are reordered without any effects on meaning or category. Phonetic metathesis is a much more com-mon phenomenon. 10 Truncation Truncation is a morphological process whereby a portion of a word is de-leted. Consider for example the following proper names of English:  FORMAL FAMILIAR  Jerry Jer  Peter Pete  Michael Mike  Susan Sue  Jennifer Jen  Jan Jan It seems that English makes a proper name familiar by deleting everything but its first syllable. Notice, however, that the first syllable must be stressed for this to work. Thus, Denise does not reduce to Den. Or Alicia to Al. A grammar for the truncation of formal names can be found in Program 24 of Appendix H. Key to that grammar is the following rule.         Part of Program 24. truncation.swipl This rule of course derives the short, familiar forms, from the long, formal ones. But notice that it would be logically possible to derive the long forms from the short ones as well. Such a move, however, would be ill-advised. We would need one rule per derived form, as there would be no way to pre-dict what the long form would be given the short one (cf. Al, which would saanich([t,l,e,k],[verb,root],[pinch]). saanich([l,p,e,x],[verb,root],[scatter]). saanich([th,l,e,q],[verb,root],[pierce]).  saanich([A,B,C,D],[verb,progressive],[E,ing]):-  saanich([A,C,B,D],[verb,root],[E]). english([A],[name,familiar]):-  english([A|_],[name,formal]). MORPHOLOGY I: THE COMBINATORICS OF WORDS / 149 be the truncated form of Albert, Alfred, Allen. Or Almerindo, for that mat-ter). Incidentally, truncation shows that morphs cannot be defined as the min-imal pairings of sound and meaning (and use them, at the same time, to de-fine words from them). What truncation shows is that some morphs must be longer than the words derived from them. Morphs must be defined instead on logical grounds as the fundamental, basic, underived pairings of sound and meaning; not on metrical terms as the shortest ones. Another example of truncation comes from the verbs of Papago (Uto-Aztecan, USA). For, notice that some verbs of Papago convey explicitly whether the action expressed by the root of the verb is happening once (and is thus a single event), or repeatedly (and is thus a multiple event). In addi-tion, Papago verbs may indicate whether an action happens over an interval of time (a durative event) or over a moment of time (a momentary event). Examples of such verbs are given in the table below (Anderson 1992, 65). EVENT DURATIVE MOMENTARY SINGLE hink 'single barking' hin 'single barked' MULTIP hihink 'multiple barking' hihin 'multiple barked' SINGLE gatwid 'single shooting' gatwi 'single shot' MULTIP gagtwid 'multiple shooting' gagtwi 'multiple shot'  SINGLE Ɂelpig ‘single peeling’ Ɂelpi ‘single peeled’ MULTIP ɁeɁelpig ‘multiple peeling’ ɁeɁelpi ‘multiple peeled’ SINGLE huksan 'single scratching' huksa 'single scratched' MULTIP huhuksan 'multiple scratching' huhuksa 'multiple scratched' EITHER hehem 'single/multiple laughing' hehe 'single/multip. laughed' EITHER cicwi     'single    /    multiple      playing    /    played'  As can be readily seen, Papago verbs convey the single/multiple distinction in terms of reduplication, while they express the durative/momentary dis-tinction in terms of truncation. Focusing here, as we should, on the latter, we observe that the momentary forms are created by the deletion of the last consonant—if any—of the durative form. Consequently, this truncation can be captured by the last statement of the grammar below. For, notice that this statement requires that the durative form C be the result of appending the momentary form A to one last consonant D. But this is another way of say-ing that the momentary form arises from the deletion of the last consonant of its durative counterpart. 150 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS Part of Program 24. truncation.swipl Notice, by the way, that we acknowledge the fact that hehem and cicwi are neutral with respect to the single/multiple distinction by inserting a name-less variable where this distinction would be marked—in the second posi-tion of their categorial lists. This has the effect of making both forms com-patible with either marking. Along the same lines, we indicate that cicwi is neutral with respect to the durative/momentary distinction by introducing a second nameless variable where that distinction would be marked (which in this case is the first position of its categorial list). This ensures that cicwi will be compatible both with the durative and with the momentary specifi-cations. This is similar to what we did to generate the numerals of Mandarin in Chapter 1. For our last example of truncation we will turn to another Native Ameri-can language that marks the single/multiple distinction in its verbal system. It is Koasati (Muskogean, USA). Consider the following fragment (cf. Sproat 1992, 64; Anderson 1992,65f).  SINGLE MULTIPLE ROOT GLOSS  lasap las   ‘lick’  lataf lat   ‘kick’  pitaf pit   ‘slice’  atakaa atak  ‘hang’  acokcana acokcan  ‘quarrel’  obakhitip obakhit  ‘go backwards’ Although hardly obvious, Koasati creates the multiple form of a verb by deleting the rhyme of the last syllable of its single counterpart. This means papago([h,i,n,k],[durative,single]). papago([h,i,h,i,n,k],[durative,multiple]). papago([g,a,t,w,i,d],[durative,single]). papago([g,a,g,t,w,i,d],[durative,multiple]). papago([?,e,l,p,i,g],[durative,single]). papago([?,e,?,e,l,p,i,g],[durative,multiple]). papago([h,u,k,s,a,n],[durative,single]). papago([h,u,h,u,k,s,a,n],[durative,multiple]). papago([h,e,h,e,m],[durative,_]). papago([c,i,c,w,i],[_,_]). papago(A,[momentary|B]):-  papago(C,[durative|B]),  append(A,[D],C),  cns(D). MORPHOLOGY I: THE COMBINATORICS OF WORDS / 151 that we could propose the following grammar for this fragment (for reasons of expediency we make an outrageous but harmless assumption in this grammar; we assume that syllable.swipl, the syllable theory of Eng-lish, applies to Koasati as well). Part of Program 24. truncation.swipl Notice that we need here three applications of append. The first of these applications isolates the last syllable (D) of the single form (B); the second of these applications picks out the beginning (E) of this syllable. The third appends C (which is the single form of the verb minus its last syllable) to E (which is the onset of this last syllable) in order to yield the multiple form (A) of the verb. All three uses of append involve the three-place version of this predicate. It goes without saying that, although it would be logically possible to de-rive the long forms of Papago and Koasati from the short ones, it would be entirely inappropriate to do so. Both on grounds of principledness and on grounds of simplicity. It follows, once again, that we need to derive the short forms from the long ones—so morphs cannot be minimal pairings of sound and meaning; only underived ones. 11 Mutation Mutation is a morphological process whereby some of the phones of a morph are substituted for others. Implicit in this definition is the claim that the phones in question are not morphs. This is achieved by two uses of append. The first is analytical, and serves to isolate the final sound of the given noun. The second is syn-thetic, and is used to create the new verb. voicing is an interesting pro-cess that we describe here in an un-interesting fashion, as follows: voicing(th,dh). voicing(s,z). voicing(f,v). Another example of mutation comes from Irish (Celtic, Ireland; see Sapir 1921, 75). Here we witness a form of ablaut or vocalic mutation in the past tense forms (be they singular, plural, or participial) of the verbs of certain classes:  irish([b,r,i,s],stem). irish([c,u,i,r],stem). irish([d,o,i,r,t],stem). irish([g,l,a,n],stem). irish([m,o,l],stem). irish([p,i,l,l],stem). irish([s,e,a,n],stem). irish([t,a,i,r,g],stem). irish([A|B],past):-  irish([C|B],stem),  aspiration(C,A). 154 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS STEM PAST SING. PAST PLUR. PAST PPIAL. GLOSS  /biit/       /beit/       /bit/        /bit/ ‘bite’ /riif/       /reif/       /rif/        /rif/ ‘tear’  /bjoodh/       /boeidh/       /bjudh/        /bodh/ ‘offer’ /strjuuk/       /stroeik/       /strjuk/        /strok/ ‘stroke’  /brest/       /brast/       /brust/        /brost/ ‘burst’  /finn/       /fann/       /fynd/        /fund/ ‘find’  The ablaut in all of these forms consists of substituting either a syllabic nu-cleus or a syllabic rhyme by a sequence of phones. An example of the first possibility can be seen in the rule that derives the past singular [b,e,i, t] from the first-class stem [b,i,i,t]. The rule is as follows:  icelandic([A],[past,singular]):-  icelandic([B],[stem,1]),  append([C,D,E],B),  onset(C),nucleus(D),coda(E),  append([C,[e,i],E],A).  Notice that we again make two uses of append here. First as an analytical tool (to split a syllable B into its onset C, its nucleus D, and its coda E), and second as a synthetic tool (to append C to a sequence [e,i] and then to E to form a new syllable A; the original nucleus D is then discarded). An example of the second possibility is found in the rule that derives the past plural form [f,u,n,d] from the fourth-class stem [f,i,n,n]:  icelandic([A],[past,participial]):-  icelandic([B],[stem,4]),  append(C,D,B),  onset(C),rhyme(D),  append(C,[u,n,d],A).  Again two uses of append are involved in the conditions of this rule. One splits a syllable B into its onset C and its rhyme D; the second appends C to [u,n,d] to form a new syllable A. The original rhyme D is then discarded. Technically, it is a form of mu-tation—of which it is an extreme case. The forms of the verb to be are prime examples of suppletion. Not just in English, but in general. In English, the form are these:     Person                  Present                    Past     Singular      Plural     Singular    Plural      First        am                  are        was                were     Second        are                  were     Third         is         was One way to capture the patterns of this data is with the following grammar:   Program 27. suppletion.swipl                                                             14 mutation.swipl is based on icelandic_syllable.swipl, a makeshift pro-gram for Icelandic syllables (see Program 25 in Appendix H).  english([b,i],[stem]). english([ae,m],[present,first,singular]):-  english([b,i],[stem]). english([a,r],[present,second,singular]):-  english([b,i],[stem]). english([i,z],[present,third,singular]):-  english([b,i],[stem]). english([a,r],[present,_,plural]):-  english([b,i],[stem]). english([w,a,z],[past,first,singular]):-  english([b,i],[stem]). english([w,e,r],[past,second,singular]):-  english([b,i],[stem]). english([w,a,z],[past,third,singular]):-  english([b,i],[stem]). english([w,e,r],[past,_,plural]):-  english([b,i],[stem]). 156 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS Notice this program recognizes the randomness of the forms of the English verb to be by not defining the conjugated form in terms of the stem. Yet, it acknowledges that all the conjugated forms are forms of the verb to be by conditioning the various forms on the stem. This is recognized in the struc-tural descriptions for the conjugated forms, which would be trees with two nodes rather than one (one for the stem and another for the conjugated verb):  english([b,i], [stem])   english([ae,m], [present, first, singular])  Notice also that there are unnamed variables in the slot of the grammatical person of present plural (are) and past plural (were). This is to say that there is only one form in each case—a form which neutral (and hence compati-ble) with all three grammatical persons. Such situation is traditionally de-scribed by saying that person is unmarked in the plural of the English verb to be.  13 Recategorization Recategorization is a morphological process whereby a word from one cat-egory is assigned to another category without changing its sound in any way. English is a very good example here. Consider the following words (O’Grady 2005, 134). I II III ink (a contract) (a) run empty (a box) butter (a toast) (a stiff) drink better (my score) ship (the package) (a long) drive right (a wrong) nail (the problem) (a good) report total (the car) button (a shirt) (a judgment) call clear (the table) It seems clear that the words in Column I are all verbs derived from nouns (denominal verbs), that the words in Column II are nouns derived from verbs (deverbal nouns), and that the words in Column III are verbs derived from adjectives (deadjectival verbs). We can derive all these words (and relate them to their correct sources) with the following grammar. MORPHOLOGY I: THE COMBINATORICS OF WORDS / 157                                      Program 28. recategorization.swipl  The point of the categories is twofold. First, we want to control which forms can be turned into nouns or verbs. Second, we want to prevent the rules to apply to their own output—so that verbs do not turn into nouns to be turned into verbs again. 14 Conclusion The words of any language form an infinite set of related items. To capture this double fact Linguistics traditionally resorts to word formation—and more specifically to morphology. Morphology is the theory that describes how words are formed from morphs and processes. The morphs of mor-phology are pairings of sound and meaning that are generated directly (and which can be used to form words and phrases indirectly from them); the processes of morphology are either combinatoric or instantiating. Nine combinatoric processes have been identified. They are prefixation, suffixa-tion, infixation, circumfixation, interfixation, reduplication, metathesis, truncation, and recategorization.  In this chapter we have accounted for examples of all these processes in terms of PROLOG. This means that these accounts are explicit. Beyond this, english(ink,[noun,verbalizable]). english(butter,[noun,verbalizable]). english(ship,[noun,verbalizable]). english(nail,[noun,verbalizable]). english(button,[noun,verbalizable]). english(run,[verb,nominalizable]). english(drink,[verb,nominalizable]). english(drive,[verb,nominalizable]). english(report,[verb,nominalizable]). english(call,[verb,nominalizable]). english(empty,[adjective,verbalizable]). english(better,[adjective,verbalizable]). english(right,[adjective,verbalizable]). english(total,[adjective,verbalizable]). english(clear,[adjective,verbalizable]).  english(A,[verb, denominal]):-  english(A,[noun, verbalizable]). english(A,[noun, deverbal]):-  english(A,[verb, nominalizable]). english(A,[verb, deadjectival]):-  english(A,[adjective, verbalizable]).  158 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS all of our accounts are arguably simple, accurate, principled, and efficient. Very efficient, in fact, as we will see in Chapter 10. It is true that the processes we have formulated in this chapter are but a sample of the combinatoric processes found in the languages of the world. But the processes we have sampled are far from trivial. As a matter of fact, one of them was Bambara reduplication, a process which is demonstrably trans-context-free (see Section 8 above). This is noteworthy because trans-context-free grammars are not guaranteed to have efficient solutions to their decision problems. As a matter of fact, all grammars with inefficient (or even nonexistent) solutions to their decision problems are trans-context-free. 15 Suggestions for Further Reading The reader could benefit at this point from specialized textbooks in Mor-phology, like Spencer (1991), Sproat (1992), Katamba (1993).
