Consider for example the following sounds—and nonsounds— of English.1 (1) [æ,m,p,l] *[æ,n,p,l] *[æ,ŋ,p,l]  [æ,m,b,l] *[æ,n,b,l] *[æ,ŋ,b,l]  [æ,n,t,l] *[æ,m,t,l] *[æ,ŋ,t,l]  [æ,n,d,l] *[æ,m,d,l] *[æ,ŋ,d,l]  [æ,ŋ,k,l] *[æ,m,k,l] *[æ,n,k,l]  [æ,ŋ,g,l] *[æ,m,g,l] *[æ,n,g,l] Notice that the nonsounds in (1)—the lists preceded by an asterisk—are not just accidentally missing from English. They are distinctly difficult to pro-duce for a competent English speaker. How are we to account for this fact? Given the tools we have developed thus far, all we can do is adopt a set of rules that will generate all the forms in the first column. And only the forms in the first column. A set of rules that will do this is given in (2).2                                                             1 [æ,n,t,l] and [æ,n,d,l] are certainly possible sounds of English, as they are actual family names. 2 I ignore here sound categories, meanings, and meaning categories, as they will play no role in this example. 162 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS (2) english([P1,P2,P3,P4]):- phone(P1),low(P1),not(bck(P1)),  phone(P2),nas(P2),lab(P2),  phone(P3),lab(P3),not(cnt(P3)),not(voi(P3)), phone(P4),snt(P4),not(nas(P4)),alv(P4), not(pal(P4)). english([P1,P2,P3,P4]):-  phone(P1),low(P1),not(bck(P1)),  phone(P2),nas(P2),lab(P2),  phone(P3),not(snt(P2)),lab(P3),not(cnt(P3)),voi(P3),  phone(P4),snt(P4),not(nas(P4)),alv(P4),not(pal(P4)). english([P1,P2,P3,P4]):-  phone(P1),low(P1),not(bck(P1)),  phone(P2),nas(P2),alv(P2),  phone(P3),alv(P3),not(cnt(P3)),not(voi(P3)),  phone(P4),snt(P4),not(nas(P4)),alv(P4),not(pal(P4)). english([P1,P2,P3,P4]):-  phone(P1),low(P1),not(bck(P1)),  phone(P2),nas(P2),alv(P2),  phone(P3),not(snt(P3)),voi(P3),not(cnt(P3)),alv(P3),  phone(P4),snt(P4),not(nas(P4)),alv(P4),not(pal(P4)). english([P1,P2,P3,P4]):-  phone(P1),low(P1),not(bck(P1)),  phone(P2),nas(P2),vel(P2),  phone(P3),not(voi(P3)),vel(P3),  phone(P4),snt(P4),not(nas(P4)),alv(P4),not(pal(P4)). english([P1,P2,P3,P4]):-  phone(P1),low(P1),not(bck(P1)),  phone(P2),nas(P2),vel(P2),  phone(P3),not(snt(P3)),voi(P3),vel(P3),  phone(P4),snt(P4),not(nas(P4)),alv(P4),not(pal(P4)). But this strategy is insufficient. Analyzing the data in (1) more closely we realize three things:   • [m]occurs only before [p] and [b].  • [n]occurs only before [t]and [d].  • [ŋ]occurs only before [k]and[g]. MORPHOLOGY II: THE INSTANTIATION OF WORDS /  163 And this can hardly be an accident.[m],[p],[b]are all labial; [n],[t], [d] are all coronal; [ŋ],[k],[g]are all velar. In short, it seems that na-sals have the same point of articulation as ensuing consonants—or that na-sals are homorganic with ensuing consonants. Yet, the strategy we adopted above makes it seem like an accident that the forms in the first column of (1) are homorganic and the ones in the other two columns are not. Although this strategy would abide by the generalization that nasals are homorganic with the ensuing consonant, it would fail to express it. And if this were all our grammar had to say about the homorganicity of nasals, our grammar would be inadequate on grounds of principledness. Our strategy cannot be salvaged by supplementing (2) with an ad hoc statement that nasals are homorganic with ensuing consonants. For, if (2) yields the desired homorganicity of nasals already, then the statement in question would be redundant in a grammar—it would not contribute to specify any pairing of sound and meaning. Consequently, adding the pro-posed statement to (2) would be inadequate on grounds of simplicity. A better way to make nasals homorganic to ensuing consonants would be to remove the information about the points of articulation of our nasals and then restore it by appealing to homorganicity. This is what we have done in (2′). To make the changes perspicuous, we have highlighted in bold the re-duced specification of nasals P2 and the additional condition that P2 be homorganic with P3. (2′)  english([P1,P2,P3,P4]):-  phone(P1),low(P1),not(bck(P1)),  phone(P2),nas(P2),  phone(P3),lab(P3),not(cnt(P3)),not(voi(P3)),  phone(P4),snt(P4),not(nas(P4)),alv(P4), not(pal(P4)),  homorganic(P2,P3). english([P1,P2,P3,P4]):-  phone(P1),low(P1),not(bck(P1)),  phone(P2),nas(P2),  phone(P3),not(snt(P2)),lab(P3),not(cnt(P3)),voi(P3),  phone(P4),snt(P4),not(nas(P4)),alv(P4),not(pal(P4)),  homorganic(P2,P3). english([P1,P2,P3,P4]):-  phone(P1),low(P1),not(bck(P1)),  phone(P2),nas(P2),  phone(P3),alv(P3),not(cnt(P3)),not(voi(P3)),  phone(P4),snt(P4),not(nas(P4)),alv(P4),not(pal(P4)), 164 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS  homorganic(P2,P3). english([P1,P2,P3,P4]):-  phone(P1),low(P1),not(bck(P1)),  phone(P2),nas(P2),  phone(P3),not(snt(P3)),voi(P3),not(cnt(P3)),alv(P3),  phone(P4),snt(P4),not(nas(P4)),alv(P4),not(pal(P4)),  homorganic(P2,P3). english([P1,P2,P3,P4]):-  phone(P1),low(P1),not(bck(P1)),  phone(P2),nas(P2),  phone(P3),not(voi(P3)),vel(P3),  phone(P4),snt(P4),not(nas(P4)),alv(P4),not(pal(P4)),  homorganic(P2,P3). english([P1,P2,P3,P4]):-  phone(P1),low(P1),not(bck(P1)),  phone(P2),nas(P2),  phone(P3),not(snt(P3)),voi(P3),vel(P3),  phone(P4),snt(P4),not(nas(P4)),alv(P4),not(pal(P4)),  homorganic(P2,P3). This would require, of course, that we have a definition of homorganic. For the fragment at hand, the following definition will suffice.3 (3) homorganic(A,B):-   lab(A) <=> lab(B),   vel(A) <=> vel(B). Here ‘<=>’ is an infix operator that is intended to stand for mutual entail-ment. So (3) asserts that two items A and B are homorganic if they agree both in labiality and in velarity. Unfortunately, ‘<=>’ is not built-in in PROLOG. So we have to do two things. First, establish it as an infix opera-tor; second, define it as mutual entailment. This is what we did in en-tailment.swipl (see Program 29 in Appendix H).4 Although the approach embodied in (2’) and (3) is an improvement over the approach in (2), the new approach is still not optimal. Notice that the homorganicity condition had to be repeated in each of the statements in (2′).                                                             3 See next section for a more accurate definition of homorganic. 4 Notice that <=> must be established as an infix operator before it is used in a program. Oth-erwise, the PROLOG interpreter will throw an error message (even if it will run correctly). This is why we establish <=> at the top of entailment.swipl and then call this pro-gram before defining homorganic. MORPHOLOGY II: THE INSTANTIATION OF WORDS /  165 This suggests that we are still missing a generalization. And we are indeed. Nasals are homorganic with all ensuing consonants, not just with the [p] in [æ,m,p,l], the [b] in [æ,m,b,l]—and so on. Having a separate homorganicity requirement in each of the rules in (2′) makes it look like a coincidence that homorganicity holds in all of those cases. One way to solve this problem is to generate the forms in (1) in two steps rather than one. First we generate underlying English forms (hence-forth underenglish forms) whose nasals may or may not be homorganic with ensuing nasals; then we require, once and for all, that only forms that are homorganic with ensuing nasals qualify as actual English (or eng-lish) sounds. The first of these steps is taken in (2″); the second in (4).5 (2″)  underenglish([P1,P2,P3,P4]):-  phone(P1),low(P1),not(bck(P1)),  phone(P2),nas(P2),  phone(P3),lab(P3),not(cnt(P3)),not(voi(P3)),  phone(P4),snt(P4),not(nas(P4)),alv(P4), not(pal(P4)). underenglish([P1,P2,P3,P4]):-  phone(P1),low(P1),not(bck(P1)),  phone(P2),nas(P2),  phone(P3),not(snt(P3)),lab(P3),not(cnt(P3)),voi(P3),  phone(P4),snt(P4),not(nas(P4)),alv(P4),not(pal(P4)). underenglish([P1,P2,P3,P4]):-  phone(P1),low(P1),not(bck(P1)),  phone(P2),nas(P2),  phone(P3),alv(P3),not(cnt(P3)),not(voi(P3)),  phone(P4),snt(P4),not(nas(P4)),alv(P4),not(pal(P4)). underenglish([P1,P2,P3,P4]):-  phone(P1),low(P1),not(bck(P1)),  phone(P2),nas(P2),  phone(P3),not(snt(P3)),voi(P3),not(cnt(P3)),alv(P3),  phone(P4),snt(P4),not(nas(P4)),alv(P4),not(pal(P4)). underenglish([P1,P2,P3,P4]):-  phone(P1),low(P1),not(bck(P1)),  phone(P2),nas(P2),  phone(P3),not(voi(P3)),vel(P3),  phone(P4),snt(P4),not(nas(P4)),alv(P4),not(pal(P4)).                                                             5  We will add below further conditions to underenglish forms.  166 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS underenglish([P1,P2,P3,P4]):-  phone(P1),low(P1),not(bck(P1)),  phone(P2),nas(P2),  phone(P3),not(snt(P3)),voi(P3),vel(P3),  phone(P4),snt(P4),not(nas(P4)),alv(P4),not(pal(P4)). (4) english(A):-   underenglish(A),   (nas(N),cns(C),nextto(N,C,A)) => homorganic(N,C). To be noted in (4) is the appeal to a new predicate. It is the built-in predi-cate nextto. The meaning of this three-place predicate can be described by saying that   Rules (2"), (3), (4) have been gathered in instantiation.swipl (see Program 30 in Appendix H). The reader is hereby invited to make sure that this account yields all and only the acceptable forms in (1) by consulting instantiation.swipl and entering english(SO). But the approach we have delineated is not just accurate; it is principled as well, as it states, once and for all in (4), that nasals are homorganic to ensu-ing consonants—something that neither (2) nor (2!) could do. And it does this without duplicating any of the efforts displayed by other rules of the grammar. It is principledness such as this that justifies approaching eng-lish indirectly through underenglish.  It should be clear that this approach would not have been possible with-out a process whereby a partially specified expression becomes fully speci-fied. This process is called instantiation. Instantiation is motivated by a number of processes. By far, the most important of these processes is assim-ilation. We will therefore devote the lion’s share of this chapter to it. But instantiation arises also as a consequence of dissimilation (which is the op-posite of assimilation). And as a consequence of syllabification as well. It is to all these processes that we now turn. Remember  nextto(A,B,C) is true iff A and B are two members of a list C such that A comes immediately before B. MORPHOLOGY II: THE INSTANTIATION OF WORDS /  167 2 Assimilation: First Pass Assimilation is the process whereby two or more phones become similar— or more similar, since similarity is a matter of degree. Taken to the extreme, assimilation becomes identification—the process whereby two or more phones become identical. Identification can be seen in the informal pronun-ciation of want to as wannu or of Antarctica as Annarctica, where a [t] be-comes identical to a preceding [n]. But assimilation is seldom taken to such an extreme. More often than not, assimilation affects only the point of ar-ticulation of two or more phones. Or just their mode of articulation. Or their voicing. Or their resonance.  The homorganicity of nasals is a good example of assimilation in point of articulation. But our discussion of this process has been rather limited. For English has quite a few nasals other than [m, n, ŋ]. And they are all ho-morganic with ensuing consonants. This fact is nicely illustrated by the dis-tribution of the different forms of the English negative prefix un: (5) The Distribution of un-  • [ʌ, m] as in unpaid, where [m] is a nasal that is bilabial like the ensuing [p].  • [ʌ, ɱ] as in unfair, where [ɱ] is a nasal that is labiodental like the ensuing [f].  • [ʌ, ņ] as in unthinkable, where [ņ] is a nasal that is interdental like the ensuing [θ].   • [ʌ, n] as in untypical, where [n] is a nasal that is alveolar like the ensuing [t].  • [ʌ, n]̺ as in unrelated, where [n]̺ is a nasal that is palato-alveolar like the ensu-ing [ɹ].   • [ʌ, ɲ] as in unchanged, where [ɲ] is a nasal that is palatal like the ensuing [č].  • [ʌ, ŋ] as in unclear, where [ŋ] is a nasal that is velar like the ensuing [k]. To account for the assimilations displayed by these forms we need to do several things. First, we need to incorporate the four new nasals [ɱ, ņ, n,̺ ɲ] into our inventory of phones. We do this in fullphone.swipl.6 Then we need to describe these new phones in terms of their fundamental proper-ties. This we do in fullproperties.swipl.7 Third, we need a nar-rower definition of homorganicity—one that can distinguish, on the one hand, between the points of articulation of the labials and, on the other hand, between the points of articulation of the medials (i.e. the phones which are neither labial nor velar). This can be done as follows.                                                             6 The unicode decimal values of [ɱ, ņ, n,̺ ɲ] are [625], [110, 825], [110, 826], and [626], re-spectively.  7 We added dental (abbreviated as dnt) as a new fundamental property in order to distin-guish the point of articulation of labials and labiodentals (labiodentals and interdentals are the only dentals in fullphone.swipl). 168 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS  (3′) homorganic(A,B):-   lab(A) <=> lab(B), dnt(A) <=> dnt(B),    alv(A) <=> alv(B), pal(A) <=> pal(B),    vel(A) <=> vel(B). Armed with all of the above, we may formulate the following grammar for Underenglish: (6) underenglish([p,e,j,d],[adjective]).  underenglish([f,e,ɹ],[adjective]).  underenglish([θ,ɪ,N,k,æ,b,l],[adjective]):- nas(N).  underenglish([t,ɪ,p,ɪ,k,l],[adjective]).  underenglish([ɹ,i,l,e,j,t,ɪ,d],[adjective]).  underenglish([č,e,ɪ,N,ʒ,d],[adjective]):- nas(N).  underenglish([k,l,i,ɹ],[adjective]).  underenglish([P1,P2],[affix,negative]):-   phone(P1),ctr(P1),str(P1),   phone(P2),nas(P2).  underenglish(A,[adjective,negative]):-   underenglish(B,[affix,negative]),   underenglish(C,[adjective]),   append(B,C,A). Critically, the nasal phone of the negative affix is underspecified for point of articulation; it is described simply as a nasal phone (see boldfaced line above). Thus, left to its own devices, this grammar will generate every form of the negative affix. This is good. Unfortunately, it will also append every form of the negative affix before every adjective. The reader should check that this is the case by consulting homorganic.swipl and entering  underenglish(SO,[adjective, negative]).  In order to enforce the desired assimilation we need a rule similar to the one in the previous section: (7) english(A,B):-   underenglish(A,B),   (nas(N),cns(C),nextto(N,C,A)) => homorganic(N,C). A rule like (4) or like (7), which takes an underlying form of a language (an underlanguage form) into an actual form of a language (a language form) can be called an instantiator clause. The procedure they trigger may be referred to as the instantiation procedure.  MORPHOLOGY II: THE INSTANTIATION OF WORDS /  169 To ascertain the effects of (7) on underenglish, the reader should enter  english(SO,[adjective, negative]). It should not escape the reader that the negative adjectives unthinkable and unchanged have two nasal assimilations each—one in the prefix and the other in the stem. Happily, the grammar in (6) can induce all four of these assimilations. It should also be noticed that the grammar in (6) acknowl-edges but one negative prefix uN, and regards the seven forms in (5) as in-stantiations of this prefix (and as positional variants of each other). This seems to be as desired. Naive native intuition recognizes but one affix here. In fact, it takes a good measure of introspection on the part of a native speaker of English in order to convince him or her that (s)he is pronouncing the affix differently in each case. The homorganicity of nasals involves complete assimilation with re-gards to point of articulation. But assimilations with regards to this parame-ter may also be partial. German has two fricative phones which are articu-lated in the back of the mouth but above the glottis. They are a palatal [ç] and a velar [x]. As it turns out, these two phones occur in complementary distribution—one may occur only where the other may not. Thus, while the velar [x] may only occur after velar vowels, the palatal [ç] may only occur elsewhere (Wiese 1996, §7.3.3).8 So, while [x] may be found after the velar vowels of acht ‘eight’, mochten ‘might’, and buch ‘book’, [ç] may be found after the palatal vowel of ich ‘I’, after the consonant of furchten ‘to fear’, or word-initially—which is to say after nothing at all (cf. the initial phone of chemisch ‘chemical’). One way to account for the complementary distribution of [ç] and [x] is to leave the fricatives in question unspecified for velarity in underger-man, while requiring them to be velar in german if and only if they come after a velar vowel. This is what we do in homovelar.swipl (see Ap-pendix H, Program 34). The key points of the program are included below. The first two lines leave the fricatives in question underspecified for velari-ty;9 the next four lines ensure that we have a velar consonant just in case it follows a velar vowel.                                                             8 We ignore here the evidence that the uvular [χ] occurs after low velar vowels. 9 To enhance readability, we pretend here that [x] and [ç] are the only two consonants of un-dergerman. And that [x] and [a] are the only velars therein. Needless to say, a more accurate account will have to incorporate more consonants and more velars. It will also require that [x, ç] be a natural class—which it is; it is the class of voiceless fricatives which are neither anterior nor glottal). 170 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS  Part of Program 34. homovelar.swipl As can be readily seen, the assimilation enacted by this procedure af-fects only velarity; not any of the other properties describing point of articu-lation—labiality, dentality, alveolarity, glottality, and so on. Their assimila-tion with regards to point of articulation is thus only partial.  Another thing to notice in homovelar.swipl is that the point of ar-ticulation of a particular sound (a vowel [i] or a vowel [a]) influences the point of articulation of an ensuing sound (an underspecified consonant P). Thus the assimilation in homovelar.swipl is progressive in the sense that the influence goes with the speech flow (or from left to right if we adopt the directionality of the English writing system):                  Figure 1. Progressive assimilation This contrasts with the assimilation in homorganic.swipl (see (7) above), where the point of articulation of a consonant affects the point of articulation of a preceding nasal. Consequently, the assimilation induced by (7) is regressive in the sense that the influence goes against the speech flow (or from right to left if we adopt the directionality the English writing sys-tem):  undergerman([i, P]):- cns(P). undergerman([a, P]):- cns(P).  german(A):-  undergerman(A),  (cns(C), member(C,A)) =>   (vel(C) <=> (vel(V), not(cns(V)), nextto(V,C,A))).  INFLUENCE VOWEL FRICATIVE SPEECH FLOW MORPHOLOGY II: THE INSTANTIATION OF WORDS /  171                       Figure 2. Regressive assimilation Regressive assimilation is said to be anticipatory, as some of the properties of a sound are anticipated in a preceding one. Progressive assimilation is also called perseverative, as some of the properties of a sound persevere in a subsequent one. As it turns out, regressive assimilation is far more com-mon than its progressive counterpart (across the languages of the world). For a final example of assimilation in point of articulation, we will turn to Finnish (Uralic, Finland). Finnish has the following system of vowels:  FRONT CENTRAL BACK HIGH y i u MID ø e o LOW æ  a But these vowels do not occur freely in a Finnish word. Thus, if p is a pair of vowels in a word of this language, then p is one of the pairs in (8); not one of the pairs in (9). (8)  yy, yø, yæ, yi, ye, øy, øø, øæ, øi, øe, æy, æø, æææ, æi, æe, iy, iø, iæ, ii, ie, iu, io, ia, ey, eø, eæ, ei, ee, eu, eo, ea, ui, ue, uu, uo, ua, oi, oe, ou, oo, oa, ai, ae, au, ao, aa. (9)  yu, yo, ya, øu, øo, øa, æu, æo, æa, uy, uø, uæ, oy, oø, oæ, ay, aø, aæ. Notice that the pairs in (9) are exactly the ones that mix a front vowel and a back vowel, while the pairs in (8) are all the rest. This requirement to segregate front vowels from back vowels in Finnish words is known as Finnish Vowel Harmony (Karlsson 1999, §11). Vowel harmony is not unique to Finnish. It occurs throughout Uralo-Altaic, a family of languages that includes Hungarian, Turkish, and Mongolian (as well as Finnish). Out-side Uralo-Altaic, it can be found in the Akan, Bantu, and Nilotic families of languages—to name a few. To account for Finnish Vowel Harmony, we will appeal to a property per (short for peripheral). per is a positive property noncentral vow-els have but central vowels lack. Armed with this property, we may de-scribe the vowels of Finnish as follows: INFLUENCE NASAL CONSONANT SPEECH FLOW 172 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS vow(P) :- name(P,[121]). (y is a vowel) vow(P) :- name(P,[248]). (ø is a vowel) vow(P) :- name(P,[230]). (æ is a vowel) vow(P) :- name(P,[105]). (i is a vowel) vow(P) :- name(P,[101]). (e is a vowel) vow(P) :- name(P,[117]). (u is a vowel) vow(P) :- name(P,[111]). (o is a vowel) vow(P) :- name(P,[097]). (a is a vowel) per(P) :- name(P,[121]). (y is peripheral) per(P) :- name(P,[248]). (ø is peripheral) per(P) :- name(P,[230]). (æ is peripheral) per(P) :- name(P,[117]). (u is peripheral) per(P) :- name(P,[111]). (o is peripheral) per(P) :- name(P,[097]). (a is peripheral) bck(P) :- name(P,[117]). ( u is back) bck(P) :- name(P,[111]). ( o is back) bck(P) :- name(P,[097]). ( a is back) hih(P) :- name(P,[121]). ( y is high) hih(P) :- name(P,[105]). First we allow some of the vowels of underfinnish words to be underspecified for both frontness and backness. Then we require all the vowels of finnish words to be fully specified—but so as to agree (or harmonize) with respect to backness. The latter can be done with the following rule: (10) finnish(A):-  underfinnish(A),  (per(B), per(C), member(B,A), member(C,A)) =>   (bck(B) <=> bck(C)).         (Part of Program 35. harmony.swipl) As this rule would have it, any two peripheral vowels B and C of a word A of underfinnish must agree for backness before A can be finnish. Needless to say, this rule assumes the PROLOG interpreter has already con-sulted entailment.swipl (for the complete program, see Program 35, Appendix H). To check the accuracy of this account, let us assume that [c,V1,c,V2] is a word of underfinnish, where c is a particular MORPHOLOGY II: THE INSTANTIATION OF WORDS /  173 consonant and V1 and V2 are vowels. We can do this by consulting har-mony.swipl and entering the following at the prompt: assert(underfinnish([c,V1,c,V2]):-(vow(V1), vow(V2))). Then we enter the following query:  finnish(Word). As the series of responses to this query will show, the vowels V1 and V2 of this “word” of finnish will come, all and only, from the list in (8); not from the list in (9). So the two vowels will harmonize with respect to back-ness. It will be noticed that this assimilation occurs “at a distance”, as the two vowels are not contiguous. And it will affect any two vowels of a word (and hence all the vowels of a word). With regards to actual words of Finnish, some of their vowels will be underspecified, as we said, for backness. Thus, the sounds of talossa ‘in the house’ and kylässä ‘in the village’, could be characterized as follows: underfinnish([t,a,l,V1,s,s,V2]):-   per(V1), not(hih(V1)), not(low(V1)),  per(V2), low(V2). underfinnish([k,y,l,V1,s,s,V2]):-   per(V1), low(V1),  per(V2), low(V2). Notice that V1 and V2 are unspecified here for both frontness and backness. Yet, the rule in (10) will make sure that these vowels will be fully instanti-ated before the words that contain them can be considered finnish. And instantiated in a way that will satisfy the language’s vowel harmony. In short, (10) will ensure that the words that correspond to the underfinn-ish words above will be talossa and kylässä. This is because the former contains the back vowel [a] while the latter contains the nonback vowel [y]. It should not escape the reader that the words talossa and kylässä are similar in both sound and meaning (both end in -ssa and both refer to enti-ties which are located ‘in’ other entities). Accounting for these similarities will be the point of one of the Exercises to this chapter. 3 Assimilation: Second Pass All of the examples of assimilation discussed in the preceding section in-volved point of articulation. But assimilation affects other acoustic parame-ters as well—including voicing, resonance, and perhaps manner of articula-tion.10 Take a look at the following words and nonwords of English:                                                             10 With regards to assimilations in manner of articulation, clear examples are hard to find, are restricted to fast or casual speech, and tend weaken an articulation (Roach 2001, 55). 174 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS [k,a,p]  [k,a,p,s] *[k,a,p,z] (cop/cops) [k,a,b] *[k,a,b,s]  [k,a,b,z] (cob/cobs) [k,a,t]  [k,a,t,s] *[k,a,t,z] (cot/cots) [k,a,d] *[k,a,d,s]  [k,a,d,z] (cod/cods) [k,a,k]  [k,a,k,s] *[k,a,k,z] (cock/cocks) [k,a,g] *[k,a,g,s]  [k,a,g,z] (cog/cogs) What this data suggests is this. This is, of course, a matter of presentation. But an important one. 4 Dissimilation Dissimilation is the process whereby two or more phones become dissimi-lar—or less similar, since similarity is a matter of degree. A classic example of dissimilation comes from Latin, which uses a suffix -alis to form adjec-tives out of nouns:  spiritu-alis 'spiritual'  anecdot-alis 'anecdotal'  doctor-alis 'doctoral'                                                             11 Notice that we had to change C to C1 in the last condition in order to prevent a clash of variables 178 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS Interestingly, this suffix -alis does not combine with stems ending in [l]. In such cases, Latin has to use the suffix -aris instead:12  angul-aris  'angular'  perpendicul-aris   'perpendicular'  pol-aris   'polar' So the adjectival derivative of mor 'custom' would be moralis 'customary' rather than *moraris, and the adjective corresponding to mol 'wisdom tooth' would be molaris 'molar' rather than *molalis. A simple way to account for this data is to say that, if you are going to appeal to this affix, then you must have either a lateral in the stem or a lat-eral in the suffix, but you cannot have both a lateral in the stem and a lateral in the affix. In other words, the calls for these laterals are exclusively dis-joined.  Notice that exclusive disjunction is the relation that holds between two propositions that have different truth values; logical equivalence, on the other hand, is the relation that holds between two propositions that have the same truth value. If the only possible truth values are truth and falsity, then the logical equivalence of two propositions requires both to be true or both to be false (it doesn't matter which), while their exclusive disjunction re-quires one of them to be true (it doesn't matter which) and the other false. In short, exclusive disjunction is the negation of logical equivalence.  The fact that exclusive disjunction is the negation of logical equivalence goes a long way towards accounting for the facts in question in terms of PROLOG. For, suppose we described [l] and [r] as liquids and [l] as lateral: liq(l). liq(r). lat(l). Suppose, furthermore, that we had two nouns mor 'custom' and mol 'wisdom tooth', and one underspecified affix [a,L,i,s] in which L was a liquid: latin([m,o,r],[noun]). latin([m,o,l],[noun]).  latin([a,L,i,s],[affix]):-  liq(L). We could then derive the adjectives moralis 'moral' and molaris 'molar' (and only them) with the following rule (see Appendix H, Program 38):                                                             12 See Kent (1936, 250). MORPHOLOGY II: THE INSTANTIATION OF WORDS /  179 latin(A,[adjective]):-  latin(B,[noun]),  latin([a,L,i,s],[affix]),  last(B,E),  not(lat(E) <=> lat(L)),  append(B,[a,L,i,s],A). As can be readily seen, the next-to-last condition of this rule prevents the underspecified phone L of the affix from being lateral when the last phone E of the noun is. And it also prevents L from being nonlateral when E is. In other words, it requires one and only one of these two phones to be lateral, thus placing these calls for laterality in exclusive disjunction. In so doing, it accounts for the dissimilation between the Latin suffix [a,L,i,s]and the stems it attach to.13 This example of dissimilation affects manner of articulation. Examples can be found that affect other parameters of articulation. Consider for ex-ample Caribbean Spanish, which forms the diminutive form of a nominal stem N by appending the suffix [i,T] directly to it, where T is an under-specified phone. Interestingly, T is [t] unless the last phone of N is [t] as well—in which case T is realized, in dissimilatory fashion, as [k].14 Thus, the diminutive of [b,o,k] 'mouth' is [b,o,k,i,t]'little mouth', but the diminutive of [b,o,t] 'boot' is [b,o,t,i,k]. And the diminutive of [b,o,l] 'ball' is [b,o,l,i,t].15 Interestingly, this suf-fix [i,T]may recur in dissimilatory fashion, so that we can get [b,o,k] [b,o,k,i,t] [b,o,k,i,t,i,k] . . . . . . . . . . To account for these facts we will introduce three nominal stems into Span-ish: spanish([b,o,k],[stem]). spanish([b,o,t],[stem]). spanish([b,o,l],[stem]).                                                             13 If Latin required that one and only one of the two consonants be [r] instead of [l], then the next-to-last condition for the rule in question would have to be replaced by not(vib(E) <=> vib(L)). Evidence that the facts are as described in the text come from the fact that we say spiritu-alis rather than *spiritu-aris. 14  I am grateful to the late James McCawley for bringing this example to my attention (McCawley discovered this phenomenon watching Spanish language telenovelas in Chicago). 15  A gender-marking [a] must be suffixed to all of these stems in order to become actual nouns. See below. 180 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS We will also introduce the underspecified diminutive affix [P1,P2], where P1 is [i] and P2 is either [t] or [k]: spanish([P1,P2],[diminutive]):-  phone(P1), hih(P1), tns(P1), not(bck(P1)),  phone(P2), not(voi(P2)), not(cnt(P2)),  not(lab(P2)), not(pal(P2)). Finally, we allow a stem to append to the diminutive affix provided either the stem or the affix, but not both, end in [t]: spanish(A,[stem]):-  spanish(B,[stem]),  spanish(C,[diminutive]),  last(B,D),last(C,E),  not((alv(D), not(cnt(D)), not(voi(D))) <=>  (alv(E), not(cnt(E)), not(voi(E)))),  append(B,C,A). The critical either/or condition has been boldfaced above (bear in mind that [t] is a voiceless alveolar noncontinuant).  None of the stems generated by the previous statements is an actual noun. To get nouns, a feminine marker [a] must append to the stems gener-ated by these statements (conveniently, all the nouns we picked as our ex-amples were feminine). This can be achieved straightforwardly by means of the following rule: spanish(A,[noun]):-  spanish(B,[stem]),  append(B,[a],A). The full program is listed as Program 39 in Appendix H. Our Latin example showed dissimilation in mode of articulation. Our Spanish example illustrated dissimilation in place of articulation. We will see now an example of dissimilation in a third parameter of articulation: voicing. Certain Western dialects of Bade (Chadic, Nigeria) exhibit a second-person past-tense prefix that instantiates as [kə] before a voiced obstruent and as [gə] elsewhere (see Schuh 2002, §3.2): gə-kwtu 'you took' kə-bdu  'you asked gə-kəru 'you stole' kə-vəru  'you went out' gə-lagu 'you stopped' kə-dəkwu 'you heard' gə-naju 'you ground' kə-gafo  'you caught' MORPHOLOGY II: THE INSTANTIATION OF WORDS /  181 To account for these facts, we begin by proposing an underspecified prefix [G, əә], where G is either [g] or [k]: bade([P1,P2],[affix]):-  vel(P1),not(snt(P1)),  phone(P2),not(cns(P2)),not(str(P2)). Then we admit a rule that appends this affix (and possibly others) to a stem, but taking care to ensure that either the first consonant of the prefix or the first consonant of the verb—either one, but not both—is a voiced obstruent: bade(A,[verb]):-  bade([B1|B2],[affix]),  bade([C1|C2],[stem]),  not((voi(B1),not(snt(B1))) <=>  (voi(C1),not(snt(C1)))),  append([B1|B2],[C1|C2],A). At this point, all that remains to do is describe the properties of the key phones, list the verb stems, and tell the PROLOG interpreter that the double arrow is the mutual entailment infix. All of this is done in Program 40, Ap-pendix H. It is worth noting that this dissimilation does not affect a single specification, but rather a bundle of specifications. In this respect, Bade dissimilation resembles homorganicity, which affects the set of specifica-tions that pertain to point of articulation.  We close this section by pointing out that assimilation is a process that simplifies articulation by allowing the speaker to coarticulate—to make one articulation last for more more than one phone. This reduces the articulatory effort the speaker has to invest in order to assign sound to meaning. But what could dissimilation be for? As it turns out, dissimilation exists for the opposite reason—to reduce the effort that the listener has to expend in order to assign a meaning to a sound. For, notice that it is in the listeners' best interest to work with a sound that is as clear, as distinctive, and as redun-dant as possible. Especially when these sounds are contained in meaningful, nonredundant morphs (as in all the suffixes discussed in this section), and most especially when they recur (as the Spanish diminutive can). In these cases, coarticulation is the last thing the listener wants to have. What (s)he needs is distinctness.16                                                             16 We owe to Andre Martinet (1908-1999) the idea that the state which a language finds itself in, at any point in time, is the result of the tension between the wishes of the speaker and the wishes of the listener—much like the price of a good is the result of the tension between the wishes of the buyer and the wishes of the seller (Martinet 1955). 182 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS 5 Syllabification All of the arguments for underspecification that we have presented thus far are based on underspecified phones. But arguments for underspecification can also be made from underspecified syllabic structure, as we shall now see.  One of the problems that remained open in Chapter 2 was that languages seemed to have sounds which, contrary to our definition, did not consist of a discrete number of syllables. The example mentioned there was the sound of the morph intr that we see in intravenous, intrinsic, and introverted. As pointed out in Chapter 2, the sound of this morph seems to consist of more than one syllable but less than two.  One apparent solution to this problem would be to assume that the sound of the morph in question is not the impossibly sesquisyllabic sound [ɪ, n, t, ɹ], but rather the apparently disyllabic sound [[ɪ, n], [t, ɹ, X]], where ‘X' is an underspecified vowel that may instantiate as [a], [ɪ], [o].  This proposal has a number of problems. First, we should acknowledge that [t, ɹ, X] is not quite a syllable. Syllables are sequences of fully speci-fied phones, and ‘X' is not one of them. So this proposal still creates prob-lems with our definition of sound as a sequence of syllables. Second, even if [t, ɹ, X] were a syllable, it would have to be one that remains such in intra-venous and introverted, but not in intrinsic. For this word syllabifies as [[ɪ, n], [t, ɹ, ɪ, n], [s, ɪ, k]], and thus fails to contain [t, ɹ, X] as a syllable. Or [t, ɹ, ɪ], for that matter. A more acute problem for our definition of sound as a sequence of sylla-bles comes from interfixation—the morphological process described in Chapter 4. Here we had morphs with sounds like [ch, ch], [k, k], [k, t, b], [q, t, l], and so on. None of these sounds can be remotely analyzed as se-quences of syllables (let alone sequences of syllables of Quechua or Arabic). One way to solve all of these problems is to distinguish between the signs (or expressions) of a language and the signs (or expressions) of an underlanguage. For then we may say that the signs of an underlanguage may be expressed as sequences of phones, while the sounds of the signs of a language must involve sequences of syllables. Thus, [i, n, t, ɹ] is a possible sound of underenglish but not of english—just like [ch, ch] and [k, t, b] are possible sounds of underquechua and underarabic even if they are not sounds of quechua or arabic. Needless to say, this solution presupposes an instantiation process—a rule that turns the sequences of phones of the underlanguage into sequences of syllables of the correspond-ing language. In short, we need a process of syllabification. For English, MORPHOLOGY II: THE INSTANTIATION OF WORDS /  183 :- ['syllable.swipl'].  syllabifies([], []). syllabifies(A, B):-  A = [A1 | A2],  syllable(A1),  append(A1, B2, B),  syllabifies(A2, B2). this process may look as follows. At least as a first approximation.17                                Part of tryout.swipl  This procedure defines a relation syllabifies that holds between a list of syllables and a list of phones, where the latter is the concatenation of the former. The relation is recursive, as it is allowed to check that smaller and smaller portions of the list of phones are concatenations of smaller and smaller portions of the list of syllables. If all goes well, PROLOG hits the empty portion of the set of phones—which is, by the first clause of the pro-gram, the concatenation of  the empty list of syllables. All we need to do now is require that a list of phones of undereng-lish be syllabified according to this program before it can be considered english. This can be done with the following instantiation clause.          Part of tryout.swipl Now, if (11a) and (12a) are strings of underenglish, (11b) and (12b) will be their counterparts in english. (11) a.  [*, n, t, %, *, n, s, *, k]  b.  [[*, n], [t, %, *, n], [s, *, k]] (12) a.  [*, n, t, %, ), v, i, n, ), s]  b.  [[*, n], [t, %, )], [v, i], [n, ), s]]                                                             17 I am indebted to Oleg Lokhvitsky for most of this procedure. english(E):-  underenglish(U),  syllabifies(E,U).  184 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS This can be checked by consulting tryout.swipl and entering first un-derenglish(U) and then english(E).  Unfortunately, asking for alternative solutions will assign syllabifica-tions to intravenous over and above (12b). This can be checked by continu-ing entering semicolons. Thus, after giving us (12b), PROLOG will respond with S = [[$, n], [t, ", *], [v, i, n], [*, s]] ; S = [[$, n], [t, ", *, v], [i], [n, *, s]] ; S = [[$, n], [t, ", *, v], [i, n], [*, s]] ; false.  The problem is that none of these last three responses is quite the proper syllabification of intravenous. So we would like PROLOG to refrain from offering them as answers to our query. Is there a way to do this? There might be. Notice that what we want to do is make PROLOG give its first answer and only its first answer. This we can make PROLOG do. It in-volves adding what is called a "cut" in the definition of syllabifies. A cut is a goal that can always be included among the conditions of a rule, as it is a condition that is always true (it is a goal that always "succeeds"). But a cut is far from a trivial addition to a rule. For, if PROLOG hits a cut in the execution of a rule R (in an attempt to verify whether a goal G is true), then PROLOG commits to the values it has assigned to the variables of R up to that point (up to and including the choice of R to evaluate G). In other words, cuts make PROLOG refrain from backtracking and consid-ering alternative values for these variables. But this is precisely what we need here. So let's add a cut ‘!’ as the last condition to the second syllabifi-cation rule of tryout.swipl:  Remember Let R be a rule invoked to test a goal G. A cut on R is a condition ‘!’ on R which satisfies two properties. The first is that ‘!’ is a goal which always succeeds. The second is that once PROLOG hits ‘!’ in the execution of R, it commits to all the choices it made, up to that point, in the execution of R. Included among the-se choices is the choice of R to evaluate G. MORPHOLOGY II: THE INSTANTIATION OF WORDS /  185  Part of Program 41. syllabification.swipl Replacing this new procedure in tryout.swipl leads to a better attempt at English syllabification. We have therefore called it syllabifica-tion.swipl.  So let's consult this new program and let's repeat the earlier queries. En-tering underenglish(U) will yield no change. Entering english(E), on the other hand, will. For when PROLOG tries to find a syllabification for intravenous, it will be appealing to the revised definition of syllabification. And, as soon as it assigns successful values to A and B in the second state-ment above, it will hit the cut (!). Since this goal is always satisfied, all the conditions of the rule will have been satisfied at this point. Consequently, PROLOG will report these values for A and B, refrain from considering others, and halt as desired.18 It might be thought that we were lucky that the first partition of intrave-nous into syllables was also the correct syllabification of this word. For, if any of the other partitions of intravenous into syllables came up first, we would be stuck with an incorrect syllabification for this word.  As a matter of fact, luck played no role here. Back in Chapter 2, we wrote syllable.swipl with outcomes like these in mind. For, notice that the correct syllabification of intravenous is also the one that minimizes the number of empty onsets and nonempty codas. But these onsets and co-das are also the rarest in the languages of the world. That's why sylla-ble.swipl admitted nonempty onsets before empty ones (and empty codas before nonempty ones). Although the original justification for doing so was to generate the natural syllables before the unnatural ones, we now                                                             18 Given the way the cut operates, we still have a problem, as the following exchange shows. ?- syllabifies([[$, n], [t, ", *], [v, i, n], [*, s]], X). X = [$, n, t, ", *, v, i, n, *, s]. The problem here is that PROLOG responded to the query directly, without first checking for the intended solutions. :- ['syllable.swipl'].  syllabifies([], []). syllabifies(A, B):-  A = [A1 | A2],  syllable(A1),   append(A1, B2, B),  syllabifies(A2, B2),  !. 186 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS see that this ordering decision (if supplemented with a syllabification rule that incorporates a cut), furthermore excludes the ungrammatical syllabifi-cations of intravenous. But then we can rely on our original motivation to argue that the exclusion of the ungrammatical syllabifications of intrave-nous was carried out in a principled way. For now the reason why (12b) is the syllabification of (12a) is that the former is the most natural partition into syllables of the latter. Be that as it may, it should be clear that the proposal we have advanced avoids the problems raised by the syllabification of intr. In short, the pro-posal is to leave this morph unsyllabified and then rely on the instantiation process detailed above to syllabify it—correctly and once and for all. Notice that this proposal saves our definition of linguistic sound as a sequence of syllables (but dooms it as a definition of underlinguistic sound). Key to these proposals was what we may call the underspecification of syllabic structure.  But the proposal also makes some strong predictions. It predicts, for ex-ample, that the syllabic integrity of a stem will be violated any time the stem is attached to an affix that is not a sequence of one or more syllables. In fact, we would predict that these violations would occur even when the affix fails to be an "optimal" sequence of syllables. Interestingly, these pre-dictions are borne out by the morphology of the English plural. For, notice that the suffixes [s] and [z] of the English plural are neither syllables nor sequences of syllables. Thus, as we would predict, they lead to a violation of the integrity of the syllabifications of the stems they attach to. This can be seen, for example, in the stems cat and dog, which do not survive as syl-lables when pluralized (neither cat is a syllable of cats nor dog of dogs).  But the English plural has a third suffix [ɪz]. Although [ɪz] is a syllable of English, it is not an optimal one. And this with a vengeance, as it con-tains both an empty onset and a nonempty coda. Consequently, we predict that it will violate the syllabifications of the stems it affixes to in order to meet the optimal syllabification of the affixed form. And this, exactly, is what happens; juice is not a syllable in juices, nor rose in roses. Rather, and as predicted by syllabifies.swipl, what we get is: [[ǰ, u], [s, ɪ, z]]  [[ɹ, o, w], [s, ɪ, z]] The reader should check this out by entering: syllabifies(X, [ǰ, u, s, ɪ, z]). syllabifies(X, [ɹ, o, w, s, ɪ, z]).  MORPHOLOGY II: THE INSTANTIATION OF WORDS /  187 Moreover, horse is not a syllable in horses, nor fox in foxes. As predicted by syllabifies.swipl, what we get instead is: [[h, o, ɹ], [s, ɪ, z]] [[f, o, k], [s, ɪ, z]] Here the queries to use are: syllabifies(X, [h, o, ɹ, s, ɪ, z]). syllabifies(X, [f, o, k, s, ɪ, z]). True, neither of the syllables above is optimal, as they all contain a nonempty coda. But dropping the [z] is not an option if we are to generate a plural. And [z] cannot constitute a syllable onto itself. What is an option is for [ɪ, z] to attract the final [s] of these stems. And this is an option that syllabifies.swipl correctly chooses, as it eschews nonoptimal consonant clusters in codas. 6 Conclusion The purpose of this chapter has been to argue that instantiation is a valid morphological process. Instantiation is the process whereby an underspeci-fied sign becomes fully specified. Instantiation is always constrained. Take for instance the following rule: latin([a,L,i,s],[affix]):- liq(L). Here we have that [a,L,i,s] is an underspecified affix of Latin that will instantiate in a way that satisfies the constraint that L is a liquid. [a,L,i,s] is furthermore constrained by the noun to which it affixes—as per the following rule. latin(A,[adjective]):-  latin(B,[noun]),  latin([a,L,i,s],[affix]),  last(B,E),  not(lat(E) <=> lat(L)),  append(B,[a,L,i,s],A). So [a,L,i,s] will instantiate in a way that does not match the last sound of the noun (in terms of laterality). In some cases, instantiations happen as a side effect of the definition of a phone. Or as a side effect of a morphological process of affixation. Occa-sionally, however, instantiation is not a side effect of a process, but is the process itself. This happens, for example, in our English rule for the homor-ganicity of nasals: 188 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS english(A,B):-  underenglish(A,B),  (nas(N), cns(C), nextto(N,C,A)) =>   homorganic(N,C). As argued at length above, this rule should not be built into the specification of any particular word or rule. Instead, it needs to be factored out of these specifications and made into a specification onto itself—at least if we are to be principled in our description of language. But this solution comes at a price. We need to recognize an entirely new family of four-place relations between sounds, sound categories, meanings, and meaning categories. They are underenglish, underlatin, underfinnish—and so on. As a matter of fact, we need to recognize one such relation for each language. Needless to say, admitting these relations runs counter to our stated goal of simplicity. But it seems that this cannot be avoided; principledness trumps simplicity here. But simplicity is also to be gained with instantiation. Take for example the case of the negative prefix uN discussed in Section 2 above. Instantia-tion allowed us to introduce this affix with one rule rather than seven—one per variant in (5). And this one rule was less specific (and hence less stipu-lative) that any one of those seven rules. This is a significant gain in sim-plicity. It is true that this underspecified rule calls for the instantiator clause. But that clause is motivated independently of the negative prefix. 7 Historical Note The notions of underspecification and instantiation have a long history in Linguistics. Nicolai Trubetzkoy (1890-1938) was one of the founding fa-thers of European Structuralism. He believed that every set of phones in complementary distribution formed a natural class.19 Consequently, he tried to account for the complementary distribution of a set of phones by positing an underspecified phone which instantiated as the various phones in com-plementary distribution, each in its correct phonetic environment (since the phones in complementary distribution formed a natural class, the under-specified phone needed could always be defined). Trubetzkoy used the term phoneme to refer to each of these underspecified phones, and reserved the                                                             19 See Chapter 2 for the definition of natural class. A set of phones is in complementary dis-tribution if no two phones in the set may occur in the same phonetic environment. Take for example the initial [p] in peep. It is followed by a puff of air that is strong enough to blow a candle. It is therefore called an aspirated [p]. This [p] contrasts with the final [p] in peep, which is not followed by a puff of air. As it turns out, the aspirated [p] occurs only at the be-ginning of a stressed syllable; plain [p], on the other hand, never does. Plain and aspirated [p] are thus phones in complementary distribution. MORPHOLOGY II: THE INSTANTIATION OF WORDS /  189 term allophone for each of the phones that instantiated a phoneme (see Trubetzkoy 1939). Occasionally, a natural class of phones exhibits complementary distribu-tion in some phonetic environments but not in others. For reasons that we do not need to address here, Trubetzkoy was forced to regard these phones as allophones of different phonemes, not as allophones of the same pho-neme. Consequently, he needed a new concept to account for what we might call semi-complementary distribution. This was the concept of archi-phoneme. An archiphoneme is an underspecified phoneme that instantiates as various phonemes in complementary distribution (as opposed to a pho-neme, which is an underspecified phone that instantiates as various phones in complementary distribution). The Spanish vibrants [r] and [ɾ] are, for example, in complementary distribution in the periphery of words (for, when peripheral, the trill [r] occurs only at the beginning of a word, while the tap [ɾ] appears only at the end of a word). They are not in complemen-tary distribution, however, inside a word, where both may occur—as they do, for example, between vowels. Thus, when /r/ and /ɾ/ occur peripherally, Trubetzkoy would regard them as different phonemes in complementary distribution—and thus as instantiations (or allophonemes) of a single archi-phoneme /R/ (an underspecified vibrant phone). The notions of phoneme and archiphoneme were unified in a single for-malism in Chomsky and Halle (1973), where we find rules like the ones in (13) and (14). (13) [–CNS] → [+NAS]  /  [+NAS] ___ [+NAS] (14) a.   [+VIB] → [+TNS]  /  # ___   b.   [+VIB] → [–TNS]  /      ___ # (13) says that underspecified vowels—or rather nonconsonants—instantiate as nasals when they occur between nasals (so, as might be gathered, the slash in this formalism separates the rule from the context in which it ap-plies). Similarly, (14a) asserts that underspecified vibrants instantiate as tense phones at the beginning of a word (the pound symbol is used here to mark the boundaries between words). And (14b) says underspecified vi-brants instantiate as lax phones at the end of a word. It should be clear that (13) expresses a phonemic regularity while (14) conveys an archiphonemic one. And the formalism can also express morphemic regularities that arise in word formation. A case in point is the voicing assimilation that accompa-nies the plural morph of English (see §3 above). Discussion of underspecification and instantiation flourished within the formalism of Chomsky and Halle (1973)—see for example Archangeli (1988), Mohanan (1991) and Steriade (1995). As did, more generally, ar-190 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS guments for deep (or abstract) representations in addition to surface (or concrete) representations of linguistic expressions. These arguments corre-spond to our arguments for underlinguistic relations in addition to linguistic ones. Multi-level representations have been treated computationally in the two-level morphology of Koskenniemi (1983)—which provides very effi-cient, finite-state (or Markov-chain), procedures to relate the abstract and the concrete representations of the expressions of a language. Kosken-niemi's approach has been implemented in PROLOG (see Boisen 1988; Lee 1991; Covington 1994, 275-282). 8 Exercises 1.
