At first glance, the problem seems trivial. A phrase is just a string of words (and words are what the preceding two chapters were about). But things are immensely more complex than that. First, not any string of words will make a phrase. Take for example the first sentence in this paragraph and read it backwards. It resembles word salad more than it does English. So much so that we have trouble giving it an intonation pat-tern. Beyond this, let us turn to the phrase in (1).  (1) old men and women  As a moment's reflection will reveal, the phrase in (1) is ambiguous. On the one hand, it refers to the set consisting of men of old age and women of any age; on the other hand, it refers to the set of old adults regardless of sex. In other words, the phrase in (1) exhibits an ambiguity with respect to the scope of the adjective in it; it exhibits an ambiguity with respect to what old applies to. One natural way to account for this ambiguity is to say that the adjective may combine either with men or with men and women. In the first case the scope of the adjective will be men; in the second it will be men and women. This would explain why (1) is ambiguous. At least if the interpreta-tion of a phrase follows its combinatorics. Diagrammatically, the phrase in (1) would have to be generated in one of the two following ways.  194 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS old            men     and      women            old         men        and       women                                 old men                      men and women                              old men and women             old men and women    So there is more to phrases than a one-dimensional concatenation of indi-vidual words. And ambiguities like (1) are legion. Another one can be found in (2). (2) small animal clinic We shall leave it as an exercise for the reader to figure out what its ambigu-ity is (and what it suggests about the way (2) should be generated). One way of summarizing the preceding discussion is to say that the con-struction of a phrase must be done in stages. Words are first concatenated with words to make phrases; phrases are then concatenated with phrases to make further phrases. These phrases may then combine with other phrases to make further phrases of their own. As we saw in Chapter 1, this process continues ad infinitum—or at least without a grammatically stipulated end.  The goal of this chapter is to explore these processes of phrase formation, henceforth referred to as syntactic processes. Several major syntactic pro-cesses will be distinguished here. Those that center around nouns, adjec-tives, verbs, prepositions, and adverbs. We now turn to them—as well as to sentences, the phrases that result from combining noun-centered phrases with verb-centered ones. 2 Modification Tradition has it that a noun is the name of a person, a place or a thing. The main problem with this definition is that it is not explicit enough. For what, indeed, is a person, a place, a thing? In this book we have approached the definition of noun in a different way—one that does not require us to solve the deep philosophical issues involved in this traditional, meaning-based, definition. In short, we will say that a noun is whatever results from nomi-nal roots and nominal-building morphological processes. Take for instance the program for Turkish suffixation we saw above (see suffixa-SYNTAX I: THE NOMINAL CLAUSE / 195 tion.swipl, Program 18 of Appendix H). It allows us to define 180 nouns of Turkish by taking three nominal roots ev 'house', deniz 'ocean', and el 'hand' and applying various morphological processes of suffixation to them. A noun may be true of any number of entities. It may even be true of in-finitely many (as the noun number is). And it may be true of none (as the noun unicorn is). The reference of a noun (the set of things it is true of) may be changed (or modified) by combining it with an adjective like good, bad, or ugly. Thus, the adjective good may combine with the noun horse and modify it, so that while horse refers to the entire set of horses, good horse refers only to the set of good ones. To make all this precise, let us assume we had two nouns parent and child. This we do by proposing the following PROLOG statements.   english([parent], [nom, wrd], [R :: findall(X,  parent(X), R)], [1,1]).  english([ child], [nom, wrd], [R :: findall(X, child(X), R)], [1,1]).  As usual, these grammatical statements are assertions that the four-place relation english holds of (i) a sound, (ii) a category of sounds, (iii) a meaning, and (iv) a category of meanings. To focus on syntactic issues, we will again pretend that English spelling represents sound accurately. So we will have [parent] and [child] instead of [[p,e,ɹ],[ə,n,t]] and [[č,a,j,l,d]] in the sound slot. For reasons that will become clear later, we will say that the category of nouns is the category [nom,wrd] of nominal words. And, for reasons given in Chapter 3, the meanings of these nouns are the procedures triggered, respectively, by the following two queries.  R :: findall(X, parent(X), R). R :: findall(X, child(X), R).  It should be clear that, at this juncture, 'parent' and 'child' do not stand for English words, but rather for language-independent predicates that characterize the set of parents and the set of children, respectively, in any given model. These sets are properties of individuals. Thus, according to the ontology presented at the end of Chapter 3, they are first-order, unary rela-tions. Consequently, we will say that they are referents of category [1,1].1                                                             1 A second-order unary relation would be an entity of category [2, 1]. More generally, then, a relation of order m and arity n would be an entity of category [m, n]. See Chapter 3, Section 12. 196 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS Next we assume two adjectives male and female: english([  male], [adj, wrd], [R :: findall(X, male(X), R)], [1,1]). english([female], [adj, wrd], [R :: findall(X, female(X), R)], [1,1]). As might be expected, these adjectives are adjectival words, and hence members of the category [adj, wrd]. Their meanings are the procedures for computing who is male and who is female in any given model. As to the sets of males and females themselves (relative to the given model), they too are referents of category [1,1]. Next we should formulate a rule for combining nouns and adjectives. This we do as follows: english(SO,[nom,phr],[R::findall(X,(Y1,Y2),R)],[1,1]):-  english(SO1,[adj,wrd],[R1::findall(X,Y1,R1)],[1,1]),  english(SO2,[nom,wrd],[R2::findall(X,Y2,R2)],[1,1]),  append(SO1, SO2, SO). As this rule would have it, the sound of a modified noun is the sound of an adjective followed by the sound of a noun, and belongs the category [nom, phr] of nominal phrases. Notice that formulating the category this way allows us to capture the similarity between plain and modified nouns (they are both nominal expressions)—while at the same time acknowledging their difference in their internal constituencies (one is a word whereas the other is a phrase and, as things stand thus far, the former is constituted solely by a nominal root; the latter by a noun and an adjective). Notice that the reference of a modified noun is the set of entities which are characterized both by the predicate Y1 contributed by the adjective, and by the predicate Y2 contributed by the noun. In other words, the reference of a modified noun is the intersection of the reference of the adjective and the reference of the noun. It should be clear that for these predicates to ap-ply conjunctively we must name the variables in them the same way. The common name we chose here for these variables was X. We achieved com-mon naming simply by fiat—we simply made the same variable X appear as the first argument of the three findall predicates in the rule. This will make the variables Y1 and Y2 range over conditions over one and the same X. All of these rules have been gathered in modification.swipl, which is Program 42 of Appendix H. Notice that this program includes an instruction to consult a model for the interpretation of all the expressions SYNTAX I: THE NOMINAL CLAUSE / 197 generated by modification.swipl (as well as other expressions we will see in the next chapter). This model is simpsons.swipl (see Pro-gram 43, Appendix H). It represents a view of the world consistent with that of a popular TV show. You may see the effects of these programs by enter-ing english(SO,SC,ME,MC). To see the reference of any of the expressions thus generated, simply enter their meaning (followed by a period). Thus, to compute the reference of female parent, enter Reference::findall(X,(female(X),parent(X)),Reference). Incidentally, it should be pointed out that not all adjectives are intersective in the sense discussed above. Consider in this regard the modified nouns in (3). (3) a. dear Abbey  b.  industrious Californians  c. big cricket  d. former husband  e. beautiful dancer (3a) is a case of an adjective modifying a proper name. Notice that interpret-ing this phrase does not involve finding all the Abbeys (in the model) and then picking the dear ones from among them. Rather, it involves picking the one and only Abbey (in the model) and asserting that she is dear (to the speaker). So adjectives may modify proper names, but without restricting their reference. They modify them improperly.  But adjectives may also modify common nouns improperly. Take for example (3b). As it turns out, this phrase is ambiguous. In one sense—the intersective one—it refers only to those Californians who are industrious; in the other sense—the nonintersective one—it refers to all Californians, and says of them all that they are industrious. In other words, (3b) has the two readings in (4). (4) a.  Californians who are industrious  b.  Californians, who are industrious For reasons that should now be clear, the phrase who are industrious is said to be a restrictive relative clause in (4a) and a nonrestrictive one in (4b). Nonrestrictive relative clauses are introduced by a pause and uttered in a low tone. English punctuation uses commas to set them off from the rest of the sentence. 198 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS Consider next (3c). Notice that this modified noun does not have an in-tersective reading either, as its reference is not the intersection of the set of crickets with the set of things which are big. For no cricket, not even a big one, can be big in general. The problem is that big expresses a relative no-tion; big with respect to a cricket is not big in general. To solve this puzzle we have to 'apply' the reference of big to the reference of the noun to be modified. In other words, we would need to assign big the meaning below, where X is a variable over sets of things that can be big, and Y is a variable over sets of elements of X that are big relative to X. (5) R :: findall([X,Y], big(X,Y), R). Interestingly, this makes big a second-order property (a property of proper-ties rather than a property of individuals). But second-order properties are notoriously difficult to compute, as they give rise to exponential complexi-ties. This would be a cause of concern if we had to compute these properties as part of computing language. Fortunately, nothing of the sort is called for. All that needs to be done is (a) represent a second-order property big as a query that will trigger a procedure that will compute its reference (relative to a model), and (b) pair this representation of its meaning to a representa-tion of its sound. None of these tasks is particularly complex. The first of these tasks is completed by exhibiting (5) above; the second by using (5) to fill the meaning slot in (6). (6) english([big], [adj, wrd], _, [2,2]). Turning next to (3d), we note that a former husband is not someone who is both a husband and "a former". In fact, the very notion of being "a former something" is actually incoherent. Anyone who is a former something is also a current something-else. All former husbands, for example, are current ex-husbands. So being a former would entail being a non-former, which is a contradiction. It should be noticed that former is non-intersective with a vengeance. For, notice that every time you intersect one set with another you produce a subset of both. In the case of former, there is no guarantee that a former x will be an x at all (there is no guarantee that a former hus-band will still be a husband; no guarantee that a former president will still be a president—and so on). Once again, the problem here is that former is not a first-order property of individuals but a second-order property of properties. Finally, the modified noun in (3e) is ambiguous, but in a new way. In one reading, it refers to set of entities who are both beautiful and dancers. This is the intersective sense of the phrase. But notice that (3e) may also refer to individuals who dance beautifully (regardless of whether they are themselves beautiful or not). This second reading is not intersective. Inter-SYNTAX I: THE NOMINAL CLAUSE / 199 estingly, it, too, is a second-order reading—but one that does not even apply to the noun dancer it combines with, but rather to its verbal root dance. So far all the modifiers we have seen are prenominal (they occur before the nouns they modify). But modifiers can also be postnominal (i.e. occur after the noun they modify). Even in English, where we see modified nouns like things Chinese and ants galore. Although English adjectives over-whelmingly occur prenominally, adjectives of many other languages occur mostly postnominally. We close this section with a feature that occurs frequently in conjunction with modification. It is agreement. Agreement is the process whereby two or more words are made to match—or at least covary—in sound. A good example of agreement, also referred to as concordance, comes from Span-ish. As we saw in the exercises to Chapter 1, Spanish has four nouns niño, niña, niños, niñas and four adjectives alto, alta, altos, altas. Interestingly, they do not combine randomly with each other; only as follows (see forms highlighted in bold).    niño alto *niño alta *niño altos *niño altas  *niña alto  niña alta *niña altos *niña altas  *niños alto *niños alta niños altos *niños altas  *niñas alto *niñas alta *niñas altos niñas altas So Spanish nouns and adjectives may be either masculine or feminine and, independently of this choice, singular or plural. Moreover, adjectives must make the same choices here as the nouns that they modify. In other words adjectives must exhibit agreement in both gender (the masculine/feminine choice) and number (the singular/feminine choice) with the nouns they modify. One simple way to handle these facts is as follows.2                                                             2 To focus on syntactic issues we omit here the tilde over the n called for by Spanish spelling (to have it display properly in our interactions with PROLOG we would need to appeal to the Unicode values as described in Chapter 2). 200 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS Program 44. agreement.swipl agreement.swipl handles agreement in the one rule it contains. It handles gender agreement by having one and the same variable B range over the genders of nouns and adjectives. Similarly, it handles number agreement by having one and the same variable C range over the numbers of nouns and adjectives. This is the case because all the instances of a vari-able in a rule must take the same value. For reasons that will become clear below, the modified noun must inherit the gender and number characteris-tics of its constituents. Hence we mention B and C on the sound category of the resulting nominal phrase [nom,phr] as well. Similar solutions are possible in languages with more than two genders, more than two numbers or, indeed, in languages with concordance parame-ters other than gender and number. We shall see one of them in the Exercis-es to this chapter. 3 Quantification Every language has a small number of short but extremely frequent words that "specify" the set of entities nouns refer to. Even after they have been modified. In English, these words include every, some, no, the, and the var-ious possessives my, your, his, her, its, our, their.3 These words are called specifiers or determiners. At their core are the quantifiers, the best known of which are every, some, and no. It is to them that we now turn.                                                             3 Certain varieties of English also include the possessive you all's (or, more colloquially, y'all's). spanish([ nino], [nom,wrd,msc,sng]). spanish([ nina], [nom,wrd,fmn,sng]). spanish([ninos], [nom,wrd,msc,plr]). spanish([ninas], [nom,wrd,fmn,plr]).  spanish([ alto], [adj,wrd,msc,sng]). spanish([ alta], [adj,wrd,fmn,sng]). spanish([altos], [adj,wrd,msc,plr]). spanish([altas], [adj,wrd,fmn,plr]).  spanish(A, [nom,phr,B,C]):-  spanish(D, [nom,wrd,B,C]),  spanish(E, [adj,wrd,B,C]),  append(D, E, A). SYNTAX I: THE NOMINAL CLAUSE / 201 For starters, what does every man refer to? Apparently, it refers to the set of men (taken individually). But this can't be right. This is what the noun man already refers to. And every does not seem to be redundant. Things are even less clear with some man. We cannot say, of course, that it refers to some man or another. That would be a circular characterization of what some man refers to. So perhaps it refers to the arbitrary man—a man that has all the properties men have but none of the properties that distinguish them from one another. Intriguing at first, the idea seems riddled with inco-herence. For every man has a height. So our arbitrary man must have a height as well. But what height can he have? Truthfully, he cannot have any particular height, not even average height, as not all men will have such a height. So he must have height… but he cannot have any particular height! What about weight, complexion, sexual preference, or IQ? He must have all of them, but none of them at the same time! But perhaps most mystifying of all is the reference of no man. What can that be? Can it even be something at all? Apparently not even that. Consider the following fallacy. A buttered toast is better than nothing. Nothing is better than eternal bliss.   ∴ A buttered toast is better than eternal bliss. What is wrong with this argument? Notice that being better than is a transi-tive relation (so that if three dollars are better than two dollars, and if two dollars are better than one, then three dollars are better than one). What seems to be wrong with the argument above is that we are treating nothing as if it were something—something that links the two premises of the syllo-gism above in the same way as the two dollars in our example. Notice that it will not do to turn to first-order logic—the logic ostensibly dealing with the quantifiers every, some, and no. For, in first-order logic, the truth conditions of (7a) are as indicated in (7b). (7) a. Every man is mortal.  b. ∀x[man(x) → mortal(x)] (7b)  is true if and only if the bracketed implication in (7b) is true for every value of x. Notice that these are, in fact, the correct truth conditions of (7a), as this sentence is true if and only the humanity of x indeed implies the mor-tality of x for every value of x. Yet, these truth conditions hardly tell us what every man means. For one thing, notice that the truth conditions just described for (7b) assume we know what every value means. So this ap-proach simply trades the meaning of every in English for the meaning of 202 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS every in the (metalanguage of the) logic. What's worse, there is no piece of (7b) that even corresponds to every man. To make this important point clear, take a look at how (7b) is put together:  ∀               x               man            x    →          mortal            x                                                                                                                                                                   ∀x                 man(x)                                        mortal(x)                                                                                                                            man(x) → mortal(x)                      ∀x[man(x) → mortal(x)]  Notice that every man does not correspond just to ∀x. Clearly, man(x) must be part of its semantic contribution as well. As should the implication sign. For changing this connective for conjunction (∧) or disjunction (∨) changes meaning: (7a) does not mean (7c) or (7d).4  (7) c. ∀x[man(x) ∧ mortal(x)]  d. ∀x[man(x) ∨ mortal(x)] On the other hand, the semantic contribution of every man does not extend beyond the implication sign. In other words, the contribution of this phrase is spread over the string of symbols that goes from the upside-down A to the arrow of implication. But this stretch is not, alas, a meaningful compo-nent of (7a).                                                             4 For, (7c) is true if and only if the conjunction bracketed in it is true for every assignment of values to x (or, equivalently, if and only if everything in the universe is both a man and a mor-tal). And (7d) is true if and only if the disjunction bracketed in it is true for every assignment to values to x (or, equivalently, if and only if everything in the universe is either a man, a mortal, or both).    SYNTAX I: THE NOMINAL CLAUSE / 203 In short, first-order logic gives us no clue as to what every man means. What's worse, it does not even recognize every man as a meaningful com-ponent of a sentence. Thus, to figure out what every man means we must leave first-order logic (which is the realm of sets of individuals) and head for second-order logic (the realm of sets of sets of individuals).5 For, if we can talk about such second-order sets, then we could say that the reference of every man is the set of supersets of the set of men. And this is, as we will see, the correct interpretation of this phrase. Formally, let E be the set of entities of a model. Let M be the set of men of that model. What the shift to second-order logic enables us to say is that the reference of every man is the following set of sets: (8) {F ⊆ E: M ⊆ F} In other words, the reference of every man is the set of sets every man be-longs to. Similarly, we could say that some man refers to the family of sets that overlap with the set of men (or the family of sets some man belongs to).6 (9) {F ⊆ E: M ° F} And we could say that no man refers to the family of sets that are disjoint with the set of men (or the family of sets that no man belongs to).7 (10) {F ⊆ E: M | F} Notice that we have succeeded in assigning each one of our phrases a co-herent reference. But are these the correct references to assign them? The answer to this question will depend largely on how well the meanings as-signed to these phrases will combine with the meanings of other phrases. Beyond this, notice that we say that the noun man refers to the set of men because the noun man is true of all the elements of this set. And only of them. By the same token, we could say that the meaning of every man is the set in (6) because the phrase every man is true of all the elements of this set. And only of them. It might be objected that the set in (8) will contain entities other than men. This is true but irrelevant. The objection would be relevant if (8) were being proposed as the reference of the phrase only men, as this phrase would not be true of all the sets that belong to (8). But (8) is not being pro-posed as the reference of only men; it is being proposed only as the refer-ence of every man.                                                             5 In so doing, we are following the lead of Montague (1970). 6 Formally, two sets overlap if and only if their intersection is not the empty set. 7 Formally, two sets are disjoint if and only if their intersection is the empty set. 204 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS But there is another advantage to the proposals in (8)–(10). It is that they lead us naturally to the meanings of the quantifiers themselves. For, notice that the phrases under discussion differ only in the quantifiers they contain (every, some, no). And the sets in (8)–(10) differ only in the set-theoretic relations they involve (superset, overlap, disjointness). It is therefore natu-ral to propose that every expresses the superset relation, some stands for the overlap relation, and no represents the disjointness relation. It should be clear that these are relations between sets of individuals, not relations be-tween the individuals that belong to those sets. They are, indeed, second-order relations. The interpretations we have provided have been cast in the language of classes. But they may be also expressed in the language of properties. Seen in this light, every man refers to the (set of) properties that generalize the property of being a man; some man refers to the (set of) properties that are compatible with the property of being a man; no man refers to the (set of) properties that are incompatible with the property of being a man. As to the quantifiers every, some and no, they would naturally refer to the property-theoretic relations of generalization, compatibility, and incompatibility themselves. Notice that, once again, these are not relations between individ-uals; only relations between properties of individuals. They are, again, se-cond-order relations. 4 Quantification in PROLOG Can we formalize all this in PROLOG? Yes we can. Before doing so, how-ever, we should notice that quantifiers combine with nominal phrases (11b) just as easily as they do with nominal words (11a).  (11) a.  every man  b.  every righteous man To handle this versatility of quantifiers, we could propose separate rules for quantifying over words and for quantifying over phrases. But this seems to miss a generalization. A better solution seems to be to deem every noun a nominal phrase, and allow quantifiers to combine with nominal phrases as a whole. To be more specific, let us first write some rules for introducing quantifiers: english([every], [qfr,wrd], [R ::  findall([X,Y],includes(X,Y),R)], [2,2]). english([some], [qfr,wrd], [R ::  findall([X,Y],overlaps(X,Y),R)], [2,2]).  SYNTAX I: THE NOMINAL CLAUSE / 205 english([no], [qfr,wrd], [R ::  findall([X,Y], excludes(X,Y), R)], [2,2]). These rules regard every, some, no as members of the category [qfr, wrd], and hence as quantifier words. They furthermore interpret each of these quantifier words as a procedure for computing a set of ordered pairs [X,Y], where X is itself a set that includes (or overlaps, or excludes) Y, which is also a set in its own right. In other words, these rules interpret quantifiers, as suggested above, as second-order binary relations—and hence as referring to objects of category [2,2]. Naturally, this assumes that we have defined the includes/overlaps/excludes predicates. This we do in sets.swipl (see Program 46 of Appendix H). Next we can press into service our earlier statements that allowed for nouns, adjectives, and all nominal phrases formed out of them (see modi-fication.swipl): english([parent],[nom,wrd],[R::findall(X,parent(X),R)],[1,1]). english([child],[nom,wrd],[R::findall(X,child(X),R)],[1,1]). english([male],[adj,wrd],[R::findall(X,male(X),R)],[1,1]). english([female],[adj,wrd],[R::findall(X,female(X),R)],[1,1]). english(SO,[nom,phr],[R::findall(X,(Y1,Y2),R)],[1,1]):-  english(SO1,[adj,wrd],[R1::findall(X,Y1,R1)], [1,1]),  english(SO2,[nom,wrd],[R2::findall(X,Y2,R2)], [1,1]),  append(SO1,SO2,SO). Then we can make nominal words double as nominal phrases with the following recategorization rule. english(SO,[nom,phr],ME,MC) :-  english(SO,[nom, wrd], ME, MC). Finally, we can describe the combination of a quantifier word and a nominal phrase. We shall call this combination a nominal clause: english(SO,[nom,cls],[R::findall(X,(Z1,Z2),R)],[2,1]):-  english(SO1,[qfr,wrd],[R1::findall([X,R2],Z1,R1)],[2,2]),  english(SO2,[nom, phr],[R2::Z2],[1,1]),  append(SO1,SO2,SO). (see quantification.swipl; Program 45 in Appendix H). As usual, you can gauge the effect of these proposals by entering  english(SO,SC,ME,MC).  206 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS Then try entering any of the computed meanings. For example, the comput-ed meaning for no female parent will be something like this: V1 :: findall(V2,(excludes(V2,V3),findall(V4, (female(V4),parent(V4)),V3)),V1). Entering that will yield V1 = [[], [lisa], [homer], [homer,lisa], [bart],     [bart,lisa], [bart,homer], [bart,homer,lisa]]. These are, indeed, the eight subsets of the universe that contain no female parent (relative to our Simpsons model). 5 The Definite Article Much has been written about the definite article—the word that is spelled the in English. The impetus to write about this little word has never been stated more forcefully than in the following passage where, writing from prison, Bertrand Russell (1919) explained that […] in this chapter we shall consider the word the in the singular, and in the next chapter we shall consider the word the in the plural. It may be thought excessive to devote two chapters to one word, but to the philosophical math-ematician it is a word of very great importances […] I would give the doc-trine of this word if I were dead from the waist down and not merely in pris-on. As Russell would have it, the definite article makes two implicit claims: the claim of existence and the claim of uniqueness. The claim of existence states that the nominal phrase that combines with the definite article must be true of at least one entity. The claim of uniqueness holds that it must be true of at most one entity. In short, use of the definite article commits us to the claim that the nominal phrase it combines with is true of exactly one entity. In support of his doctrine, Russell could point to the clauses in (12) and (13) and say that, while (12) may partake of a true sentence, neither (13a) nor (13b) can—at least relative to models that reflect the political realities of contemporary France, where there is exactly one president, more than one senator, and less than one king. (12) the president of France (13) a.  the senator of France  b.  the king of France  SYNTAX I: THE NOMINAL CLAUSE / 207 Capturing the Russellian doctrine of the definite article in PROLOG is easy. All we need to do, at least for English, is admit the definite article via the following fact: (14) english([the],[qfr,wrd],[R::  findall([X,[Y]],includes(X,[Y]),R)],[2,2]. This is what we did in determination.swipl—except for one trifle: following received terminological conventions, we placed both the definite article and the quantifiers in the category [det,wrd] of determiner words (see Program 47, Appendix H). In other words, all we had to do was regard the as a version of every—a version whose includes predicate has a one-member list [Y] as its sec-ond argument. For this ensures that every nominal clause formed with a definite article will refer either to the set of supersets of a singleton, or else to the empty set. As we shall see in the next chapter, the former makes it possible for a nominal clause headed by a definite article to contribute to a true sentence, while the latter makes partaking in truth impossible.8 To gather the effect of the small change represented in (14), notice that, according to determination.swipl, the meaning of the child will be something like (15), while the meaning of the female child will be some-thing like (16): (15) R :: findall(X,(includes(X,[Y]),findall  (Z,child(Z),[Y])),R). (16) R :: findall(X,(includes(X,[Y]),findall  (Z,(female(Z),child(Z)),[Y])),R). The key point about (15) and (16) are the second occurrences of the single-ton [Y] in them. While [Y] is required to represent the set of children in (15), it is required to represent only the set of female children in (16). If our model allows for two children only one of whom is female, [Y] will not be able to successfully represent the desired set in (15); only in (16). Conse-quently, the reference these meanings gather are (15') and (16'), respectively.                                                              8 Alternatively, we could regard the as a version of some whose overlaps predicate has a singleton as its second argument: english([the],[qfr,word],[R ::  findall([X,[Y]],overlaps(X,[Y]),R)],[2,2]). This analysis is perfectly equivalent to the one in the text, since a set includes a singleton iff it overlaps with it. 208 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS (15') R = [ ]. (16') R = [[bart,homer,lisa,marge],[homer,lisa,marge], [lisa,marge],[lisa],[homer,lisa],[bart,lisa,   marge],[bart,lisa],[bart,homer,lisa]] It should be emphasized that both the child and the female child are perfect-ly grammatical pairings of sound and meaning. Both are, in particular, meaningful expressions. It is just that the former will happen to refer to the empty set in the given model (and hence will not be able to be part of a sen-tence that is true relative to that model). But the class of determiner words includes signs beyond the quantifiers and the definite article. It includes the possessives—my, your, his, her, its, our, and their. As it turns out, accounts of all of them are forthcoming as well.  For starters, notice that possessives also seem to make claims of existence and uniqueness. Thus, it is odd to say my friend that does not exist (whereas it is better to say a friend of mine that does not exist). It feels as if you were both asserting and denying the existence of a friend of yours. This could be explained by claiming that an assertion of existence is in effect made by the possessive determiner contained in the subject (only to be denied in the predicate). Even more clearly contradictory would be to say my friend is tall and my friend is short (even if you had two friends one of whom was tall and the other short; it is fine, however to say a friend of mine is tall and a friend of mine is short). This could be explained by saying that the posses-sive asserts that there is at most one friend—which is then ascribed contra-dictory properties. If possessives indeed make assertions of existence and uniqueness, then we could interpret them along the lines of the definite article. Thus, the de-terminer my could be introduced by the following statement:    english([my],[det,wrd],[R::findall([X,[Y]], (includes(X,[Y]), mine(Y)),R)],[2,2]). my friend would then refer to the set of all sets that include my one and only friend. Its interpretation is just that of the—plus the notion of mine. It is interesting to note that some languages express separately the two meaning components of a possessive determiner—the definiteness and the posses-sion. In Italian, for example, my friend is expressed either as (17a) or (17b), depending on whether the friend in question is male or female. SYNTAX I: THE NOMINAL CLAUSE / 209 (17) a.  il               mio            amico     theMASC    mineMASC   friendMASC    'my friend, who is male'  b.  la           mia            amica    theFEM    mineFEM    friendFEM    'my friend, who is female'  And in the history languages we sometimes see the progressive replacement of one of these constructions by the other. Thus, the history of the Spanish possessive construction has witnessed the replacement of an analytic, Ital-ian-type, construction into a synthetic, English-type, construction. All of these facts argue that possessive determiners should be analyzed as synthe-sizing the meanings contributed by a definite article and a possessive modi-fier. This does not mean, however, that possessive modifiers must also in-volve the sounds of a definite article and a possessive modifier. What this means exactly will be clarified as you work out one of the exercises at the end of this chapter. 6 Proper Names We saw in Chapter 3 that proper names referred to entities. But this seems to fly in the face of the account of quantified nominal clauses we have de-veloped thus far. For quantified nominal clauses refer not to entities. Or even to sets of entities—but rather to sets of sets of entities. So should we have a split theory of nominal clauses? Should some nominal clauses refer to entities and others to sets of sets thereof? Clearly, that would not be an optimal solution. Nominal clauses of both kinds behave in essentially the same ways, serving as subjects of sentences and objects of verbs and prepo-sitions. Having a split theory of nominal phrases would force us to account for these behaviors separately—thus complicating the overall description and missing important generalizations. Fortunately, there is a way to achieve a unified theory of nominal claus-es. It involves regarding an entity as the set of properties it satisfies (and hence as an object of the same type as the reference of a quantified nominal clause). The rationale for this move is that there is a one-to-one correspond-ence between entities and the sets of properties they satisfy (for each entity there is one and only one set of properties it satisfies. And, conversely, for each one of these sets of properties, there is one and only one entity that satisfies them). To achieve our unified theory of nominal clauses, we begin by introduc-ing proper names as nouns that refer to individuals—namely the individuals that bear those names (what entity bears what name is given by the model): 210 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS  (18) english([homer], [nom,wrd,prp], [R ::   bearer(R,[homer])], [0]).  english([marge], [nom,wrd,prp], [R ::   bearer(R,[marge])], [0]).  english([ bart], [nom,wrd,prp], [R ::   bearer(R,[ bart])], [0]).  english([ lisa], [nom,wrd,prp], [R ::   bearer(R,[ lisa])], [0]). Notice that we have assigned our proper names Homer, Marge, Bart, Lisa to the category [nom,wrd,prp] of proper nominal words. This is be-cause proper names behave differently from common nouns like parent and child (which will be assigned to the category [nom,wrd,com] of com-mon nominal words). And these nominal words differ also from proper nominal phrases and nominal clauses, as we will soon see. Notice next that we have assumed in (18) the indirectly referential the-ory of proper names (see Chapter 3 for details). It should be pointed out that nothing that we will say in this section hinges on this choice. For example, had we wished to assume the directly referential theory of proper names, we could do so simply by introducing proper names as follows: (18') english([homer], [nom,wrd,prp], [R ::    R = homer], [0]).   english([marge], [nom,wrd,prp], [R ::    R = marge], [0]).   english([ bart], [nom,wrd,prp], [R ::    R =  bart], [0]).   english([ lisa], [nom,wrd,prp], [R ::    R =  lisa], [0]).  As can be readily seen, both (18) and (18') assert that the meaning category for proper names will be [0], which is the basic category of entities (or urelements, as they are sometimes called). Next we assign proper names to a second category—the category of proper nominal phrases. As a nominal phrase, a proper name will refer to the set of bearers of the name in question (and not just to an isolated bearer of said name; even if there was only one such bearer): SYNTAX I: THE NOMINAL CLAUSE / 211 (19) english(SO,[nom,phr,prp],[R::  findall(X,Y,R)],[1,0]):-  english(SO, [nom,wrd,prp], [X :: Y], [0]). Finally, we assign proper names to a third category—that of nominal claus-es. Here is where we regard a proper name N as a set of properties (namely the set of properties that generalize the property of bearing the name N): (20) english(SO,[nom,cls,prp],[R::  findall(X,(Y,includes(X,Z)),R)],[2,1]):-   english(SO,[nom,phr,prp],[Z :: Y],[1,0]). Thus, the proper name Homer will be assigned three meanings depending on whether it is a word (21a), a phrase (21b), or a clause (21c): (21) a. R :: bearer(R,[homer]).  b. R :: findall(X,bearer(X,[homer]),R).  c. R :: findall(X,(findall     (Y,bearer(Y,[homer]),Z),includes(X,Z)),R). Relative to the Simpsons model, these meanings would be procedures that elicit three distinct  referents: an entity (22a), a set of entities (22b), and a set of sets of entities (22c). (22) a. homer  b. [homer]  c. [[bart,homer,lisa,marge], [homer, lisa, marge], [homer,marge],[homer],[homer,lisa],[bart,homer, marge],[bart,homer],[bart, homer,lisa]]. (22a) is the bearer of the name Homer, (22b) is the set of bearers of that name, and (22c) is the generalization of the property of bearing that name (which is also the set of properties homer has or, equivalently, the set of sets that contain him). It should be emphasized that the triple assignment of proper names to words, phrases, and clauses allows us both to have a unified interpretation of nominal classes and to capture the intuition that proper names refer to individual entities. The former follows from the interpretation of a proper name as a nominal clause; the latter from its interpretation as a word. Be-yond this, the triple categorization of proper names is independently moti-vated by the fact that some languages allow proper names to be both modi-fied and specified. Consider once again Italian, where we can say   212 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS (23) il             mio            Fabrizio   theMASC   mineMASC   Fabrizio  'my Fabrizio'. This shows that the proper name Fabrizio is a nominal word that belongs both to a nominal phrase and to a nominal clause. We have included all our statements about proper names in nomi-nalclause.swipl, a program which furthermore collects all the rules discussed in this chapter (see Program 48, Appendix H). 7 Suggestions for Further Reading Syntax has been a central component of grammars since classical antiquity, but the idea that syntax could be the subject of logic-like formal rules was first proposed in Chomsky (1957), and has been developed vigorously ever since. This development can be tracked in Chomsky (1965; 1982; 1995)— and in the massive response sparked by these works. Of the myriad ideas advanced in this literature, one holds that the categories of phrases are but "projections" of the categories of words (see Jackendoff 1977)—so that alongside nominal words we would have nominal phrases and clauses (and alongside verbal words we would have verbal phrases and clauses, as we will see in next chapter).  The idea that the semantics of natural languages can be formulated with mathematical precision can be traced back to Boole (1854) and Frege (1879). It too has enjoyed robust development throughout the 20th century and to this day. Accessible overviews of this development can be gathered from McCawley (1993) and Chierchia & McConnell-Ginet (2000). The idea that noun clauses refer to sets of sets was first proposed in Montague (1970), and is known as the theory of generalized quantification, as it was ad-vanced in order to interpret quantifiers like most, which cannot be described by the restrictive forms of quantification offered by first-order logic. Im-portant advances in the theory of generalized quantification can be found in Barwise and Cooper (1981). See Bach (1989) and Larson (1990) for acces-sible presentations of this material. Of all the ideas implemented in this chapter, none has been more contro-versial than the Russellian theory of the definite article. As will be recalled, Russell believed that the definite article made two implicit claims; one of existence and the other of uniqueness. Consequently, failure of either one of these claims entailed failure of the entire sentence containing the definite article. Russell's views on the definite article held sway for half a century, when it was seriously challenged by Peter Strawson. As Strawson (1950) would have it, the definite article does not contribute claims of existence SYNTAX I: THE NOMINAL CLAUSE / 213 and uniqueness, but rather presuppositions of existence and uniqueness. Consequently, sentences in which these presuppositions fail are not just false; they are meaningless. Or at least non-referential (for want of a truth value). They simply fail to introduce anything about which something can then be said. The issue of the truth or falsity of what is said simply does not arise; the issue has been voided (or rendered moot) by presupposition fail-ure. As we see it, Strawson's challenge has a lot to recommend it. To sharpen it, notice that presuppositions of existence and uniqueness stand or fall ac-cording to the models against which they are evaluated. This suggests that the problem with presupposition failure is the failure to convey a referent, not the failure to convey a meaning.  To make this point clear, let us assume that the definite article was in-troduced not by (14), but rather by the following rule. (14') english([the],[art,wrd],[R ::  findall([X,[Y]],includes(X,[Y]),R)],[2,2]). as a cursory review of (14) and (14') will reveal, the only difference be-tween them is the category of the definite article. In (14) it is an ordinary quantifier qfr; in (14') it is a special determiner art. But the Strawsonian view calls for this difference, as the combination of an article with a nomi-nal phrase will be different, in this view, from that of an ordinary quantifier with its respective nominal phrase. The combination of ordinary quantifiers is repeated in (24); that of an article with its nominal phrase is indicated in (24'). (24) english(SO,[nom,cls],[R::findall(X,(Z1,Z2),R)],[2,1]):-  english(SO1,[qfr,wrd],[R1::findall([X,R2],Z1,R1)],[2,2]),  english(SO2,[nom,phr],[R2::Z2],[1,1]),  append(SO1,SO2,SO). (24') english(SO,[nom,cls],[R::findall(X,(Z1,Z2),R),  not(R =[])], [2,1]):-    english(SO1,[art,wrd],[R1::findall    ([X,R2],Z1,R1)],[2,2]),    english(SO2,[nom,phr],[R2::Z2],[1,1]),    append(SO1,SO2,SO). As can be readily seen, the only difference between (24) and (24') is the requirement that the reference of the nominal clause not be empty. The ef-fect of this requirement is that the nominal clause will fail to have a referent in models in which this reference would be null (were it not for this re-214 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS quirement). In other words, the nominal clause would fail to have a refer-ence if the presupposition of existence failed. The case of the presupposi-tion of uniqueness was built into the meaning of the in (14'), and will there-fore carry over to the second line of (24'). That, incidentally, was why we needed a special determiner category for the definite article. A program that implements the effects of the Strawsonian view on nom-inal clauses is strawsonian.swipl (see Program 49, Appendix H). Relative to this program, the male parent will be referential, as there is one and only one male parent in the model assumed by this program. But the parent will not be, as there are two parents in that model, and the presuppo-sition of uniqueness thus fails.  To verify this, enter (25) to obtain the meaning of the male parent. You will get something which is, strictly speaking, equivalent to (26). Using this as a query gets you (27), which would thus be the reference of the male parent. Notice that (27) is, indeed, the set of sets that contain the one and only male parent (homer). (25) english([the,male,parent],_,ME,_). (26) R::findall(X,(includes(X,[Y]),findall(Z,(male(Z),  parent(Z)),[Y])),R),not(R = []). (27) [[bart,homer,lisa,marge],[homer,lisa,marge], [homer,marge],[homer],[homer,lisa],[bart,homer, marge],[bart,homer],[bart,homer,lisa]]. On the other hand, if you enter (28) to obtain the meaning of the parent you will get (29) (except for the names of the variables in it). And using this as a query will get you (30)—which is another way of saying that there is no reference assigned to the parent. This, of course, is as desired. (28) english([the,parent],_,ME,_). (29) R :: findall(X,(includes(X,[Y]),findall  (Z,parent(Z),[Y])),R),not(R = []). (30) false Notice that the response in (30) is not that the reference is the empty set (as was with the Russellian view). The response is that there is no way to solve for the referential variable. In other words, that there is no reference. It should be clear that both the male parent and the parent have meanings— they are the procedures triggered by (26) and (29). What the male parent has but the parent lacks, is a reference. SYNTAX I: THE NOMINAL CLAUSE / 215 8 Exercises 1. What is the ambiguity of small animal clinic?
