Make sure the model will satisfy the assertions (or presuppositions) of existence and uniqueness implicit in these pronouns (as interpreted above).   217 7 Syntax II: The Verbal Clause  1 Verbs and Such Verbs have been commonly defined as action words—words that name actions. Taken point blank, this definition is at once too strong and too weak. It is too strong because it excludes verbs that name states rather than actions. Examples of such verbs are know, own, and matter. Notice that we have good tests to distinguish between actions and states. Thus, while true actions like kick can partake of the -ing construction, true states like matter do not: (1) a.    I am kicking a ball.  b.  *I am mattering a lot. But the action-based definition is also too weak, as there are clear nouns like construction, refusal and deceit that arguably name actions as well. It is possible, of course, that action could be defined in a way that picks all and only verbs. But such a definition seems hard to come by. In any event, we would need more than just a definition of verbs as action words.  Things do not fare better for other semantic definitions of verbs. Re-call that we interpreted verbs in Chapter 3 as relations between entities. This, they are. But nouns name relations between entities as well. Take nouns that name kinship relations. Uncle, for example. It names the rela-tion between someone that has an uncle and that uncle. And prepositions arguably name relations as well. Between, for example, can be said to name a relation that three collinear things are in. We will get farther if we define verbs in terms of sound rather than meaning. At least in some languages. In English, for example, we can 218 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS define verbs as those words that agree with subjects (this assumes, of course that we have independent ways of defining subjects and agree-ment). A more reliable definition would be to admit a word as a verb if it can mark tense—say past tense, so kick is a verb because it has a past tense kicked. Notice that this sorts out our words nicely: kick, know, own, matter can mark tense; construction, refusal, deceit, uncle, and between cannot. Unfortunately, not all languages have tenses to mark. English does, for example, but Mandarin does not.  In any case, we believe that verbs are best defined logically. Recur-sively, in fact. Thus, starting from a finite number of verbal roots, we define verbal stems, and from these verbal stems we define verbal words. These verbal words would then be our verbs. Additionally, we could take some nonverbal words and simply recategorize them as verbal. An appeal to the first strategy can be found in our treatment of Icelandic mutation developed in Chapter 4; an appeal to the latter, in our treatment of Eng-lish recategorization in the same chapter. Verbs combine with nominal clauses to form sentences. Thus, the verb loves may combine with the nominal clauses Romeo and Juliet, for example, to form the sentence Romeo loves Juliet. Or the sentence Juliet loves Romeo. In fact, it may even combine with them to form the confus-ing but grammatical sentences Romeo loves Romeo and Juliet loves Juliet. But how do these combinations come about? Interestingly, they come about incrementally: the verb loves first combines with one nominal clause to form a phrase; this phrase then combines with a second nominal clause to form a sentence. Thus, the sentence Romeo loves Juliet would be formed as follows.  (2) Romeo   loves   Juliet                                                                                              loves Juliet                                                            Romeo loves Juliet But what is the evidence that English sentences are formed incrementally in this way? Plainly, that English treats the verb loves followed by the nominal clause Juliet as a unit. Consider, for example, the ellipsis indi-cated in (3) by means of the strikethrough notation. SYNTAX II: THE VERBAL CLAUSE / 219 (3) Of course Romeo loves Juliet; who wouldn't love Juliet?! How are we to describe this ellipsis? Well, there are two ways about it. At least in principle. The first is to say that each word that has been stricken through in (2) was elided independently of the other. The other is to say that the entire unit love Juliet was elided in one fell swoop. Clearly, the latter is the better alternative of the two, as it requires one determina-tion and one execution rather than two of each. But this alternative re-quires, of course, that we recognize that loves Juliet is, as claimed, a unit. That ellipsis or deletion was involved in (3) in the first place follows from the fact that who wouldn't is not a complete grammatical construc-tion (in the absence of context that completes it). Ellipsis is not the only phenomenon that treats an English verb and its ensuing arguments as a unit. Displacement is another. Consider the sen-tences in (4). (4) a.  Romeo decided he would love Juliet—and he did love Juliet.  b.  Romeo decided he would love Juliet—and love Juliet he did. Of these two sentences, (4a) seems to have the more normal word order; (4b), on the other hand, seems to have displaced the words love Juliet.1 Again, we could describe this displacement in one of two ways. We could say that the words love and Juliet were displaced individually. Or we could say that the single unit love Juliet was displaced as a whole. For the same reasons as before, the latter of these two options is to be pre-ferred. So we should admit that love and Juliet form a unit. Syntactic units are so important that they have a special name. Each unit of a phrase or sentence is said to be a constituent thereof. Notice that words themselves are also considered constituents of the phrases or sentences they form. As is the phrase or sentence formed. So the sentence Romeo loves Juliet has five constituents—one per node of the tree in (2). We will say that verbs are verbal words; that the immediate constitu-ents verbs form are verbal phrases, and that sentences they ultimately form are verbal clauses. We do this to emphasize the key role verbs play in the formation of sentences. We do this also to draw a parallel between the classifications of nominal and verbal expressions:                                                              1 Nothing changes if you think that (4a) was the case that involved the displacement. The argument we are about to make is neutral with respect to this. 220 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS   NOMINAL VERBAL  WORD  1  1'  PHRASE  2  2'  CLAUSE  3  3'  In this table, [1] represents nouns; [1'] represents verbs; [2] has not re-ceived a standard name; [2'] is regarded as a verb phrase; [3] is usually called a nounphrase and [3'] a sentence. 2 Argument Structure: Valence We have seen that loves is a verb that combines with two nominal clauses to form a sentence. But verbs can be found that combine with three nom-inal clauses to form a sentence. As well as verbs that combine with one nominal clause to form a sentence. An example of the former is sends; an example of the latter is dies: (5) Romeo sent Juliet a  message. (6) Juliet died. Notice that we can construct arguments parallel to the ones in (3) and (4) for sends, thus arguing that this verb likewise forms a constituent—a ver-bal phrase, in fact—with the ensuing nominal clauses Juliet and a mes-sage:  Romeo               sent        Juliet            a            message                                                                                             a message                                                          sent Juliet a message                       Romeo sent Juliet a message The case of dies is, in a sense, trivial, as it has to form a constituent with the only nominal clause it combines with. Yet, we will still want to          acknowledge that this verb forms a verbal phrase. This is because we want to capture the generalization that a sentence is the concatenation of SYNTAX II: THE VERBAL CLAUSE / 221 a nominal clause and a verbal phrase—even when the latter is degenerate in the sense that it consists of only one word.          Juliet                          diedWORD                                      diedPHRASE                                                      Juliet died Formally, we do this in PROLOG in several steps. First, we place verbs that combine with one nominal clause in the category [vbl,wrd,int]. This is the category of intransitive verbal words. Two such verbs are contemplated in the present context: rocks and sucks. Next we assign verbs that combine with two nominal clauses to the category [vbl,wrd,trn] of transitive verbal words. These verbs are loves and hates. Finally, we leave verbs that combine with three nominal clauses for the category [vbl,wrd,dtr] of ditransitive verbal words. Here we have only one verb in mind: prefers. english([rocks], [vbl,wrd,int]). english([sucks], [vbl,wrd,int]). english([loves], [vbl,wrd,trn]). english([hates], [vbl,wrd,trn]). english([prefers], [vbl,wrd,dtr]).  Next we formulate the rules that combine these verbs with nominal clauses to form verbal phrases. We will need three such rules (one for each transitivity class of verbs):  english(SO, [vbl,phr]):-  english(SO,[vbl,wrd,int]).  english(SO, [vbl,phr]):-  english(SO1, [vbl,wrd,trn]),  english(SO2, [nom,cls,  _]),  append( SO1,  SO2, SO). 222 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS english(SO,[vbl,phr]):-  english(SO1, [vbl,wrd,dtr]),  english(SO2, [nom,cls,  _]),  english(SO3, [nom,cls,  _]),  append([SO1,  SO2,  [to],  SO3], SO). The nominal classes in these rules have an variable in them so that both kinds of nominal clauses can be involved (proper and common; see Chapter 6). The variable is unnamed because no reference to it is called for. Finally, we state the rule that admits sentences as concatenations of nominal clauses and verbal phrases: english(SO, [vbl,cls]):-  english(SO1, [nom,cls,_]),  english(SO2, [vbl,phr  ]),  append( SO1,  SO2,  SO). Adding these statements to the sound portion of nomi-nalclause.swipl discussed in the preceding Chapter yields va-lence.swipl (see Program 50, Appendix H). We have called it "va-lence" for reasons that will be made clear below. Suffice it to say here that this program generates only the sounds of expressions, not their meaning. The meanings of these sounds will be addressed in the next section. In the meantime, notice that this program can generate tens of thousands of grammatical English expressions—or rather the sounds thereof. To generate them sequentially, all we need to do, as usual, is enter english(SO,_). (and follow up with semicolons). To generate these sounds simultaneous-ly, we would of course enter this instead: findall(SO, english(SO,_), L). Crucially, valence.swipl will not generate any ungrammatical forms (although it will generate decidedly awkward expressions like every parent prefers every parent to every parent). To verify this, enter expres-sions like english([every,male,parent,prefers,marge,to,some, some],_). It will reject it swiftly—in spite of having to wade through tens of thou-sands of well-formed grammatical expressions. SYNTAX II: THE VERBAL CLAUSE / 223 In addition, valence.swipl will assign the sounds it generates the right constituent structure. Thus, the structural description for Homer prefers Lisa to some male child would be essentially as follows. Homer[nom,wrd] prefers[vbl,dtr] Lisa[nom,wrd] some[det,wrd]  male[adj,wrd] child[nom,wrd]     Homer[nom,phr]         Lisa[nom,phr]                           male child[nom,phr]        Homer[nom,cls]         Lisa[nom,cls]                     some male child[nom,cls]                                                                                                                 prefers Lisa to some male child[vbl,phr]                Homer prefers Lisa to some male child[vbl,cls] And it will do it fast and efficiently (see Chapter 10). We note in closing that the foregoing gives but a taste of the valencies verbs may have. Verbs can be found that combine with adjectival clauses (cf. Romeo got very lucky), verbal phrases (cf. Romeo must please Juliet) or with verbal clauses (cf. Juliet believes that Romeo is dead). In addition, verbs may combine with both nominal and adjectival clauses (cf. Romeo considers Juliet pretty). Or with both nominal and verbal clauses (cf. Romeo convinced Juliet that he loved her). In all of these cases, argu-ments may be made that verbs form constituents with these clauses as well. Each one of these valencies induces a particular argument struc-ture—an arboreal template analogous to the one above, into which actual nominal, verbal, and adjectival clauses may be stuck.  3 Argument Structure: Binding Getting the sounds of verbal words, phrases, and clauses was relatively easy; getting their meanings is a bit more involved. This is what syn-tax.swipl intends to do—and for all of the sounds valence.swipl 224 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS generated. As will be seen, this is a small but interesting sample of Eng-lish phrases.2 Assigning meanings to verbal words is a pretty straightforward affair. In fact, we have done this already in Chapter 3, where we interpreted the meaning of a verb as a procedure for the computation of a relation among entities. This is something to be expected if (a) the meaning of an expression is a procedure for the computation of its reference—as we argued in Chapter 3— and if (b) the reference of a verb is, indeed, a rela-tion among entities. syntax.swipl will continue along these lines and propose the following interpretations for the verbal words rocks, sucks, loves, hates, and prefers. Reference :: allsuch(X,rocks(X),Reference). Reference :: allsuch(X,sucks(X),Reference). Reference :: allsuch([X,Y],loves(X,Y),Reference). Reference :: allsuch([X,Y],hates(X,Y),Reference). Reference :: allsuch([X,Y,Z],prefers(X,Y,Z),Reference). It will be noticed that these five interpretations include a predicate all-such that we have not yet defined. We do this now as follows (see also Program 51, Appendix H): allsuch(A,B,C):-   findall(A,B,D),  sort(D,C). In other words, allsuch is just like findall, except that the mem-bers of the lists it generates will be modified according to the predicate sort (which is built-in in PROLOG). To be more specific, the members of the lists generated by allsuch are required to appear in "standard order," and with duplicates removed.3 Here we opt for allsuch rather than findall because we want to prevent lists from differing solely because their members have been repeated or have been reordered.4 Needless to say, the choice of alphabetical order is a pragmatic conven-tion of no theoretical significance. As might be expected, the five interpretations above work in conjunc-tion with a model that specifies who rocks, who sucks, who loves whom, who hates whom, and who prefers whom to whom. simpsons.swipl                                                             2 See Program 52, Appendix H. 3 Standard order is alphabetical order for lower case letters and numerical order for num-bers. See the SWI PROLOG Manual for more details. 4 This is the price we pay for modeling sets, which are inefficient, as lists, which are effi-cient. SYNTAX II: THE VERBAL CLAUSE / 225 is one such model (see Program 43, Appendix H). For, as this model would have it, children rock, parents suck, and everyone loves himself but hates everyone else; moreover, everyone prefers those he loves to those he hates. Thus, if we consult this model (as well as all-such.swipl), and if we use the interpretations above as queries, then we obtain the following as references for rocks, sucks, loves, hates, and prefers (respectively). Reference = [bart, lisa]. Reference = [homer, marge]. Reference = [[bart, bart], [homer, homer], [lisa, lisa], [marge, marge]. Reference = [[bart, homer], [bart, lisa], [bart, marge], [homer, bart], [homer, lisa], [homer, marge], [lisa, bart], [lisa, homer], [lisa, marge], [marge, bart], [marge, homer], [marge, lisa]]. Reference = [[bart, bart, homer], [bart, bart, li-sa], [bart, bart, marge], [homer, homer, bart], [homer, homer, lisa], [homer, homer, marge], [li-sa, lisa, bart], [lisa, lisa, homer], [lisa, lisa, marge], [marge, marge, bart], [marge, marge, homer], [marge, marge, lisa]]. Different models may of course yield different references. Allowing for such variability was, in fact, the purpose of having models in the first place. Semantics should not tell us how the world is; only what expres-sions mean relative to a given view of the world. And each model is one such view of the world. The foregoing illustrates how syntax.swipl interprets intransitive, transitive, and ditransitive verbs. Now we need to look at how it inter-prets the verbal phrases these verbs induce. The case of intransitives is simple, as intransitive verbs mean the same thing as the verbal phrases they constitute (cf. the two occurrences of ME in the following rule of syntax.swipl). english(SO, [vbl, phr], ME):-  english(SO,[vbl,wrd,int], ME). The interpretation of verbal phrases induced by transitive verbs is much more involved. In fact, it might be the most involved point made in this book. Essentially, the idea in syntax.swipl is the following. If the 226 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS meaning of a transitive verb is the procedure followed to respond to (7), and if the meaning of a nominal clause is the procedure invoked by (8), then the meaning of the verbal phrase that results from appending, said verb to said clause, is the procedure triggered in response to (9).5 (7) R1 :: S1 (8) R2 :: S2 (9) R  :: allsuch(X,(S1,S2,member([X,_],R1),   allsuch(Y,member([X,Y],R1),Z),member(Z,R2)),R) Notice that (7) is just the general form of a meaning—at least if we as-sume that R1 is a referential variable and S1 stands for the condition this variable must satisfy (see Chapter 3 for definitions of these terms). And so is, mutatis mutandis, (8). Clause (9), on the other hand, could use some help. So let us break it down into its constituents: (10)  •      R  ::   •       allsuch   •       X   •    R     S1  S2 • • •     member    • allsuch   • member   •      [X,_]   R1     Y    •    Z   Z   R2       member     •            [X,Y]  R1 A top-down reading of the constituent structure in (10) will make plain that the semantic representation of our verbal phrase is of the general form defended in Chapter 3. The referential variable of this representa-tion is R, and the condition this variable must meet is an allsuch clause. This allsuch clause requires that R be the list consisting of all X such that the five conditions in (11) are satisfied:                                                             5 See the third to last rule of syntax.swipl (Program 52, Appendix H). SYNTAX II: THE VERBAL CLAUSE / 227 (11) a. S1  b. S2  c. member([X,_],R1).  d. allsuch(Y,member([X,Y],R1),Z).  e. member(Z,R2). (11a) is the requirement that the referential variable in (7) had to meet. (11b) is the requirement that the referential variable in (8) had to meet. (11c) makes sure that X bears R1 to something (notice that, as indicated in (7) above, R1 is the relation the transitive verb refers to). (11d) makes Z a repository of all the entities that R1 is borne to (notice that, since this is an allsuch condition, Z will be a sorted list. In other words, Z will not contain any duplicate items, and the items it does contain, will appear in alphabetical order). (11e) imposes an additional requirement on Z. It is that Z must be one of the items of R2 (and R2 is, as indicated in (8), the list the nominal clause refers to). Of these five conditions, the last two are the most interesting, as they ensure that the nominal clause serving as the object "binds" the second position of the binary relation conveyed by the transitive verb. For, notice that (11d) takes all the entities that can serve as a second term of the rela-tion expressed by a transitive verb and gathers them in a list Z. And (11e) then requires that this Z be a member of R2 (where R2 is the list of prop-erties the object nominal clause refers to). The meaning of verbal phrases induced by ditransitive verbs is en-tirely analogous. Here the idea is that, if the meaning of a ditransitive verb is associated to (12), and if the meanings of its two objects corre-spond to (13) and (14), then the meaning of the verbal phrase as a whole may be represented as in (15). (12) R1 :: S1 (13) R2 :: S2 (14) R3 :: S3 (15)  R :: allsuch(X,(S1,S2,S3,member([X,_,_],R1),   allsuch(Y,member([X,Y,W],R1),Z),member(Z,R2)   ,allsuch(W,member([X,Y,W],R1),V),member(V,R3   )),R) We leave it as an exercise to the reader to diagram (15) in a way that will make it plain that this monstrosity is, indeed, a semantic representation of the general form defended in Chapter 3. As before, the referential varia-ble of such a representation will be R, and the condition this variable must meet will be an allsuch clause. This allsuch clause will re-228 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS quire that R be the list consisting of all X such that the eight conditions in (16) are satisfied: (16) a. S1  b. S2  c. S3  d. member([X,_,_],R1).  e. allsuch(Y,member([X,Y,W],R1),Z).  f. member(Z,R2).  g. allsuch(W,member([X,Y,W],R1),V).  h. member(V,R3). These conditions mirror those of (11), differing from them only on ac-count of the fact that here we have a verb that takes two objects (plus a subject) rather than a verb that takes only one. Consequently, in this case, the interesting clauses are the last four, as they ensure that the two objects of the verbal phrase "bind" the last two positions of the ternary relation conveyed by a ditransitive verb. To be more specific, (16e) takes all the entities that can serve as a second term of the relation expressed by a ditransitive verb and gathers them in a list Z; (16f) then requires this Z to be a member of R2 (where R2 is the list of properties the direct object refers to). It will be noticed that this is, exactly, what the last two condi-tions of (11) ensured. (16g) and (16h) do the same thing for the entities that can serve as a third term in the relation conveyed by a ditransitive verb and for the list of properties the indirect object refers to. This is achieved by placing appropriate constraints on the variables V and R3 mentioned in (16g) and (16h). To illustrate the effect of these complex interpretations, let us con-sider the meanings syntax.swipl assigns to loves some parent, hates some parent, and prefers some parent to some child. We can generate these meanings by entering (17a), (17b), and (17c), respectively. (17) a. english([loves,some,parent],[vbl,phr],[ME]).  b. english([hates,some,parent],[vbl,phr],[ME]).  c. english([prefers,some,parent,to,some,child],    [vbl,phr],[ME]). Hitting return will invariably yield a monstrosity as the value of ME. En-tering each of these values in turn (and hitting Return) will yield the references of the verbal phrases in question. These references are the dis-armingly simple lists in (18a), (18b), and (18c), respectively. SYNTAX II: THE VERBAL CLAUSE / 229 (18) a. [homer,marge]  b. [bart,homer,lisa,marge]  c. [homer,marge]. Notice that these are, indeed, the correct references for the verbal phrases in (17). At least relative to the simpsons.swipl model we have as-sumed. For recall that, in this model, (a) every parent loves some parent or another (but nobody else does),  (b) everyone hates some parent or another (the parents hate each other and the children hate both), and (c) only the parents prefer some parent to some child (each loves himself and hates every child). This concludes our discussion of the interpretations syntax.swipl assigns to verbal phrases. Thus, all that is left for us to review is the in-terpretations this grammar assigns to verbal clauses (or sentences). But this is a relatively simple matter. For syntax.swipl uses one simple rule to assign interpretations to all sentences. This rule is the following. (19)  english(SO, [vbl,cls], [R :: evaluation((S1, S2, member(R2, R1)), R)]):-   english(SO1, [nom,cls,_], [R1 :: S1]),   english(SO2, [vbl,  phr], [R2 :: S2]),   append( SO1,  SO2,  SO).  Rule (19) interprets sentences that consist of a nominal clause and a ver-bal phrase. Taken in the context of the theory of meaning presented in Chapter 3, (19) asserts that the meaning of a sentence (which consists of a nominal clause and a verbal phrase) is the procedure PROLOG must follow in order to compute the truth value of the conjunction of the three clauses in (20). (20) a. S1  b. S2  c. member(R2, R1).  (20a) asserts that S1 holds—where S1 is the condition that R1 must meet and R1 is the referential variable contributed by the nominal clause of the sentence; (20b) asserts that S2 holds—where S2 is the condition that R2 must meet and R2 is the referential variable contributed by the verbal phrase of the sentence. (20c) simply asserts that R2 is a member of R1.  Once again, the interesting clause here is the last one. For notice that (20c) is just another way of saying that the nominal clause serving as the subject of the sentence must "bind" the first position of the relation con-veyed by the main verb of the verbal phrase (notice that R2 will be the 230 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS list R that, according to (9) and (15) above, gathers all the entities X that may serve as the first term of the relation R1 conveyed by the main verb of the verbal clause). It follows that a sentence is not the assertion that a particular tuple stands in a particular relation. Rather, a sentence is the assertion that the lists contributed by its nominal clauses bind the positions of the relation conveyed by its main verb—where a list binds a position if and only if the sorted list of the entities that may occupy the latter are a member of the former. As explained above, a list is sorted if and only if it satisfies the sort predicate incorporated in the definition of allsuch we have been working with).6 To illustrate the interpretations of sentences provided by syn-tax.swipl, let us consider the meaning this grammar assigns to Homer prefers Lisa to some male child. We can compute this meaning by consulting syntax.swipl and entering english([homer,prefers,lisa,to,some,male,child], _,[ME]). PROLOG will respond with a monstrosity as the value of ME. Instead of reproducing that monstrosity here, we will avert our gaze from it and use it instead as a new query to syntax.swipl. This time PROLOG will respond with the reference of this sentence relative to the Simpsons model we have been assuming. It will be falsity. This value is, of course, desired, as in this model Homer prefers no one but himself to anybody else. To everybody else, in fact.7 This exercise illustrates how syntax.swipl models the listening modality of language—the path that takes us from sound to meaning. To illustrate how this program models the speaking modality of language— the path from meaning to sound— we may enter english(SO, _,[µ]). where µ stands for the monstrous value of ME we computed above.8 PROLOG will respond swiftly with                                                             6 Notice that binding refers here only to the relation that a (generalized) quantifier bears to a variable (in a particular argument position), not to the relation of coreference that holds between two nominal clauses. In other words, we use the term binding as it is understood in Logic rather than in Linguistics. 7 We should point out that we are not stipulating monstrosities here and above; we are only predicting them. Consequently, the complexity of these predicted meanings should not count against us. 8 Readers should not forget that µ must be enclosed in square brackets in this query (mean-ings are lists—even when they consist, as they do here, of only one member). SYNTAX II: THE VERBAL CLAUSE / 231 SO = [homer,prefers,lisa,to,some,male,child] Thus we return, as desired, to the sound we started from (see above). En-tering a semicolon will return false, which is PROLOG's way of telling us that no other sound licensed by the grammar will convey µ—or that Homer prefers Lisa to some male child has no synonyms among these sounds. In other words, what this sentence says, nothing else can. To make yourself comfortable with syntax.swipl, you may enter other sentential sounds and find their meanings. Take for example english([every,parent,hates,some,child], _,[ME]). PROLOG responds with another monstrosity which returns truth when used as a query. This, again, is the correct truth value for this sentence and this model, as every parent hates every child—and, since the model allows for children, every parent will indeed hate some child. At least in the Simpsons model we have assumed.  Readers who actually computed the meaning of Every parent hates some child will have noticed that PROLOG did not only return truth as the value of the referential variable of the sentence. It also returned val-ues for the referential variables of the subject and the predicate of this sentence (the nominal clause and the verbal phrase constituting it). These values were (21) and (22) respectively.  (21) [[bart,homer,lisa,marge],[bart,homer,marge],  [homer,lisa,marge],[homer,marge]] (22) [bart,homer,lisa,marge] Interestingly, this provides the immediate reason why the sentence is true: (22) is a member of (21)—a fact that may well be of future use. Notice that syntax.swipl works well with definite articles. Con-sider, for example, the contrast between (23a) and (23b). (23) a.  The female parent loves Marge.  b.  The parent loves Marge. The interpretation syntax.swipl assigns to (23a) can be gathered by entering english([the,female,parent,loves,marge], _, [ME]). Using that meaning as a query leads to truth. This is again as expected, since Marge is the female parent, and everyone loves himself/herself in our model. As to the meaning syntax.swipl assigns to (23b), that can be obtained by entering 232 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS  english([the,parent,loves,marge], _, [ME]). And using the meaning obtained as a query yields falsity instead. This is because there is more than one parent in our Simpsons model and, according to the Russellian theory of the definite article built into syn-tax.swipl, sentences with definite articles that fail the presupposition of uniqueness are false.9 4 The Computational Complexity of Argument Struc-ture syntax.swipl generates tens of thousands of grammatical pairings of sounds and meanings. And does this without generating any ungrammati-cal expressions. In addition, it describes the constituent structure of the sounds it pairs to meanings. And it uses this constituent structure to in-terpret these sounds compositionally. Moreover, for all we know, it can do all of this efficiently. And this holds regardless of whether syn-tax.swipl is used to synthesize pairings of sound and meaning or to analyze them. Or whether this program is used to model a speaker or to model a listener. We discuss all the above in Chapter 10 and in Appendix G. 5 Historical Note The essentials of the rules describing the sounds of verbal clauses has been around since Chomsky (1957)—if not earlier. The only exceptions to this are the idea that the categories phrases belong to are "projections" of the categories words belong to (Jackendoff 1977), the idea that sen-tences are projections of verbs (Gazdar et al. 1985), and the syncate-gorematic introduction of prepositions in verbal phrases (see, for example, the way in which the preposition to is generated in phrases of the form prefers X to Y).  The idea that the number and nature of the arguments of a verb—what we have called its valence—is an idiosyncrasy of the verb was advanced in Chomsky (1965), where verbs were "subcategorized" according to the arguments they could make—a subcategorization that had to be explicitly stipulated in the lexicon. Such stipulations were explicitly distinguished from cooccurrence restrictions—semantic restrictions that the verb im-posed on its arguments (the fact that the object of frightens has to be an-imate, for example, is a coocurrence restriction; the fact that there has to                                                             9 As opposed to nonreferential, as in the Strawsonian theory of definite article. See the preceding chapter for details. SYNTAX II: THE VERBAL CLAUSE / 233 be an object and that it has to be a nominal clause is a fact of subcatego-rization). In this chapter we have dealt with subcategorizations with the same machinery of conditional and unconditional statements used for grammars in general. Whether subcategorization follows from cooccur-rence restrictions is a fundamental question that has not been resolved. A comprehensive study of English subcategorization can be found in Levin (1993). The essentials of binding—understood as the relation between a nom-inal clause and one of the positions of a verbal word—derive from the work on generalized quantifiers initiated in Montague (1970). This work has been discussed in Chapter 6. 6 Exercises 1. Argue that sends forms a constituent with Juliet and a message in the sentence Romeo sends Juliet a message. 2. As reflected in syntax.swipl, English tends to place subjects before verbs and verbs before objects, thus exhibiting canonical SVO word order. Thai, Swahili, and Hausa are like English in this regard. But Turkish, Japanese, and Persian are not. Like English, these languages place their subjects before their objects; but unlike English, they tend to place their objects before their verbs, thus producing a canonical SOV word order. But it has one important shortcoming as well: it claims that the sentence Homer prefers Lisa to some male child has, essentially, the following constituent structure.      Homer[nom,wrd] prefers[vbl,dtr] child[nom,wrd] male[adj,wrd] some[det,wrd] Lisa[nom,wrd] Homer[nom,phr] Lisa[nom,phr] male child[nom,phr]   Homer[nom,cls] Lisa[nom,cls] some male child[nom,cls] prefers Lisa to some male child[vbl,phr] Homer prefers Lisa to some male child[vbl,cls] 238 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS But this structure seems wrong in one important respect. Arguably, the word to found in it forms a constituent with the ensuing nominal clause. Consider in this regard the sentence in (1). (1)  It is to some male child, that Homer prefers Lisa. What this sentence shows is that to some male child can be displaced, as a unit, towards the front of the sentence. But, in order to achieve this dis-placement we must first consider to some male child as a unit, i.e. as a constituent.1 To consider our string of words a constituent we proceed in two steps. First we introduce the word to categorematically as a dative preposi-tion—or rather as a dative prepositional word: (2) english([to],[prp,wrd,dat]). Next we allow ourselves to append this preposition to a nominal clause in order to produce a dative nominal clause—or [nom,cls,dat]:  (3) english(SO,[nom,cls,dat]):-  english(SO1,[prp,wrd,dat]),  english(SO2,[nom,cls]),  append(SO1,SO2,SO). This allows us to generate the following structure.     to[prp,wrd,dat]           some[det,wrd]     male[adj,wrd]    child[nom,wrd]                                                                male child[nom,phr]                                               some male child[nom,cls]                         to some male child[nom,cls,dat]  And this structure can then be used to repair the offending structure above. We would of course have to rewrite the relevant rule for verbal phrases so                                                             1 See Chapter 7 for a discussion of these arguments from displacement. SYNTAX III: THE PREPOSITIONAL CLAUSE / 239 that it would involve a dative nominal clause—and would not introduce to syncategorematically. But all this can be done straightforwardly: (4) english(SO,[vbl,phr]):-  english(SO1,[vbl,wrd,dtr]),  english(SO2,[nom,cls]),  english(SO3,[nom,cls,dat]),  append([SO1,SO2,SO3],SO). Semantically, there wouldn't be much to report either. The dative nominal clause should have the same meaning as the plain nominal clause it con-tains. And this nominal would bind the third position of the relation con-veyed by prefers.2 The preceding solution works nicely for the problem at hand. Unfor-tunately, it will not generalize. And there is plenty to generalize over. For many are the verbs that call for specific prepositions on the nominal clauses they combine with. Thus, put calls for nominal clauses headed by in; compare requires nominal clauses introduced by with; boast demands nominal clauses preceded by about; arrive requires nominal clauses headed by at. And insist insists on prepositional phrases introduced by on: (5) a.  Homer prefers Lisa to some male child.  b.  Please put the check in the bank.  c.  You are comparing apples with oranges.  d.  The technicians were boasting about their accomplishments.  e.  The plane will arrive at Gate 33.  f.   She insists on being addressed as 'Your Honor'. As a matter of fact, the phenomenon is so widespread that it has merited a special name: government. Thus, grammarians say that a verb, an adjec-tive, or a preposition governs a particular form of a nominal clause if and only if the verb, adjective, or preposition requires that one of its argu-ments be a nominal clause of that very form. The verb prefers is therefore said to govern a dative, while the verb put, would be said to govern a locative. Seemingly capricious, government eases the identification of the arguments of a verb. So much so, that it allows these arguments to appear in any order in a sentence—thus permitting speakers to use the order of arguments in a sentence to highlight some arguments and downplay oth-ers. Or to mention known arguments before novel ones. Or to leave for                                                             2 We have implemented all this in syntax.ii.swipl, where the meaning of the dative preposition is a procedure for the computation of a binary relation to, and the meaning of the dative nominal clause is the same as that of the plain nominal clause it contains. See Program 53, Appendix H. 240 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS last those arguments that are hard to process (and thus place a heavy bur-den on the online processing of a sentence). To account for government in general we will proceed in four steps. First, we expand our category of prepositional words to all the items in (6). (6) about, above, across, after, against, around, at, before, behind, below, beneath, beside, between, beyond, by, down, during, except, for, from, in, inside, into, like, near, of, off, on, out, outside, over, since, through, throughout, to, toward, under, until, up, upon, with, without. This can be done with the rules in (2'), where numerals are just conven-ient identifiers of prepositions (or shorthand for a constellation of gram-matical properties that the identified preposition—and only the identified preposition—will satisfy). (2') english([about  ], [prp,wrd,001]).  english([above  ], [prp,wrd,002]).  ... ... ... ... ... ... ... ... ..  english([without], [prp,wrd,048]). Second, we need to be able to append prepositional words to regular nominal clauses in order to produce oblique nominal clauses. This can be done, once and for all, with a single, underspecified rule, that requires every oblique nominal clause to be of the same subcategory as the prepo-sition it contains (see the two occurrences of the single variable SUBCAT in (3') below). A nominal clause is oblique if and only if it is generated by (3').  (3') english(SO,[nom,cls,SUBCAT]):-  english(SO1,[prp,wrd,SUBCAT]),  english(SO2,[nom,cls]),  append(SO1,SO2,SO).  Third, we subcategorize verbs according to the oblique nominal clauses they call for. This can be done by rules like the following where, as with prepositions, numerals are convenient identifiers of subcategories (or abbreviations for conjunctions of properties that the verbs of the identi-fied subcategory—and only the verbs of the identified subcategory—will satisfy).  (7) english([arrives ], [vbl,wrd,trn,001]).  english([boasts  ], [vbl,wrd,trn,002]). SYNTAX III: THE PREPOSITIONAL CLAUSE / 241  english([compares], [vbl,wrd,dtr,003]).  english([insists ], [vbl,wrd,trn,004]).  english([prefers ], [vbl,wrd,dtr,005]).  english([puts    ], [vbl,wrd,dtr,006]). Fourth and last, we appeal to rules that make sure that the subcategories of verbs and the subcategories of oblique nominal clauses match as de-sired. There will be one rule per verbal subcategory. For the subcategory of prefers, the rule would be as follows (as per the rules above, prefers is the ditransitive verb of the fifth subcategory and to is the preposition of the forty-first). (4') english(SO,[vbl,phr]):-  english(SO1,[vbl,wrd,dtr,005]),  english(SO2,[nom,cls]),  english(SO3,[nom,cls,041]),  append([SO1,SO2,SO3],SO). Taken together, the rules in (2'), (3'), (4') and (7) will account for the core facts of verbal government in English.3 And similar solutions will be available for the government induced by adjectives and prepositions. In closing, we should point out that government does not always involve nominal clauses introduced by prepositions; sometimes it involves nomi-nal clauses whose head nouns bear specific inflections instead. In fact, these inflections—better known as cases—represent the most common forms of government in the languages of the world. An example of such forms will be introduced in Exercise 2 below.  2 Prepositional Phrases: Verbal Constructions The prepositions mentioned in the preceding section were semantically irrelevant in the sense that they did not contribute to the meaning of the sentences that contained them. It is not that they were meaningless, but rather that the meanings they had were of no consequence for the phrases that contained them. But prepositions can make substantial contributions to the meanings of the phrases they partake of. In fact, they often do. Take for example (8).                                                              3 Consult government.swipl (Program 54, Appendix H) to see these rules in action. 242 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS (8) I made it to London on Continental Airlines at noon with all my  luggage.  (8) contains four prepositions, all of which make significant contributions to the meaning of a sentence regarding a flight arrival of mine. Thus, to relates this arrival to a place; on to a carrier, at to a time, and with to a "concomitance". More generally, all of these prepositions express rela-tions between an event—a flight arrival of mine—and something else, where that something else is expressed by the nominal clause introduced by the preposition.  It follows that the prepositions in (8) make significant contributions to the sentence that contains them. In fact, the contributions made by the-se prepositions are very much like the ones made by transitive verbs (every one of these prepositions names a binary relation and combines with a nominal clause that serves as its second argument). Beyond this, the phrase that arises from the combination of a preposition and a nomi-nal clause refers to the set of entities that bear the relation named by the preposition to the item named by the nominal clause. Thus, to London refers to the set of entities that relate to London; on United Airlines refers to the set of entities that are on Continental Airlines; at noon refers to the set of entities at noon, and with all my luggage refers to the set of entities that accompany all my luggage (it goes without saying that included among these entities are actual events—like flight arrivals of mine that took place in the past). To capture all of these facts we will claim that prepositions may combine with ensuing nominals as dictated by (9), where [prp,phr] is a category that we are introducing here for the first time—the category of so called prepositional phrases. (9) english(SO,[prp,phr], [R :: allsuch(X,(S1,S2,  member([X,_],R1), allsuch(Y,member([X,Y],R1),  Z),member(Z,R2)),R)]):-    english(SO1, [prp,wrd], [R1 :: S1]),   english(SO2, [nom,cls], [R2 :: S2]),   append(SO1,   SO2, SO).  SYNTAX III: THE PREPOSITIONAL CLAUSE / 243 The rule in (9) should be compared with the rule for the creation of tran-sitive verbal phrases, which is repeated here as (9').  (9') english(SO,[vbl,phr], [R :: allsuch(X,(S1,S2,  member([X,_],R1), allsuch(Y,member([X,Y],R1),  Z),member(Z,R2)),R)]):-    english(SO1, [vbl,wrd,trn], [R1 :: S1]),   english(SO2, [nom,cls    ], [R2 :: S2]),   append(SO1,   SO2, SO).  Notice that we have boldfaced in (9') all of the differences between (9) and (9'). In a nutshell, prepositional phrases would be transitive verbal phrases if they involved transitive verbs rather than prepositions. So we propose prepositional phrases in addition to oblique nominal clauses. These two phrases are similar in that they consist of a preposi-tion followed by a nominal clause. Yet, the two phrases differ in four important respects. First, the preposition is semantically relevant in one case but irrelevant in the other. Second, the phrase that results is preposi-tional in one case but nominal in the other. Third, the nominal clause binds one of the arguments of the relation contributed by the preposition in one case but not in the other. And, fourth, the reference of one of them is a property (what PROLOG regards as a list), while the reference of the other is a property of properties (what PROLOG regards as a list of lists) instead. This concludes our description of the internal structure of a preposi-tional phrase. Now we need to turn to its external structure. In other words, we need to address the way in which prepositional phrases com-bine with other phrases. Let's set aside for the moment the semantics of such combinations and focus only on the sounds involved therein. If we do, all we need to say, at this point, is that prepositional phrases combine with verbal phrases to form verbal phrases as indicated by (10).  (10) english(SO,[vbl,phr]):-  english(SO1,[vbl,phr]),  english(SO2,[prp,phr]),  append(SO1,SO2,SO). 244 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS For this would mean that the constituent structure of (8) would be as fol-lows. made[vbl,trn] to[prp,wrd]  on[prp,wrd] at[prp,wrd]  with[prp,wrd]    it[nom,cls]    London[nom,cls]   C.A.[nom,cls]      noon[nom,cls]      …[nom,cls]    made it[vbl,phr]  to London[prp,phr]   on C.A.[prp,phr]  at noon[prp,phr]   w…[prp,phr]        made it to London[vbl,phr]         made it to London on Continental Airlines[vbl,phr]              made it to London on Continental Airlines at noon[vbl,phr]   made it to London on Continental Air. at noon with all my luggage[vbl,phr] And evidence for this constituent structure—and hence for the rule in (10)—comes from the following sentences. (11) I made it to London on Continental Airlines at noon with all my lug-gage; you managed to do this too, a. but only with your carry-on luggage [do this = make it to Lon-don on Continental Airlines at noon]. b. but only at midnight and only with your carry-on luggage [do this = make it to London on Continental Airlines]. c. but only on Subcontinental Airlines, only at midnight, and only with your carry-on luggage [do this = make it to London]. d. but only to New York, only on Subcontinental Airlines, only at midnight, and only with your carry-on luggage [do this = make it]. What these sentences show is that (a) make it, (b) make it to London, (c) make it to London on Continental Airlines, and (d) make it to London on Continental Airlines at noon are all constituents of (8). And the fact that the continuations (a)-(d) are all moreover optional shows that do this could also stand for (e) make it to London on Continental Airlines at SYNTAX III: THE PREPOSITIONAL CLAUSE / 245 noon with all your luggage—which means that this phrase is a constitu-ent of (8) as well. But this, of course, is what is shown in the preceding tree (and is required by (10)). It should be clear that the rule in (10) is recursive in the sense that it uses verbal phrases to make verbal phrases. In so doing, it embeds verbal phrases into verbal phrases ad infinitum. And the multiple stacking of prepositional phrases in (8) suggests that this is a welcome result. It is true that some of these phrases will be questionable (cf. made it to Lon-don to London to London) if not downright incoherent (cf. made it to London to New York). Following received wisdom, we will claim such verbal phrases are all grammatical but awkward. For us, this means that the grammar will generate these phrases fully (i.e. both in sound and in meaning), but that these phrases will not be able to partake of true state-ments given realistic models of arrivals. In fact, it should be predictable in these models that these phrases will not be able to partake of true statements. And this predictability is what would account for their awk-wardness. We will have more to say about the unbounded stacking of preposi-tional phrases (see next section). But before we do, let us take a look at the sentence in (12). (12)  We decided on the boat. As it turns out, (12) is ambiguous. On one reading it tells us what was chosen (the boat), but not where that choice was made; on the other read-ing, it tells us where a choice was made (on the boat), but not what that choice was. Interestingly, we may account for this ambiguity by claiming that the first reading involves an oblique nominal phrase while the second involves a prepositional phrase. To be more specific, the two readings of (12) arise as indicated in (13a) and (13b), respectively.   246 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS  (13) a. decided[vbl,wrd]       on[prp,wrd,034]  the boat[nom,cls]                                          on the boat[nom,cls,034]                decided on the boat[vbl,phr]    b. decided[vbl,wrd]      on[prp,wrd]        the boat[nom,cls]      decided[vbl,phr]          on the boat[prp,phr]                                          decided on the boat[vbl,phr]    Notice that on the boat is a nominal clause in (13a). Consequently, it may bind the second argument of the verb decided in that structure. At the same time, on the boat is a prepositional phrase in (13b). As such, it re-fers to the set of entities that are on the boat. Consequently, it may take the subject of the verb decided in (13b) and make it "be" one of those entities (intuitively, on the boat may require us to be on the boat; formal-ly, the prepositional phrase may take the reference of the subject of the sentence and use it to bind the first argument of the binary relation con-veyed by the preposition it contains).  Moreover, the verb decided is an entire verbal phrase in (13b). This was required by the rule in (10), which appends prepositional phrases to verbal phrases, and not to verbal words. Interestingly, the verb decided is not, in and of itself, a verbal phrase in (13a); it is only a verbal word there. This would be in turn required by a rule like (4'), which combines verbal words—not phrases—with oblique nominal clauses. This difference is supported by the fact that (14) is not ambiguous; it has only the preposi-tional reading (13b). (14)  I decided on the boat and you did so on the bus. SYNTAX III: THE PREPOSITIONAL CLAUSE / 247 This can be explained because do so always stands for a full verbal phrase. In (14), this verbal phrase can only be decided[vbl,phr]—at least if we were right in claiming that decided is a verbal phrase in the structure for the prepositional reading (13b), and only in the structure for the prep-ositional reading (13b), not in the structure for the nominal reading (13a). It follows that (10) handles very well the way in which the sounds of prepositional phrases combine with the sounds of verbal phrases to form the sounds of new verbal phrases. But what should we say about the way in which the meanings of these phrases combine? Here we must address a number of thorny issues. For we have seen that the prepositions in (8) relate an event (a flight arrival) with something else (a destination, a car-rier, and a concomitance). But what is an event? And how does an event relate to anything? To approach these issues, let us say that an event is what a PROLOG fact conveys. All of the statements in (15) are PROLOG facts. They may all, therefore, convey events.  (15) a. made_it(i).  b. to(made_it(i),london).  c. on(to(made_it(i),london),continentalairlines).  d. at(on(to(made_it(i),london),continentalair-    lines),noon). Although this does not tell us what events actually are, this allows us to talk about them in ways which are explicit and coherent—thus allow-ing us to state them, affirm them, refute them, and relate them to each other. Much as when we say that a property is a PROLOG list—or rather what a PROLOG list conveys. Facts like the ones in (15) are what models may contain. And models which contain (15d) will render (8) true. But how will the truth value of (8) be actually computed? With a procedure for the computation of its reference—i.e. with its meaning. And how would its meaning be com-puted? Compositionally, as always, from the meanings of its parts. Take for instance the meaning of made it to London. If it weren't for quantified noun phrases, the meaning of the idiom made it and the prepositional phrase to London would be as follows. R1 :: allsuch(X1, made_it(X1), R1). R2 :: allsuch(X2, to(X2, london), R2). Thus, the reference of made it would be the set of entities that made it, and the reference of to London would be the set of entities—events in-cluded—that related to London. Consequently, the meaning of made it to London would be  248 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS R3 :: allsuch(X3,to(made_it(X3),london), R3). In other words, the meaning of made it to London would be a procedure for the computation of the set of entities that in fact made it to London. And the meaning of made it to London on Continental Airlines at noon would be R4 :: allsuch(X4, at(on(to(made_it(X4), london), ca), noon), R4). Things get much more involved when quantified nominal clauses are brought in. Essentially, what needs to happen is that we use the meanings of the prepositional phrases and the verbal phrases in (8) to form (a pro-cedure for computing) the (sorted) list of entities which made it to Lon-don on Continental Airlines at noon with all my luggage. The sentence in (8) would then assert that this list is among the lists the subject of (8) refers to. 3 Prepositional Phrases: Nominal Constructions We have seen that prepositional phrases can combine recursively with verbal phrases to form verbal phrases; now we will see that prepositional phrases may also combine recursively with nominal phrases to form nominal phrases. To figure out how this comes about, consider the fol-lowing nominal clause. (16) a child at home by himself for a week in 2011 This nominal clause contains four stacked prepositional phrases. They are at home, by himself, for a week, and in 2011. To account for these con-structions we will propose the rule in (17). This rule omits from consid-eration all information about how the meaning of whole is derived from the meanings of the parts.  (17) english(SO,[nom,phr]):-  english(SO1,[nom,phr]),  english(SO2,[prp,phr]),  append(SO1,SO2,SO).  Notice that this rule emulates the one in (10) above. Indeed it is just like (10) except that it involves nominal phrases rather than verbal. Be that as it may, the rule in (17) assigns the nominal in (16) the following structure.  SYNTAX III: THE PREPOSITIONAL CLAUSE / 249 (18) child[nom,wrd]          at[prp,wrd]   by[prp,wrd               for[prp,wrd]         in[prp,wrd]               home[nom,cls]        himself[nom,cls] a week[nom,cls] 2011[nom,cls]    child[nom,phr]  at home[prp,phr] by himself[prp,phr]    for a week[prp,phr]  in 2011[prp,phr]         child at home[nom,phr]                 child at home by himself[nom,phr]                            child at home by himself for a week[nom,phr]                                        child at home by himself for a week in 2011[nom,phr]  Evidence for this constituent structure comes from the nominal clauses in (19). For, in order for one to stand for something, that something must be a constituent. (19) a child at home by himself for a week in 2011, and another one  a. … in 2012 [one = child at home by himself for a week].  b. …  for a month in 2012 [one = child at home by himself].  c. … with a sibling for a month in 2012 [one = child at home].  d. … at a friend's with a sibling for a month in 2012 [one = child]. This argument is of course the same as the one we made with do this in (11). In either case, the argument is that an anaphor—an expression that stands for another one, like do this or one—can only refer back to a con-250 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS stituent (the expressions that anaphors refer back to are said to be their antecedents). In any event, the rule in (16) will admit, like the one in (10), an infi-nite number of phrases. Some of them will be pointlessly repetitive, de-cidedly awkward, or even incoherent. It will admit them as grammatical nonetheless, as repetitiveness, awkwardness, or incoherence have no bearing on grammaticality proper.  For another example of the generative power of (16), consider the clause a walk on the park by the house. The structure (16) assigns this clause is (20). (20)                                                     by[prp,wrd] the[det,wrd]   house[nom,wrd]                   house[nom,phr]            park[nom,wrd]          the house[nom,cls]      park[nom,phr]         by the house[prp,phr]   walk[nom,wrd]                the[det,wrd]     park by the house[nom,phr]                      on[prp,wrd]      the park by the house[nom,cls]   walk[nom,phr] on the park by the house[prp,phr]     walk on the park by the house[prp,phr]  It should be clear that (20) could grow further. Unboundedly so. And this is something that the recursiveness of (16) will allow as well. Notice that the structure in (18) was left-branching—starting from the phrase as a whole (the one at the root of the tree), the left and only the left branches branch. This contrasts with (20), which is a right-branching structure—starting from the phrase as a whole, the right and only the right branch branches. But syntactic structures, like their morphological counterparts, do not have to be exclusively left-branching or exclusively SYNTAX III: THE PREPOSITIONAL CLAUSE / 251 right-branching. Some could branch to the left and others to the right. And some may even do both! Another difference between (18) and (20) is that, in the first case, the rule in (16) applies directly to its own output—it combines a nominal phrase with a prepositional phrase to yield a nominal phrase which it immediately combines with a prepositional phrase to yield a new nominal phrase. In (20), on the other hand, the rule in (16) applies indirectly to its own output—it combines a nominal phrase (park) with a prepositional phrase (by the house) in order to form a nominal phrase (park by the house) which must form a prepositional phrase (on the park by the house) before it is combined with a second nominal phrase (walk) to form a third (walk on the park by the house). In either case, the end result is the same: unbounded stacking of prepositional phrases is attained thanks to recur-siveness. We close this section with another ambiguity, namely the one in (21). (21) We found the guy with the map. On one reading of (21), we found the guy by means of the map (what guy that was, the sentence does not say); in the other reading, the guy we found was the one that had the map (how we found him, the sentence does not say). We can assign these two readings to (21); the first with the rule in (10) and the second with the rule in (16). The structures these rules induce are given in (22a) and (22b), respectively. (22) a.    found[vbl,wrd]  the[det.wrd]  guy[nom,phr]  with the map[prp,phr]                                                             the guy[nom,cls]        found the guy[vbl,phr]                                           found the guy with the map[vbl,phr]       252 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS b.     found[vbl,wrd]  the[det,wrd]  guy[nom,phr]  with the map[prp,phr]                                                             guy with the map[nom,phr]                 the guy with the map[nom,cls]                     found the guy with the map[vbl,phr]    The structures in (22) differ as to where the prepositional phrase with the map attaches. In (22a) it attaches to a verbal phrase (as allowed for by the rule in (10)). As such, it modifies the event conveyed by that verbal phrase. With the map thus describes the manner of the finding (by men-tioning the map as a concomitance or an instrument). In (22b), on the other hand, the prepositional phrase forms a constituent with the nominal phrase guy (as allowed for by the rule in (16)). In so doing, the preposi-tional phrase modifies this nominal phrase—or rather identifies its refer-ence uniquely, as required by the definite article. It does this by stating that the map is a concomitance of the guy.4 4 More on Oblique Nominal Clauses We have seen that prepositional phrases enter into construction with both nominal and verbal phrases. Oblique nominal clauses, on the other hand, have only combined with verbal phrases. This will be revised in the pre-sent section, where oblique nominal clauses will be allowed to combine with nominal phrases as well. To begin, suppose we adopted the rules in (23).  (23) english([parent], [nom,wrd,rel]).  english([child ], [nom,wrd,rel]).                                                              4 A more precise account of how these prepositional phrases contribute to the meanings of the phrases they partake of would require us to be explicit about the semantic component of the rules in (10) and (16)—something we have purposefully avoided in this introductory exposition.   SYNTAX III: THE PREPOSITIONAL CLAUSE / 253 These rules introduce relational nouns into English (a noun is rela-tional if it refers to a relation, that is a list of lists all of which are of the same length). Suppose next we introduced to English a genitive preposi-tion of along the lines of the dative preposition we introduced in (2) above. (24) english([of],[prp,wrd,gen]). Next we re-adopt the rule for the creation of oblique nominal clauses formulated in (3') above and repeated here as (25). (25) english(SO,[nom,cls,SUBCAT]):-  english(SO1,[prp,wrd,SUBCAT]),  english(SO2,[nom,cls]),  append(SO1,SO2,SO).  This rule will of course apply to the genitive preposition introduced by (24) and form a genitive nominal clause (as opposed to a genitive prepo-sitional phrase). Finally, we allow for the relational nouns in (23) to combine with the genitive nominal clauses of (25) in order to form nomi-nal phrases.  (26) english(SO,[nom,phr]):-  english(SO1,[nom,wrd,rel]),  english(SO2,[nom,cls,gen]),  append(SO1,SO2,SO). Thus we would be able to assign (27) to parent of every kid (assuming, of course, that we can generate plain nominal clauses like every kid).5 (27) parent[nom,wrd,rel]     of[prp,wrd,gen]      every kid[nom,cls]                                                       of every kid[nom,cls,gen]                                parent of every kid[nom,phr]                                                              5 Notice that the ungrammaticality of the following sentence shows that genitive nominal clauses like of every child attach to nominal words rather than nominal phrases: *I invited the parent of every kid, not just the one of every boy. 254 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS If we are interested in how the meanings of these phrases are computed from the meanings of their constituents, then we need to add meanings to all of the rules above. We can do this as follows. (23') english([parent], [nom,wrd,rel],    [R :: allsuch([X,Y],parent(X,Y), R)]).  english([child], [nom,wrd,rel],    [R :: allsuch([X,Y], child(X,Y), R)]). (24') english([of],[prp,wrd,gen],    [R :: allsuch([X,Y], of(X,Y), R)]). (25') english(SO, [nom,cls,SUBCAT], ME):-   english(SO1,[prp,wrd,SUBCAT], _),   english(SO2,[nom,cls], ME),   append(SO1,SO2,SO). (26') english(SO, [nom,phr], [R ::   allsuch(X,(S1,S2,member([X,_],R1), allsuch  (Y,member([X,Y],R1),Z),member(Z,R2)),R)])  :-   english(SO1, [nom,wrd,rel], [R1 :: S1]),   english(SO2, [nom,cls,gen], [R2 :: S2]),   append(SO1, SO2, SO). (23') and (24') are straightforward as they interpret relational nouns and prepositions as relations. (25') assigns the same meaning to genitive nom-inal clauses as the plain nominal clauses they contain. This, of course, is what we expect from oblique nominal clauses in general; they are just governed nominal clauses. The computation of meaning in (26') is a sub-stantially more involved. But it is essentially the same as the one per-formed for ditransitive verbal clauses in syntax.ii.swipl (see Pro-gram 53 in Appendix H). In fact, the only difference between the two rules stems from the fact that one has a binary relational noun where the other has a ditransitive verb. For, since the relational noun is binary, it will take two arguments, and since the ditransitive verb is ternary, it will take three. To see these rules in action, take a look at nominalgovern-ment.swipl (Program 55, Appendix H), where we have added a pro-cedure to generate plain nominal clauses like every kid. Both in sound and in meaning. Notice that this program also contains a model for the interpretation of the nominal expressions it generates. Relative to the model it contains, nominalgovernement.swipl assigns references as shown in (28).  SYNTAX III: THE PREPOSITIONAL CLAUSE / 255 (28) | parent of every kid |  = [homer, marge]  | child of every kid |  = [ ]  | parent of some kid |  = [homer, marge]  | child of some kid |  = [ ] Since the model built into in this program is consistent with the Simpsons model we have been working with, it can be verified that these references are all as desired. Interestingly, parent of no kid is interpreted as the emp-ty list (i.e. as the set of parents who are parents of no kids). And parent of no parent is interpreted as [homer,marge] (i.e. as the set of parents who are parents of no parent). The interpretation of nominal clauses with definite articles is as expected: (29) | parent of the kid |  =  [ ]  | parent of the female kid |  =  [homer, marge] 5 Oblique Nominal Clauses vs. Prepositional Phrases In this chapter we have introduced two new kinds of syntactic categories: oblique nominal clauses (ONs) and prepositional phrases (PPs). Although both consist of a preposition followed by a nominal clause, important differences exist between them. As of now, these differences pertain to the following questions. Is the preposition semantically relevant? Is the nominal clause one of the arguments of the preposition? What is the category of the resulting phrase? What is the category of the resulting reference? An ON is a nominal category [nom | _] whose preposition is not se-mantically relevant, whose second position is not bound by the nominal clause it contains, and whose reference is to a set of properties. A PP, on the other hand, is a prepositional category [prp | _] whose preposi-tion is semantically relevant, whose second position is bound by the nom-inal clause it contains, and whose reference is to a property. In addition to these four differences, there might seem to be a fifth: in all the cases we have seen thus far, oblique nominal clauses combined with words while prepositional phrases combined with phrases. But does this difference generalize beyond the cases we have seen thus far? Con-sider the phrases underlined in (30). (30) The horse is before the cart / behind the cart / on the cart / by the         cart / … 256 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS Every one of the prepositions in (30) makes a specific contribution to the meaning of the sentence it partakes of (it names a specific relation be-tween the horse and the cart). The prepositions moreover alternate with each other. Consequently, they cannot be required (or governed) by the verb is. In addition, the phrase formed by the preposition and the ensuing nominal refers not to the cart, but rather to a property to be predicated of the horse—the property of being before the cart, for example. Or being behind it. In short, the phrases alternating in (30) seem to be prepositional phrases rather than oblique nominal clauses. Yet, as far as we can deter-mine, they combine with the word is, not with any phrase is. So the struc-ture seems to be as follows.   the horse[nom,cls]   is[vbl,wrd]   before[prp,wrd]   the cart[nom,cls]                                                                    before the cart[prp,phr]                                                is before the cart[vbl,phr]                       the horse is before the cart[vbl,cls]  This means that we have found a case of a PP combining with a word rather than with a phrase. This construction is, it must be admitted, somewhat peculiar. It consists of a form of the verb to be that has no specific content to speak of. All it does is facilitate the combination of the subject with the prepositional phrase, convey present tense (cf. the horse was before the cart), and exhibit agreement with the subject (cf. the horses are before the cart). These constructions are therefore called cop-ulative, as their verbs are mere copulas or connectives. We hasten to add that not all occurrences of the verb to be are copulative. There is also existential be (cf. There is a god) and equational be (cf. The Nile is the longest river in the world). SYNTAX III: THE PREPOSITIONAL CLAUSE / 257 6 Exercises 1.
