But there is one major clause type that we have not seen thus far: the adverbial clause. Adverbial clauses are cen-tered around adverbs. Traditionally, adverbs are defined as modifiers of verbs, adjectives, or other adverbs. But this definition is problematic. First, it invokes an undefined notion, namely that of modifier. Second, this definition blurs the difference between categories (nouns, verbs, adjectives, adverbs) and functions (predicate, argument, modifier). As a consequence of this, clear nominal clauses like every morning would have to be considered adverbial in I run every morning but nominal in Every morning presents us with a new beginning. Along the same lines, a clear prepositional phrase would have to be considered prepositional (if not adjectival) in one of the senses of We found the guy with the map and adverbial in the other (see (22), Chapter 8). For us, adverbs should be defined recursively. First, there will be a finite number of words that will be listed as adverbial in the lexicon. Included among them are the following. (1) abroad, almost, alone, already, always, anytime, anywhere, away, back, backwards, down, early, enough, everywhere, far, first, here, indeed, inside, just, last, late, later, nearby, never, next, now, off, often, on, outside, quite, up, seldom, since, so, somewhat, soon, sometimes, still, then, there, too, well, yet. Additionally, there will be a number of adverbial words that will be de-rived from others by rule. Chief among these are the many adverbs end-260 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS ing in -ly (cf. loudly, softly, happily, sadly, certainly, doubtfully).1  To be more precise, the adverbs in (1) can be generated, piecemeal fashion, by the facts in (2). Adverbs in -ly, on the other hand, can be generated collectively by a single morphological process of suffixation, namely the one in (3).2 (2) english([abroad], [adv,wrd]).  english([almost], [adv,wrd]).  . . .    . . .   . . .  english([yet], [adv,wrd]). (3) english(SO,[adv,wrd]):-   english(SO1,[adj,wrd]),   english([ly],[affix]),   append(SO1,[ly],SO). It goes without saying that the process in (3) requires that both adjec-tives and an affix -ly be given antecedently (see Program 56, Appendix H). Notice, by the way, that not all adjectives will lead to well-formed adverbs in -ly. In fact, the only adjectives that will do so are the ones that describe the ways or manners in which things are done (thus we have loudly and softly, but not bluely or bigly). And we can make the rule in (3) sensitive to this fact. Just require that the adjectival root of a -ly ad-verb belong to a certain semantic category—call it [manner]: (3') english(SO,[adv,wrd],_,_):-   english(SO1,[adj,wrd],_,[manner]),   english([ly],[suffix],_,_),   append(SO1,[ly],SO). Adverbs may combine with words like very, way, rather, somewhat, mostly, barely, hardly, thus producing combinations like very loudly, way softly, rather happily, somewhat sadly. To allow for these combina-tions we will list the preceding words as intensifiers (4) and propose the rule in (5). Notice that this rule deems these combinations adverbial phrases.                                                               1 Needless to say, not all adverbs ending in -ly are derived by this rule (cf. early). And appending -ly to an adjective will not always lead to an adverb (cf. lowly). 2 See Chapter 4, Section 4, for a discussion of suffixation. SYNTAX IV: OTHER TOPICS / 261 (4) english([very  ], [int,wrd]).   english([way   ], [int,wrd]).   . . .    . . .   . . .   english([hardly], [int,wrd]). (5) english(SO, [adv,phr]):-   english(SO1, [int,wrd]),   english(SO2, [adv,wrd]),   append(SO1, SO2, SO). Once again, not all adverbs may be intensified in this way. In fact, only the adverbs that are gradable may (an adverb is gradable if it refers to a property that may hold to a degree). Thus, we may say very loudly and very softly, but not very abroad, very already, very off. We may capture this fact by restricting the rule in (5) to gradable adverbs as follows. (5') english(SO,[adv,phr],_,_):-   english(SO1,[int,wrd],_,_),   english(SO2,[adv,wrd],_,[gradable]),   append(SO1,SO2,SO). Adverbial phrases may combine with verbal phrases to produce verbal phrases, as in dresses very loudly. And these verbal phrases may again combine with adverbial phrases to produce new verbal phrases (cf. dresses very loudly often). Notice that these verbal phrases may also combine with prepositional phrases to form verbal phrases yet again (dresses very loudly often for work).  At this point, all we need to do to acknowledge these facts is allow plain, unintensified adverbs, to be adverbial phrases (6), and allow all adverbial phrases, be they intensified or not, to combine with old verbal phrases to yield new ones (7). (6) english(SO,[adv,phr]):-   english(SO,[adv,wrd]). (7) english(SO,[vbl,phr]):-   english(SO1,[vbl,phr]),   english(SO2,[adv,phr]),   append(SO1,SO2,SO). These rules assign the structures in (8) to dresses loudly and dresses very loudly. 262 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS (8) dresses[vbl,wrd] (very[int,wrd]) loud[adj,wrd] ly[affix]                                   loudly[adv,wrd]    dresses[vbl,phr]   (very) loudly[adv,phr]                           dresses (very) loudly[vbl,phr] It should be acknowledged that the rule in (7) will recur, allowing verbal phrases to be combined with adverbial and other modifiers in ways that are puzzling (dresses loudly somewhat loudly) if not incoherent (dresses loudly softly). As before, we regard this as a problem that is best han-dled by the semantics (which we continue to ignore in this section). The idea is that these sentences are syntactically well-formed even if seman-tically they are not. But the recursion brought about by (7) is not without its problems. As will be seen in Chapter 10 below, this will preclude the grammar that contains it from rejecting ungrammaticalities. Why this should be the case (and what should be done about it is something we will postpone until then). In any event, to gauge the effect of all the rules presented in this sec-tion, the reader is again invited to consult Program 56, Appendix H. 2 The English Auxiliary The English auxiliary construction is a rather complicated affair. As a first approximation, we may say that English verbs are either auxiliary or nonauxiliary. Auxiliary verbs are either modal, perfective, or pro-gressive. The modal auxiliaries are can, could, may, might, must, shall, should, will, would. The perfective auxiliaries are the forms of the verb to have, namely have, has, having, had. The progressive auxiliaries are the forms of the verb to be, namely be, am, are, is, was, were, being, been. All other verbs are nonauxiliary.3  Notice that the foregoing classifies verbs according to their roots. Turning from roots to inflections, we may say verbs are either finite or nonfinite. Finite verbs are those whose forms exhibit agreement with their subjects. Sometimes this agreement is silent (see for example the agreement exhibited by the verb to have in I have laughed). At other                                                             3 We will improve on this description in one of the exercises to this chapter. SYNTAX IV: OTHER TOPICS / 263 times, the agreement is expressed (see for example the agreement exhib-ited by the verb to have in He has laughed). It can be argued that modals are finite forms that agree silently with their subjects.4 With regards to nonfinite verbs, they are either base forms, present participles, or past participles. The base forms are the bare forms of the verb (cf. have, be). The present participles are the -ing forms of the verb (cf. having, being). The past participles tend to end in -en (cf. been) but have a variety of other forms as well (cf. had, put). In short, we will say that the class [aux] of auxiliary verbs is subcategorized as follows.                      Notice that silent agreement will make some finite and nonfinite forms sound alike. See for example the finite have in I have laughed and the nonfinite have in I may have laughed. Or the finite had in He had laughed and the past participial had in He has had a laugh. Yet, we will still regard them as different forms of the verb—and hence as cases of homophony.                                                               4 As opposed to forms that are silently nonfinite. Notice for example that modals cannot appear in place of base forms (as the ones underlined in to be or not to be, that is the ques-tion) or present participles (as the one underlined in having watched the movie, he decided to stay home). [aux] [fin] [bse] [prp] [pap] [mod] can could may might must shall should will would    [pfc] have has had have having had [prg] am are is was were be being been 264 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS Equipped with this classification, we may describe the basic facts governing the English auxiliary construction as follows.  (9) a.  Every simple sentence contains at most one modal auxiliary     verb.   b.  Every simple sentence contains at most one perfective auxilia-    ry verb.   c.  Every simple sentence contains at most one progressive auxil-    iary verb.  d.  Every simple sentence contains at least one nonauxiliary verb.  e. Modals precede perfectives, which precede progressives, which    precede nonauxiliaries  f.  Modals govern base forms.  g.  Perfectives govern present participles.  h.  Progressives govern past participles.  The intuitions behind the statements in (9) are quite robust. Thus, notice that, as indicated in (9a), one cannot say *I may can go, even though it could have meant the same thing as I may be able to go. The former is just impossible. Similarly, I have had a job does not involve two cases of the perfective auxiliary; only the first occurrence of the verb to have is auxiliary here.5 And in He is being himself, only the first occurrence of the verb to be is auxiliary; the second is a copula (see section 5, Chapter 8).6 The fact that every simple sentence must have a nonauxilia-ry verb was, to a large extent, our rationale for categorizing sentences as verbal clauses. The ordering stipulated in (9e) is supported by stark contrasts like the following.  (10) a. Homer may have been laughing.  b. *Homer may be having laughed.  c. *Homer is may(ing) have laughed.  d. *Homer is having may(ed) laugh.  e. *Homer has may(ed) be laughing.  f. *Homer has been may(ing) laugh.                                                             5 This categorial difference is backed by semantic intuitions: the second occurrence of the verb to have helps convey the fact that I held a job in the past; the first occurrence of the verb conveys only the present relevance of that bygone fact. 6 A claim which is also supported by semantic intuition. While the second occurrence of the verb to be in this sentence helps convey an activity (acting like a fool), the first one transforms this activity into a state. SYNTAX IV: OTHER TOPICS / 265 Finally, the facts in (9f)-(9h) are based on no less clear contrasts like the ones below. (11) a. may laugh/*laughs/*laughing/*laughed  b. has *laugh/*laughs/*laughing/laughed  c. is *laugh/*laughs/laughing/*laughed To account for the facts in (9) in an explicit and principled way, we will proceed in two steps. First, we will propose twenty-three PROLOG facts to generate the English auxiliaries as categorized above (there will be one fact per auxiliary). As usual, these facts are part of the lexicon or dictionary of English. (12) english([can  ], [vbl,wrd,aux,mod,fin]).  . . .  english([would], [vbl,wrd,aux,mod,fin]).  english([have ], [vbl,wrd,aux,pfc,fin]).  . . .  english([had  ], [vbl,wrd,aux,pfc,pap]).  english([am   ], [vbl,wrd,aux,prg,fin]).  . . .  english([been ], [vbl,wrd,aux,prg,pap]). Next, we will propose six PROLOG rules for generating auxiliary ver-bal phrases. Three of these rules produce modal phrases out of a modal verb and a base phrase, where this base phrase can be either progressive (13), perfective (14) or nonauxiliary (15).7 (13) english(SO,[vbl,phr,aux,mod,fin]):-  english(SO1,[vbl,wrd,aux,mod,fin]),  english(SO2,[vbl,phr,aux,prg,bse]),  append(SO1,SO2,SO). (14) english(SO,[vbl,phr,aux,mod,fin]):-  english(SO1,[vbl,wrd,aux,mod,fin]),  english(SO2,[vbl,phr,aux,pfc,bse]),  append(SO1,SO2,SO).                                                              7 The categories of nonauxiliaries will have "non" where the categories of auxiliaries have "aux".  266 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS (15) english(SO,[vbl,phr,aux,mod,fin]):-  english(SO1,[vbl,wrd,aux,mod,fin]),  english(SO2,[vbl,phr,non,bse]),  append(SO1,SO2,SO). Two of the six rules produce perfective phrases out of a perfective auxil-iary and a past participial phrase, where this participial phrase can be either progressive (16) or nonauxiliary (17).8 (16) english(SO,[vbl,phr,aux,pfc,INFL]):-  english(SO1,[vbl,wrd,aux,pfc,INFL]),  english(SO2,[vbl,phr,aux,prg,pap]),  append(SO1,SO2,SO). (17) english(SO,[vbl,phr,aux,pfc,INFL]):-  english(SO1,[vbl,wrd,aux,pfc,INFL]),  english(SO2,[vbl,phr,non,pap]),  append(SO1,SO2,SO). Finally, the last of these rules forms progressive phrases out of a pro-gressive auxiliary and a nonauxiliary phrase: (18) english(SO,[vbl,phr,aux,prg,INFL]):-  english(SO1,[vbl,wrd,aux,prg,INFL]),  english(SO2,[vbl,phr,non,prp]),  append(SO1,SO2,SO). Notice that all of these rules do two things. First, they stipulate what auxiliary words can combine with what verbal phrases (the valence of the various auxiliaries). Second, they classify the generated phrases in terms of the first auxiliary words they contain. Take for instance (13). It stipulates, first, that a modal auxiliary may combine with a progressive [prg] base [bse] phrase. Second, it stipulates that the generated ver-bal phrase is both modal and finite (like the auxiliary that heads it). Of these two things, only the first one is essential; the second one is redun-dant. As such, it could be omitted (albeit only by complicating the ways in which we refer to the verbal phrases that an auxiliary may combine with).  It should not escape the reader that the six rules given above assign a right-branching structure to the English auxiliary construction.                                                               8 INFL is a variable which ranges over the set {fin, bse, prp, pap} of inflections. SYNTAX IV: OTHER TOPICS / 267 may[vbl,wrd,aux,mod,fin]have[vbl,wrd,aux,pfc,bse]been[vbl,wrd,aux,prg,pap]laughing[vbl,wrd,non,prp]                                                                                                                                           laughing[vbl,phr,non,prp]                                                               been laughing[vbl,phr,aux,prg,pap]                                                                                                                 have been laughing[vbl,phr,aux,pfc,bse]                                               may have been laughing[vbl,phr,aux,mod,fin] Evidence for this right-branching structure is as follows. (19) a. He shouldn't have been laughing; he really shouldn't have been   laughing. b. He shouldn't have been laughing; he really shouldn't have been  laughing. c. He shouldn't have been laughing; he really shouldn't have been  laughing. We have implemented all of the above in auxiliaries.swipl (see Program 57, Appendix H). We have also implemented there some facts and rules for a token nonauxiliary verb (to laugh). We did this in order to illustrate the external syntax of the auxiliary construction—which must abide by (9d) and (9e). To generate all and only the sequences that abide by (9), consult auxiliaries.swipl, enter the following, and follow up with semicolons). english(SO,SC). To verify that the sequences in (10) and (11) will not be generated, enter the following two queries in succession.9 english([may,be,having,laughed],_). english([may,laughs],_).                                                             9 This analysis of the English auxiliary relies heavily on the one proposed in Gazdar, Pul-lum, and Sag (1982). 268 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS 3 Inversion As we saw in Chapter 3, questions can be open or closed. A closed question is one that can be fully answered with a yes or a no, whereas an open question is one that can only be fully answered with something other than a yes or a no. Examples of closed questions are given in (20); instances of open questions are given in (21). (20) a.  Can Homer annoy Marge? b.  Has Homer annoyed Marge? c.  Is Homer annoying Marge? d.  Is Marge annoyed by Homer? (21) a. Who annoys Marge? b. Who does Homer annoy? c. Who annoys whom? Notice that closed questions involve switching the subject and the auxil-iary around. This much is easy to account for. Simply string together the following three things: (i) the first item A of a verbal phrase which is both auxiliary and finite, (ii) a nominal clause B, and (iii) the remainder C of the said verbal clause. In PROLOG, this comes down to the follow-ing. (22) english(SO, [vbl,cls,inv]):-  english([A|C], [vbl,phr,aux,_,fin]),  english(B, [nom,cls]),  append([[A],B,C],SO). Notice that (22) marks the generated sentence [inv] for inverted. This might be useful, as there are certain constructions that call for inverted sentences. See for example (21b). Or the tagged question boldfaced in He doesn't know the answer, does he?  Notice also that the first condition of (22) incorporates an unnamed variable. The fact that we have a variable acknowledges that there are different kinds of auxiliary phrases, and that it does not matter which kind we have here; all will undergo inversion. The fact that this variable is unnamed acknowledges that there is no need to refer back to it.  To get the semantics of closed questions, we need to remember that to know the meaning of a question is to know its answer given enough information about the world (see Chapter 3). So we can take the mean-ing of a question to be a procedure for the computation of its answer (relative to a view or model of the world). Thus, if R1::S1 represents the meaning of can annoy Marge, and if R2::S2 represents the mean-SYNTAX IV: OTHER TOPICS / 269 ing of Homer, then the meaning of Can Homer annoy Marge would be the procedure followed by PROLOG as it attempts to respond to the following query—thereby assigning a value to Answer. (23) Answer::evaluation((S1,S2,member(R1,R2)),Answer). (23) is, of course, a referential query whose referential variable has been called Answer rather than Reference (or R) simply for the sake of perspicuity. Incorporating these observations into (29) leads to (22'). (22') english(SO,[vbl,cls,inv], [Answer ::   evaluation((S1,S2,member(R1,R2)),Answer)]):-  english([A|C],[vbl,phr,aux,_,fin],[R1 :: S1]),  english(B,[nom,cls],[R2 :: S2]),  append([[A],B,C],SO). This accounts for closed questions that involve all the auxiliaries we have seen thus far, be they modals, perfective have, or progressive be. We have incorporated (the sound portions of) all these rules in inver-sion.swipl (see Program 58, Appendix H). Together with rules for generating nominal clauses and sentences, both direct and inverted.  inversion.swipl can generate 336 sentences. Half of these sentences are direct and half inverted. But all of them are grammatical. One of these sentences is Can Homer laugh?, which is assigned the structure in (24). (24)  Homer[nom,cls]    can[vbl,wrd,aux,mod,fin]       laugh[vbl,wrd,non,bse]                                                can laugh[vbl,phr,aux,mod,fin]          Can Homer laugh[vbl,phr,aux,stv,fin]    It should be noticed that this analysis allows us to recognize a verbal phrase even though it is split (or rendered discontinuous) by its subject. Thus, the devices that trigger subject verb agreement need not be modi-fied for inverted sentences. Nor do the provisions for making the subject bind the first argument of the verb (see Chapter 7).   270 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS 4 The Passive Voice Passive sentences convey the converse of what their active counterparts convey. Thus, the passive sentence in (25) states the converse of (26a) but the same as (26b). (25) The lion was killed by the hunter. (26) a. The lion killed the hunter.  b. The hunter killed the lion. Passive sentences come in handy when one wants to downplay the role of a "doer" (like the hunter in (25)) and highlight the role of a "done-to" (say the lion in (25)). As a matter of fact, the passive voice—as it is usu-ally called—allows the doer to be downplayed to such an extent that it can even be omitted from a sentence. Thus, it is grammatically admis-sible to drop all reference to the hunter in (25) and say simply The lion was killed. This contrasts with what active sentences allow. Take for instance the actives in (26). Their doers simply cannot be dropped any more than their done-to's can.10 With respect to the sounds of passive sentences, they always involve a form of the verb to be and a past participial form of the verb. In (25), the former is was and the latter is killed. The most straightforward way to account for the facts considered thus far is to begin by proposing the rule in (27). Intuitively, it says that certain active verbs become passive by referring to the converse of the relation they convey. (27) english(SO,[vbl,wrd,pas,pap],   [R :: allsuch([Y,X], Z, R)]) :-   english(SO,[vbl,wrd,act,pap],   [R1 :: allsuch([X,Y],Z,R1)]). For notice that the order [Y,X] of the variables in the head of (27) is the reverse of the order [X,Y] of these variables in the body of the rule. It is through this reversal—and only through this reversal—that the statement is made that passive verbs convey the converse of the relation conveyed by their active counterparts. As to the fact that passive verbs sound exactly like past participles, this follows if the former are all gen-                                                            10 Technically, a doer is called an agent in the linguistic literature, while a done-to is referred to as a patient or as an undergoer. SYNTAX IV: OTHER TOPICS / 271 erated by (27), which simply recategorizes past participles as passive ones (albeit with the change in meaning we have just discussed).11 The rule in (27) says nothing about the by-phrases that may accom-pany a passive verb (cf. by the hunter in (25)). Or about the form of the verb to be that must accompany it. Let's address these shortcomings in turn. With respect to the by-phrase, we will regard it as an oblique nom-inal clause rather than a prepositional phrase. In fact, we will regard it as an oblique nominal clause of subcategory 015, as 015 is the makeshift name we have assigned to the subcategory of the preposition by (see preceding chapter). We will moreover allow this oblique phrase to ap-pend to a passive participle in order to yield a passive verbal phrase: (28) english(SO, [vbl,phr,pas,pap],   [R :: allsuch(X,(S1,S2,member  ([X,_], R1), allsuch(Y,member  ([X,Y],R1),Z),member(Z,R2)),R)]):-   english(SO1, [vbl,wrd,pas,pap], [R1 :: S1]),   english(SO2, [nom,cls,015    ], [R2 :: S2]),   append(SO1,SO2,SO). As the reader will be able to verify, the semantic component of this rule is exactly the same as the semantic component of transitive verbal phrases (see the formula given as (9) in Chapter 7; this is the formula that we diagrammed as (10) there). It follows that the by-phrase of a passive verbal phrase will bind the second argument of a passive parti-ciple. Since passive participles convey the converse of their active coun-terparts, this second argument will be, as desired, the first argument of the corresponding active. To get forms of the copulative verb to be alongside passive forms, we will propose a rule that combines the [cop] forms of this verb with the passive verbal phrases generated by the rule in (28). (29) english(SO, [vbl,phr,cop,INF], ME):-    english(SO1, [vbl,wrd,cop,INF],  _),    english(SO2, [vbl,phr,pas,pap], ME),    append(SO1, SO2, SO).                                                             11 See Chapter 4, Section 13 for a discussion of the morphological process of recategori-zation. We leave it open what the class [act] of active verbs is. As we pointed out al-ready, the class of verbs that undergo passive rule is not the same as the class of transitive verbs. Take for instance weigh or resemble. Although transitive (cf. the brain weighs a few pounds and he resembles his parents), they cannot serve in passive sentences (cf. *a few pounds are weighed by the brain and *his parents are resembled by him). 272 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS Note that this rule gives an unnamed variable as the meaning of the form of the copulative verb to be. This is because this verb makes no semantic contribution to the meaning of the phrase being constructed here. The meaning of the constructed phrase is just the meaning of the passive phrase in it. Notice that we are not saying that the form of the verb to be is meaningless; all we are saying is that its meaning, if any, is irrelevant in the present context. The rules we have given thus far will assign the following structure to is annoyed by Homer. is[vbl,wrd,cop,fin]      annoyed[vbl,wrd,pas,pap]        by[prp,wrd,015]         Homer[nom,cls]                                                                                                                                                                        by Homer[nom,cls,015]                                                                                                    annoyed by Homer[vbl,phr,pas,pap]                                  is annoyed by Homer[vbl,phr,cop,fin] And evidence that annoyed by Homer is indeed a constituent is provided by the following possibility of deletion. (30) Marge is really annoyed by Homer; she really is annoyed by  Homer. At this point, we can form passive sentences by the independently motivated rule in (31). (31) english(SO, [vbl,cls], [R ::    evaluation((S1,S2,member(R2,R1)),R)]) :-  english(SO1,[nom,cls      ], [R1 :: S1]),  english(SO2,[vbl,phr,_,fin], [R2 :: S2]),  append( SO1,  SO2,  SO). (31) is not really new; it is the sentential rule of syntax.ii.swipl. But with one small twist: the second condition of (31) involves finite verbal phrases (whether they are active or not is irrelevant). It should be clear, however, that this change has nothing to do with the passive voice; only with the fact that main sentences must involve finite verbs SYNTAX IV: OTHER TOPICS / 273 (of different kinds). And with the fact that now our verbs may be finite or not. All of the rules discussed in this section have been gathered in voice.swipl (see Program 59, Appendix H). In addition to these rules, voice.swipl contains rules for the generation of nominal clauses, be they plain (Homer, Marge, Bart, Lisa) or oblique (by Homer, by Marge, by Bart, by Lisa). Also included in this program are rules to generate an actual passive verb (annoyed). This passive verb will then partake of passive phrases (annoyed by Homer, annoyed by Marge, an-noyed by Bart, annoyed by Lisa), of verbal phrases with passive com-plements (is annoyed by Homer, is annoyed by Marge, is annoyed by Bart, is annoyed by Lisa), and of sentences which contain these verbal phrases (Homer is annoyed by Homer, Homer is annoyed by Marge, and so on). To compare the semantics of passives and nonpassives, voice.swipl generates also an active verb (annoys). This verb is allowed to form phrases (annoys Homer, annoys Marge, annoys Bart, annoys Lisa) and sentences which contain these verbal phrases (Homer annoys Homer, Homer annoys Marge, and so on).  To top this program off, we add to it information that really belongs in the Simpsons model. It is an indication of who annoys whom in an assumed view of the world (it will be Homer and Bart that annoy Marge and Lisa, respectively). Taken in conjunction, these rules will indeed account for the fact that passives are the converse of actives. To see how, consult voice.swipl and enter the following queries in turn. english([annoys, homer], _, [ME1]). english([annoys, marge], _, [ME2]). english([annoys, bart ], _, [ME3]). english([annoys, lisa ], _, [ME4]). Then enter, as successive queries, the values PROLOG assigns to ME1, ME2, ME3, M4. PROLOG will respond to the first and third of these latter queries with the empty list, but with [homer] and [bart] to the second and forth (respectively). This is as desired, as only Homer and Bart respectively annoy Marge and Lisa (according to the infor-mation intended for our Simpsons model).  Compare, by way of contrast, the responses PROLOG will give to the following queries: english([is,annoyed,by,homer], _, [ME1]). english([is,annoyed,by,marge], _, [ME2]). english([is,annoyed,by,bart ], _, [ME3]). 274 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS english([is,annoyed,by,lisa ], _, [ME4]). Then enter, once again, as successive queries, the values PROLOG as-signed to these instances of ME1, ME2, ME3, M4. In this case, PROLOG will respond to the second and the fourth queries with the empty list, but with [marge] and [lisa] to the first and third. This, again, is as desired. Finally, enter the following queries. english([homer,annoys,marge], _, [ME1]). english([marge,annoys,homer], _, [ME2]). Then enter as queries, one after the other, the values PROLOG assigns to these instances of ME1, ME2. PROLOG responds with truth and falsity; yet, doing all of the above with the following two queries yields falsity and truth instead.12  english([homer,is,annoyed,by,marge],_, [ME1]). english([marge,is,annoyed,by,homer],_, [ME2]).  All this is, of course, as desired. And similarly desirable results arise in this regard with every other combination of nominal clauses. 5 Control Structures Consider next the sentences in (32). (32) a. Homer promised Marge to drive.  b. Homer persuaded Marge to drive. Notice that if things were as described in (32a), it would be Homer who would be supposed to drive, but if things were as described in (32b), it would be Marge who would be driving instead. Notice also that the con-trast in (32) affects the choice of pronouns and determiners. Consider for example (33) and (34), where him(self) and her(self) cannot be sub-stituted for each other. (33) a.  Homer promised Marge to drive by himself.  b.  Homer persuaded Marge to drive by herself. (34) a.  Homer promised Marge to drive on his own.  b.  Homer persuaded Marge to drive on her own.                                                             12 As usual, when PROLOG returns truth, it will indicate what the semantic values were, of the subject and predicate of the sentences in question, that led to this assignment of truth. SYNTAX IV: OTHER TOPICS / 275 In short, we have that the first argument of a subordinate verb (here drive) may be bound either by the subject of promise or by the object of persuade.13 And whatever pronouns or determiners are supposed to refer back to the bound argument must be of a form that allows them to do so. With regards to terminology, constructions in which the argumen-ts of a subordinate verb are bound by the nominal clauses of a subordi-nating verb are called control structures, and the nominal clauses bind-ing these arguments are said to control them. Given these terms, it is natural to say that promise is a subject-control verb and that persuade is an object-control verb. The contrast in (32) has been accounted for in many ways. Here we will account for it not in terms of the grammar of English, but in terms of the general nature of promising and persuading.14  For starters, take the verb promise. It seems natural to claim that this verb refers to a ternary relation that holds between (i) entities that make promises, (ii) entities promises are made to, and (iii) promises the for-mer make to the latter. One way to define this relation in the models used to interpret these verbs is through a brute listing of facts. Thus, if Homer and Marge promise each other to drive, then models suitable to these circumstances would contain: (35) promise(homer,marge,drive(homer)).  promise(marge,homer,drive(marge)). Similarly, it seems natural to interpret the verb persuade as a ternary relation that holds between (i) persuading entities, (ii) persuaded entities, and (iii) persuasions of the latter by the former. And, if the individuals involved in the persuading relations are the same as those in the promis-ing relation, then models for the interpretation of this verb in these cir-cumstances would contain the following clauses. (36) persuade(homer,marge,drive(marge)).  persuade(marge,homer,drive(homer)). Models that incorporate (35) will ensure that (32a) entails that Homer will be the one who is supposed to drive; models that incorporate (36) will ensure that (32b) entails that Marge will be the one that is supposed to drive. Thus, models that incorporate both (35) and (36) will account                                                             13 See Chapter 7 for an explanation of the term bound. Evidence that Marge is the object of persuade comes from the fact that this nominal clause both pronominalizes as the accu-sative her (cf. Homer persuaded her to go) and passivizes (cf. Marge was persuaded to go by Homer). The same arguments apply to Marge as the object of promise. 14 See Farkas (1987) and Culicover and Jackendoff (2006). 276 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS for the contrast in (32). But hardly in a satisfactory way. For notice that the entity that does the promising in (35) is also the one that is supposed to do the driving. And that the entity that is being persuaded in (36) is also the one that is supposed to do the driving. But these two generaliza-tions are simply missed by the brute listings of (35) and (36).  More importantly, defining promise and persuade by the facts of a model makes these definitions specific to that model. But these def-initions do not hold only in those models; they hold in all. Promising to do something is promising to be the one to do it. And persuading someone to do something is persuading him or her to be the one to do it. Thus, Homer may assure Marge that someone other than him will drive, but he cannot promise that someone other than him will do so. For you can only promise what you intend to do; you cannot promise what someone else intends to do; that is simply incompatible with the general nature of promising. Similarly, Homer may make Marge decide that someone other than her will drive, but he cannot make her intend that. That would be inconsistent with the nature of intending—and persuad-ing is making someone intend something.15 Just like convincing is ma-king someone decide something.  To capture these universal regularities we will define promise and persuade along the following lines. (37) promise(X,Y,Z):-  assure(X,Y,Z),  arg(1,Z,X). (38) persuade(X,Y,Z):-  convince(X,Y,Z),  arg(1,Z,Y). These definitions are rather broad, and call for further conditions to nar-row them.16 Yet, even as is, they will ensure that (32a) entails that Ho-mer is the one that is supposed to drive, and that (32b) entails that Mar-ge is the one that is supposed to drive. And, in so doing, account for the contrast in (32).  But (37) and (38) do more than this. For notice that the last condi-tion in each of these rules articulates the generalizations that the facts in (35) and (36) missed. Moreover, as they are intended to be general defi-                                                            15 See Pulman (2005). 16 Promises are fulfilled in the future, for example, not in the past or in the present. They are also regarded as desirable to the persons they are made to (otherwise they would be threats). And they are actions that depart from the normal course of events (see Searle 1969, Chapter 3). SYNTAX IV: OTHER TOPICS / 277 nitions, (37) and (38) will be universal. Consequently, they are to be factored out of specific models and stated, once and for all, among the conditions models must meet before they can be used in the computa-tion of linguistic reference. It follows that (37) and (38) are not just par-ticular views of particular individuals, but rather part of the characteri-zation of the class of models that are suitable for the interpretation of human language. As such, they join definitions like that of mother as female parent and that of boy as male child (see Chapter 3).  As to whether (32a) and (32b) are actually true or not, that should vary from model to model. But this will be achieved by doing nothing over and above what we have already done. For notice that (32a) and (32b) will be true just in case they are interpreted relative to models that contain the following facts. (39) assure(homer,marge,drive(homer)). (40) convince(homer,marge,drive(marge)). And nothing we have said thus far will prevent models from containing (39), (40), both, or neither. To verify that this approach will actually work we need to do two things. First we need to pair the sounds of (32) to their meanings. Se-cond, we need to show that these meanings will determine the desired references. This is exactly what we do in control.swipl (See Pro-gram 60 in Appendix H). control.swipl has but one new feature— its analysis of to drive. So let us turn to that. As traditional grammar would have it, to drive would be an infini-tive. And we may consider it as much. Provided we acknowledge that an infinitive is more than a preposition followed by a verb in its base form; it is a preposition followed by a verbal phrase which may sometimes consist—as it does in this case—of nothing more than a verb in its base form. Thus, to drive is as much of an infinitive as to drive a red Ferrari faster than the speed limit would allow is. So an infinitive is a phrase consisting of a preposition followed by verbal phrase that is headed by a verb in base form. As to the preposition involved in an infinitive, it var-ies from language to language. In English, it is invariably to.17 And this preposition has little to no content. Not just in English but cross-linguistically as well. So the case can be made that an infinitive is an oblique verbal phrase—alongside the oblique nominal phrases that we discussed in Chapter 8.                                                             17 In Spanish, it would be either a 'to' or de 'of'. 278 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS Formally, the internal syntax of an English infinitive (or an infiniti-val verbal phrase) is as given by the following rule. (41) english(SO,[vbl,phr,inf],ME) :-  english(SO1,[prp,wrd,dat], _),  english(SO2,[vbl,phr,bse],ME),  append(SO1,SO2,SO).  We assume that the category[prp,wrd,dat] of dative prepositional words will contain to and nothing else. We assert that the meaning of an infinitive is the same as the meaning of the plain verbal phrase it con-tains (see the two occurrences of ME in (41)). Thus the meaning, if any, of the preposition, plays no role in the meaning of the infinitive—and is therefore represented by a variable in (41). Turning to the external syntax of an infinitive (or an infinitival ver-bal phrase), it will suffice for our purposes to add an infinitive to our old rule for transitive verbal phrases (this old rule was the seventh condi-tional statement of syntax.swipl; see the discussion of (10) in Chapter 7). This addition will of course require us to adjust the condi-tions on the main verb so as to allow for the added infinitive. Both in sound and in meaning.  In other words, the rule governing the external syntax of an infini-tive is (42), whose three essential departures from the plain transitive rule have been boldfaced. (42) english(SO, [vbl, phr, INFL], [R ::   allsuch(X,(S1,S2,member([X,_,_],R1),   allsuch(Y, member([X, Y,_], R1), Z),  member(Z,R2),member([X,Y,W],R1)),R)]) :-  english(SO1, [vbl,wrd,002,INFL], [R1 :: S1]),  english(SO2, [nom,cls         ], [R2 :: S2]),  english(SO3, [vbl,phr,inf     ], [R3 ::   allsuch(_,W,R3)]),  append([SO1, SO2, SO3], SO). The three essential departures are: (i) mention of an infinitival verbal phrase, (ii) appeal to append/2 so that the sounds of the infinitival may concatenate to those of the verb and its object, and (iii) require that the clause W conveyed by the infinitival be one of the arguments of the main verb. As to the adjustments to the main verb, they amount to making it a three-place relation rather than two (this would be the defin-ing feature of verbs of makeshift subcategory 002). Thus, the structure of the sentences in (32) is as follows. SYNTAX IV: OTHER TOPICS / 279 promised/persuaded[vbl,wrd,02,fin] Marge[nom,wrd,prp] to[prp,wrd,dat]  drive[vbl,wrd,01,bse]                       Marge[nom,phr,prp]                            drive[vbl,phr,bse]                                             Marge[nom,cls              to drive[vbl,phr,inf]   Homer[nom,wrd,prp]     promised/persuaded Marge to drive[vbl,phr,fin]   Homer[nom,phr,prp]   Homer[nom,cls]    Homer promised/persuaded Marge to drive[vrb,cls] control.swipl in its entirety has been listed as Program 60 of Appendix H. To see how this grammar works, consult this program and enter the following query: english(SO, [vbl,cls], ME). PROLOG will respond with eight pairings of sound and meaning, name-ly the ones corresponding to the following sentences. Homer promises Homer to drive. This is because control.swipl incorporates a model simpsons.ii.swipl, which is just like the original Simpsons mod-el—except that it contains, in addition, the definitions (37)–(38), and the facts (39)–(40) we have mentioned above.18                                                              18 Together with its response of truth, PROLOG furthermore indicates that the reference of the verbal phrase is [homer], and the reference of the subject is the set of lists which contain homer. This is as desired, as Homer is the only one who both promises and per-suades Marge to drive. For the sake of completeness, simpsons.ii.swipl further-280 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS Promise and persuade are not the only control verbs of English. Try is like promise. Except that it does not allow for a direct object (cf. Homer tried to drive). And make is like persuade. Except that it takes a non-oblique verbal phrase (cf. Homer made Marge drive). Want is like persuade if it has a direct object (cf. Homer wanted Marge to drive) but like promise if it doesn't (cf. Homer wanted to drive). And English has control adjectives as well. Consider for example the sentences in (43). (43) a.  John is easy to please.  b.  John is eager to please. While (43a) is about John's being pleased by someone, (43b) is about John's pleasing someone instead. And hard is like easy (their subjects control the objects of the verbs embedded under them). And reluctant is like eager (their subjects control the subjects of the verbs embedded under them). Here, opposites seem to attract. 6 Subordination We cannot leave syntax without mentioning two other sources of syn-tactic recursion. They are subordination and coordination. With regards to subordination, it can be seen in the ability to place sentences within sentences. This may happen in a variety of constructions—notably alongside nouns to form nominal phrases (44a), alongside verbs to form verbal phrases (44b), alongside adjectives to form adjective phrases (44c), alongside prepositions to form prepositional phrases (44d), and alongside adverbs to form adverbial phrases. (44)  a.  fact that the world is round[nom,phr]   b.  believe that the world is round[vbl,phr]   c.  aware that the world is round[adj,phr]   d.  in that the world is round[prp,phr]   e.  so that the world be round[adv,phr]  To account for these sentences-within-sentences, we will place the word that in a category [cmp] of complementizers. Now we may define a subordinate sentence as the result of appending a complementizer to an ordinary sentence (a sentence that is not already subordinate). In PROLOG, this can be done as follows, where [vbl,cls] is the class of ordinary sentences and [vbl,cls,sub] is the subclass of subordi-nate sentences.                                                                                                                        more contains the information that nobody drives and that nobody stands in the to-relation. See Program 61, Appendix H. SYNTAX IV: OTHER TOPICS / 281 (45) english([that],[cmp]). (46) english(SO, [vbl,cls,sub]):-   english(SO1,[cmp]),  english(SO2,[vbl,cls]),  append(SO1, SO2, SO). The external syntax of subordinate sentences is straightforward as well. As far as the phrases in (44) are concerned, all we need to do is (i) place those words that can combine with subordinate sentences in categories of the appropriate type, and (ii) combine all the words of these cate-gories with subordinate sentences. The first of these tasks can be carried out by statements like the following, (47) english([fact   ], [nom,wrd,snt]).  english([believe], [vbl,wrd,snt]).  english([aware  ], [adj,wrd,snt]).  english([in     ], [prp,wrd,snt]).  english([so     ], [adv,wrd,snt]). where snt marks, as might be expected, the categories of words that can take subordinate sentences. The second task can be done, once and for all, with the following rule: (48) english(SO,[CAT,phr]):-  english(SO1,[CAT,wrd,snt]),  english(SO2,[vbl,cls,sub]),  append(SO1,SO2,SO). The reason this one rule works for all the constructions in (44) is the double occurrence, in (48), of the variable CAT, which will range, with-out additional stipulation, over the set {nom, vbl, adj, prp, adv} of categorial features. Also playing an important role here is our uniform theory of syntactic categories, which procedes from words to phrases to clauses.  We have gathered all of the statements in (45)-(48) in subordi-nation.swipl, which also contains, albeit solely for the purposes of illustration, the unconditional statement that the world is round is a sen-tence. In a adequate grammar of English, such a statement would be made, of course, by a variety of conditional and unconditional state-ments, all of which would be independently motivated of the purposes at hand. See Program 62, Appendix H. It goes without saying that adopting the unified rule in (48) assumes that the semantics of the constructions in (44) can be unified as neatly as their syntax. Whether this assumption is warranted or not remains to be 282 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS seen. As far as the verb believe is concerned, (48) would need to be en-riched with the semantic information boldfaced in (48').19 (48') english(SO,[CAT,phr],[R :: allsuch(X,S1,R)]) :-  english(SO1,[CAT,wrd,snt],[R1 ::   allsuch([X,Y],S1,R1])),  english(SO2,[vbl,cls,sub],Y),  append(SO1,SO2,SO). For then the double occurrence of the variable Y in (48') ensures that the proposition conveyed by the subordinate sentence SO2 is also the object of believes, which is SO1. This means that the double occurrence of the variable X in (48') will ensure that the verbal phrase generated by this rule refers to the set of entities that bear the relation conveyed by SO1 to the proposition conveyed by SO2—which is exactly what one would want the reference of this verbal phrase be. The issue is whether the boldfaced material in (48') describes the semantics of the other construc-tions in (44) as well—i. e. whether it generalizes, cross-categorially, beyond verbs. Continuing with the semantics of the constructions in (44), we should add that we take complementizers to be meaningless—hence a fuller form of (45) would have the empty list as its semantic parame-ter.20   (45') english([that],[cmp], []). We also assume that subordinate sentences have the same meaning as the ordinary sentences in them—hence the two occurrences of the varia-ble ME in (46'). (46') english(SO, [vbl,cls,sub], ME):-    english(SO1, [cmp]),   english(SO2, [vbl,cls], ME),   append(SO1, SO2, SO).                                                             19 The verb believe itself would be introduced by the following rule: english([believe],[vbl,wrd,snt],[R ::  allsuch([X,Y],believe(X,Y),R])). 20 Other good candidates to be meaningless expressions are certain occurrences of it (cf. the two occurrences of this pronoun in it is true that it rained) and there (cf. there is a reason for that). Meaningless expressions justify having the semantic parameters of our grammatical statements be lists. For lists can be empty whereas argument positions can be neither empty nor suppressed. SYNTAX IV: OTHER TOPICS / 283 Finally, notice that the theory of propositions (or sentence meanings) that we proposed in Chapter 3 has a high power of resolution, as it is able to distinguish, not only between the meanings of two sentences that are coreferential (and thus have the same truth value), but also between two sentences that are semantically equivalent (and thus have the same truth conditions), and between two sentences that are logically equiva-lent (and are thus provable from each other). To illustrate, take the Prin-ciple of Noncontradiction (49a) and the Principle of the Excluded Mid-dle (49b). (49) a.  ¬(p ∧ ¬p)  b.  p ∨ ¬p According to Classical Logic, (49a) and (49b) have the same truth value (they are both true), have the same truth conditions (they are true under all conditions), and are logically equivalent (they are provable from each other by the de Morgan, the double negation, and the commutative laws). Yet, they still differ in meaning. And our theory of propositions will predict as much. For they will correspond to different procedures for the evaluation of their respective truth values; (49a) will involve conjunction, for example, while (49b), disjunction.  The power of resolution of the theory of propositions we have es-poused is called for by the semantics of verbs of propositional atti-tude—verbs like believe, which convey attitudes towards propositions. For, as is generally agreed, beliefs are quite discriminating of proposi-tions. Thus, it is possible for someone to believe in the Principle of Noncontradiction but not in the Principle of the Excluded Middle. Even if (s)he believes that they are referentially, semantically, and logically equivalent. Belief in one and only one of these principles might then be inconsistent with other beliefs held—but it would be a distinct belief nonetheless.  To see how this all would work let us provide a simple model speci-fying who believes what. Say (50): (50) believe(homer, not((rains, not(rains)))).  believe(marge, (rains;not(rains))). If this is all the believing that goes on in the model, then Homer believes in (an instance of) the Principle of Noncontradiction but not in (the equivalent instance of) the Principle of the Excluded Middle. And 284 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS Marge holds the converse beliefs. Notice that both beliefs will be prova-ble from each other. Even in PROLOG.21 ?- not((rains, not(rains))) => (rains;not(rains)). true. ?- (rains;not(rains)) => not((rains, not(rains))). true. ?- not((rains, not(rains))) <=> (rains;not(rains)). true. Yet, Homer and only Homer will believe in the Principle of Noncontra-diction and only in the Principle of Noncontradiction. And Marge and only Marge will believe in the Principle of the Excluded Middle and only in the Principle of the Excluded Middle. See the following ex-change ?- believe(Believer,Belief). Believer = homer, Belief = not(rains, not(rains)) ; Believer = marge, Belief = (rains;not(rains)). ?- (note this exchange ends in PROLOG reporting that it is ready for an-other query—which signals to us that it is done telling us who believes what according to the model). This model is called beliefs.swipl and is listed as Program 63 in Appendix H. 7 Coordination Coordination is a process whereby two or more phrases of like category concatenate to form a new phrase of like category. The concatenated phrases are called coordinants and the concatenation itself is called the coordinate. Examples of coordination are given in (51).  (51) a.  the truth, the whole truth, and nothing but the truth  b.  reading, studying and sleeping  c.  on and on and on and on  d.  again and again and again and again                                                             21 To carry out these computations, we must consult entailment.swipl first. SYNTAX IV: OTHER TOPICS / 285 As (51) illustrates, coordinants are often accompanied by conjunctions, which are short linking words like and and or.  The process of coordination is nothing short of remarkable, as it brings about three recursions at the same time. First, coordination in-volves the concatenation of two or more phrases—which is another way of saying that it licenses the concatenation of an unbounded number of phrases. Second, coordination applies to any category whatsoever— possibly infinitely many, in fact. Third, included among these categories will be coordinates themselves. Coordinates may therefore serve as co-ordinants of more inclusive coordinates. Coordination is thus a process that applies to its own output—which yields the third kind of recursion. How should we account for coordination? As far as binary coordi-nation is concerned, it seems that we should describe it with the rule in (52). (52) english(SO,SC):-  english(SO1,SC),  english(SO2,[conjunction]),  english(SO3,SC),  append([SO1,SO2,SO3], SO). Unfortunately, (52) will disable any PROLOG interpreter that attempts to run it. For, suppose (52) were the first rule of a grammar—or at least the first rule to define the english predicate. Suppose further that the interpreter was prompted to determine whether english(SO,SC) was true. Since (52) is the first rule to define the english predicate, the interpreter will try to bring this rule to bear on the issue. To see whether it can, the interpreter must determine whether the first of its conditions can be met. But, to do this, it must apply the rule itself, as there is nothing but (52) at this point to adjudicate the issue of whether english(SO1,SC) holds or not. This, of course, brings the inter-preter back to square one, where the inevitable process taking it there, begins anew. In other words, a grammar-initial (52) will throw the in-terpreter into an unproductive loop from which it cannot escape. Inter-preters ingesting (52) first will therefore run with manic determination until they exhaust their resources—at which point they will be forced to halt without a single coordinate to show for. Things are not much better if (52) is preceded by one or more clauses defining the english predicate. Prompted to determine wheth-er (52) can instantiate english(SO,SC), the interpreter will identify SO1 with the first sound generated by the grammar and then try to finish testing every possible continuation of SO before it instantiates SO1 to any other grammatical sound. Since (52) will allow for infinitely many 286 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS such continuations, the interpreter will not be able to dispose of any query that involves a coordinate whose first coordinant is a grammatical sound other than the first. The interpreter will simply run out of re-sources before it can do so. And the same will happen if the interpreter is queried about an ungrammatical pairing of sound and meaning.22 The standard way to avoid these problems involves distinguishing between the category of the coordinants and the category of the coordi-nate. One way to do this is to make coordinants of category SC yield a coordinate of category[crd|SC], where crd is nothing but a mark, mnemonic at best, that succeeds in distinguishing the category of a co-ordinate from the categories of the coordinants that make it up. Revising (52) along these lines leads to (52'). (52') english(SO,[crd|SC]):-  english(SO1,SC),  english(SO2,[conjunction]),  english(SO3,SC),  append([SO1,SO2,SO3],SO). Needless to say, this approach flies in the face of simplicity, as the addi-tion of crd to a category name is not motivated by the linguistic de-scription of coordination; only by the way in which the linguistic de-scription of coordination in PROLOG is processed by an interpreter.  It should be emphasized that there is nothing wrong with (52) as a declarative statement. There certainly is no syntax error involved in it. And there is nothing wrong about its intended meaning either. Moreover, the rule can sure run—in fact, the problem is, precisely, that the rule will run to the point of exhaustion. The only way to fault (52) is to consider it a procedural statement. But this is odd, as these are the statements that PROLOG is not supposed to make! But the addition of crd to a category name is not the only affront against simplicity that we must be prepared to endure. The rule in (52) captures, in one elegant formulation, four kinds of coordination. To wit: the coordination of a noncoordinate with a noncoordinate the coordination of a coordinate with a noncoordinate the coordination of a noncoordinate with a coordinate the coordination of a coordinate with a coordinate                                                             22 No reordering of the conditions of (52) will improve matters significantly. In fact, it might make matters worse. And no appeal to a length query will help either (see leftrecursion.swipl, Program 64 of Appendix H). SYNTAX IV: OTHER TOPICS / 287 Of these four kinds of coordination, (52') captures but the first and the last (the two whose coordinants are of the same status with respect to coordination). The other two would call for the rules (52'') and (52''').23 (52') english(SO,[crd|SC]) :-  english(SO1,SC),  english(SO2,[conjunction]),  english(SO3,SC),  append([SO1,SO2,SO3],SO). (52'') english(SO,[crd|SC]) :-  english(SO3,SC),  english(SO2,[conjunction]),  append([SO1,SO2,SO3],SO),  english(SO1,[crd|SC]). (52''') english(SO,[crd|SC]) :-  english(SO1,SC),  english(SO2,[conjunction]),  append([SO1,SO2,SO3],SO),  english(SO3,[crd|SC]). (52'''') english(SO,[crd,crd|SC]) :-  english(SO1,[crd|SC]),  english(SO2,[conjunction]), english(SO3,[crd|SC]),  append([SO1,SO2,SO3],SO). It is at this point that we realize that the categorial distinction between coordinates and coordinants is not just overly complex; it is also unprin-cipled, as it forces us to say that coordination applies separately to cate-gories according to coordinate status. But what we want to say is that this process applies jointly to categories regardless of coordinate status. Having made a spurious distinction between coordinates and coordi-nants, we must now unmake this distinction and ensure that whatever happens to the one happens to the other. Something must be done about this. But what? It is clear that the difficulty posed by (52) lies not with (52) itself, but rather with the in-terpreters that run it. Consequently, it is these interpreters that must be revised, while (52) should be retained as is. As to what these revisions should ultimately be, is not for a linguist to say. Or for this linguist to                                                             23 See coordination.swipl (Program 65, Appendix H) for a weaker version of this grammar. 288 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS say, anyway. All he can do is call for a PROLOG interpreter that sup-ports the declarative nature of PROLOG. We will return to these issues in Chapter 10, where we discuss the shortcomings of PROLOG programming in more general terms. There we will see other examples of constructions whose left-recursions are toxic for PROLOG interpreters—and develop a way around to this ob-stacle. 8 Historical Note: Generative Grammar The English auxiliary, inversion, the passive voice, control structures, subordination, and coordination have all played key roles in the history of generative grammars. Conceived in the middle of the twentieth centu-ry by Noam Chomsky (1928 - ), a generative grammar is a deductive system intended to deduce or generate the sentences of a human lan-guage. And nothing but the sentences of a human language. Each human language would have its own generative grammar, so we would speak of the generative grammar of English, the generative grammar of Span-ish—and so on. Yet, it was expected that all generative grammars would be similar in significant ways, and that these similarities could be used to define human language in general, and be considered part of the hu-man endowment. Being a deductive system, a generative grammar consists of a set of postulates and a set of rules of inference. At first, generative grammars had but one postulate and two kinds of rules of inference. The one pos-tulate was the symbol S, which was intended to stand for sentence. This postulate was supposed to be (a) a postulate in every generative gram-mar, and (b) the only postulate in any generative grammar. In other words, it was supposed to be both universal and unique. As to the rules of inference of a generative grammar, they were statements that allowed us to deduce strings of symbols, be it from a single symbol or from a string of symbols. For reasons that will become clear presently, the for-mer may be called the formative rules of inference and the latter may be referred to as the transformative rules of inference. To illustrate, take the two English sentences Homer likes Marge and Marge is liked by Homer. A generative grammar of English could de-duce these sentences if it encompassed the following deductive sys-tem.24                                                               24 NP stands here for nounphrase (akin to our nominal phrase), and VP for verbphrase (our verbal phrase). SYNTAX IV: OTHER TOPICS / 289  Figure 1. A simple generative grammar  A proof under this deductive system would procede as follows: S   (By postulate) NP VP   (From previous step by first formative rule of inference) Homer VP  (From previous step by second formative rule of inference) Homer V NP  (From previous step by third formative rule of inference) Homer likes NP  (From previous step by fourth formative rule of inference) Homer likes Marge (From previous step by fifth formative rule of inference) This proof would be completed as no other formative rule of inference may be applied at this point. Consequently, the string of symbols Homer likes Marge would be predicted to be a sentence of English. And rightly so, as this string of symbols is, indeed, a well-formed sentence of Eng-lish. Completed proofs like the preceding were represented in trees like this.                                              S                    NP   VP                                                 V                        NP         Homer             likes                                 Marge But notice now that this tree matches the antecedent of the transforma-tive rule of inference in Figure 1 above, as the string Homer likes Postulates: S Formative rules of inference:  S  #  NP   VP      NP  #  Homer       VP  #  V   NP       V # likes      NP  #  Marge  Transformative rules of inference:  [S  NP1    V    NP2 ]    $              [S  NP2  is  liked  by  NP1 ] 290 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS Marge can be "bracketed as a sentence" of the form NP V NP.25 Conse-quently, this rule of inference allows us to deduce Marge is liked by Homer from it. And to "bracket it as a sentence" as well.26 This means that this new string is also predicted to be a sentence of English. Again correctly. It should be clear that the new sentence is a transformation of the first one—hence the name transformative rule of inference. There are several important differences between these early genera-tive grammars and the grammars we have been developing in this book. One is immediately obvious, and deals with the directionality of the deductions. Generative grammars start from sentences and work their way 'down' to lexical items; the grammars we have been developing in this book move in the opposite direction—from underived lexical items 'down' to derived sentences. Beyond this, early generative grammars involve a division of labor in which formative rules do nothing but concatenate and categorize, while transformative rules do everything else (infix, circumfix, interfix, metathesize, delete, reduplicate, mutate, supplete, recategorize, assimi-late, dissimilate, syllabify, invert, reorder, and so on). Even more im-portantly, early generative grammars focused on sounds and disregarded meanings altogether. In the grammars we have been developing, on the other hand, grammars are as much about sound as they are about mean-ing. In fact, they are about pairing them to one another.  We close this section by presenting one of the most influential anal-yses ever casted within generative grammars—the analysis of the Eng-lish auxiliary. The facts that need to be accounted for are those of Sec-tion 2 above. Within early generative grammars, these facts were ac-counted for in terms of the formative rules in (53) and the transforma-tive rule in (54): (53) VP  →  AUX   V  AUX  →  (MOD   BSE)   (PFC   PAP)   (PRG   PRP)  MOD  → can | could | may | might | must | shall | should | will |    would  PFC →  have  PRG →  be  V →  eat | drink | … (54)  AFFIX   VERBAL   ⇒   VERBAL + AFFIX,  where AFFIX  ∈                                                             25 The subindices in the rule are not intended to be part of the symbols being generated; they are just annotations to the rule to keep the two NPs apart in the rule. 26 What else can be said about the tree of the new sentence is not something that early generative grammars cared much about. SYNTAX IV: OTHER TOPICS / 291   {BSE, PAP, PRP} and where VERBAL ∈ {MOD, PFC, PRG, V} It should not escape the reader that the second line of (53) is not really a formative rule but rather an abbreviation for eight of them—the eight possibilities that arise from choosing to include or choosing to exclude each of the parenthesized strings (these choices can be made inde-pendently of each other). Similarly, the third line of (53) abbreviates nine formative rules (one per verbal element mentioned to the right of the arrow). And the last line of (53) abbreviates as many formative rules as there are main verbs listed to the right of the arrow (stroked strings are in exclusive disjunction). One of the trees admitted by the formative rules in (53) is given in (55).  (55)                                                              VP                                     AUX                                                 V    MOD  BSE  PFC   PAP   PRG   PRP             may           have                be                                  eat Now, this tree matches the antecedent of the transformative rule in (54). Consequently, this rule can transform (55) into (56). (56)                                                         VP                                   AUX                                          V      MOD         PFC          PRG              may     have + BSE   be + PAP               eat + PRP (56) is a grammatical sentence of English. Or at least would be one once the affixes BSE, PAP, and PRP in it are "spelled out". The transforma-tive rule in (54) is called Affix Hopping. It makes an affix "hop over" a 292 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS verbal element (and somehow integrate with it; that's the intent of the plus sign, anyway). Inversion and control (see Sections 3 and 5 above) would also be achieved via transformative rules. Inversion would be achieved by a transformative rule that says that, if the subject of a sentence may im-mediately precede an auxiliary verb, then it can also follow it directly. This rule was called Subject-Aux Inversion. Control would be attained by a rule that said that the subject of a subordinate sentence could be deleted under identity with the superordinate subject if the subordinating verb was promise but with the object if the subordinating verb was per-suade. This transformative rule was called Equi-NP Deletion. 9 Suggestions for Further Reading: Definite Clause Grammar SWI-PROLOG has a built-in predicate with which we can represent, at least in part, the formative rules of inference of a generative grammar. It is the double-dash arrow. With it we can cast the formative rules of Figure 1 as follows. (57)  s --> np, vp.   np --> [homer].   vp --> v, np.   v --> [likes].   np --> [marge]. (57) is a well-formed PROLOG program (see Program 66, Appendix H). And its statements resemble the formative rules in Figure 1. But appear-ances can be misleading. For starters, consult this program (it is called dcg.swipl). Now try querying it as you would any program—say, by entering (58)  np --> X. We would expect PROLOG to respond with X = [homer], as per the second rule of (52). In fact, it will reply with an error message. The rea-son for this is that the double-dash arrow is not the predicate the PRO-LOG interpreter is working with here; it is just user-friendly notation that it displays for our benefit. To see what PROLOG is really working with, enter listing. PROLOG will respond with the program it has actually consulted. It is the following (up to the order of the clauses). SYNTAX IV: OTHER TOPICS / 293 (59)  np([homer|A], A).   np([marge|A], A).   v([likes|A], A).   s(A, C):-      np(A, B),    vp(B, C).   vp(A, C):-    v(A, B),    np(B, C). The program in (54) seems to be at quite a remove from the program in (52). And from the formative rules in Figure 1, for that matter. But let's analyze it in some detail. As all PROLOG programs worth their salt, (54) makes two kinds of statements. They are the unconditional ones  and the conditional ones. The unconditional statements are made in the first three lines of the program; the conditional ones in the rest. Take the first of the unconditional statements, namely (60)  np([homer|A], A). (60) is a remarkably odd statement to make. At least in a couple of ways. First, formative rules are rewrite statements; they indicate how to replace a symbol by a string of symbols. The statement in (60), on the other hand, does not regard np as a generic symbol to be rewritten. Rather, it considers it a two-place predicate. It moreover asserts that this predicate holds of two things: (a) a list that is split into a head and a tail, and (b) the tail itself. What could the point of this be? (60) is intended to say that Homer is a nounphrase. This it does not say. But it says something very close to it. For, suppose the second ar-gument of (60) was the empty list. If it was, then so would the tail of the first argument be. In other words, the first argument would be [homer|[]]. But this is another way of saying that the first argument of np would be [homer]. So the statement (60) is actually making is that its first argument would be [homer] if its second argument were the empty list. It follows that the right query to make is not (58), but rather (61). (61)   np(X, []).  Entering this query would now yield X = [homer]. And entering semicolons would yield first  X = [marge] and then false. All of this is as desired. 294 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS Why the foregoing should be an efficient implementation of forma-tive rules is something that is best discussed after reading next chapter, where we address the issue of computational complexity. Suffice it to say at this point that entirely similar points can be made about the other two unconditional statements of (59), whose correct associated queries would be (61) above and (62) below. (62)   v(X, []).  But let's turn next to the conditional statements. One of them is (63)  vp(A, C):-    v(A, B),    np(B, C). What (63) is intended to say is that a verbphrase is the concatenation of a verb and a nounphrase. Admittedly, this is not what (63) says. But sup-pose that the second argument C of vp in it was the empty list. This means that the first argument B of the np in (63) would the full noun-phrase (see discussion above). And so would the second argument B of the v be. Consequently, the first argument A of the v would have to be [likes|B]. And so would the first argument A of the vp be. It fol-lows that, if C is the empty list, then a verbphrase is, indeed, the concat-enation of a verb and a nounphrase. To make PROLOG list the verb-phrases of the grammar, we would therefore have to enter (64)  vp(X, []). We leave it as an exercise to the reader to verify that the same holds of (65), which is the other conditional statement of (59). And that the right query for it would be (6). (65)  s(A, C):-     np(A, B),    vp(B, C). (66)   s(X, []).  Programs like (57) are called definite clause grammars. This is why we called (57) dcg.swipl. The format in which its rules are written is called the grammar rule notation. Definite clause grammars (DCGs) are generally taken to be mere translations of formative rules into PROLOG. Yet, as can be gathered from the preceding discussion, they are not quite that. DCGs can gener-ate the same stringsets that formative rules generate (when queries to them are posed in the right way), but they do not embody the same intui-SYNTAX IV: OTHER TOPICS / 295 tions about the stringsets. Formative rules make string-rewrite state-ments while DCGs make string-categorization statements. At least when interpreted the right way. Beyond this, formative rules start from a unique and universal sen-tential postulate (see the postulate S in Figure 1) and derive strings of lexical items from it; DCGs, on the other hand, start from individual lexical items as postulates and derive sentences from them. See for ex-ample the unconditional statements in (59). These lexical postulates are neither unique nor universal. All the DCG rules we have seen thus far involve covert binary rela-tions. Thus, a DCG rule like s --> np, vp involves the binary rela-tions s, np, vp. They are covertly binary because, as explained above, the rule really involves s(A,C), np(A,B), and vp(B,C). Interesting-ly, the DCG formalism allows you to regard these relations as having a flexible valence—which in turn enables you to add more arguments to them.  To see why this flexibility might be useful, let us suppose we wanted to add the pronouns he, him, she, and her to the DCG in (57). As we have seen before, two of these pronouns can occur in subject position and two in object position (the former are he and she and the latter are him and her). To account for this within DCGs we can regard np as a ternary relation and write: (67)  s --> np(nom), vp.   vp --> v, np(acc).   v --> [likes].   np(_) --> [homer].   np(_) --> [marge].   np(nom) --> [he].   np(nom) --> [she].   np(acc) --> [him].   np(acc) --> [her]. Consult this grammar (it is called dcg.i.swipl and is listed in Ap-pendix H as Program 67) and enter s(Sentence, []). You will see that you can now generate our four pronouns in their right positions, so nominative pronouns will appear in subject position and accusative pronouns in object position. Proper names, being unspecified for case, will be able to appear in all positions. To see how, exactly, PROLOG is working with your new code, enter again 296 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS listing. as you will see, PROLOG places the extra argument in initial position. So this flexibility is very useful indeed. It can be used, for example, if we wanted to introduce nounphrases of different numbers or persons (and have the verb agree with them). Or if we had verbs of different va-lences (and want them to combine with the right number of arguments). And we can combine all these extensions with each other as well. More-over, there is nothing to prevent us from regarding representations of meanings—say, referential queries—from serving as additional argu-ments to the relations mentioned in a DCG rule as illustrated below.27 np(masculine, singular, R :: bearer(R,[homer])) -->  [homer]. np(feminine,  singular, R :: bearer(R,[marge])) -->  [marge]. Beyond this, the statement made by a DCG rule may be refined by adding conditions to the ones already found to the right of the arrow (as our appeals to listing have shown, the material to the right of a dou-ble-dash arrow is just a conjunction of conditions).28 These conditions can be any statements that PROLOG can make. But PROLOG is Turing-complete. Consequently, DCGs will be able to formalize not only the formative rules of generative grammars, but the transformative ones as well. DCGs were originally developed in Pereira and Warren (1980). For excellent introductions to them, the reader may turn to Blackburn et al. (2006, Chapters 7 and 8), Matthews (1998, Chapter 11), Covington (1994), Pereira and Shieber (1987), and the references cited therein. 10 Exercises 1.
