But do they run fast enough? After all, speakers seem to pair sound and meaning in no time flat. And, although each of these programs runs fast enough in isolation, would they still run fast in combination? After all, they were only grammatical frag-ments—not complete grammars. And what about facts and rules not considered thus far? Will grammars that include them run fast as well? And if they don't, would this be due to the complexity of the grammars proper or to the shortcomings of the hardware and the software that we used to run them?  The best way to approach these questions is to inquire not about speed, but about efficiency instead. In this book we have proposed a notion of efficiency based on the number of inferences PROLOG had to draw in order to arrive at a result. This seemed appropriate, as we re-garded grammars as deductive systems—collections of statements from which pairings of sound and meaning can be deduced. But to deduce is to carry out inferences. In addition, PROLOG has a built-in facility to count the inferences it draws to answer any query we wish to pose to it. Indeed, in order to calculate the number of inferences associated to a query, call it Q, all we need to do is open a PROLOG session, consult the programs needed to answer Q, and enter time(Q). The PROLOG interpreter will then try to respond to Q and, if it succeeds, it will in ad-dition report the number of inferences it had to make in order to arrive at 300 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS that response. So the inference counts we propose to use are easy indeed to obtain. To be more specific, consider a grammar that generates a nonempty set of pairings of sound and meaning. These pairings will be generated in a certain order (one of them will be the first, another the second, and so on). If this set of pairings is finite, then we can calculate the number of inferences that need to be drawn in order to admit each pairing in the set. Then we could place, in a one-to-one correspondence, the order in which that pairing was generated (henceforth the rank of that pairing) with the number of inferences needed to admit that pairing. This one-to-one correspondence is a rank-to-inference function that describes how inferences grow with rank. It is then tempting to say that the grammar was efficient if this function is polynomial and inefficient if it is not. For, as explained in Chapter 1, the polynomial nature of this function is not just a quantitative advantage; it is also a qualitative one. For, while un-enlightened, case by case, trial and error processes tend to yield nonpol-ynomial performances, processes informed by an understanding of the task at hand tend to yield polynomial performances instead. The calculation of the efficiency (or lack thereof) of a rank-to-inference function can be done fairly automatically. And Appendix G tells how. But this procedure has four shortcomings that must now be addressed. The first is that realistic grammars will generate an infinite set of pairings of sound and meaning. In such cases, calculating the en-tire rank-to-inference function is out of the question, and sampling be-comes unavoidable. In short, we must select some parings of sound and meaning, calculate the number of inferences needed to admit them, con-struct the function that assigns, to each selected pairing its inference count, and determine whether such a function is polynomial or not. If the sample selected was representative, then nothing of importance will have been lost in the process. Take for instance the rank-to-inference function for one of our pre-fixation programs (english/2). The first hundred points of this func-tion can be charted as follows:  CONCLUSION: THE COMPUTATIONAL COMPLEXITY OF LANGUAGE / 301  Figure 1. The rank-to-inference function for the initial output of a pre-fixation program (english/2). Now, if we picked every third of these points and charted them, we would obtain the following graph instead.  Figure 2. The rank-to-inference function for a sample of the initial out-put of a prefixation program (english/2). Clearly, nothing of importance has been lost in this sampling process— at least not for the first hundred points. In both cases a linear function fits the data all but perfectly. Just about the same linear function, in fact. But this was a relatively large sample of the first one-hundred points (it is a sample that stands in a 3:1 ratio to the universe from which it was drawn). By way of contrast, Appendix G calculates the complexity of syllable.swipl from a much smaller sample of the entire universe of generated syllables—a sample that stands in a 1000:1 ratio to the universe from which it was drawn. The second shortcoming we encounter with the process we have y = 3.6552x - 13.867 R! = 0.99907 -50 0 50 100 150 200 250 300 350 400 !" #!" $!" %!" &!" '!!" This is because inference counts do not always grow steadily with rank. For occasions arise in which inference counts remain constant from one rank to the next. So we will henceforth refer to such functions as degree-to-inference functions. We take it that the function in Figure 4 offers a more revealing measure of complexity than the rank-to-inference func-tion in Figure 3. And that degree-to-inference functions of a grammar in general offer a better complexity measure than their rank-to-inference counterparts (when these functions differ). Thus, from now on we will prefer them to rank-to-inference functions (whenever the two functions differ). The third shortcoming of the procedure we have sketched above is that we are not always able to work with actual degree-to-inference functions. Or even with samples of those functions. More often than not, we must settle for approximations to these functions. This can be seen most clearly in Figure 4. Although the curve in that chart is very close to the points in it, the curve does not quite pass through all of them. Consequently, we will want to evaluate the closeness of our approxima-tions to degree-to-inference functions. And here is where the R2 scores come in.  As a glance at the previous charts will reveal, the equations men-tioned in them are always accompanied by an R2 value. An R2 value is a measure of how well an equation fits, models, accounts for, or deter-mines the data. This is why R2 is called the coefficient of determination. In our case, the data to be determined is an actual degree-to-inference function (as represented by the dots in a chart); the fitted equation is that of the approximated function (as represented by the curve that ap-proaches the dots in a chart). R2 values range from 0 to 1, where R2 = 0 means the equation offers no determination whatsoever of the data and R2 = 1 means that the equation models the data perfectly (or determines it fully). The R2 value of .96349 in Figure 4 means that the cubic func-tion mentioned therein determines 96.349% of the data. Speaking generally, the more terms an equation has, the better it will fit the data—and hence the higher its R2 score will be. Consider for ex-ample one of our grammars for suffixation (spanish/2). As it turns out, a linear function provides a very good approximation (> 94%) to the degree-to-inference function for it (or the rank-to-inference function for it, as both functions coincide here):  304 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS  Figure 5. A linear approximation to the degree-to-inference function one of our suffixation grammars (spanish/2). Yet, a quadratic function, having three terms rather than two, provides an even better approximation.1 In fact, it provides a perfect (100%) ap-proximation to the data:  Figure 6. A quadratic approximation to the degree-to-inference function of one of our suffixation grammars (spanish/2). To remain practical, we will content ourselves with approximations of 90% or better. Such approximations are universally regarded as very good ones indeed.2 We will therefore say that the complexity of a grammar will be the order of complexity of the slowest growing func-tion whose fit with the actual degree-to-inference function of the gram-                                                            1 The three terms of a quadratic equation are the x2 term, the x1 term, and the x0 term. The fourth and final shortcoming of the procedure we wish to adopt is that we do not have a measure to calculate the computational com-plexity of rejection. For rejected strings are not generated by a grammar. Thus, they will have no rank, and will not be included in any rank-to-inference function. Consequently, they will not be included in any de-gree-to-inference function either. This is not to say that rejected strings do not involve a number of inferences. Or that PROLOG will not be able to report such number when requested to do so. It is just that we have no natural way of incorporating those numbers in a function that also measures the complexities of the accepted strings. How big of a shortcoming is this? Recall that the reason we got into issues of complexity was that we wanted to account for the fact that native speakers were able to pair sound and meaning fast. But these are the sound and the meaning of accepted expressions, not of rejected ones. It follows that the complexity of rejection is important if we want to evaluate the ability of a grammar to distinguish grammatical from un-grammatical expressions, but not if we want to evaluate our ability to account for the speed with which native speakers can pair the sounds and meanings that they actually pair when they listen and speak gram-matically. To recapitulate, the computational complexity of a grammar is the order of complexity of its degree-to-inference function. This is a func-tion which takes the numbers of inferences needed to accept a pairing of sound and meaning and measures how this number grows by degrees. Degree-to-inference functions are built from rank-to-inference functions, and may even coincide with them. A grammar is simple or efficient if its degree-to-inference function is polynomial; otherwise, it is complex or inefficient. We concede that some times we may only arrive at approxi-mations to the computational complexity of a grammar. This happens when the grammar generates an infinite language (and we must calcu-late our complexity from a sample of the language). And when we work with a function whose fit to the true degree-to-inference function is less than perfect. We also concede that the definition of complexity we have proposed does not measure the complexity of rejecting a non-pairing of sound and meaning. Notice that the notion of complexity we have developed depends not on the time it takes to decide an issue, but rather on the number of infer-ences that must be drawn to do so. Second, the notion proposed is not based on any single number of inferences, but rather on the rate at which these numbers grow. Third, it focuses not on the rate itself, but only on 306 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS whether this rate is polynomial or not. Taken in conjunction, these points allow the proposed notion of complexity to avoid the vagaries mentioned at the outset of this section. Notice in particular that polyno-mial functions are closed, both under arithmetical operations and under function composition. Thus, it can be hoped that the combination of two efficient grammars will still be an efficient grammar itself.  We shall therefore adopt the notion of complexity we have devel-oped in this section. 2 How Complex Are Our Grammars? Having adopted a notion of computational complexity we may now ask how complex (or efficient) our grammars are. At least according to this notion. As it turns out, our grammars are all efficient, as their degree-to-inference functions are polynomial if not better. In fact, they are very efficient, as they are no worse than low-degree polynomial functions. In fact, one is cubic or third degree, a few are quadratic or second degree, and all the rest are linear or first degree—if not logarithmic.3  Computations of the complexities of all our grammars can be found in the companion website to this book (https://sites.google.com/site/ acildlipp).4 As a visit to that website will reveal, the only grammar of cubic complexity is syntax.swipl (see preceding section); the grammars of quadratic complexities are alis.swipl, control. swipl, homorganic.swipl, and syntax.ii.swipl. As to the grammars with logarithmic complexity, they are kinterms.swipl and all the portions of reduplication.swipl other than Bambara. All other grammars are linear in complexity. So our grammars are very efficient. But they are not all equally effi-cient, as we have grammars of different complexities. What accounts for these differences? Interestingly, a variety of factors. Unquestionably, one of them is the built-in operation append. To make this point, let us consider the grammars for control.swipl (Program 60, Appendix H) and homorganic.swipl (Program 33, Appendix H). These two grammars rely on append to perform concatenation. But suppose we were to rewrite these grammars without using this operation. One way to do so would be using definite clause grammars (see Section 9.9). An-other would be using the stroke notation (see Section 2.5). CONCLUSION: THE COMPUTATIONAL COMPLEXITY OF LANGUAGE / 307 applied the first strategy in control.swipl and the second in ho-morganic.swipl.5 As a glance at the complexities of these revised grammars will reveal (see companion website), they are both but linear in complexity. Since the only difference between the original and the revised formulations of these grammars lies in the use of append to perform concatenation, the complexity of the former relative to the latter must be due to this operation. But this should come as no surprise. The relative inefficiency of the append operation in PROLOG—and the response thereto in terms of difference lists—is well known (Matthews 1998, 175ff; Sterling and Shapiro 2001, 283ff; Bratko 2001, §8.5.3; Blackburn et al. 2006, Chs. 6-7). But concatenation via append is not the only source of (relative) complexity in our grammars. Semantic interpretation is another one. The best way to make this point is to consider syntax.swipl. As indicated in the preceding section, this grammar is cubic in its complexi-ty. But consider now valence.swipl (Appendix H, Program 50). This grammar is just like syntax.swipl. As to the complexity of syntax.ii.swipl, its source has to be the same as that of syntax.swipl, as these two grammars differ only in the way they handle oblique nominal clauses, not in the semantic in-terpretation they perform.  It should be noted that semantic interpretation will not, in and of it-self, lead to complexity. kinterms.swipl carries out semantic inter-pretation. Yet, it is not quadratic in complexity. In fact, it is not even linear. Its complexity is only logarithmic. And voice.swipl also carries out semantic interpretation. Yet, it is not quadratic in complexity either. It is only linear (albeit barely). What, exactly, accounts for the complexity of the semantic interpretation of syntax.swipl is some-thing that must be left for future research. It may well be, however, that the source of complexity in this grammar is generalized quantification, as neither kinterms.swipl nor voice.swipl incorporate it.  But there is yet a third source of complexity in our grammars. It is the dissimilation (or inequivalence) clause used in alis.swipl (see Program 38 in Appendix H and its complexity calculation in the com-panion website). To show that append is not the source of this com-plexity, consider an append-free version of alis.swipl (see Pro-gram 71, Appendix H). It still has quadratic complexity (see its calcula-tion in our companion website). And these programs do not involve semantic interpretation. So semantics cannot be their source of complex-ity either.  Interestingly, there is another grammar that involves dissimilation (or inequivalence). It is diminutive.swipl (see Program 39, Ap-pendix H). Technically, it is linear in complexity, as the R2 score of the linear function that best approximates is 0.9235. But the R2 score of the quadratic function that best approximates it is all but perfect (0.99999). Such are the vagaries of statistical thresholds. *          *          * We have discussed the complexities involved in having syntax. swipl and syntax.ii.swipl generate pairings of sound and meaning. But we could also calculate the complexities of speaking (computing a sound given a meaning) and understanding (computing a meaning given a sound). To calculate the complexities involved in these, the modalities of language, you may enter the following queries in suc-cession. The first of these queries will return the number of inferences needed to find the meaning ME of a given sound so; the second will CONCLUSION: THE COMPUTATIONAL COMPLEXITY OF LANGUAGE / 309 yield the number of inferences required to find the sound SO paired to a given meaning me. time(english(so, _, ME, _)). time(english(SO, _, me, _)). These queries can be repeated for different sounds and meanings, and the inference counts for them can be charted as before. Thus, the com-plexity of understanding within syntax.swipl is linear (see Figure 8), and the complexity of speaking relative to this grammar is even less than linear; it is logarithmic (see Figure 9).  Figure 8. The linear complexity of the understanding modality relative  to syntax.swipl.   Figure 9. The logarithmic complexity of the speaking modality  relative to syntax.swipl.  Similar calculations are possible with syntax.ii.swipl, con-trol.swipl, voice.swipl, spanish.swipl, and indeed with any actual grammar—one that generates pairings of sound and meaning. *"+"#),)33-"."&%&',(" /0"+"!,111%# .#!!!!!" !" #!!!!!" $!!!!!" %!!!!!" !" '#"310 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS 3 How Complex is Language? The grammar fragments we have developed in this book account for a very small portion of language. That is because they were developed as illustrative of the kinds of procedures languages involve as they pair sound and meaning, and not with the goal of accounting for a large body of data. Yet, the facts these grammars cover are quite diverse. They in-volve both sound and meaning. And the various ways to pair them. Both at the level of words and at the level of sentences. And across a wide range of languages. The question thus arises as to whether the grammars we have developed in this book are representative of the set of gram-mars as a whole. For, if they were, then we would have a measure of the complexity of language itself. At least if we understood language as a procedure for pairing sound and meaning and if we construed complex-ity as we have done above. If the grammars developed in this book are indeed representative of grammars as a whole, language would then exhibit cubic complexity (the degree-to-inference function of grammars would be no more than third-degree polynomial). This result would be interesting in itself, of course. But it would also be interesting because it would provide the beginnings of an explanation of the fact that language, as a procedure for pairing sound and meaning, is not just possible, but also viable. Determining whether human languages have low polynomial com-plexities is a fundamental question that guides current research in lin-guistics. As a matter of fact, a good number of theories of grammar al-ready predict that they do. Consider, for example, context-free gram-mars. They are grammars that consist solely of what we called formative rules in the preceding chapter. It has been shown that they have cubic parsing complexities (Hopcroft and Ullman 1979, 145). Or take tree-adjunction grammars. They are mild extensions of context-free gram-mars (see Joshi 2003). Their parsing complexities have been shown to be sextic (op cit. p. 491). Along the same lines, parsers with low poly-nomial complexities have been developed for head-driven phrase struc-ture grammars and for minimalist grammars—even if the actual degrees of the polynomials involved in these functions will vary from grammar to grammar (cf. Haji-Abdolhosseini and Penn 2003; Harkema 2005). If it turned out that human languages had low polynomial complexi-ties, then the theories of language should predict this. That way we would have a principled reason for their efficiency. It should be acknowledged that such a prediction is not forthcoming from the gram-mars of the kind proposed in this book. For these grammars are but sets of PROLOG clauses which are, mostly, of the following format. CONCLUSION: THE COMPUTATIONAL COMPLEXITY OF LANGUAGE / 311 predicate(SO,SC,ME,MC):- C1, C2, ... CN. But these clauses will lead to unbounded complexity unless and until substantial constraints are placed on what conditions C1 through CN can say.6 And here is where contemporary linguistic theories come in. In-cluded among them are the context-free grammars, the tree adjunction grammars, the head-driven phrase structure grammars, and the minimal-ist grammars mentioned in the preceding paragraph. And others as well, like categorial grammars (Steedman and Baldridge 2011) and lexical-functional grammars (Falk 2001). They can all be regarded as theories on what conditions C1 through CN can in fact say—in order to describe language in a general and principled way. 4 Shortcomings of PROLOG Programming We have seen in this chapter that the built-in predicate append can be a significant source of computational complexity. As it turns out, this is because append scans the first list it is given, item by item, before it can concatenate it with any other list. As a consequence of this, ap-pend will have a complexity that is linear on the length of the first list. In an ideal world, this should not happen. The PROLOG interpreter should instead concatenate the lists it is given regardless of their lengths. For if it did, then the complexity of the append operation would be a constant function on the lengths of the lists given—long lists would be as hard to concatenate as short ones.7 The fact that we are not in such an ideal world accounts for one of the shortcomings of PROLOG pro-gramming.  Sometimes, append can be avoided—for example when we want to concatenate lists of fixed lengths. See for example syllable.swipl or any of the grammars in interfixation.swipl.8 But what if we wanted to concatenate lists of indefinite lengths? The exhaustive listing used in these programs is of no use here. True, in some of these latter cases append can be replaced by list-matching. At least if have at our                                                             6 See Barton, Berwick, and Ristad (1987) for ways to show that a grammatical formalism has NP-Complete complexity. The grammatical formalism we have proposed also encom-passes a number of ancillary rules like instantiation clauses and definitions (cf. our defini-tions of phones, properties, entailments, lexical decompositions, and so on). As to the unconditional statements made by a grammar, they are already encompassed by the for-malism mentioned the text above, as they can be regarded as conditional statements in which CN = C1 = true, where true is a condition that is always met (so an un-conditional statement is a conditional statement whose conditions are always met). 7 See the references cited in Section 10.2 for more discussion. 8 Programs 7 and 21, Appendix H. 312 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS disposal richer representations of lists.9 This works because these richer representations identify the ends of lists. And this is what we need in order to concatenate a list without scanning it item by item (see Bratko 2001, §8.5.3). Unfortunately, this richer representation also introduces a linguistically unmotivated complication into our grammars. And it pre-cludes our definition of linguistic sound as a list of linguistically moti-vated units—be they phones, syllables, prosodic feet, or what have you. Moreover, it is not clear that this representational strategy can be used every time we need to call on append. But the inefficiencies of append are not the worst of our problems. To engage in perfectly declarative logic programming is to convey a deductive system to a machine that can then deliver, on demand and without further ado, the set of deductions admitted by the system con-veyed. PROLOG programming is still far from being perfectly declara-tive logic programming. And this for no fault of the PROLOG pro-gramming language itself. Indeed, at the time of writing, deductive systems cast in the PROLOG programming language (what we have been calling PROLOG programs) are conveyed to machines that carry out their deductions in ways that exceed what the deductive systems detail. And this excess is of great consequence. Not just for the order in which they deliver their deductions, but for the very identity of the deductions themselves. The point is most visible in the context of recursive rules. For, sup-pose we had a PROLOG program that made the single, unquestionably true, statement that if it rains it rains: rains :- rains. Unfortunately, issuing the simple query "rains." will make PROLOG enter into an unproductive loop from which it cannot escape. For, in order to verify whether the head of the rule obtains, the PROLOG inter-preter goes on to verify whether the body of the rule obtains. But to ver-ify whether the body obtains, PROLOG is sent again to verify the head (the statement above is, remember, the only one in the program). At this point, the process begins anew. As a consequence of this, PROLOG will just run recklessly until it consumes its resources and halts without issu-ing any useful response. Now, it is doubtless that the rule above is perfectly uninformative. But it is also logically true. Consequently, it should not disable the de-ductive system that adopts it. The rule in question is a perfectly gram-                                                            9 These are the difference lists mentioned earlier. See our discussion of the grammar rule notation of definite clause grammars in Section 9.9. CONCLUSION: THE COMPUTATIONAL COMPLEXITY OF LANGUAGE / 313 matical PROLOG program. And, if the machines running PROLOG would only stick to the information in that program, they would respond to the query with false (as one cannot prove that it rains from the tau-tology that if it rains it rains). They would certainly not be disabled by it. For an example that strikes closer to home, recall that kin-ship.swipl had a long list of facts to define the spouse relation (see Program 11, Appendix H). But one half of these facts simply stated the converse of the other half, as the spouse relation is symmetric. As we wrote them, we had to resist the natural temptation to eliminate one half of the spouse facts and replace them with the acknowledgement that the spouse relation is indeed symmetric: spouse(A,B) :- spouse(B,A). Yet, as the reader should verify, had we done that, a query of  findall([A,B], spouse(A,B), C). would have whipped PROLOG into a looping frenzy from which it would never have recovered. In fact, PROLOG returns the message  ERROR: Out of global stack. Machines that carry out computations according to a PROLOG pro-gram make two choices that go beyond the statements made by the pro-gram. One regards the order in which the statements of the program are applied; the other pertains the order in which the conditions of a condi-tional statement are verified. In short, to reach a desired conclusion, machines apply the statements and the conditions in the order in which they were written. And tries to verify any statement or condition it hits before going on to the next. This is what is called a top-bottom, depth-first, left-to-right search strategy. But this search strategy is not the only one an interpreter could follow. A bottom-up strategy, for example, is one that begins with established facts and tries to combine them in legit-imate ways (according to a program) in order to reach the desired goal. A breadth-first strategy skips a condition that it cannot verify at the current level of embedding, moves on to the next condition, and returns to the one it skipped once it has finished checking all the other condi-tions of the rule at the current level of embedding. The top-bottom, depth-first, left-to-right search strategy is of enor-mous importance for a rule with a recursion in its first condition. Like the rules we have seen above. Or with coordination (see Chapter 9). So what should we do about left-recursion? In their lucid discussion of this issue, Sterling and Shapiro (2001, 132) state that the best solution to the problem of left recursion is avoidance. Yet, they also admit, candidly, 314 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS that "unfortunately, it is not generally possible to remove all occurrences of left recursion". As examples of the impossibility of carrying out this removal task, Sterling and Shapiro mention quite a few elegant pro-grams, including one for the definition of number.10 And we have also argued, on the grounds of simplicity and principledness, against avoid-ing left-recursion in the case of coordination (see again Chapter 9).  So, if left-recursion cannot be avoided, what should change is the in-terpreter that runs programs with the unavoidable recursions, as we concluded in Chapter 9. We should add that coordination and the sym-metry of the predicates of a model are not the only linguistic reasons to preserve left-recursions. There is also recursive modification. We saw an example of this in adverbials.swipl, specifically in the follow-ing rule, whose first two lines are identical up to the names given to their variables. english(SO,[vbl,phr]):-  english(SO1,[vbl,phr]),  english(SO2,[adv,phr]),  append(SO1,SO2,SO). This rule is unable to reject ungrammatical strings. And no amount of condition-reordering will do. Or appeals to richer representations of lists, for that matter. The same holds with other types of modification. Nomi-nal, for example: english(SO,[nom,phr]):-  english(SO1,[nom,phr]),  english(SO2,[adj,phr]),  append(SO1,SO2,SO). Also problematic in this regard are sentential subjects. At least in subject-initial languages like English. For these subjects would call for verbal clauses [vbl,cls] left-recursing inside verbal clauses [vbl,cls], as per the following rule.  english(SO, [vbl,cls]):-   english(SO1,[vbl,cls]),   english(SO2,[vbl,phr],   append(SO1, SO2, SO). We should add that left-recursion is not just called for in syntax; it is necessary in morphology as well. As a matter of fact, we have encoun-tered it already in diminutive.swipl and in the Bambara portion                                                             10 This is essentially Program 9 of Appendix H. CONCLUSION: THE COMPUTATIONAL COMPLEXITY OF LANGUAGE / 315 of reduplication.swipl.  Neither one of these grammar frag-ments is able to reject ungrammatical strings. And left-recursion is the reason why. 5 Facing Left-Recursion Left-recursion thus seems to be a core feature of human language, and we would be ill-advised to ban it from our grammars. What we need is a different way to run our grammars. One way to do so is to develop a program that will run the grammars the way we would like them to be run. Such programs are known as parsers. In this section we present a parser that will allow us to run grammars with left-recursions in a way that is not disabled by them.11 We begin by turning once again to leftrecursion.swipl (Program 64, Appendix H). As pointed out in Section 9.7 above, this grammar has a disabling instance of left-recursion. To develop a parser that will avert the noxious looping it induces, let us first reformulate this grammar in terms of the formative rules of inference discussed in Sec-tion 9.8 above—but stated in a way that PROLOG can understand— straight away, and without further ado. Here is one way to do so. rule(nom, [homer]). rule(nom, [marge]). rule(cnj, [  and]). rule(cnj, [   or]).  rule(nom, [nom,cnj,nom]). In the present context, rule(A,B) means that A entails B (or that an A may consist of B). Notice that this reformulation preserves the left-recursion of leftrecursion.swipl, as the last line of the preced-ing code embeds (a sound of category) nom as the leftmost constituent of (a sound of category) nom.12 A parser that can run this grammar without being disabled by the left-recursion in it is this:                                                              11 We are indebted to an anonymous reviewer for the contents of this section. 12 The code we have just introduced differs in a couple of trivial respects from that of leftrecursion.swipl: where the latter has [nom,wrd], the former has nom, and where the latter has cnj, the former has [cnj]. We have indulged in these differ-ences solely for the sake of readability. 316 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS parse([], String, String).  parse([Goal | Goals], [Goal | String], Rest) :-  parse(Goals, String, Rest).  parse([Goal | Goals], [Item | String0], Rest) :-  rule(Lhs, [Item | Rhs]),  parse(Rhs, String0, String1),  parse([Goal | Goals], [Lhs | String1], Rest).                                  A bottom-up parser  The preceding code is a procedure for defining parse as a ternary rela-tion between lists. The intended meaning of this predicate is such that parse(A,B,C) is true if and only if A parses B except for its tail end C (but bearing in mind that the tail end of a given list may be as big as all of the given list and as small as none of it—in which case the tail end would be nothing but the empty list). We say that A parses B completely when there is nothing left in C to parse. In other words, when C is empty—or, equivalently, when it is true that parse(A,B,[]). Of particular importance for us will be the case in which A parses B completely and, in addition, there is but a single item in A. Such a member would then be the category of the entire string B being parsed. It follows that the queries we will be mostly interested in will be of the following form. parse([SC], SO, []). We have collected all the rule and parse clauses above in a program called bup.swipl (See Program 71, Appendix H). The reader is now invited to consult this program and enter the following query. parse([nom], [homer,and,marge], []). PROLOG will respond true, which is to say that nom is the category of the entire string [homer,and,marge]. Or that [homer,and, marge] is a sound of category nom. This much, of course, leftre-cursion.swipl could do on its own. But let us now enter  parse([nom], [homer, and, bart], []). This time PROLOG will respond with false, which is to say that it is not able to parse [homer, and, bart] as a nom. In fact, it will not be able to parse this string as any category whatsoever, as the following exchange will reveal. CONCLUSION: THE COMPUTATIONAL COMPLEXITY OF LANGUAGE / 317 ?- parse([SC], [homer, and, bart], []). false This is, of course, as desired, since our grammar did not allow for the phrase [homer,and,bart] either. Crucially, however, leftre-cursion.swipl loops on this query and runs out of memory without yielding any answer. And the same happens with any other list that is not accepted by the grammar.  Figuring out how the parser above works is a bit involved. The first clause of the parse procedure says that the empty list of categories will parse the empty string (for, since the empty string can be said to occur at the beginning of any given string, the clause says that the empty list of categories will parse that empty string and leave the original string, in its entirety, as the unparsed remainder). The second clause says that, if a particular string can be parsed into a list of categories, then that string, extended by one initial category can also be parsed by that list of categories (as long as that list is also extended by the same initial category). The third clause of the procedure says that a list [Goal|Goals] can parse a string [Item|String0] (except for a tail end Rest) if three conditions are met. The first is that the grammar contains a rule that expands some category Lhs as a string [Item|Rhs]. The second is that Rhs parse String0 except for a tail end String1. The third is that [Goal|Goals] can parse [Lhs|String1] (except for a tail end Rest). Key to this procedure is the fact that the rule invoked in the third clause is selected on the basis of Item, which is the first item of the string to be parsed (and without regard to the goals to be accomplished at the moment). In other words, it is selected after analyzing the string to be parsed word by word. Thus, it is parsed bottom-up. To gather the importance of this fact, consider an alternative parser that we could have written instead: parse([], String, String). parse([Goal | Goals], [Goal | String], Rest):-  parse(Goals, String, Rest).  parse([Goal | Goals], String0, Rest):-  rule(Goal, Rhs),  parse(Rhs, String0, String1),  parse(Goals, String1, Rest).  A top-down parser  318 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS This parser is entirely like the preceding one. Except in one crucial respect: the rule invoked in its third clause is selected on the basis of the first item of the list of goals to be accomplished at the moment (and without regard to the words of the string to be parsed). Thus, it is parsed top-down.  As we might expect, this second parser fails for the same reasons as leftrecursion.swipl, looping fruitlessly and without end as soon as it is asked to parse an ungrammatical string. You can verify this for yourself by consulting tdp.swipl and entering the last two queries above (tdp.swipl is a program that combines the rule clauses and the second set of parse clauses; see Program 72, Appendix H). The bottom-up parser discussed in this section will avert the noxious looping created by the left-recursion in our grammars for modification, sentential subjects, and reduplication. All that is needed to verify this is to reformulate the relevant grammars in terms of the rule predicate and enter the appropriate parse queries. Avoiding the looping in di-minutive.swipl would take more work, as this grammar involves more than just append.  The purpose of this section has been to show how the noxious effects of left-recursion can be averted by adopting a different parsing strategy. This should not be construed as an endorsement of bottom-up parsing, of reformulating grammars to meet the specifications of parsers, or even of parsers as supplements to grammars. For it is our belief that well-designed grammars will do their own parsing in the context of well-designed computing environments. 6 Some of the Perils Ahead All the grammars we have seen in this book generate but small fragmen-ts of language. Our commitments to accuracy thus compel us, going forward, to enhance the fragments to be generated. But this can be dan-gerous. Take backtracking, for example. Backtracking is what PROLOG engages in when it has followed through on an alternative that does not, ultimately, work. When found in such circumstances, PROLOG simply traces its steps back to the point at which the alternative arose and makes a different choice.  Backtracking is a workhorse of PROLOG, and is one of the keys to its declarative nature, as it guides the behavior of the interpreter without the intervention of the programmer or the user. But backtracking is also the source of massive inefficiencies, as PROLOG does not remember the results of any of the computations it made while pursuing a failed alternative. And if some of those results are needed after they are forgot-CONCLUSION: THE COMPUTATIONAL COMPLEXITY OF LANGUAGE / 319 ten, then they will have to be recomputed—once again and from scratch. The inefficiencies that ensue from this may translate into exponential complexities as we enhance the accuracy of our grammars (Pereira and Shieber 1987, 146). Fortunately, there is a response to backtracking inefficiencies. It is dynamic programming. Stripped to its essentials, dynamic programming is the attempt to make an interpreter remember what computations have been carried out already so that they do not have to be done again. In practice, what is called for is the development of a special kind of par-ser—one that stores previously parsed strings in charts or tables, and then retrieves them from such locations as needed. This parsing strategy is known as chart-parsing. As it turns out, chart-processing can be done either top-down or bottom-up. So chart-parsing and bottom-up parsing are compatible, and we do not have to choose between parsers that might be needed for left-recursion and parsers that might be needed for backtracking inefficiencies. It should be pointed out, though, depending on the grammars used, chart-parsing may bring about some massive computational complexities of its own. As massive as top-down back-tracking, in fact (see Pereira and Shieber 1987, 162f). Another potential source of exponential peril arises with ambiguity. Ambiguity in language is commonplace. As we have seen, it may arise from coordination (1), prepositional phrase attachment (2)-(3), affixa-tion (4), or simple modification (5). And this list is only a start. (1)  old men and women (Section 6.1) (2)  I decided on the boat (Section 8.2)  (3)  We found the guy with the map (Section 8.3) (4)  counterrevolutionary (Section 4.16.1)  (5)  small animal clinic (Section 6.8.1)  The problem with ambiguity is that it produces an explosion in the number of parses that the interpreter has to wade through in order to carry out any of the tasks we would like grammars to perform, be it analysis, synthesis, speaking, or understanding. Unchecked, this explo-sion will be nonpolynomial (Church and Patil 1982). One nice way to bring this out is by considering the growth in the number of possible constituent structures (or parses) allowed by binary coordination. The listing below includes coordinations of increasing lengths (followed by the number of binary parses each allows).13                                                             13 To see what is going on in this listing, it will suffice to notice that there are exactly two ways to parse a three-coordinant phrase; they are [[A and B] and C], on the one hand, and 320 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS Ann (1) Ann and Bob (1)      Ann and Bob and Cam (2) Ann and Bob and Cam and Dan (5) Ann and Bob and Cam and Dan and Eva (14) Ann and Bob and Cam and Dan and Eva and Fay (42) Ann and Bob and Cam and Dan and Eva and Fay and Gil (132) Ann and Bob and Cam and Dan and Eva and Fay and Gil and Han (429) Ann and Bob and Cam and Dan and Eva and Fay and Gil and Han and Ian  (1430)   Ann and Bob and Cam and Dan and Eva and Fay and Gil and Han and Ian  and Joe (4862) … As it turns out, the number of binary parses of a coordination with n conjunctions is the nth Catalan number (n ≥ 0). But this means that the number of parses that an interpreter needs to wade through as it deals with binary coordination grows factorially. And factorial growth is, alas, nonpolynomial (see Section 1.5). But there is more. In Section 6.6 we noticed that whenever sounds of word-level category [_,wrd] combine with sounds of sentential cate-gory [vbl,cls], the former invariably come before the latter: (6) a.  fact[nom, wrd]  +       that the world is round[vbl, cls]  b.  believe[vbl, wrd]  +       that the world is round[vbl, cls]  c.  aware[adj, wrd] +       that the world is round[vbl, cls]  d.  in[prp, wrd] +       that the world is round[vbl, cls]  e.  so[adv, wrd] +       that the world be round[vbl, cls] Now, if we were to account for all of these cases separately, as in (7) below, then we would be missing the relevant generalization—namely that word-level expressions precede sentential-level expressions. (7) english(SO, [nom,phr]):-   english(SO1, [nom, wrd]),   english(SO2, [vbl, cls]),   append(SO1, SO2, SO).  english(SO, [vbl,phr]):-   english(SO1, [vbl, wrd]),   english(SO2, [vbl, cls]),   append(SO1, SO2, SO).                                                                                                                        [A and [B and C], on the other (this listing was taken from material posted by Gerald Gaz-dar at http://www.informatics.susx.ac.uk). CONCLUSION: THE COMPUTATIONAL COMPLEXITY OF LANGUAGE / 321  english(SO, [adj,phr]):-   english(SO1, [adj, wrd]),   english(SO2, [vbl, cls]),   append(SO1, SO2, SO).  english(SO, [prp,phr]):-   english(SO1, [prp, wrd]),   english(SO2, [vbl, cls]),   append(SO1, SO2, SO).  english(SO, [adv,phr]):-   english(SO1, [adv, wrd]),   english(SO2, [vbl, cls]),   append(SO1, SO2, SO). One way to capture this generalization would be to factor out the infor-mation about linear order from the five rules in (7), and to state that in-formation explicitly, once and once only, as a single, separate, linear ordering principle. Although we will not attempt to spell out this idea any further, we will point out that Principledness seems to compel us to move towards more abstract rules—rules that state immediate constitu-ency (ID) separately from linear order (LP). Unfortunately, as Barton, Berwick & Ristad (1987) have shown, trying to recognize the expres-sions of language by such ID/LP grammars might be an NP-Complete problem.14 To say that a problem is NP-Complete (or NPC) is to say that it is a decision problem that meets two critical conditions. The first is that there has to be a polynomial method to check that a solution to the problem is, in fact, a solution to the problem. This is the nondeterminis-tic polynomial (or NP) character of the problem. The second condition is that every other decision problem for which this checking result holds has to be reducible to it (with polynomial ease). This is the completeness (or C) of the problem. NP-Complete problems have no known polyno-mial solutions (notice that checking that a solution is a solution is not the same thing as downright finding the solution). And it is widely be-lieved that no such solutions exist.15 So the recognition problem for grammars with principled accounts of word order has no known poly-nomial solution. And it looks like the reason for this is that no such so-lution exists.                                                             14 See Carpenter (2003, §9.7) for more discussion. 15 Whether they actually do or not is one of the principal unsolved problems of computer science.  322 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS So we seem to encounter exponential complexity at every turn. Such are the perils ahead. It should be emphasized that the trouble with expo-nential complexity is twofold. Quantitatively, exponential complexity is such that it will overwhelm, sooner or later, any computational re-sources brought to task. Qualitatively, exponential complexity is a tell-tale sign that no insight into the nature of a problem has been gained, and that we are stuck with a case-by-case, trial-and-error, search. So it may be that the average sentence is only a few words long. Or that very long sentences are exceedingly rare. And it is certainly the case that exponential growths can start slow. So it could turn out that the full brunt of exponential growth will be seldom felt in real-life linguistic processing. It follows that these pragmatic considerations can alleviate our quantitative woes. In fact, they may even bode well for practical processing of  real language (Carroll 2003, 245). Yet, they do nothing to mitigate our qualitative woes.  To overcome these concerns we might need to make gains in compu-tational efficiency. And we might need a better understanding of the way humans actually process language as well. Perhaps most important-ly, however, we will need a deeper understanding of the nature of lan-guage itself. For it could well be that deeper analyses of language will keep some of the problems noted above at bay. Take the issue of ID/LP grammars. As it turns out, the facts in (6) seem to obey a deeper princi-ple than the ordering generality mentioned above. It is that they are all instances of a single, underspecified rule, that applies to the sounds of all [H,wrd] categories, regardless of whether H is a nominal, verbal, adjectival, prepositional, or adverbial specification. The rule, then, would be something like this.16 english(SO, [H, phr]):-  english(SO1, [H, wrd]),  english(SO2, [vbl, cls]),  append(SO1, SO2, SO). Notice that the desired ordering principle now follows, as a consequence, from the append condition on this rule. No ID/LP rules had to be in-voked. And, as a bonus, overall simplicity was enhanced. We have re-duced five rules to one—one that says less, in fact, than any one of the original five.                                                             16 See Section 9.6. 323 Appendix A Getting Started with PROLOG on a Windows Platform  Here you will learn how to do five important things—how to install a PROLOG interpreter, how to install all the programs discussed in this book, how to consult any of these programs, how to create a new PROLOG program, how to revise an existing one, and how to display phonetic characters properly. You can get a PROLOG interpreter by downloading it from the internet—safely, legally, and for free. To do this, go to a computer that is connected to the internet and follow these steps.  1. You should have succeeded in installing the SWI-PROLOG interpreter 324 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS in your computer. SWI PROLOG is an excellent PROLOG interpreter being developed at the University of Amsterdam. SWI-PROLOG is be-ing updated all the time. To get more recent versions of this interpreter, just follow the instructions above. You should be advised, though, that the behavior of SWI-PROLOG described in this book corresponds to SWI-PROLOG Version 5.10—the most current version of the interpreter at the time of writing. 2 How to Install all the Programs Discussed in this Book A PROLOG program is a procedure that a computer equipped with a PROLOG interpreter can carry out. Go to this book's companion website  (https://sites.google.com/site/acildlipp). 2. Find the Programs link therein. 3. Click on it. A .zip file will download.  4. Double-click on it. A folder will appear. 5. In Explorer, browse to c:\prolog\bin 6. Open the folder mentioned on Step 4. You will see lots of files there. 7. Drag all of these files out of that folder and into the c:\prolog folder.  8. Close the c:\prolog folder and trash the folder you just emptied.  All the programs discussed in this book should now be properly in-stalled in the computer. If the Windows versions of the programs did not display well, the Unix versions should. 3 How to Consult an Installed PROLOG Program To have a computer swiftly consult an installed PROLOG program you will first need to prepare that computer. To do this, install both a PRO-LOG interpreter and a set of PROLOG programs on that computer (see previous instructions). At this point, take the following steps.  1. Go to c:\prolog and drag the file swipl.exe using the right mouse button onto a convenient location (perhaps the Desktop). 2. Upon dropping the file, a menu will pop up. Select "Create Shortcut Here".  GETTING STARTED WITH PROLOG ON A WINDOWS PLATFORM / 325  Caution: you should ignore the start menu shortcut and opt instead for the one you have just constructed. After a welcome message, you will be placed at the prompt (the cursor will rest right after a question mark followed by a dash). There, type the following (but without forgetting the single quotes or the two periods):  ['blather.swipl']. 5. Hit Enter. Look for the program called test.swipl. Double click on it. You will see it is a blank text file. Write in it the program you want to create. You do this the same way you would enter text in any text file (as to what text you should enter, that is pretty much what this book is about). Once you are done, save it in the same location it currently is, but using the "Save as" mo-dality. When prompted, give it the name you want (but be sure the name ends in .swipl).  This will create the program you want to create and leave the original test.swipl file for when you want to create another program.  If you have second thoughts about the program you have just created and want to revise it, see next section. If you delete test.swipl by mistake, you may take any other pro-gram you have installed and save it in the same location it currently is using Save-As. When prompted, give it the name you want as indicated above. This will create a copy of the old program under a new name (the name you just gave it). To revise the text of the new copy with the pro-gram you want to create, see next section. 326 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS 5 How to Revise an Installed PROLOG Program Oftentimes you will want to revise a program that you have installed. Thus, you may type in new text, select and delete, backspace to delete, select and overwrite, copy-and-paste, and so on. You will of course have to respect the grammar of PROLOG if you want your revisions to run. The essen-tials of this grammar are presented in this book. Or in the resources men-tioned in Appendix C. Once you have revised a program, remember to save the revisions. Otherwise they will have no effect.  Also note that you will have to consult the revised program again (see Section 3) so the interpreter is made aware of the revisions. 6 How to Display Phonetic Characters Properly In this book we use phonetic characters to represent linguistic sounds. To check whether your set-up will display these characters properly, consult phone.swipl, which is one of the programs you have in-stalled (see Section 2 above). If you need instructions on how to consult programs, see Section 3. Once you have been placed at the prompt, write the following (with-out forgetting to write the period): phone(X). You should then hit Enter, wait for a response, and follow it up by entering semicolons repeatedly until you get false for an answer. If PROLOG responded, at any point, that X equals a box or that X equals a question mark, then you are having trouble displaying phonetic charac-ters properly. Fortunately, this problem is easy to solve. All you have to do is change the font the console is using to one that supports something called "Unicode". To do this, do as follows: 1. With swipl running in the console, right click the title bar for the console window and select Properties.  2. Select the Fonts tab.  3. Choose "Consolas" from the list that appears (it is about halfway down the window). As of this writing, Consolas is the only font in the Windows console that supports Unicode.  That should do it. But make sure by running the phone(X) test again. No boxes or question marks should now appear. 327 Appendix B Getting Started with PROLOG on a Mac Platform  Here you will learn how to do five important things—how to install a PROLOG interpreter, how to install all the programs discussed in this book, how to consult any of these programs, how to create a new PROLOG program, and how to revise an existing one. 1 How to Install a PROLOG Interpreter  A PROLOG interpreter is a piece of software that will enable a comput-er to run PROLOG programs. The order in which it generates a given pairing is called the rank of that pair-ing.  Suppose a PROLOG interpreter consults spanish.swipl. To de-termine whether this grammar makes a particular pairing, the interpreter must make a number of inferences. We can determine this number by making the interpreter count them (see again Chapter 1).1 The number of inferences the interpreter must make to recognize a particular pairing grows with the rank of that pairing. This growth can be charted as fol-lows.2                                                              1 To measure the relevant complexities accurately we must disregard the inference count of the first query of a session, which reflects preliminary activity of the PROLOG inter-preter. To appreciate the differences between initial and subsequent queries, launch a new PROLOG session, consult spanish.swipl, and enter the following query twice: time(spanish([adan,mira,a,eva],[sentence],[adam,watches,e ve],[proposition])). The first query will take more inferences than the second. And all subsequent entries of this query will yield as many inference counts as the second one did. I am indebted to Roger Parks for help with this issue.  2 Caution: it will usually make a difference whether or not any of the arguments of a timed query is a variable. If it is, the complexity will usually be higher than if it isn't (find-ing values for variables is harder than checking them—both for PROLOG and for humans). THE EFFICIENCY OF A SIMPLE GRAMMAR: SPANISH.SWIPL / 333   The growth of the number of inferences can be approximated very close-ly by a polynomial function—the quadratic function f(x) = 0.1841x2 – 1.8915x + 5.3277. And, as expected, the approximation improves, albeit marginally, as the order of the polynomial function grows. The R2 fit of an exponential function, on the other hand, is inferior, and involves ex-ceedingly small coefficients on the exponential terms. The growth is therefore best described as polynomial—polynomial of low order, in fact. The actual numbers of inferences involved in each pairing are given in the table below. Incidentally, if we eliminate all the repeated values from this table and place the rest in a strictly ascending order, then the resulting function can be approximated very closely by a linear function (see chart below). As explained in Chapter 10 and in Appendix G, in this case we are measuring how computational complexity of grows step by step rather than by rank.   !"#"$%&'(&)*"+"&%',&-)"."-%/*00" 12"#"$%,3-3, $" &$" *$" /$" ($" -$" 3$" 0$" '$" ,$" $" -" &$" &-" *$" *-" /$" 456758&" 9:;!%"<456758&="334 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS RANK NUMBER OF INFERENCES 1 1 2 1 3 1 4 1 5 1 6 1 7 2 8 2 9 4 10 4 11 11 12 15 13 19 14 23 15 8 16 10 17 21 18 29 19 39 20 47 21 45 22 47 23 58 24 66 25 76 26 84   !"#"(%0(0*)"+"&(%$,'" 12"#"$%,/**-+*$" $" *$" ($" 3$" '$" &$$" $" -" &$" &-" *$"335 Appendix E The International Phonetic Alphabet    336 Appendix F Fundamental Properties of English Phones  This appendix presents a table that summarizes the fundamental pho-netic properties of English phones. The grammar was simple because it was short, generated a few expressions, and the inferences involved in generating these expressions were relatively few—less than 30, in fact. Throughout this book we have seen substantially more complex gram-mars.1 Take for instance syllable.swipl, the program we devel-oped in Chapter 2. It generates over 20,000 syllables, and involves making more than a million inferences to generate many of them. Calcu-lating the complexity of such a grammar by calculating the number of inferences associated to each generated expression piecemeal is just not practical. And is furthermore prone to error. As a matter of fact, calcu-lating inferences by hand is impractical even if we work only with a small sample of the generated expressions. Fortunately, the computation of complexities is a well defined process. As such, it can be mechanized. rank.swipl is a PROLOG program developed by Oleg Lokhvitsky that calculates mechanically the complexity of any grammar (assuming said grammar fits the format that we have presented in this book). This program has been posted in the companion website to this book (https://sites.google.com/site/acildlipp).2                                                             1 Or grammar fragments. We ignore the difference in this appendix. 2 Michael Seydel pointed out one simple way to generate a few inference counts piecemeal. It involves triple queries like the following: findall(X,syllable(X),Y), nth1(1001,Y,X), time(syllable(X)).  CALCULATING THE COMPLEXITY OF A GRAMMAR / 339 To use rank.swipl to generate the complexity of a given grammar (say, syllable.swipl), consult these two programs. You will notice that PROLOG will automatically consult the three pro-grams rank.swipl needs in order to run properly. At this point, enter rank(syllable/1, 1000, Inferences). PROLOG will respond Inferences = 83096, meaning that it takes 83096 inferences to generate the 1000th syllable. To find out what this syllable is we can use the four-place version of rank.swipl and enter rank(syllable/1, 1000, Inferences, Syllable). PROLOG will respond: Inferences = 83096, Syllable = [t,e,w,ð]. thus informing us that the 1000th syllable generated by syllable. swipl is[t,e,w,ð].3 Notice that our queries said "syllable/1" rather than just "syl-lable". This is because syllable.swipl contains two procedures called "syllable". But one of them is a unary predicate and the other, binary. By mentioning the valences of these predicates we are able to identify which of the predicates we mean.                                                                                                                         This asks PROLOG to (i) find all syllables, (ii) identify the one occupying the 1001st position, and (iii) count the number of inferences PROLOG makes to determine whether that syllable is, indeed, a syllable. Remember to enter the following if your screen floods with the output of the findall clause (see Chapter 2): set_prolog_flag(toplevel_print_options,[portray(true),max_de pth(6)]). 3 There is also a five-place version of rank that will allow us to find inference numbers for proper subsets of the generated expressions. Thus, if we want to calculate the numbers of inferences required, say, by three-phone syllables, we can enter the following. rank(syllable/1, Rank, Inferences, Syllable, [[_,_,_]]). PROLOG will respond by giving us the number of inferences needed for the three-phone syllable of the first rank. Entering semicolons repeatedly will make PROLOG respond with the numbers of inferences of the three-phone syllables of the successive ranks. rank/5 will also come in handy when we have consulted several programs that define the same predicate (but we are only interested in the inferences associated to the predicate defined only by some of these programs). 340 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS The rank predicate can be used more generally to find out how many inferences are needed to generate each and every syllable admit-ted by syllable.swipl. To do this you could try entering the following (and following it up with semicolons). It will be noticed that the only difference between this query and the preceding one is that this query has a variable over ranks instead of a specific rank as second ar-gument (the variable in this query is Rank and the specific rank in the preceding one was 1000). rank(syllable/1, Rank, Inferences, Syllable). Needless to say, this task would be overwhelming, as sylla-ble.swipl admits 20608 syllables! A more manageable task would be to find out the inferences for a sample of the syllables generated— say, the 1st, the 1001st, the 2001st, and so on to the 20001st. But this, too, seems needlessly repetitive. Since the procedure is well defined, we should be able to mechanize it as well, by asking PROLOG to perform all of these computations in one and the same request. This can be done through the ranks predicate (note the plural on ranks here), which is also defined in rank.swipl. Queries to this predicate will have the following general form (notice that this query has only two arguments, the second one being a list of three items): ranks(Predicate/Arity,[Ranks,Inferences,Expressions]). For the case at hand, we would therefore enter: ranks(syllable/1,[[1,1001,2001,3001,4001,5001,6001, 7001,8001,9001,10001,11001,12001,13001,14001,15001, 16001,17001,18001,19001,20001],Inferences,Syllables ]). PROLOG will respond, after a couple seconds, with a list of numbers (inferences) and a list of expressions (syllables), where the former are the numbers of inferences needed to generate the latter.4 The numbers of                                                             4 Since the ranks of the syllables of our sample were evenly spaced, it would have been easier to use printData instead of ranks. printData is a predicate that is packaged within rank.swipl. Its querying format is this: printData(Table,Interval,FirstRank,LastRank,Destination). Table is a display of the ranks that are to be sampled and their associated inference num-bers (as well as the expressions with these ranks and inference numbers); Interval represents the distance between the ranks that are to be sampled; FirstRank is the first rank to be sampled; LastRank is the last rank to be sampled; Destination is a varia-ble over three printing destinations. The computational complexity of syllable.swipl can therefore be very accurately described as linear. We could of course work with a larger sample of syllables (the infer-ences for the syllables at every 500th rank, at every 100th rank, and so on). But these growing samples soon reach a point of diminishing re-turns. So good sampling procedures are key here. And unavoidable with more realistic grammars—i.e. those that generate infinitely many ex-pressions. We must leave it to future research to determine what the best sampling procedures are in such cases. The computational complexities of the syllables generated by syl-lable.swipl grew steadily with their ranks. But this will not happen in every grammar. Take for example  syntax.swipl, the grammar we discussed in Chapter 7. Here is a chart that diagrams how the com-plexities of the expressions generated by this grammar grow by rank (the expressions charted here are those found at 500-rank intervals and starting with the first).  As can be readily seen, the complexities of the expressions generated by syntax.swipl do not rise steadily with rank. This is because com-plexities of some generated expressions may either repeat or reduce the complexities of expressions of earlier ranks. And this obstructs the view of the way in which the complexities of the generated expressions grow. So suppose we were to delete repeated complexities and reorder the ," '," ;," %," $," 0,," 0'," 0;," 0%," 0$," ',," ," +,,," 0,,,," 0+,,," ',,,," '+,,," &,,,," 1234250"344 / A COMPUTATIONAL INTRODUCTION TO LINGUISTICS remaining ones in strictly increasing order. We would obtain a scatter which may be approximated exceedingly well by a cubic function:    It should be clear that this new function does not describe the complexi-ties at each rank, but rather the step-by-step growth of the complexities. It is therefore called a degree-to-inference function. We take it that such degree-to-inference functions represent more accurately the complexity of a grammar.   !"#",/0%$*(&")"&/;<%('"="'+/'$&(")"0&/<0<" -."#",/*%&;*" ," +," 0,," 0+," ',," '+," ," +" 0," 0+" '," 1234250" >?@!/"91234250:"345 Appendix H Program Listings  PROGRAM 0:  fulldisplay.swipl ?- set_prolog_flag(toplevel_print_options, [portray(true),max_depth(0)]).
